About this edition

An English rendering of Futūḥ Miṣr wa-l-Maghrib. Arabic text: Maktabat al-Thaqāfa al-Dīniyya edition, 1415 AH.

In the name of God, the Most Compassionate, the Most Merciful. In Him I seek help. May God bless Muḥammad, His noble Prophet.

The shaykh, the jurist, the imām, the scholar, the ḥāfiẓ, Abū Ṭāhir Aḥmad ibn Muḥammad ibn Aḥmad ibn Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm al-Silafī al-Aṣbahānī, informed us—this was read to him while I listened at the frontier city of Alexandria, may God Most High protect it. He said: The shaykh Abū Ṣādiq Murshid ibn Yaḥyā ibn al-Qāsim ibn ʿAlī al-Madanī informed us; this was read to him by me. He said: The shaykh Abū al-Ḥasan ʿAlī ibn Munīr ibn Aḥmad al-Khallāl informed us in his book in the year 435. Abū Bakr Muḥammad ibn Aḥmad ibn al-Faraj al-Qammāḥ informed us. Abū al-Qāsim ʿAlī ibn al-Ḥasan ibn Khalaf ibn Qudayd al-Azdī informed us. Abū al-Qāsim ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam related to us. Muḥammad ibn Ismāʿīl al-Kaʿbī related to us. My father related to me, from Ḥarmala ibn ʿImrān al-Tujībī, from Abū Qubayl, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, who said:

“The world was created in five forms, like a bird, with its head, breast, two wings, and tail. The head is Mecca, Medina, and Yemen; the breast is Syria and Egypt; the right wing is Iraq. Beyond Iraq is a people called Wāq, and beyond Wāq a people called Wāq Wāq. Beyond them are peoples known only to God, Mighty and Glorious. The left wing is Sind, beyond Sind is India, beyond India a people called Nāsik, and beyond Nāsik a people called Mansik. Beyond them are peoples known only to God, Mighty and Glorious. The tail extends from Dhāt al-Ḥammām to the place where the sun sets. The worst part of the bird is its tail.”

The account of the Messenger of God’s bequest concerning the Copts

Narrated Ashhab ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz and ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama, who said: Mālik ibn Anas related to us, from Ibn Shihāb, from a son of Kaʿb ibn Mālik, that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: “When you conquer Egypt,

treat the Copts well, for they have a covenant and kinship .” Ibn Shihāb said: It used to be said that the mother of Ismāʿīl, son of Ibrāhīm ﵉ both—was one of them.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ and Muḥammad ibn Ramḥ related to us, and they said: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Ibn Shihāb, from a son of Kaʿb ibn Mālik, from the Messenger of God, a report like it.

Al-Layth said: I asked Ibn Shihāb, “What is their kinship?” He said: “The mother of Ismāʿīl was one of them.” Our father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, and Ḥāmid ibn Yaḥyā informed us, and they said: Sufyān ibn ʿUyayna related to us, from al-Zuhrī—I think from a son of Kaʿb ibn Mālik—from the Messenger of God, a report like it.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Hishām related to us. Ziyād ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Bakkāʾī related to us, from Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq, who said: Muḥammad ibn Muslim ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Shihāb al-Zuhrī related to me that ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Kaʿb ibn Mālik al-Anṣārī, then al-Sulamī, related to him from the Messenger of God, a report like it. Ibn Isḥāq said: I asked Muḥammad ibn Muslim, “What kinship did the Messenger of God mention that they had?” He replied: “Hājar, the mother of Ismāʿīl, was one of them.” Our father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, related to us; Rashdīn ibn Saʿd related to me. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wahb related to us, from Ḥarmala ibn ʿImrān al-Tujībī, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shumāsa al-Mahrī, who said: I heard Abū Dharr say: The Messenger of God ﷺ said: “You will conquer a land in which the qīrāṭ is mentioned, so treat its people well, for they have a covenant and kinship .”

Saʿīd ibn Maysara related to us, from Isḥāq ibn al-Furāt, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from al-Aswad ibn Mālik al-Ḥimyarī, from Buḥayr ibn Dhākhir al-Maʿāfirī, from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, from ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵄—that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: “God, Mighty and Glorious, will open Egypt to you after me, so treat its Copts well, for you have through them marriage ties and a covenant.”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama and Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Ibn Hubayra, that Abū Sālim al-Jayshānī, Sufyān ibn Hāniʾ, informed him that one of the Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ had informed him that he heard the Messenger of God say: “You will become armies, and the best of your armies will be the people of the West among you. So fear God in your treatment of the Copts; do not devour them as one devours fresh green vegetation .”

My father related to us; Ismāʿīl ibn ʿAyyāsh related to us, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ziyād, from Muslim ibn Yasār, that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: “Treat the Copts well, for you will find them excellent helpers in fighting your enemy .”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from al-Layth and Ibn Lahīʿa. ʿAbd al-Malik said: Ibn Wahb informed us, from ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that Abū Salama ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān related to him that the Messenger of God ﷺ charged at the time of his death that the Jews be expelled from the Arabian Peninsula, and said: “God, God, concerning the Copts of Egypt: You will prevail over them, and they will be a reserve and helpers for you in the path of God.”

He said: ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Wahb related to us, from Mūsā ibn Ayyūb al-Ghāfiqī, from a man of al-Zubad, that the Messenger of God ﷺ fell ill, lost consciousness, and then awoke. He said, “Treat the dark, curly-haired people well.” Then he lost consciousness a second time and awoke, and said the same thing. Then he lost consciousness a third time and said the same thing. The people said, “If only we could ask the Messenger of God who the dark, curly-haired people are!” He awoke, and they asked him. He said, “The Copts of Egypt: they are kin by marriage and affinity, and they are your helpers against your enemy and your helpers in your religion.” They said, “How will they be our helpers in our religion, Messenger of God?” He said, “They will spare you the work of this world, so that you may devote yourselves to worship. Whoever is content with the wrong done to them is like one who does it to them; and whoever abhors the injustice done to them is like one who keeps clear of it.” ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Wahb related to us, from Abū Hānīʾ al-Khawlānī, from Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Ḥublī, ʿAmr ibn Ḥurayth, and others, that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: “You will come upon a people with curly hair. Treat them well, for they are a strength to you and a means of reaching your enemy, by God Most High’s permission”—meaning the Copts of Egypt. Abū al-Aswad related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Abū Hānīʾ, who heard al-Ḥublī and ʿAmr ibn Ḥurayth relate from the Messenger of God ﷺ the like of this report. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Hishām related to us; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wahb informed us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, who told me: ʿUmar, the client of Ghufra, related that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: “God, God, concerning the people of the covenant—the people of the black earth, the dark, curly-haired people—for they are kin by lineage and affinity.” ʿUmar, the client of Ghufra, said: Their affinity is that the Messenger of God ﷺ took a concubine from among them; their lineage is that Hājar, the mother of the Prophet Ismāʿīl ﷺ was one of them. Ibn Wahb said: Ibn Lahīʿa informed me that the mother of Ismāʿīl, Hājar, was from Umm al-ʿArab, a village that lay opposite al-Faramā in Egypt. ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; Marwān al-Qaṣṣāṣ informed us, saying: Three of the prophets, blessings and ﵈, formed marital ties with the Copts: Ibrāhīm, the Intimate Friend of the All-Merciful ﵇ took Hājar as a concubine; Yūsuf ﵇ married the daughter of the lord of ʿAyn Shams; and the Messenger of God ﷺ took Māriya as a concubine. Hānīʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that Hājar’s village was Yāq, which is by Umm Dunayn. Hājar was buried, when she died, in the al-Ḥijr, as Ibn Hishām related to us, from Ziyād ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from Ibn Isḥāq. Ibn Hishām said: The Arabs say Hājar and Ājar, substituting the alif for the hāʾ, just as they say harāqa al-māʾ and arāqa al-māʾ (“he poured out the water”), and the like.

Mention of Some of Egypt’s Merits

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Bakr ibn Sawāda and Bakr ibn ʿAmr al-Khawlānī, who traced the report back to ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr. He said: “The Copts of Egypt are the noblest of all non-Arabs, the most open-handed, the finest in lineage, and the closest in kinship to the Arabs generally and to Quraysh in particular. Whoever wishes to recall Paradise, or to see its like in this world, let him look at the land of Egypt when its crops turn green and its fruits ripen.” Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn ʿAmr al-Maʿāfirī, from Kaʿb al-Aḥbār, who said: “Whoever wishes to see something like Paradise, let him look at Egypt when its harvest is ripe.” Someone other than Abū al-Aswad said: “At the land of Egypt when it blossoms.” Someone other than Ibn Lahīʿa said: Among them were the magicians, and they all believed at the same hour. We know of no group that embraced the faith at one hour in greater numbers than the Copts. He said: They were as ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Hubayra al-Sabaʾī and Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb al-Mālikī, each adding to the other’s account: twelve chief magicians, and under the authority of each magician were twenty chiefs of a thousand. Under the authority of each of those chiefs was a thousand magicians. Thus all the magicians numbered two hundred and forty thousand, and two hundred and fifty-two people, including the chiefs and the commanders. When they witnessed what they witnessed, they became certain that it was from Heaven, and that magic could not withstand God’s command. At that, the twelve chiefs fell down prostrate; the commanders followed them, and those who remained followed the commanders. They said: “We believe in the Lord of the worlds, the Lord of Moses and Aaron.”

The Copts of Egypt are the noblest of all non-Arabs, the most open-handed, the finest in lineage, and the closest in kinship to the Arabs generally and to Quraysh in particular. Whoever wishes to recall Paradise, or to see its like in this world, let him look at the land of Egypt when its crops turn green and its fruits ripen.

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn ʿAmr al-Maʿāfirī, from Kaʿb al-Aḥbār, who said: “Whoever wishes to see something like Paradise, let him look at Egypt when its harvest is ripe.” Someone other than Abū al-Aswad said: “At the land of Egypt when it blossoms.”

Someone other than Ibn Lahīʿa said: Among them were the magicians, and they all believed at the same hour. We know of no group that embraced the faith at one hour in greater numbers than the Copts.

He said: They were as ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Hubayra al-Sabaʾī and Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb al-Mālikī, each adding to the other’s account: twelve chief magicians, and under the authority of each magician were twenty chiefs of a thousand. Under the authority of each of those chiefs was a thousand magicians. Thus all the magicians numbered two hundred and forty thousand, and two hundred and fifty-two people, including the chiefs and the commanders. When they witnessed what they witnessed, they became certain that it was from Heaven, and that magic could not withstand God’s command. At that, the twelve chiefs fell down prostrate; the commanders followed them, and those who remained followed the commanders. They said: “We believe in the Lord of the worlds, the Lord of Moses and Aaron.”

Hānīʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that Tubayʿ said: “They were among the companions of Moses, blessings be upon him.” Not one of them was led astray along with those of the Children of Israel who were led astray into worshipping the calf.

Hānīʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that Tubayʿ used to say: “Never did a group believe at a single hour as large as the group of the Copts.”

Abū Ṣāliḥ related to us; al-Layth related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who had been told that Kaʿb al-Aḥbār used to say: “The Copts of Egypt are like a thicket: whenever it is cut down, it grows back, until God, by means of them and their craft, destroys the islands of the Romans.” Egypt was as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ and ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shumāsa al-Mahrī, from Abū Ruhm al-Samāʿī: a land of aqueducts and bridges, laid out with calculation and careful planning, so that water ran beneath its houses and canals . They would hold it back as they wished and release it as they wished. This is the meaning of God Almighty’s words, reporting what Pharaoh said: “Is the kingdom of Egypt not mine, and these rivers flowing beneath me? Do you not see?” At that time, there was no kingdom on earth greater than the kingdom of Egypt.

The gardens stretched along both banks of the Nile, from its beginning to its end, on both sides—from Aswān to Rashīd—and there were seven canals: the Canal of Alexandria, the Canal of Sakhā, the Canal of Damietta, the Canal of Memphis, the Canal of al-Fayyūm, the Canal of al-Manhā, and the Canal of Sardūs. They were continuous gardens, with no part separated from another. The crops extended between the two mountain ranges, from one end of Egypt to the other, wherever the water could reach. The whole land of Egypt was irrigated by sixteen cubits, owing to the way they had measured and planned its aqueducts and canals. and its bridges. This is what God—mighty and exalted is He—says: “How many gardens and springs they left behind, and fields of crops and a noble station!” He said: The noble stations were the pulpits; there were a thousand pulpits there.

He said: As for the Fayyūm and al-Manhā canals, Joseph ﵇—dug them . I shall relate how this came about in its proper place, God willing.

As for the Sirdūs Canal, Hāmān was the one who dug it.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ and ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; they said: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yaḥyā ibn Maymūn al-Ḥaḍramī, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ: Pharaoh appointed Hāmān to dig the Sirdūs Canal. When he began digging it, the people of every village came to him, asking him to make the canal run beneath their village and offering him money. He would take it toward one village on the eastern side, then turn it back toward a village behind the qibla, then turn it toward a village in the west, then turn it toward the people of a village on the qibla side. He took money from the people of every village, until he had amassed one hundred thousand dinars. He brought this, carrying it to Pharaoh. Pharaoh asked him about it, and he told him what he had done while digging the canal. Pharaoh said to him: “Woe to you! A ruler ought to show kindness to his subjects , give generously to them, and not covet what they possess. Return to the people of every village what you took from them.” So he returned it all to its owners. He said: No canal in Egypt is known to be more winding than this one, because of what Hāmān did in digging it. Asad related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from a person who had related to him, that Hāmān was a Nabataean.

The Lake of Alexandria, as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, was entirely a vineyard belonging to the wife of al-Muqawqis. She used to collect its tax from them in wine, at a fixed rate imposed upon them. The wine became so plentiful for her that she was hard pressed by it. She said: “I have no need of wine. Give me dinars instead.”

They said: “We have none.” So she sent water upon them and flooded it . It became a lake in which fish were caught, until the Banū al-ʿAbbās drained it. They closed its embankments and cultivated it.

The Settlement of the Copts in Egypt

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from ʿAyyāsh ibn ʿAbbās al-Qitbānī, from Ḥanash ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Ṣanʿānī, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās, who said: Noah ﵇—had four sons: Sām ibn Nūḥ, Ḥām ibn Nūḥ, Yāfith ibn Nūḥ, and Yaḥṭūn ibn Nūḥ. Noah ﵇—earnestly petitioned God, mighty and exalted is He, asking Him to grant him a response concerning his sons and their descendants once they had fully grown and prospered. He promised him this. At the hour before dawn, Noah called his sons while they were asleep. He called Sām, and Sām answered him, hurrying to him. Sām called out to his sons, but none of them answered except his son Arfakhshadh. He took him along with him until they came to Noah. Noah placed his right hand upon Sām and his left upon Arfakhshadh ibn Sām, and asked God, mighty and exalted is He, to bless Sām with the best of blessings, and to place kingship and prophethood among the descendants of Arfakhshadh. Then he called Ḥām. He looked to his right and left but did not answer, and neither he nor any of his sons rose to go to Noah. Noah prayed to God, mighty and exalted is He, to make Ḥām’s sons humiliated and to make them slaves to the descendants of Sām.

Miṣr ibn Yabṣur ibn Ḥām was asleep beside his grandfather Ḥām. When he heard Noah’s prayer against his grandfather and his sons, he hurried to Noah and said: “Grandfather, I have answered you, though my father and none of his sons answered. Give me a share of your prayer .” Noah ﵇—was glad, placed his hand upon Miṣr’s head, and said: “O God, he has answered my call. Bless him and his descendants, and settle him in the blessed land that is the mother of lands and the succor of people, whose river is the finest of the world’s rivers. Place in it the best of blessings, subject the earth to him and his sons and make it easy for them, and strengthen them upon it.”

Then he called his son Yāfith, but neither he nor any of his sons answered him. Noah prayed to God, mighty and exalted is He, against them, that He make them the worst of creatures.

Then he called his son Yaḥṭūn, and he answered him. Noah prayed to God, mighty and exalted is He, to grant him blessing, but Yaḥṭūn had neither child nor descendants.

Sām lived blessed until he died, and his son Arfakhshadh ibn Sām lived blessed until he died. Kingship pleasing to God, prophethood, and blessing were among the descendants of Arfakhshadh ibn Sām.

The eldest son of Ḥām was Kanʿān ibn Ḥām. He was the one conceived amid the convulsion aboard the Ark , and Noah prayed against him. He emerged black. His descendants were marked by coarseness, weariness, and tyranny. He is the forefather of all the Sudanese and Ethiopians. His second son was Kūsh ibn Ḥām, the forefather of the Sindis and Indians; his third son was Fūṭ ibn Ḥām, the forefather of the Berbers; and his fourth and youngest son was Bayṣar ibn Ḥām, the forefather of all the Copts.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Sulaymān ibn Bilāl related to us. Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us; al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Yaḥyā ibn Saʿīd, from Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyab, who said: The Prophet Noah had three sons: Sām, Ḥām, and Yāfith. Each of the three had three sons. Sām was the forefather of the Arabs, Persians, and Romans; Yāfith was the forefather of the Slavs, Turks, Gog, and Magog; and Ḥām was the forefather of the Sudanese, Berbers, and Copts.

Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān. He said: Bayṣar ibn Ḥām had four sons: Miṣr ibn Bayṣar, the eldest of them and the one for whom Noah ﷺ—prayed as he did; Fāriq ibn Bayṣar; Māḥ ibn Bayṣar; and Yāḥ ibn Bayṣar.

Someone other than ʿUthmān said: Miṣr had four sons: Qifṭ ibn Miṣr, Ashmun ibn Miṣr, Atrīb ibn Miṣr, and Ṣā ibn Miṣr.

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ and Yaḥyā ibn Khālid related to us from Ibn Lahīʿa and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khālid, one of them adding to the account of the other. ʿUthmān would sometimes say: Khālid ibn Najīḥ related to me, from Ibn Lahīʿa and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khālid. They said: The first to settle in Egypt after God drowned the people of Noah was Bayṣar ibn Ḥām ibn Nūḥ. He settled in Memphis—it was the first city built after the Flood—with his children, who numbered thirty persons, all grown and married. For this reason it was called Māqa; Māqa means “thirty” in the Coptic language. He said:

Bayṣar ibn Ḥām had grown old and weak. Miṣr was his eldest son, and it was he who brought his father and all his brothers to Egypt, where they settled. Egypt was therefore named Miṣr after Miṣr ibn Bayṣar. He secured for himself and his descendants the land extending lengthwise from the two trees behind al-ʿArīsh to Aswān, and breadthwise from Barqa to Aylah. He said: Then Bayṣar ibn Ḥām died and was buried at the site of Abū Hirmīs. Someone other than ʿUthmān said: This was the first cemetery in which anyone was buried in the land of Egypt.

Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ and others. He said: Then Bayṣar ibn Ḥām died, and his son Miṣr succeeded him. Each of Miṣr’s brothers secured a tract of land for himself, apart from the land of Egypt that Miṣr had secured for himself and his descendants. When Miṣr’s descendants and their descendants’ children became numerous, Miṣr allotted each of his sons a tract to possess for himself and his descendants, and divided this Nile among them. He allotted to his son Qifṭ the site of Qifṭ, where Qifṭ settled; hence the name Qifṭ. He also allotted him the lands above it up to Aswān and below it as far as Ashmūn, on both the east and west sides. He allotted to Ashmun the lands from Ashmūn down to Memphis, on both the east and west sides. Ashmun settled in Ashmūn, which was named after him. He allotted to Atrīb the land between Memphis and Ṣā. Atrīb settled in Atrīb, which was named after him. He allotted to Ṣā the land between Ṣā and the sea. Ṣā settled there, and it was named after him. Thus all of Egypt was divided into four parts: two parts in Upper Egypt and two parts in Lower Egypt.

He said: Then Miṣr ibn Bayṣar died, and his son Qifṭ ibn Miṣr succeeded him. Then Qifṭ ibn Miṣr died, and his brother Ashmun ibn Miṣr succeeded him. Then Ashmun ibn Miṣr died, and his brother Atrīb ibn Miṣr succeeded him. Then Atrīb ibn Miṣr died, and his brother Ṣā ibn Miṣr succeeded him. Then Ṣā ibn Miṣr died, and his son Tādārus ibn Ṣā succeeded him. Then Tādārus ibn Ṣā died, and his son Mālīq ibn Tādārus succeeded him. Then Mālīq ibn Tādārus died, and his son Kharbatā ibn Mālīq succeeded him. Then Kharbatā ibn Mālīq died, and his son Kallakan ibn Kharbatā succeeded him. He ruled them for about a hundred years, then died without a child. His brother Māliyā ibn Kharbatā succeeded him. Then Māliyā ibn Kharbatā died, and his son Ṭūṭīs ibn Māliyā succeeded him. He was the one who gave Hājar to Sārah, the wife of Abraham, the Friend of the All-Merciful ﷺ.

The Account of Abraham’s Entry into Egypt

The reason Abraham ﵇—entered Egypt, as Asad ibn Mūsā and others related to us, was that when he was commanded to leave the land of his people and migrate to Syria, he set out with Lot and Sarah. They came to Ḥarrān and settled there. A famine struck the people of Ḥarrān, so he set out with Sarah, intending for Egypt. When he entered it, its king was told of her beauty, and she was described to him .

Sarah’s beauty, as Asad ibn Mūsā related to us, was as follows. He said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khālid related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said: Sarah’s beauty was like Eve’s.

He said: Then he returned to the account of Asad and others. He said: He ordered that she be brought in to him. He asked Abraham ﵇—saying: “Who is this woman?” He said: “My sister.” The king desired her, so God withered his hands and legs. He said to Abraham: “This is your doing; pray to God for me. By God, I will do her no harm.” Abraham prayed to God for him, and God restored the use of his hands and legs. He gave them sheep and cattle. He said: “It is not fitting that this woman should serve herself,” and so he gave her Hājar. 1)

Abū Hurayra used to say: “That, then, is your mother, O sons of the Water of Heaven”—meaning the Arabs.

They related to us from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wahb, from Jarīr ibn Ḥāzim, from Ayyūb, from Muḥammad ibn Sīrīn, from Abū Hurayra, that the Messenger of God ﷺ—said: “Abraham came to the land of a tyrant with Sarah, who was the most beautiful of people. He said to her: ‘This tyrant, if he learns that you are my wife, will overpower me and take you from me. So if he asks you, tell him that you are my sister, for you are my sister in Islam.’ When he entered the land, one of the tyrant’s people saw her and came to him, saying: ‘A woman has entered your land who ought to belong to none but you.’ He sent for her, and she was brought to him. Abraham stood to pray. When she entered before him, he could not restrain himself from reaching out his hand to her but she seized his hand in a fierce grip. He said to her: ‘Pray to God to release my hand, and I will not harm you.’ She did so, and he seized her hand again, more fiercely than the first time. He said the same to her, and she did so, but he seized it again, more fiercely than the first two times. He said: ‘Pray to God to release my hand, and I swear to God I will not harm you.’ She did so, and his hand was released. He summoned the man who had brought her and said: ‘You have brought me a devil, not a human being. Take her out of my land.’ He gave her Hājar, and she set out walking. When Abraham saw her, he came over and said to her: ‘What is it?’ She said: ‘Good news: God has restrained the hand of the wicked man and given me a servant.’ Abū Hurayra said: ‘That, then, is your mother, O sons of the Water of Heaven.’

Ibn Wahb said: Ibn Abī al-Zinād informed me, from his father, from al-Aʿraj, from Abū Hurayra, from the Messenger of God ﷺ—with a similar account. He said: He stood before her, and she rose to perform ablution and pray. Then she said: ‘O God, I have believed in You and in Your Messenger, and I have guarded my chastity from all but my husband; so do not give the unbeliever power over me.’ He collapsed until he kicked with his leg. Al-Aʿraj said: Abū Salama said: Abū Hurayra said: She said, ‘O God, if he dies, people will say that she killed him.’

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us, from Isrāʾīl, from Abū Isḥāq, from Ḥāritha ibn Muḍarrib, from ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib ﵁—that Sarah was the daughter of a king and had been granted great beauty. Abraham ﵇—married her. He passed by a king, who was taken with her, and said to Abraham: “Who is this?” Abraham said to him whatever God willed him to say. When Abraham and Sarah feared that the king would approach her, they prayed against him, and God withered his hands and legs. He said to Abraham: “I know this is your doing. Pray to God for me, and by God, I will do her no harm.” Abraham prayed for him, and God restored the use of his hands and legs. Then the king said: “This is a woman who ought not to serve herself,” and he gave her Hājar, who served her for as long as God willed. Then one day Sarah became angry with her and swore that she would alter three things about her. He said: “You shall circumcise her and pierce her ears.” Then she gave her to Abraham on condition that he do her no harm in this matter. He had intercourse with her, and she conceived; she gave birth to Ishmael, son of Abraham ﵉ both.

He said: As for Sarah, as Wathīma ibn Mūsā related to us, from Salama ibn al-Faḍl and ʿAmr ibn al-Azhar, or one of the two, from Ibn Isḥāq, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, from Abū Hurayra: when she realized she would not bear children, she wanted to offer Hājar to Abraham, but jealousy prevented her.

As for Hājar, as Wathīma ibn Mūsā related to us, from Salama ibn al-Faḍl and ʿAmr ibn al-Azhar, or one of them or both, from Ibn Isḥāq: she was the first to trail her train in order to hide its trace from Sarah. Sarah had sworn to cut off a limb from her. When Hājar heard of this, she put on a shift and trailed her train to hide its trace. Sarah sought her but could not catch her. Abraham said: “Would you be willing to pardon her?” She said: “But what of my oath?” He said: “Circumcise her; that will become a custom for women, and you will be released from your oath.” She did so, and the custom of circumcision continued.

The Amalekites’ Seizure of Egypt and the Account of Joseph

(3 He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān and others. He said: Ṭūṭīs ibn Māliyā died, and his daughter, Khurūbā daughter of Ṭūṭīs, succeeded him. He had no child other than her, and she was the first woman to rule. He said: Then Khurūbā daughter of Ṭūṭīs died, and her paternal cousin Zālfā daughter of Māmūm ibn Māliyā succeeded her. She lived a very long time. They multiplied and prospered, and filled the whole land of Egypt. The Amalekites coveted them, so al-Walīd ibn Dūmaʿ invaded them and fought them fiercely. Then they agreed to make him their king. He ruled them for about a hundred years, then became tyrannical and arrogant and openly committed abominations. God set a lion upon him, which mauled him and ate his flesh. 3)

He said: As ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Hishām related to us, the ʿAmālīq were descended from ʿImlāq; it is also said, ʿAmliq, son of Lāwudh son of Sām.

Abū al-Aswad, Asad ibn Mūsā, and Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn ʿAmr al-Muʿāfirī, from Ibn Ḥujayra, who said: Seventy men from the people of Moses once took shelter in the skull of a man of the ʿAmālīq.

He said: (1 His son al-Rayyān ibn al-Walīd ibn Dūmag succeeded him as their king. He was the patron of Joseph the Prophet ﷺ. The king sent for him and brought him out of prison. 1)

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said: The messenger came to him and said: “Take off your prison clothes, put on new clothes, and go before the king.” The people of the prison prayed for him. He was then thirty years old. When he came before the king, the king saw a young man and said: “Can this young man know my dream when the sorcerers and soothsayers cannot?” He seated him before him and said: “Do not be afraid.”

ʿUthmān and others said in their account: When the king had him speak and questioned him, Joseph grew in his esteem, and his standing became great in the king’s heart. The king gave him his signet ring and entrusted him with everything beyond his own door.

(2 Asad ibn Mūsā related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said: The king clothed him in a gold collar and silk garments, gave him a saddled and adorned mount like the king’s own, and had the drum beaten throughout Egypt to announce that Joseph was the king’s deputy. 2)

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, who said: Abū Saʿīd related to me, from ʿIkrima, that Pharaoh said to Joseph: “I have given you authority over Egypt, but I want my throne to be four fingers higher than yours.” Joseph said: “Very well.”

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān and others. He said: The king seated Joseph on the throne and went into his private quarters with his wives, entrusting the whole affair of Egypt to him. Thus, because Joseph interpreted the king’s dream, he ruled Egypt.

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us; al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to me, saying: Some elders of ours related to us:

Famine became severe among the people of Egypt. They bought food with gold until they had no gold left; then they bought it with silver until they had no silver left; then they bought it with their sheep until they had no sheep left. He continued selling them food until, during those two years, they had no silver, gold, sheep, or cattle left. They came to him in the third [year] and said to him, “Nothing remains for us but ourselves, our families, and our lands.” So Joseph bought all their land for Pharaoh. Then Joseph gave them food to sow, on the condition that Pharaoh receive one fifth.

Mention of the reclamation of al-Fayyūm He said: At that time al-Fayyūm was reclaimed. The reason for this, as Hishām ibn Isḥāq related to us, was that when Joseph ﵇—ruled Egypt, his standing with Pharaoh grew great, and he had passed the age of one hundred. The king’s viziers said to him, “Joseph has lost his knowledge, his mind has changed, and his wisdom is exhausted.” Pharaoh rebuked them, rejected what they had said, and spoke harshly to them. They fell silent, but after some years they returned to the same claim. He said to them, “Bring whatever you wish, from whatever subject, as a test for him.” At that time al-Fayyūm was called al-Jawbah. It was merely a receptacle for the water of Upper Egypt and its surplus. Their judgment agreed that this should be the trial by which they tested Joseph ﵇. They said to Pharaoh, “Ask Joseph to divert the water of al-Jawbah away from it and drain it, so that you may add a town to your realm and revenue to your revenues.” He summoned Joseph and said, “You know the place my daughter so-and-so holds in my heart. I have decided that, when she comes of age, I will seek a town for her. I have found none for her but al-Jawbah, because it is a town both far away and near: it can be approached from every direction only through forest and desert.” Someone other than Hishām said: Al-Fayyūm is in the middle of Egypt just as Egypt is in the middle of the lands, because Egypt can be approached from no direction except through [desert or wilderness ; and likewise it can be approached from no direction within Egypt except through] wilderness and desert.

Hishām said in his account: “I have granted it to her, so do not leave any approach or prospect without reaching it.” Joseph said, “Yes, O king. Whenever you wish, send for me, and, God willing, I shall do it.” He said, “What I most desire and consider most fitting is that you hasten it.” God inspired Joseph to dig three canals: one from a place in Upper Egypt, from such-and-such place to such-and-such place; an eastern canal, from such-and-such place to such-and-such place; and a western canal, from such-and-such place to such-and-such place. Joseph assigned the laborers. He dug the al-Manhā Canal from the upper part of Ashmūn to al-Lāhūn, and ordered the builders to dig al-Lāhūn. He dug the al-Fayyūm Canal, which was the eastern canal, and dug a canal at a village called Tanhamat, one of the villages of al-Fayyūm, which was the western canal. Its water flowed out of the eastern canal and poured into the Nile; it flowed out of the western canal and poured into the desert of Tanhamat to the west, until no water remained in al-Jawbah. Then he brought in the laborers, who cut down and removed the reeds and tamarisk that were in it. This was the beginning of the Nile’s course. Al-Jawbah had become land both cultivated and desert, and the Nile’s water rose. It entered the head of al-Manhā and flowed through it until it reached al-Lāhūn, passed through it to al-Fayyūm, and entered its canal, watering it until it became a deep body of Nile water. The king and his viziers went out to see it. All this took seventy days. When the king looked at it, he said to those viziers, ‘This is the work of a thousand days.’ So it was called al-Fayyūm. It remained under cultivation like the low-lying lands of Egypt.”

He said: I have heard another account of the reclamation of al-Fayyūm. Yaḥyā ibn Khālid al-ʿAdawī related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿah, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that Joseph the Prophet ﷺ—ruled Egypt when he was thirty years old. He spent forty years administering its affairs. The people of Egypt said, “Joseph has grown old and his judgment has become inconsistent.” They deposed him and said, “Choose for yourself a wasteland to which we may grant you, for your own use, so that you can improve it and we can see your judgment there. If we see in your judgment and sound administration what convinces us that your mind is growing sharper, we shall restore you to your rule.” He surveyed the wilderness in the regions of Egypt and chose the site of al-Fayyūm, which they granted to him. He cut the al-Manhā Canal from the Nile to it, until he brought it throughout all al-Fayyūm, and completed the whole excavation in one year.”

It reached us that he did this only by revelation, and that he was able to accomplish it through the great number of laborers and assistants. They looked and saw that they knew of no equal or counterpart anywhere in Egypt to what Joseph had brought to life in al-Fayyūm. They said, “Joseph has never had a better mind, judgment, or administration than he has today.” So they restored him to rule. He remained for another sixty years, completing one hundred years, until he died at the age of one hundred and thirty. God knows best.

He said: Then he returned to the account of Hishām ibn Isḥāq. He said: Joseph ﵇—learned what the king’s viziers had said, and that they had made this a test for him. He said to the king, “I have wisdom and administrative skill beyond what you have seen.” The king said to him, “What is that?” He replied, “I shall settle in al-Fayyūm one household from each district of Egypt, and order the people of each household to build a village for themselves. The villages of al-Fayyūm shall be as numerous as the districts of Egypt. When they have finished building their villages, I shall allot each village as much water as I allot it land: neither more nor less than its land requires. I shall assign each village its water share at a time when it alone receives the water. I shall make low-lying ground serve the high ground, and high ground serve the low-lying ground, at appointed hours of the night and day. I shall assign it measured shares, so that no one is given less than his due or more than his allotment.” Pharaoh said to him, “Is this from the dominion of Heaven?” He replied, “Yes.” Joseph ﵇—began by ordering the villages to be built and setting their boundaries. The first village built in al-Fayyūm was a village called Shānah, the village where Pharaoh’s daughter used to reside. Then he ordered the canal to be dug and the bridges to be built. When they had finished, he began measuring the land and measuring the water. From that day, the science of geometry was introduced; people had not known it before.”

He said: The first to measure the Nile in Egypt was Joseph ﵇. He set up a nilometer at Minf. Then the old woman Dalūkah, daughter of Zabbāʾ, who owned the Wall of the Old Woman, set up a nilometer at Anṣinā, which was small in its cubit measure , and another at Akhmīm. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān set up a nilometer at Ḥulwān, which was small. Usāmah ibn Zayd al-Tanūkhī set up a nilometer at al-Jazīrah during the caliphate of al-Walīd; it was the largest of them. Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us: “I saw the measurer taking measurements at the Minf nilometer, and bringing its increase into al-Fusṭāṭ.”

Mention of the entry of Joseph’s people into Egypt, and of Jacob’s death and burial

He said: During the time of al-Rayān ibn al-Walīd, Jacob ﵇—and his children entered Egypt, as Hishām ibn Isḥāq related to us. They were ninety-three persons, men and women, and Joseph ﵇—settled them between ʿAyn Shams and al-Faramā, a land both cultivated and desert. Asad ibn Mūsā related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said: “Jacob and his children entered Egypt, and they were seventy persons; they left as six hundred thousand.” Asad related to us: Isrāʾīl related to us, from Abū Isḥāq, from Masrūq, who said: “Joseph’s people entered as ninety-three persons, and left as six hundred thousand.”

Joseph, as Asad related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, brought his father and five of his brothers before the king. They greeted him, and the king ordered that land be allotted to them. When Jacob drew near to Egypt, he had sent Judah to Joseph. Joseph went out to meet him, embraced him, and wept.

He said: Then he returned to the account of Hishām ibn Isḥāq. He said: When Jacob entered before Pharaoh and spoke with him—Jacob ﵇ was an old man, forbearing, handsome in face and beard, and loud-voiced—Pharaoh said to him, “How old are you, O elder?” He replied, “One hundred and twenty.” Bimīn, Pharaoh’s magician, had described the characteristics of Jacob, Joseph, and Moses ﵈, in his books, and had foretold that Egypt’s ruin and the destruction of its people would come at their hands. He had set down the barbāyāt and descriptions of those at whose hands Egypt would be ruined. When he saw Jacob, he rose to his seat. The first thing he asked him was, “Whom do you worship, O elder?” Jacob replied, “I worship God, the God of everything.” He said to him, “How can you worship what you cannot see?” Jacob replied, “He is too great and exalted for anyone to see Him.” Bimīn said, “But we can see our gods.”

Then he said to him: “Your gods are the work of human hands; they perish and decay. But my God is greater and more exalted, and He is nearer to us than the jugular vein.” Bimīn looked toward Pharaoh and said, “This is the one at whose hands the ruin of our land will come.” Pharaoh said, “In our days or in the days of others?” He replied, “Not in your days nor in the days of your sons, O king.” The king said, “Do you find this among what your God has decreed?” He replied, “Yes.”

He said, “How, then, can we kill someone through whose hands his God intends the destruction of his people? Pay no heed to this talk.” Asad ibn Mūsā related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, who said: Abū Ḥafṣ al-Kilāʿī related to me, from Tubayʿ , from Kaʿb, that Jacob lived in the land of Egypt for sixteen years. When death came upon him, he said to Joseph, “Do not bury me in Egypt, and…”

Jacob said: “Your gods are the handiwork of the children of Adam, who die and decay,¹ whereas my God is mightier and more exalted. He is nearer to us than the jugular vein.” Bimīn looked at Pharaoh and said: “This is the one at whose hands our lands will be destroyed.” Pharaoh said: “In our days, or in the days of others?” He replied: “Not in your days, nor in the days of your sons, O king.” The king said: “Do you find this among the things your God has decreed?” He said: “Yes.”

He said: “How, then, could we kill someone through whom his God intends the destruction of his people? Pay no heed to this talk.” Asad ibn Mūsā related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, who said: Abū Ḥafṣ al-Kilāʿī related to me, from Tubayʿ,³ from Kaʿb, that Jacob lived in the land of Egypt for sixteen years. When death came upon him, he said to Joseph: “Do not bury me in Egypt. When I die, carry me and bury me in the cave of Mount Hebron.” Asad related to us, from Khālid, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, that Hebron is the mosque of Abraham ﵇—today, and lies eighteen miles from Jerusalem.²

Then he returned to the account of al-Kilāʿī, from Tubayʿ, from Kaʿb. He said: When Jacob died, they embalmed him with myrrh and aloe. Someone other than Asad said: They placed him in a teak coffin. Asad said in his account: They did this to him for forty days, until Joseph spoke with Pharaoh and informed him that his father had died and had asked to be buried in the land of Canaan. Pharaoh gave him permission, and Joseph went out with the noblest people of Egypt, buried him, and returned. ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from someone who related to him, saying: Jacob was buried in Egypt and stayed there for about three years; then he was carried to Jerusalem. He had charged them to do this when he died.* God knows best.

The Death of Joseph

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ. He said: Then Rayyān ibn al-Walīd died—*)

—and his son Dārim ibn al-Rayyān ruled them after him. Someone other than ʿUthmān said: Joseph—blessings of God be upon him—died in his time. When death came upon him, he said: “You will be brought out of the land of Egypt to the land of your forefathers, as Asad ibn Mūsā related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, who said: Abū Ḥafṣ al-Kilāʿī related to me, from Tubayʿ, from Kaʿb. So carry my bones with you.” He died, and they placed him in a coffin¹ and buried him.

Muḥammad ibn Asʿad related to us; Abū al-Aḥwaṣ related to us, from Simāk ibn Ḥarb, who said: (2 Joseph—blessings of God be upon him—was buried on one bank of the Nile. The bank where he lay became fertile, while the other became barren. They moved him to the other bank, and the bank to which they moved him became fertile while the other became barren. When they saw this, they gathered his bones, placed them in an iron chest, and attached a chain to it. They set up a post on the bank of the Nile and placed an iron bar at its base. They fastened the chain to the bar and cast the chest into the middle of the Nile, whereupon both banks became fertile.²)

Al-ʿAbbās ibn Ṭālib related to us; ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Ziyād related to us, from Yūnus, from al-Ḥasan, that Joseph ﵇—was cast into the well when he was seventeen years old. He remained there until he met Jacob ﵇—and his family, eighty years later; then he lived another twenty-three years. He died at the age of one hundred and twenty. It is also said that he died at the age of one hundred and thirty.

The Kings of Egypt after the Time of Joseph

(3 Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ and others, who said: Dārim became tyrannical and arrogant after Joseph ﵇—and openly worshipped idols. He embarked on a ship on the Nile, and God sent against him a violent wind that drowned him and those with him between Ṭurā and the place of Ḥulwān. Kāshim ibn Maʿdān ruled them after him; he was a tyrant and an oppressor.³)

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from Abū Ḥafṣ al-Kilāʿī, from Tubayʿ, from Kaʿb, who said: When Joseph ﵇—died, the people of Egypt enslaved the Children of Israel.

Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān, who said: Kāshim ibn Maʿdān died, and Pharaoh of Moses ruled them after him. Someone other than ʿUthmān said: His name was Ṭalmā, an Egyptian Copt.

(1 My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, related to us, saying: I heard al-Layth ibn Saʿd and Ibn Lahīʿa—or one of them—say: He was an Egyptian Copt called Ṭalmā.¹)

Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Fāṭima related to us, from his teachers, who said: He was from Firʿān ibn Baliyy. His name was al-Walīd ibn Muṣʿab. He was short, speckled, and his beard reached down to his feet.

Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us, saying: It was related to us from Hāniʾ ibn al-Mundhir that he was one of the ʿAmālīq and was known by the kunya Abū Marra.

Yazīd ibn Abī Salama related to us, from Jarīr, from ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maysara, from al-Nazzāl ibn Sabra, from Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq ﵁—who said: “Pharaoh was toothless.” It is said, rather, that he was a man from Lakhm. God knows best.

Whoever claims that he was one of the ʿAmālīq: we have already mentioned the reason why the ʿAmālīq ruled Egypt. Whoever claims that he was from Firʿān ibn Baliyy may refer to what Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us. He said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Fāṭima related to us, from his teachers, that the king of Egypt died, and a group of the king’s sons disputed the kingship—for the king had appointed no successor. When the dispute grew serious, they called for a settlement and agreed that the first person to appear at the mountain pass, the Pass of the Mountain, would judge between them. Pharaoh appeared between two loads of natron, which he had brought to sell. He was a man from Firʿān ibn Baliyy. They stopped him and said: “We have appointed you to judge between us in our dispute over the kingship.” They gave him their pledges that they would accept his judgment. When he had secured their agreement, he said:

“I have decided to make myself king over you. That will best remove your grudges and bring your affairs together; thereafter, the decision is yours.” They appointed him over them because they each disdained the others, and they seated him in the royal residence at Memphis. He sent for the retainer of each man among them, promising and enticing each with the kingship over his rival, and set a night for each man to kill his rival. They did so, and those men acknowledged his divinity. He did not have the arrogance of kings. God knows best. He ruled them for about five hundred years. What happened between him and Moses ﵇—is what God ﵎ has recounted of their story in the Qurʾān.

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ and others, who said: Pharaoh ruled Egypt for five hundred years, until God Most High drowned him.

My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, related to us; Khallād ibn Sulaymān al-Ḥaḍramī related to us, saying:

I heard Abū al-Ashras say: “Pharaoh lived for four hundred years, with youth coming to him and going away from him.”

My father related to us; Khallād ibn Sulaymān related to us, saying: I heard Ibrāhīm ibn Miqsam say: “Pharaoh lived for four hundred years without a headache.¹ It is said that his realm extended from Egypt to Ifrīqiya.”

He used to sit on the thrones of Pharaoh, as Asad related to us, from Khālid, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās: two hundred thrones, upon which sat people wearing brocade and gold bracelets.

He had appointed Hāmān over the people. He said: “O Hāmān, build for me a tower, that I may reach the ways—the ways of the heavens.”²

That is, from each heaven to the next there is a way. God occupied Pharaoh with the signs Moses ﵇—had brought, and Hāmān did not build the tower for him.

The Carrying of Joseph’s Bones to Syria

He said: In his time Joseph’s bones were carried from Egypt to Syria. The reason for their being carried, according to what Muḥammad ibn Asʿad al-Taghlibī related to us, from Abū al-Aḥwaṣ, from Simāk ibn Ḥarb, was that the Messenger of God ﷺ—was returning from Syria, accompanied by Zayd ibn Ḥāritha. He passed a solitary tent at evening and approached it. He said: “Peace be upon you.” The master of the house returned his greeting. The Messenger of God ﷺ—said: “A guest.” He replied: “Come down and spend the night as my guest.” When morning came and the Messenger wished to depart, the old man said: “Take some of what remains of your hospitality.” They took some, then the Messenger of God ﷺ—set out. When the Messenger’s cause became manifest and God granted him victory, the old man came on his mount and halted at the mosque door. He entered and began looking over the faces of the men. They said to him: “There is the Messenger of God.” The Messenger of God ﷺ—said: “What do you need?” He replied: “By God, I do not know, except that a man once stayed with me and I honored his stay.” The Messenger of God ﷺ—said to him: “And are you so-and-so?” He said:

“Yes.” He said: “And how is so-and-so’s mother?” He replied: “Well.” He said: “And how are you all?” He replied: “Well.” And the Messenger of God ﷺ had said to him as he departed: “When you hear of a prophet who has appeared in Tihāmah, go to him, for you will find good with him.” The Messenger of God ﷺ said to him: “Wish for whatever you like; today, you will not wish for anything without being given it.” He said: “I ask you for eighty sheep.” The Messenger of God ﷺ laughed, then said: “ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf, get up and give them to him in full.” Then the Messenger of God ﷺ turned to his Companions and said: “How much this old man needed to be like Moses’ old woman!” We said: “Messenger of God, who was Moses’ old woman?” He said: “She was Joseph’s daughter. She lived until she had become a very old woman who had lost her sight. When Moses set out by night with the Children of Israel, a mist came over them and blocked their view of the road. Moses was told: ‘You will not cross unless you bring Joseph’s bones with you.’ He said: ‘Who knows where they are?’ They said: ‘His daughter is a very old woman, blind; we left her in the settlements.’ Moses turned back. When she heard his voice, she said: ‘Moses?’ Moses said: ‘Yes.’ She asked: ‘What brought you back?’ He said: ‘I have been ordered to carry Joseph’s bones.’ She said: ‘You would not cross unless I came with you.’ He said: ‘Show me where Joseph’s bones are.’ She said: ‘I will do so only if you grant me what I asked of you.’ He said: ‘You shall have what you asked.’ She said: ‘Take my hand.’ He took her hand, and she led him to a post on the bank of the Nile. At its base was an iron stake, driven into the ground, with a chain attached to it. She said: ‘We had buried him on that side, and that side became fertile while this side became barren. We moved him to this side, and this side became fertile while the other became barren. When we saw that, we gathered his bones, placed them in an iron chest, and cast it into the middle of the Nile. Then both banks became fertile.’” He carried the chest on his neck, took her hand, and brought her to the army. He said to her: “Ask whatever you wish.” She said: “I ask that I be with you, wherever you are, in Paradise, and that my sight and youth be restored, so that I become young again as I was.” He said: “You shall have that.”

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said: When Joseph ﵇ was dying, he had instructed them to take his bones out of Egypt with them. The people made preparations and set out, but lost their way. Moses said to them: “You have lost your way because of Joseph’s bones. Who can show me where they are?” An old woman called Sāriḥ, daughter of Āshir ibn Jacob, said: “I saw my uncle”—meaning Joseph, when he was buried—“so what will you give me if I show you where he is?” He said: “Whatever you decide.

She showed him where they were. He took Joseph’s bones and then said: “Now choose what you wish.” She said: “I want to be with you in Paradise, wherever you are.”

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; Ibn Lahīʿah informed me, from someone who had related to him, who said: Joseph’s grave was in Egypt, where he remained for about three hundred years; then he was carried to Jerusalem.

The Departure of the Children of Israel from Egypt

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān and others. He said: Then God drowned Pharaoh and his troops in the sea when he pursued the Children of Israel. With him He drowned more than two million of Egypt’s nobles, leading men, and notables.

He said: The reason Pharaoh pursued the Children of Israel was, as Asad ibn Mūsā related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās: God ﵎ revealed to Moses ﵇: “Set out by night with My servants.” The Children of Israel had borrowed jewelry and clothes from Pharaoh’s people, saying: “We have a festival to which we are going.” Moses led them out by night. They numbered six hundred thousand and three thousand and some more; none among them was under twenty or over sixty years of age. This is what Pharaoh meant when he said: “These are indeed a small band, and they have enraged us.”¹

Asad related to us: al-Masʿūdī related to us, from Abū Isḥāq, from Abū ʿUbaydah, who said: They left Egypt numbering six hundred and seventy thousand. Pharaoh said: “These are indeed a small band.”

He said: Then he returned to the account of Asad ibn Mūsā, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said: Pharaoh set out with five hundred thousand, besides the two wings and the center.

Khālid said: Abū Saʿīd related to us, from ʿIkrimah, who said: Pharaoh did not take out anyone older than forty or younger than twenty. This is what God—mighty and majestic is He—said: “He made his people think lightly of the matter, and they obeyed him,”² meaning that he made his people think lightly of pursuing Moses.

He said: The Children of Israel, as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from Mūsā ibn ʿAlī, from his father, were one quarter of Pharaoh’s people.

Asad related to us: Isrāʾīl related to us, from Abū Isḥāq, from ʿAmr ibn Maymūn, who said: Moses ﵇ set out with the Children of Israel. When morning came, Pharaoh ordered a sheep to be brought. It was brought, and he ordered it slaughtered. Then he said: “It must not be finished being skinned until five hundred thousand Copts have gathered before me.” They gathered before him, and Pharaoh said to them: “These are indeed a small band.” Moses’ followers numbered six hundred and seventy thousand.

He said: Moses and his followers took a dry path through the sea. When the last of Moses’ followers had come out and the last of Pharaoh’s followers had entered, the sea surged violently over them. No greater crowd had ever been seen than that day.¹ Pharaoh drowned and was cast onto the seashore so that they could look upon him.

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us: Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh related to us, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said: When Moses reached the sea, Joshua ibn Nūn came forward on his horse and walked over the water. The others drove their horses into it, but they sank into the water. Pharaoh went out in pursuit of them in the morning, after the sun had risen. This is what God—mighty and majestic is He—said: “They pursued them at sunrise. When the two companies came into sight of one another, the companions of Moses said: ‘We are surely overtaken!’”² Moses ﵇ called upon his Lord, mighty and majestic is He. A mist came over them and blocked their view of him. He was told: “Strike the sea with your staff.” He did so, and it split, “each part like a great mountain.”³ That is, like a mountain. Twelve paths opened in it. They said: “We fear the horses will get bogged down in it.” Moses called upon his Lord, and the east wind blew over them until it dried. They said: “We fear some of us will drown without our knowing.” He struck with his staff, and pierced the water, making windows between them so that they could see one another. Then they entered and crossed the sea. Pharaoh came forward until he reached the place where Moses had crossed; its paths were still as they had been. His guides said to him: “Moses has bewitched the sea so that it has become as you see.” This is what God said: “And leave the sea calm and open.”⁴ That is, as it is.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Muʿāwiyah ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭalḥah, from Ibn ʿAbbās, regarding the words “calm and open”: He said, “straight ahead.”

¹ Ḥafṣ ibn ʿUmar al-ʿAdanī related to us: al-Ḥakam ibn Abān related to us, from ʿIkrimah, who said: “A path.”¹

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Abū Ṣakhr, from Muḥammad ibn Kaʿb al-Quraẓī, who said: “A path laid open.”

Abū Sahl Aḥmad ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥīm related to us: Muḥammad ibn Yūsuf related to us: Isrāʾīl related to us, from Ibn Abī Najīḥ, from Mujāhid, who said: “Open.”

We were also related from Saʿīd ibn Abī ʿArūbah, from Qatādah, from al-Ḥasan, who said: “Easy, level ground.”

He said: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam said: “The calm sea is the easy sea.”

Then he returned to the account of Asad, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās: “Take this way until we catch up with them.”² It was a three-day journey across the land. Pharaoh was then riding a stallion. Gabriel ﵇ came forward on a mare, accompanied by thirty-three angels. They dispersed among the people, while Gabriel ﵇ went ahead of Pharaoh, and Pharaoh followed him. The angels shouted among the people: “Catch up with the king!” When the last of them had entered and the first had not yet come out, the sea closed over them and they drowned. The Children of Israel heard a crash when it closed over them and said: “What is that?” Moses said: “Pharaoh and his companions have drowned.” They turned back to look, and the sea cast them onto the shore.

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us: al-Ḥasan ibn Bilāl related to us, from Ḥammād ibn Salamah, from ʿAlī ibn Zayd, from Yūsuf ibn Mihrān, from Ibn ʿAbbās, that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: When God drowned Pharaoh’s people, Pharaoh said: “I believe in the one in whom the Children of Israel believe.” Gabriel said: “Muḥammad, had you seen me as I took silt from the sea and stuffed it into Pharaoh’s mouth, for fear that mercy might reach him!”

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us: Abū ʿAlī related to us, from Ḥammād ibn Salamah, from ʿAṭāʾ ibn al-Sāʾib, from Mujāhid, who said: Gabriel was between the Children of Israel and Pharaoh’s people. He kept saying to the Children of Israel, “Let the last of you catch up with the first of you,” while facing Pharaoh’s people and saying, “Wait a little, so that your last may catch up with you.” The Children of Israel said, “We have never seen a driver better at driving than this one.” Pharaoh’s people said, “We have never seen a restrainer better at restraining than this one.” When Moses and the Children of Israel reached the sea, the believer from Pharaoh’s household said, “Prophet of God, where have you been commanded to go? The sea is before you, and Pharaoh’s people have overtaken us.” He said, “I have been commanded to go to the sea.” The believer from Pharaoh’s household urged his horse into it, but the current turned him back. He said, “Prophet of God, where have you been commanded to go?” He replied, “To the sea.” He urged his horse into it again, but the current turned him back. Moses ﵇ did not know what to do. God, mighty and exalted, had revealed to the sea: “Obey Moses.” The sign of that, [He said,] would be when Moses struck it with his staff.

He said: Then he returned to the report of Asad, from Khālid, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said: Pharaoh and his vanguard went out—five hundred thousand, apart from the two wings and the center.

Khālid said: Abū Saʿīd related to us, from ʿIkrima, who said: No one older than forty or younger than twenty went out with Pharaoh. That is what God ﵎ means by: “He made light of his people, and they obeyed him” —that is, he made light of his people in pursuit of Moses. He said: Asad related to us, from Isrāʾīl, from Abū Isḥāq, from ʿAmr ibn Maymūn, who said: Moses went out with the Children of Israel. When morning came, Pharaoh ordered that a sheep be brought to him. He ordered it slaughtered, then said, “Its skin must not be finished being removed before five hundred thousand horsemen from the Copts have gathered here.” They gathered around him, and Pharaoh said to them, “These are but a small band” . Moses’s followers numbered six hundred and seventy thousand. He said: Moses and his followers took a dry path through the sea. When the last of Moses’s followers had emerged and the last of Pharaoh’s followers had entered, the sea surged over them. Never had a greater mass been seen than on that day. Pharaoh drowned and was cast up on the seashore, where they saw him.

It is said that Moses ﵇ killed ʿŪj in Egypt.

ʿAmr ibn Khālid related to us; Zuhayr ibn Muʿāwiya related to us; Abū Isḥāq related to us. Zuhayr said: I think it is from Nawf. He said: The bed of ʿŪj, whom Moses killed, was eight hundred cubits long and four hundred wide. Moses’s staff ﵇ was ten cubits long; his leap when he leapt toward him was ten cubits; and Moses’s height was such-and-such. He struck him and hit his heel, and ʿŪj fell upon the Nile of Egypt. His backbone and ribs served as a bridge for the people for a year, and they crossed over them.

On Queen Dalūka

He said: Then he returned to the report of ʿUthmān and others. He said: After they drowned, Egypt was left without any of its notable people; only slaves, hired laborers, and women remained there. The most prominent women in Egypt considered it too great a matter for one of them to appoint someone from among the men. They agreed that they would appoint one of their own, a woman called Dalūka daughter of Zabbāʾ. She had intelligence, knowledge, and experience, and was among the women of high standing and rank. She was then one hundred and sixty years old. They made her queen, and she feared that the kings of the earth would attack her. So she gathered the noblewomen and said to them: “Our land was one that no one coveted or cast an eye upon. Our elders and notables have perished, and the sorcerers by whose aid we were strong are gone. I have decided to build a fortress enclosing all our lands, and to place watch posts around it on every side. We cannot be sure that people will not covet us.” She built a wall enclosing all the land of Egypt, including its fields, cities, and villages. She made a canal outside it, with water flowing through it, and established bridges and irrigation channels. She placed watch posts and military stations along it, one every three miles, with smaller watch posts in between, one every mile. She stationed men at each post, assigned them stipends, and ordered them to keep watch with bells. If anyone they feared approached, they were to ring the bells from one post to the next. News reached them from whichever direction it came within a single hour. They would then consider what to do. In this way she protected Egypt from anyone who sought to attack it.

ʿUthmān said: She finished building it in six months. It is the wall in Egypt called the Wall of the Old Woman. Many remains of it still exist in Upper Egypt .

On the construction of the barābī

In his report, ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ said: (2 There was then an old sorceress called Tadūra. The sorcerers held her in great esteem and gave her precedence in their knowledge and sorcery. Dalūka daughter of Zabbāʾ sent to her: “We need your sorcery and have turned to you for help. We fear that kings may covet us. Make something for us by which we can overcome those around us. Pharaoh used to need you; how much more do we, now that our elders are gone and only a few of us remain?” She made a barabī of stone in the middle of the city of Memphis and gave it four doors, each facing one of the directions: the qibla, the sea, the west, and the east. In it she carved images of horses, mules, donkeys, ships, and men. She said to them: “I have made for you a device that will destroy anyone who comes against you from any direction, by land or sea. This will spare you the need for a fortress and free you from its upkeep. Whoever comes at you from any direction—whether they are on land, mounted on horses, mules, or camels, or are in ships or on foot—these images will move from the direction they come. Whatever you do to the images will be done to them in turn.” When the kings around them heard that Egypt had come under the rule of women, they coveted them and marched against them. As they approached the territory of Egypt, the images in the barabī moved. They did not touch those images or do anything to them without the same thing happening to the army advancing against them. If the army came on horses, then whatever they did to the carved horses in the barabī—cutting off their heads or legs, gouging out their eyes, or splitting open their bellies—was done likewise to the horses that came against them. The same happened if the army came in ships or on foot. They were the most knowledgeable people in sorcery and the strongest in it. The story spread, and people warned one another about them. On the kings of Egypt after the Old Woman, Dalūka

When the women of Egypt’s people lost those of their noblemen who had drowned with Pharaoh, and none remained but slaves and hired laborers, they could not bear to live without men. Each woman began by emancipating her slave and marrying him, while another married her hired laborer. They stipulated to the men that they do nothing without their permission, and the men agreed to this. Thus the women held authority over the men.

ʿUthmān said: Ibn Lahīʿa related to me, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that the Copts have followed this practice to this day, following those who came before them: none of them buys or sells without saying, “I will consult my wife.”

Dalūka daughter of Zabbāʾ ruled them for twenty years, managing their affairs in Egypt, until a boy from among their leading and noble families came of age. He was called Darkūn ibn Balūṭus, and they made him king over them. Egypt remained secure through the management of that old woman for about four hundred years.

He said: Then Darkūn ibn Balūṭus died, and his son Būdus ibn Darkūn succeeded him. Then Būdus ibn Darkūn died, and his brother Lūqās ibn Tādārus succeeded him. He lasted only three years before he died, leaving no child, so his brother Marīnā ibn Marīnūs succeeded him.

He said: Then Marīnā ibn Marīnūs died, and Istimārus ibn Marīnā succeeded him. He became tyrannical and arrogant, shed blood, and openly committed abomination. They considered this a grave matter, agreed to depose him, deposed him, and killed him. They pledged allegiance to a man from among their notables called Balūṭus ibn Manākīl. He ruled them for forty years. Then Balūṭus ibn Manākīl died, and his son Mālūs ibn Balūṭus succeeded him.

Then Mālūs ibn Balūṭus died, and his brother Manākīl ibn Balūṭus ibn Manākīl succeeded him. He ruled them for a time, then died, and his son Būla ibn Manākīl succeeded him. He ruled them for one hundred and twenty years. He was the lame man who captured the king of Jerusalem and brought him to Egypt. Būla had gained a firm hold over the land and attained a stature no one before him had reached after Pharaoh. He became tyrannical, and God Most High killed him: his mount threw him, crushed his neck, and he died.

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh; al-Kalāʿī related to us, from Tubayʿ, from Kaʿb, who said: When Solomon son of David ﵇ died, Marḥab, Solomon’s paternal uncle, ruled after him. The king of Egypt marched against him and fought him, and captured the golden shields that Solomon ﵇ had made, and took them away.*)

A learned elder from the people of Egypt informed me that the deposed man whom the people of Egypt deposed was in fact He was Būlah. This is because he summoned the ministers and those upon whom the kings before him had bestowed stipends and gifts. He seems to have considered this too much, and said to them: “I wish to ask you about certain things. If you inform me about them, I shall increase your stipends and raise your standing; but if you do not inform me, I shall have your heads struck off.” They said to him:

“Ask us whatever you wish.” He said to them: “Tell me what God ﵎ does each day, how many stars there are in the sky, and how much the sun is owed each day by the son of Adam.” They asked him to give them time, and he granted them a month. Each day they would go outside the city of Memphis and sit in the shade of a qirmūs, discussing the matter at hand; then they would return. The owner of the qirmūs watched them. One day he came to them and asked about their affair. They told him. He said: “I know what you want, except that I have a qirmūs which I cannot leave unattended. Let one of you sit in my place and work there, and give me a mount like your mounts and dress me in clothes like yours.” They did so.

There was in the city a son of one of their kings whose circumstances had grown poor. The qirmūs-worker came to him, asked him to take up his father’s kingship, and urged him to pursue it. He said: “This man who seeks kingship will not leave the city of Memphis.” The qirmūs-worker said: “I will get him out for you.” He gathered money for him. Then the qirmūs-worker went in to Būlah and told him that he knew the answers to what he had asked. Būlah said: “Tell me how many stars there are in the sky.” The qirmūs-worker took out a sack of sand that he had with him, poured it out before him, and said: “As many as this.” Būlah said: “How do you know?” He replied: “Send someone to count it.” Būlah said: “And how much is the sun owed each day by the son of Adam?” He answered: “A qīrāṭ, because the laborer works through the day until night and receives that as his wage.” Būlah said: “And what does God—mighty and exalted is He—do each day?” He replied: “I will show you tomorrow.”

He went out with him and brought him to one of his ministers, whom the qirmūs-worker had seated in his place. He said to him: “Each day God—mighty and exalted is He—humiliates some people, exalts others, and causes others to die. Among these things is that this man, one of your ministers, is sitting and working at a qirmūs, while I, the owner of a qirmūs, am riding one of the kings’ mounts and wearing one of their garments”—or words to that effect. “And so-and-so son of so-and-so has shut the city of Memphis against you.”

Būlah hurried back, and found that the city of Memphis had been shut. They rose up with the young man against Būlah and deposed him. He became deranged: he would sit at the gate of the city of Memphis, raving and babbling. That is why the Copts, when someone addresses them with something they do not want to hear, say: “You have deranged us like Būlah,” meaning the king, because of his derangement.

God knows best.

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān and others. He said: Then Marīnūs son of Būlah succeeded him and ruled them for some time, then died. His son Qarqūra son of Marīnūs succeeded him and ruled them for sixty years, then died. His brother Luqās son of Marīnūs succeeded him. Whenever any part of the barbī, in which the images were, collapsed, no one could repair it except that old woman, her son, or her grandson. They were a household, and no one else knew how to do it. The people of that household died out, and a section of the barbī collapsed during the reign of Luqās son of Marīnūs. No one could repair it or understand its craft, and it remained as it was. The means by which they had subdued the people came to an end, and they became like everyone else, except that they were numerous and had wealth.

The Account of Bukht Naṣṣar’s Entry into Egypt

He said: Then Luqās died, and his son Qūmis son of Luqās succeeded him and ruled them for a long time. When Bukht Naṣṣar came to Jerusalem—as Wuthaymah son of Mūsā and others related to us—and overcame the Children of Israel, taking them captive and leading them to the land of Babylon, Irmiyā remained in Īliyāʾ, which lay in ruins, lamenting and weeping over it. A remnant of the Children of Israel, who had been scattered, gathered around Irmiyā when they learned that he had stayed in Īliyāʾ. Irmiyā said to them: “Let us remain in our land, seek God’s forgiveness, and repent to Him; perhaps He will turn toward us.” They said: “We fear that Bukht Naṣṣar will hear of us and send for us, while we are only a small band. Let us instead go to the king of Egypt, seek his protection, and enter under his guarantee.” Irmiyā said to them: “God’s guarantee—mighty and exalted is He—is the surest of guarantees for you. No one’s protection on earth can suffice you if He makes you afraid.”

Those men from among the Children of Israel went to Qūmis son of Luqās and sought refuge with him, knowing of his strength. They told him their plight, and he said: “You are under my protection.” Bukht Naṣṣar sent him a message: “I have slaves of mine in your territory who have run away from me. Send them to me.” Qūmis wrote back: “They are not your slaves. They are people of prophecy and scripture, sons of free men. You have wronged them and acted unjustly toward them.” Bukht Naṣṣar swore that if he did not return them, he would invade his land. Both pressed their demands. God revealed to Irmiyā: “I shall give Bukht Naṣṣar victory over this king whom they have taken as a refuge. Had they obeyed you, even if heaven and earth had closed in around them, I would have made a way out for them. By My might, I swear that I shall teach them that they have no escape and no refuge except in obeying Me and following My command.”

When Irmiyā heard this, he pitied them and hastened to them. He said: “If you do not obey me, Bukht Naṣṣar will take you captive and kill you. The proof of this is that I saw the place where he will set his throne after he conquers Egypt and takes possession of it.” Then he went and buried four stones in the place where Bukht Naṣṣar would set his throne. He said: “Each leg of his throne will rest on one of these stones.” But they persisted in their opinion. Bukht Naṣṣar marched against Qūmis son of Luqās, king of Egypt, and fought him for a year. Then Bukht Naṣṣar prevailed, killed Qūmis, and took all the people of Egypt captive, killing those he killed.

When he wished to kill those he had taken captive, his throne was set in the place Irmiyā had described, and each leg of it rested on one of the stones he had buried. When the captives were brought to him, Irmiyā came with them. Bukht Naṣṣar said to him: “Why do I see you among my enemies, after I gave you protection and honored you?” Irmiyā said: “I came only to warn them, and I told them about you. I placed a sign for them beneath your throne and showed them where it would stand.” Bukht Naṣṣar said: “What proves that?” Irmiyā replied: “Raise your throne; beneath each leg is a stone I buried.” When he raised his throne, he found this confirmed. He said to Irmiyā: “If I knew there were any good in them, I would give them to you.” But he killed them, devastated the cities and villages of Egypt, and took all its people captive. He left no one there, until Egypt remained desolate for forty years, with no inhabitant. Its Nile flowed on, but no one benefited from it.

Irmiyā remained in Egypt and made a garden and planted crops to live on. A revelation came to him: “Your crops and your stay in Egypt are keeping you occupied. How can the land be spacious enough for you when you know of My anger against your people? Go to Īliyāʾ until My decree reaches its appointed term.” Irmiyā left it and went to Jerusalem. Then Bukht Naṣṣar returned the people of Egypt to it after forty years, and they rebuilt it. From that time onward, Egypt remained subjugated.

My father ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam and Abū al-Aswad related to us; they said: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Abū Qabīl, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ghanm al-Ashʿarī, that he came from Syria to ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr said to him: “What brings you to our land?” He replied: “You.” He asked: “Why?” He said: “You used to tell us that Egypt was the land quickest to fall into ruin, yet now I see you have acquired estates there, built palaces there, and settled there.”

(continued) “and made yourself at ease there.” He replied: “Egypt has had its fill of ruin; Bukht Naṣṣar devastated it and left nothing in it but beasts of prey and hyenas. Its ruin has passed. Today its soil is the finest of lands and the farthest from ruin. Blessing will remain in it as long as blessing remains in any land.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to me, from Abū Qabīl, with a similar account. He said: Some of the elders of the people of Egypt claimed that, in the time of its kings, the practice there was that they left the villages in the hands of their inhabitants, each village for a fixed rent. They did not revise it except once every four years, on account of drought and the shifting of prosperity. When four years had passed, they revised it and reassessed it anew: they treated with leniency those who deserved leniency, and increased the assessment on those able to bear an increase, without imposing anything that would be burdensome on them.
