When the land tax had been collected and gathered, one quarter of it belonged outright to the king, for him to do with it as he wished; the second quarter was for his soldiers and those by whose means he could wage war, collect his taxes, and repel his enemy; the third quarter was for the upkeep of the land and what it required in the way of its embankments, the digging of its canals, and the building of its bridges; and support for the farmers in sowing their crops and cultivating their land. From the fourth quarter, one quarter of the land tax collected from each village was taken and buried there, in case of an emergency or a calamity that befell the village people. That was their practice. This quarter, buried in each village from its land tax, is the treasure of Pharaoh that people speak of as destined to appear, and which those who seek treasures will pursue.

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us; Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Abū Qabīl, who said:

Wardān left the presence of Maslamah ibn Mukhallad, who was governor of Egypt, and hurried past ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr. He called out to him: “Where are you going, Abū ʿUbayd?” He replied: “The governor, Maslamah, has sent me to go to Memphis and dig for him for the treasure of Pharaoh.” He said: “Go back to him, convey my greetings, and tell him: ‘The treasure of Pharaoh is neither yours nor your companions’. It belongs only to the Abyssinians. They will come in their ships, making for al-Fusṭāṭ, and they will travel until they alight at Memphis. The treasure of Pharaoh will appear to them, and they will take from it whatever they wish. They will say: ‘We seek no booty better than this.’ Then they will return, and the Muslims will come out in pursuit of them and catch up with them. They will fight, and the Abyssinians will be routed; the Muslims will kill them and take them captive, until an Abyssinian will be sold for a cloak.

The Account of the Byzantines and Persians Gaining Power over Egypt

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ and others. He said: Then the Byzantines and Persians gained power over all the kings in the middle of the earth. The Byzantines fought the people of Egypt for three years, laying siege to them. They endured the fighting against them on land and at sea. When the people of Egypt saw this, they made peace with the Byzantines on condition that they pay them a specified amount each year, in return for the Byzantines protecting them and their being under Byzantine protection. Then the Persians gained power over the Byzantines. When …

They proceed until they alight at Memphis, where Pharaoh’s treasure appears to them. They take from it whatever they wish, and say, “What booty could we desire better than this?” Then they turn back, and the Muslims go out in pursuit of them and catch up with them. They fight, and the Abyssinians are routed; the Muslims kill them and take them captive, until an Abyssinian man is sold for a cloak.

The Romans and Persians Gain the Upper Hand over Egypt

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ and others. He said: Then the Romans and Persians gained the upper hand over all the kings in the middle of the earth. The Romans fought the people of Egypt for three years, laying siege to them. The Egyptians stood firm against them in battle, on land and at sea. When the people of Egypt saw this, they made peace with the Romans, agreeing to pay them a specified amount each year, in return for the Romans protecting them and their being under Roman protection. Then the Persians gained the upper hand over the Romans. When they had overcome them in Syria, they coveted Egypt and set their sights on it. The people of Egypt resisted, and the Romans came to their aid and stood in their defense. The Persians pressed them hard. When the Egyptians feared that the Persians would prevail over them, they made peace with them, agreeing that what they had agreed to pay the Romans would be divided between the Romans and the Persians. The Romans accepted this when they feared the Persians would prevail over them. Thus that agreement was imposed on the people of Egypt. Egypt remained divided between the Romans and Persians for seven years. Then the Romans gathered their strength and joined forces against the Persians, pressing the fight and sending reinforcements until they prevailed over them and destroyed all their fortifications and their lands in Syria and Egypt. This took place during the lifetime of the Messenger of God ﷺ—and before his death, after the advent of Islam. All of Syria and the entire peace settlement with the people of Egypt thus became the Romans’ alone; the Persians had no share in any part of Syria or Egypt.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from ʿUqayl ibn Khālid, from Ibn Shihāb, who said: The polytheists used to argue with the Muslims in Mecca, saying, “The Romans are People of the Book, yet the Magians have defeated them, while you claim that you will prevail by means of the Book that was sent down to your Prophet. We shall defeat you just as Persia defeated the Romans.” Then God ﵎ sent down: “Alif Lām Mīm. The Romans have been defeated in the nearest land, but after their defeat they will prevail within a few years. The command belongs to God before and after. And on that day the believers will rejoice in God’s victory. He grants victory to whom He wills, and He is the Mighty, the Merciful.”

Ibn Shihāb said: ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUtba ibn Masʿūd informed me that he said: When these two verses were sent down, Abū Bakr wagered with some of the polytheists, before gambling was forbidden, on something of value, that the Romans would defeat Persia within seven years. The Messenger of God ﷺ—said, “Why did you do that? Everything below ten is a few.” The Persians defeated the Romans within seven years. Then God gave the Romans victory over the Persians at the time of al-Ḥudaybiya, and the Muslims rejoiced at the victory of the People of the Book.

Someone other than ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related from al-Layth ibn Saʿd: 3 The Persians had begun building the fortress called Bāb al-Yūn, the fortress that is in the Fusṭāṭ of Egypt today. When the Persian forces withdrew from the Romans and the Romans drove them out of Syria, the Romans completed the construction of that fortress and settled there. Egypt remained under Roman rule until God Most High opened it to the Muslims. 3

Saʿīd ibn Tulayd related to us, from Ibn Wahb: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, saying: It is said that the Persians and Romans are the Quraysh of the non-Arabs.

The Persians’ Withdrawal from the Romans

He said: The reason for the Persians’ withdrawal from the Romans was, as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from al-Hayqal ibn Ziyād, from Muʿāwiya ibn Yaḥyā al-Ṣadafī, who said: al-Zuhrī related to me: ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUtba related to me that Ibn ʿAbbās told him he had heard ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵁—ask al-Hurmuzān, the great man of al-Ahwāz, about what had caused the Persians to withdraw from them. Al-Hurmuzān said to him: Kisrā sent Shahrbarāz, along with Persian troops, toward Syria and Egypt. He destroyed most of the Roman fortresses and remained for a long time in Syria, Egypt, and that region. Kisrā began to think he was taking too long and wrote to him: “If you wished to conquer a Roman city, you could do so; but you are content with where you are and want to settle there for a long time.”

He wrote to one of the Persian grandees who was with Shahrbarāz, ordering him to kill Shahrbarāz and take command of the troops. The grandee wrote back, explaining that Shahrbarāz was fighting diligently and loyally, and that he had distinguished himself in war more than he had.

He said: Kisrā wrote to him, insisting that he kill him. The grandee wrote back again, replying to him: “You have no servant like Shahrbarāz. If you knew how he manages the stratagems of the Romans, you would excuse him.”

Kisrā wrote to him, insisting that he kill Shahrbarāz and take command of the troops. The grandee wrote back again, replying to him. Kisrā grew angry and wrote to Shahrbarāz, insisting that he kill that grandee. Shahrbarāz sent for the Persian grandee and read Kisrā’s letter to him. He said to him, “Appeal on my behalf.” The man replied, “I know that Kisrā does not reconsider. I also know how well you have treated me, but what has come to me is something I cannot set aside.” The man said to him, “May I not go to my family and give them my instructions and my final charge?” He said, “Yes, that is what I can grant you.”

The man went to his family, took the three letters from Kisrā that had been written to him, and put them in his sleeve. Then he came back and entered Shahrbarāz’s presence. He handed him the first letter. Shahrbarāz read it and said to him, “You are better than I am.” Then he handed him the second letter, and Shahrbarāz read it. He rose from his seat and said to him, “Sit in it,” but the man refused. He handed him the third letter. Shahrbarāz read it, and before he had finished reading it, he said, “By God, I shall make Kisrā suffer, and I shall plot against him.” He corresponded with Heraclius, telling him that Kisrā had turned against him, that he had prepared military expeditions, and that he himself had been afflicted by the length of his reign. He asked Heraclius to meet him at a place midway between them, where they could settle the matter and make an agreement; then Shahrbarāz would withdraw the Persian troops and be free to march against Kisrā.

When Heraclius received Shahrbarāz’s letter, he summoned a group of Roman grandees and said to them, “Sit down. Today I am either the most prudent of men or the most incapable. Something has come to me that you do not expect. I shall present it to you, so advise me about it.” He read Shahrbarāz’s letter to them. They disagreed in their advice. Some said, “This is a stratagem on Kisrā’s part.” Others said, “This servant wants to meet you and, fearing Kisrā, is seeking help; then he will not care what he encounters.”

Heraclius said: “This is not as you have concluded. Kisrā would not willingly tolerate the insults I find in Shahrbarāz’s letter. Shahrbarāz would not have written this to me while he was prevailing over most of my realm unless something had happened between him and Kisrā. By God, I shall meet him.”

Heraclius wrote to him: “Your letter has reached me, and I understand what you have said. I will meet you. I appoint our meeting at such-and-such a place. Come with four thousand of your men; I will come with the same number. When you reach such-and-such a place, leave behind five hundred of your men. I shall leave the same number at such-and-such a place. Then leave the same number at such-and-such a place, until you and I meet, each with five hundred men.”

Heraclius sent messengers to Shahrbarāz, telling him that if Shahrbarāz agreed, he should send word; if not, they were to hasten back with a message so Heraclius could decide what to do. Shahrbarāz considered the matter and did as Heraclius had asked.

Heraclius set out with the four thousand men he had taken, leaving none behind, until the two met at the appointed place. Heraclius had four thousand men with him, and Shahrbarāz had five hundred. When Shahrbarāz saw them, he sent a message to Heraclius: “Have you betrayed me?” Heraclius sent back: “I have not betrayed you, but I feared betrayal from your side.” Heraclius ordered a pavilion of brocade to be set up between the two lines. He dismounted, entered it, and went in with an interpreter.

Shahrbarāz came and entered to see him. The interpreter mediated privately between them until they settled their affairs and each secured the other’s commitment by covenants and pledges, until they had concluded their business.

Heraclius came out and signaled to Shahrbarāz to kill the interpreter, so that he would keep their secret. Shahrbarāz killed him. Then Shahrbarāz withdrew and raised an army, while Heraclius marched against Kisrā and raided him and those who remained with him. This was the beginning of Kisrā’s downfall. Heraclius kept his promise to Shahrbarāz to leave Persian territory; Shahrbarāz withdrew after he had laid waste Kisrā’s lands, and the Persians killed Kisrā. Shahrbarāz then reached Persia with the troops.

The Building of Alexandria

He said: Heraclius, king of the Romans, appointed al-Muqawqis governor of Egypt, as a shaykh from among the people of Egypt related to me, and entrusted him with its defense and the collection of its land tax. He settled in Alexandria.

The one who built Alexandria and laid its foundations was Dhū al-Qarnayn the Roman, whose name was Alexander. Alexandria was named after him. He was the first to make brocade, and his father was the first of the Caesars.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Hishām related to us. He said: His name was Alexander.

Wuthayma ibn Mūsā related to us, from Saʿīd ibn Bashīr, from Qatāda, who said: Alexander is Dhū al-Qarnayn.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Hishām related to us, from Ziyād ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq, who said: Someone who transmitted accounts of the non-Arabs and what they had inherited of their knowledge related to me that he was a man from Egypt named Marzabbā ibn Marzaba al-Yūnānī, a descendant of Yūnān ibn Yāfith ibn Noah ﵇.

He said: A shaykh from Egypt related to me: He was from Lūbiyya, a district among the districts of western Egypt. Ibn Lahīʿa said: Its people are Romans. It is also said that he was a man of Ḥimyar. Tubbaʿ said:

Dhū al-Qarnayn, my grandfather, was a Muslim,

A king whom kings obeyed and for whom they gathered;

He reached the western and eastern lands, seeking

The means to knowledge from a wise guide.

He saw the sun’s setting place at its setting,

In a spring of dark mud and black mire.

It is also related as: “Dhū al-Qarnayn before me was a Muslim.”

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to me: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wahb related to me, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ziyād ibn Anʿum, from Saʿd ibn Masʿūd al-Tujībī, from two shaykhs of his people, who said: We were in Alexandria and our day had grown long. We said, “Why don’t we go to ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir and talk with him?” We went to him and found him sitting in his house. We told him that our day had grown long. He said, “So has mine; I only came out because it had grown long.” Then he turned to us and said: I was with the Messenger of God ﷺ—serving him, when I saw some men from among the People of the Book who had scrolls or books with them. They said, “Ask permission for us to see the Messenger of God ﷺ.” I went to him and told him they were there. The Messenger of God ﷺ—said, “What have I to do with them? They ask me about what I do not know. I am only a servant, and I have no knowledge except what my Lord has taught me.” Then he said, “Bring me water for ablution.” He performed ablution, then went to the prayer place in his house and prayed two units. He did not turn away until I saw joy and gladness in his face. Then he came out and said:

“Admit them, and admit any of my companions you find at the door.”

He said: I admitted them. When they came before the Messenger of God ﷺ—he said to them: “If you wish, I will tell you what you wanted to ask me before you speak; or, if you prefer, you may speak and I will answer you.” They said, “Tell us before we speak.” He said: “You have come to ask me about Dhū al-Qarnayn. I will tell you about him as you will find him written of in your books. At the beginning of his life, he was a young man from among the Romans, to whom kingship was granted. He traveled until he came to the seashore in the land of Egypt, and there he founded a city called Alexandria. When he had finished building it, an angel came to him, raised him up, and lifted him high. The angel said, ‘Look at what is beneath you.’ He said, ‘I see my city, and I see cities beside it.’ Then the angel raised him higher and said, ‘Look.’ He said, ‘My city has blended with the cities, and I cannot recognize it.’

“Then the angel raised him higher still and said, ‘Look.’ He said, ‘I see my city alone and nothing else.’ The angel said to him, ‘All that land is only what you see; the sea surrounds it. Your Lord wished to show you the earth, and He has given you authority over it. You will teach the ignorant and strengthen the learned.’ He traveled until he reached the place where the sun sets, then traveled until he reached the place where it rises. Then he came to the two barriers, two smooth mountains from which everything slips. He built the barrier, then crossed beyond it to Gog and Magog. He found a people whose faces were like dogs’ faces, fighting Gog and Magog. He cut through them and found a short-statured people fighting the people whose faces were like dogs’ faces. He found a people of cranes fighting the short-statured people. Then he went on and found a people of snakes, one of which swallowed a great rock, then came out to the encircling sea around the earth.” They said, “We bear witness that his account is as you have described it, and that we find it just so in our book.”

3 ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Hishām related to us: Ziyād ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Bakkāʾī related to us, from Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq, who said: Thawr ibn Yazīd related to me, from Khālid ibn Maʿdān al-Kilāʿī—who was a man of long experience—that the Messenger of God ﷺ—was asked about Dhū al-Qarnayn. He said: “A king who traversed the earth, using the means beneath it.” Khālid said: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵁—heard a man say, “O Dhū al-Qarnayn!” ʿUmar said, “May God forgive us! Were you not satisfied to be named after the prophets, that you have now taken the names of angels?” 3

Wuthayma ibn Mūsā related to us, from someone who informed him, from Saʿīd ibn Abī ʿArūba, from Qatāda, from al-Ḥasan, who said: Dhū al-Qarnayn was a king and a righteous man.

He said: He was given the name Dhū al-Qarnayn for the reason reported to us by Wuthayma: Sufyān ibn ʿUyayna related to us, from Ibn Abī Ḥusayn, from Abū al-Ṭufayl, that ʿAlī ﵁—was asked about Dhū al-Qarnayn. He said: “He was neither a king nor a prophet, but a righteous servant. He loved God, so God loved him; he gave sincere counsel to God, so God gave him sincere counsel. God, Mighty and Majestic, sent him to his people, and they struck him on one of his horns, and he died. God then brought him back to life and sent him to his people again. They struck him on the other horn, and he died. Thus he was called Dhū al-Qarnayn.”

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: It is also said that he was called Dhū al-Qarnayn because he went beyond the sun’s horn in the west and the east. It is also said that he was called Dhū al-Qarnayn because he had two locks of hair on his head that reached down to them, according to what Ibrāhīm ibn al-Mundhir related, from ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿImrān, from Khāzim ibn Ḥusayn, from Yūnus ibn ʿUbayd, from al-Ḥasan.

ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Manṣūr al-Yaḥṣubī related to us, from ʿĀṣim ibn Ḥakīm, from Abū Sarīʿ al-Ṭāʾī, from ʿUbayd ibn Taʿlā, who said: He had two small horns that his turban concealed.

Aḥmad ibn Muḥammad related to us, from ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿImrān, from Sulaymān ibn Asīd, from Ibn Shihāb, who said: He was called Dhū al-Qarnayn because he reached the sun’s horn at its setting place and the sun’s horn at its rising place.

He said: Some of the shaykhs of Egypt mentioned, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from someone who related to him, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, that he said: The earliest history of Alexandria was that Pharaoh established there workshops and meeting places. He was the first to settle it and build in it. It remained as he had built it, with his workshops, until the kings of Egypt succeeded one another after him. After Pharaoh, Dalūka daughter of Zabʿā built the lighthouse of Alexandria and the lighthouse of Abū Qīr. When Solomon son of David ﵈, gained power over the earth, he established a meeting place there and built a mosque. Then Dhū al-Qarnayn ruled it. He demolished all the buildings there that had been built by kings, Pharaohs, and others, except the building of Solomon son of David: he did not demolish or alter it, but repaired what had become dilapidated and left the lighthouse as it was. Then he rebuilt Alexandria from the ground up, with its parts made alike. After him, kings of the Romans and others succeeded one another there; every king built something of his own in Alexandria, by which he was known and to which his name was attached.

He said: It is also said that it was Queen Cleopatra who built the lighthouse of Alexandria. She brought its canal to the city, for the water had not reached it before. It had been diverted from a village called Kassā, opposite al-Karyūn. She dug the canal until she brought it into Alexandria, and she paved its floor.

Ibn Lahīʿa said: I was told that a stone was found in Alexandria bearing this inscription: “I am Shaddād ibn ʿĀd. I am the one who erected the pillars, raised the lofty heights, and blocked the valley with his arm. I built them when there was neither old age nor death, and when stones were as soft as clay.” Ibn Lahīʿa said: “The lofty heights” means something like caves. It is said that Shaddād ibn ʿĀd was the one who built Alexandria. God knows best.

Idrīs ibn Yaḥyā al-Khawlānī related to us: ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAyyāsh al-Qitbānī related to us, from his father, from Tabīʿ said: “There are five mosques in Alexandria: the Mosque of Moses the Prophet ﷺ beside the lighthouse, the one nearest the church; the Mosque of Solomon ﵇; the Mosque of Dhū al-Qarnayn or al-Khiḍr ﵉, which is beside the al-labakhāt in the qaysāriyya; the Mosque of al-Khiḍr or Dhū al-Qarnayn at the city gate, when you leave through the gate—for each of them there is a mosque, but we do not know where it is; and the Great Mosque of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ.”

Hāniʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shurayḥ related to us, from Qays ibn al-Ḥajjāj, from Tabīʿ, who said: “In Alexandria there are five sacred mosques, among them the mosque in the qaysāriyya where inheritances are sold, the Mosque of al-Labakhāt, and the Mosque of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ.”

As my father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, related to us, Alexandria consisted of three cities, one beside the other: Minna, which was the site of the lighthouse and its surroundings; Alexandria, which was the site of the main settlement of Alexandria today; and Nuqayṭa. Each had a wall, and behind them all was a wall surrounding all three cities.

Hāniʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṭarīf al-Hamdānī related to us, who said: “Alexandria had seven fortresses and seven moats.”

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us, from Khālid ibn ʿAbd Allāh, who related to me from Ibn al-Suddī, from his father, who said:

“Alexander’s nose was three cubits”.

Khālid and Abū Ḥamza said: When Dhū al-Qarnayn built Alexandria, he had it faced with white marble—its walls and its floors. Its people wore black and red. For this reason, the monks wear black, because of the dazzling whiteness of the marble. They did not light lamps there at night, because of the marble’s whiteness. And when it was a moonlit night, a man sewing at night, in the moonlight against the white marble, could pass the thread through the eye of a needle.

He said: Regarding Alexandria’s history, one of the shaykhs mentioned that it was being built for three hundred years, inhabited for three hundred years, and in ruins for three hundred years. For seventy years, no one entered it without a black cloth over his eyes, because of the whiteness of its plaster and paving; and for seventy years no lamp was lit in it.

Ibn Abī Maryam informed us, from al-ʿAṭṭāf ibn Khālid, who said: Alexandria was white, shining by night and by day. When the sun set, none of its people would leave his house, and whoever did was snatched away. There was a shepherd among them who pastured his flock on the seashore. Something would come out of the sea and take one of his sheep. The shepherd hid in a place until it came out—and it was a young woman. He clung to her hair, and she resisted him, but he overpowered her and took her to his house. She became comfortable with them and saw that they did not go out after sunset. She asked them about it, and they said: “Whoever among us goes out is snatched away.” So she prepared talismans for them. She was the first to introduce talismans in Egypt, in Alexandria.

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us; Ismāʿīl ibn ʿAyyāsh related to us, from Hishām ibn Saʿd al-Madanī, who said: A stone was found in Alexandria with an inscription on it. Then he mentioned a report exactly like Ibn Lahīʿa’s report, with this addition: “I buried a treasure in the sea, at a depth of twelve cubits. No one will bring it out until a community of Muḥammad ﷺ brings it out.”

Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Baghdādī related to us, from Dāwūd, from ʿUthmān ibn ʿAṭāʾ, from his father, who said: Marble had been made subservient to them, so that from morning until midday it was like dough; when midday came, it hardened.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: The pyramids were built in the time of Shaddād ibn ʿĀd, as was related from some traditionists. I have found no report about the pyramids, among the people of knowledge in Egypt, that can be established. The poet says of them:

The pyramids have confounded the minds of the people of understanding,

And their greatness has made their thoughts seem small.

Smooth, pointed in construction, towering high,

Against them, arrows fell short, though aimed at what is lofty.

I knew not, when thought stumbled before them,

And fancies were bewildered by their marvels:

Are they the tombs of foreign kings,

Or sand-talismans—or markers?

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us; Isrāʾīl related to us, from Abū Isḥāq, from Nawf, with a similar report. He did not mention the couch.

When God drowned Pharaoh and his troops, as Hāniʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from Tabīʿ, those who had believed among the sorcerers asked Moses for permission to return to their families and property in Egypt. He gave them permission and prayed for them. They became ascetics in the mountain peaks, and they were the first to become ascetics. They were called the Shīʿa. A group of them remained with Moses ﵇ until God, mighty and exalted is He, took him. Asceticism then ceased after them, until the followers of Christ ﷺ later introduced it.)

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; Muʿāwiya ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭalḥa, from Ibn ʿAbbās, concerning His words, “Alif Lām Mīm. The Romans have been defeated in the nearest land, but after their defeat they will be victorious within a few years”.

He said: The Persians defeated them; then the Romans defeated the Persians in the nearest land—that is, in the region of Syria at the edge of the land.

There was disagreement about the meaning of “a few”. Al-Ḥārith ibn Miskīn related to us; Ibn al-Qāsim related to us, from Mālik ibn Anas, who said: “A few” means anywhere from three to seven.

Asad related to us; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Khālid related to us, from al-Kalbī, from Abū Ṣāliḥ, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said: “A few years” means from five to seven.

Asad related to us; Ibrāhīm ibn Saʿd related to us, from Abū al-Ḥuwayrith, that the Messenger of God ﷺ said:

“‘A few years’ means from five to seven.” It is also said that “a few” means a number not reaching more than four; or, it is said, more than seven, nine, or ten. It is also said that “a few” means from ten to twenty. The same applies to each decade up to a hundred. If it exceeds a hundred, “a few” no longer applies, and it becomes “and some more.”

Mention of the letter of the Messenger of God ﷺ to al-Muqawqis

Hishām ibn Isḥāq and others related to us, saying: In the sixth year after the migration of the Messenger of God ﷺ when the Messenger of God ﷺ returned from al-Ḥudaybiya, he sent messages to the kings.

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wahb related to us; Yūnus ibn Yazīd informed me, from Ibn—)

Shihāb said: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAbd al-Qārī told me that one day the Messenger of God ﵌ stood on the pulpit, praised and lauded God, and bore witness; then he said: “To proceed: I intend to send some of you to the kings of the foreigners. Do not disagree with me as the Children of Israel disagreed with Jesus son of Mary. This was because God ﵎ revealed to Jesus: ‘Send [messengers] to the kings of the earth.’ So he sent the disciples. The one whose destination was near was willing, but the one whose destination was far away was reluctant and said, ‘I do not know how to speak the language of the one to whom you are sending me.’ Jesus said, ‘O God, I commanded the disciples to do what You commanded me, but they have disagreed with me.’ God then revealed to him, ‘I shall suffice you.’ By morning, each of them could speak the language of the people to whom he had been sent .”

The Emigrants said, “Messenger of God, by God, we shall never disagree with you in anything. Command us and send us.” He sent Ḥāṭib ibn Abī Baltaʿa to al-Muqawqis, ruler of Alexandria; Shujāʿ ibn Wahb al-Asadī [to al-Ḥārith ibn Abī Shamir al-Ghassānī; and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Ḥudāfa al-Sahmī ] to Khusraw; Diḥya ibn Khalīfa to Caesar; and ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ to the two sons of al-Julanda, the rulers of ʿUmān. Then he related the report.

Then he returned to the account of Hishām ibn Isḥāq and others. They said: Ḥāṭib set out with a letter from the Messenger of God ﵌. When he reached Alexandria, he found al-Muqawqis in a chamber overlooking the sea. Al-Muqawqis went out by sea, and when he came level with his chamber, Ḥāṭib held up the letter of the Messenger of God ﵌ between his fingers. When al-Muqawqis saw it, he ordered that the letter be taken and brought to him. After reading it, he said, “If he is a prophet, what prevents him from praying against me and bringing someone to overpower me?” Ḥāṭib said to him, “What prevented Jesus son of Mary from praying against the one who refused to do as he asked, and from bringing someone to do this or that to him?” He fell silent for a while, then asked him again. Ḥāṭib repeated his answer, and he fell silent. Ḥāṭib said to him, “Before you there was a man who claimed to be the supreme lord. God first avenged Himself through him, then took vengeance upon him. So take a lesson from someone else; do not let others take a lesson from you. You have a religion that you will abandon only for one better than it: Islam, through which God has made all that came before it sufficient. Moses’s glad tidings of Jesus were no different from Jesus’s glad tidings of Muḥammad. Our invitation to you to accept the Qurʾān is no different from your invitation to the people of the Torah to accept the Gospel. We do not forbid you the religion of the Messiah; rather, we command you to follow it.” Then he read the letter:

“In the name of God, Most Gracious, Most Merciful. From Muḥammad, the Messenger of God, to al-Muqawqis, the great man of the Copts. Peace be upon whoever follows the guidance. To proceed: I invite you with the invitation of Islam. Become Muslim and you will be safe; become Muslim and God will give you your reward twice over. ‘O People of the Book, come to a common word between us and you: that we worship none but God, associate nothing with Him, and do not take one another as lords apart from God. But if they turn away, say, “Bear witness that we are Muslims.”’”

When he had read it, al-Muqawqis took it, placed it in an ivory case, and sealed it .

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿīd al-Madhaḥijī told us, from Rabīʿa ibn ʿUthmān, from Abān ibn Ṣāliḥ, who said: (3 Al-Muqawqis sent for Ḥāṭib one night when no one was with him except his interpreter. He said, “Will you tell me about some matters I wish to ask you? I know that your master chose you when he sent you.” He replied, “Ask me anything, and I will answer you truthfully.” “What does Muḥammad call people to?” “To worship God without associating anything with Him, to renounce everything besides Him, and he commands prayer.” “How many times do you pray?” “Five prayers in the day and night; fasting for the month of Ramaḍān; pilgrimage to the House; keeping one’s pledge; and he forbids eating carrion and blood.” “Who follows him?” “The young men of his people and others.” “Does he fight his people?” “Yes.” “Describe him to me.” He described some of his characteristics, but had not covered them all. Al-Muqawqis said, “There are things you have left unmentioned. His eyes are reddish, a redness that rarely leaves them; between his shoulders is the seal of prophethood. He rides a donkey, wears a woollen wrap, and makes do with dates and pieces of bread. He does not care whether the person he meets is an uncle or a cousin.” I said, “That is how he is.” He said, “I had known that a prophet remained to come, and I had thought he would emerge in Syria; that is where the prophets before him emerged. But it seems he has emerged among the Arabs, in a land of hardship and distress. The Copts will not follow him at my behest, and I do not want him to learn that I have spoken with you. He will gain power over the lands, and after him his companions will alight in this very territory of ours and gain power over what is here. I will not mention any of this to the Copts. So return to your master.” 3) .

Then he returned to the account of Hishām ibn Isḥāq, who said: Al-Muqawqis then summoned a scribe to write in Arabic, and the scribe wrote: “To Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from al-Muqawqis, the great man of the Copts. Peace. (1 To proceed: I have read your letter and understood what you mentioned and what you call people to. I knew that a prophet remained to come; I had thought he would emerge in Syria. I have treated your messenger generously and sent you two slave girls, both of high standing among the Copts, along with garments. I have also presented you with a mule to ride. Peace.” 1) .

(2 Asad ibn Mūsā told us: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wahb told us: Yūnus ibn Yazīd informed me, from Ibn Shihāb, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAbd al-Qārī, who said: When Ḥāṭib set out with the letter of the Messenger of God ﵌ al-Muqawqis accepted the letter, treated Ḥāṭib generously, lodged him well, and then sent him back to the Messenger of God ﵌. With Ḥāṭib he sent a robe, a mule with its saddle, and two slave girls. One was Umm Ibrāhīm; he gave the other to Jahm ibn Qays al-ʿAbdarī. She was the mother of Zakariyyā ibn Jahm, who succeeded ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ as governor of Egypt. It is said instead that he gave her to Ḥassān ibn Thābit, and that she was the mother of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥassān. It is also said that the Messenger of God ﵌ gave her to Muḥammad ibn Maslama al-Anṣārī; and it is said instead that he gave her to Diḥya ibn Khalīfa al-Kalbī. 2) .

Al-Naḍr ibn Salama al-Sāmī told us, from Ḥātim ibn Ismāʿīl, from Usāma ibn Zayd al-Laythī, from al-Mundhir ibn ʿUbayd, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥassān ibn Thābit, from his mother Sīrīn, who said:

“I was present when Ibrāhīm was dying. Whenever my sister and I cried aloud, I saw the Messenger of God ﵌ refrain from forbidding us. When Ibrāhīm died, he forbade us to wail.”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Hishām told us: Ziyād ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Bakkāʾī told us, from Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq, from Yaʿqūb ibn ʿUtba, that Ṣafwān ibn al-Muʿaṭṭal struck Ḥassān ibn Thābit with a sword. Ibn Isḥāq said: Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm al-Taymī told me that Thābit ibn Qays ibn Shammās leapt upon Ṣafwān ibn al-Muʿaṭṭal when he struck Ḥassān, bound his hands to his neck with a rope, [then took him to the dwelling of the Banū al-Ḥārith ibn al-Khazraj]. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Rawāḥa met him and said, “What is this?” He replied, “He struck Ḥassān with a sword! By God, I think he has killed him.” ʿAbd Allāh asked, “Does the Messenger of God ﵌ know anything about what you have done?” He said, “No.” ʿAbd Allāh said, “You have been rash. Release the man.” So he released him. Then they went to the Messenger of God ﵌ and told him about it. He summoned Ḥassān and Ṣafwān ibn al-Muʿaṭṭal. Ḥassān said, “Messenger of God, he insulted me and satirized me, and anger overcame me, so I struck him.” The Messenger of God ﵌ said, “Treat well, Ḥassān, the one who has harmed you.” Ḥassān said, “It is yours.” In exchange, the Messenger of God ﵌ gave him Bayraḥā, which is today the palace of the Banū Ḥadīla [in Medina]. It had belonged to Abū Ṭalḥa, who gave it in charity to the Messenger of God ﵌. He gave it to Ḥassān in return for the blow, and gave him Sīrīn, a Coptic slave woman, who bore him ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥassān.

Hānīʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil told us: Ibn Lahīʿa told us: Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb told me that when al-Muqawqis received the letter of the Messenger of God ﵌ he pressed it to his chest and said, “This is the time when the prophet whose description and attributes we find in the Book of God will emerge. We find in his description that he does not unite two sisters in his ownership or in marriage; that he accepts a gift but does not accept alms; that the poor are his companions; and that the seal of prophethood is between his shoulders.” Then he summoned a discerning man. He summoned no woman in Egypt more beautiful or comely than Māriya and her sister. They were from Ḥafn, in the district of Anṣinā. He sent them to the Messenger of God ﵌ and presented him with a light gray mule, a gray donkey, garments made of Egyptian linen, and honey from Banhā. He also sent him money as alms and instructed his messenger to observe who his companions were and to look at his back to see whether he could find a large mole with hair. The messenger did so. When he came to the Messenger of God ﵌ he presented him with the two sisters, the two mounts, the honey, and the garments, and informed him that all of it was a gift. The Messenger of God ﵌ accepted the gift, for he never refused a gift from anyone. When he looked at Māriya and her sister, he was pleased with them, but disliked having both sisters together, and they resembled one another. He said, “O God, choose for Your Prophet.” God chose Māriya for him. He had said to the two women, “Say: ‘We bear witness that there is no god but God and that Muḥammad is His servant and Messenger.’” Māriya hastened to bear witness and believe before her sister. Her sister waited for a while, then bore witness and believed. The Messenger of God ﵌ gave her sister to Muḥammad ibn Maslama al-Anṣārī. Some say instead that he gave her to Diḥya ibn Khalīfa al-Kalbī.

Hānī ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shumāsa al-Mahrī—I think from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ—who said: The Messenger of God ﷺ entered upon Umm Ibrāhīm, the mother of his son, the Coptic woman, and found there a relative of hers who had come with her from Egypt. He used to visit her often. Something entered his mind, so he left. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb met him and recognized it in his face. He asked him about it, and he told him. ʿUmar took his sword, then entered upon Māriya, with her relative there. He lunged at him with the sword. When the man saw that, he exposed himself: he was castrated, with nothing between his legs. When ʿUmar saw him, he returned to the Messenger of God ﷺ and told him. The Messenger of God ﷺ said: “Gabriel came to me and told me that God has declared her and her relative innocent, and that she is carrying a boy of mine in her womb who will be the most like me of all creation. He commanded me to name him Ibrāhīm and gave me the teknonym Abū Ibrāhīm.”

And Duḥaym related to us: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ibrāhīm related to us, from Ibn Wahb, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from al-Zuhrī, from Anas, who said: When Umm Ibrāhīm gave birth to Ibrāhīm, it was as though something entered the Prophet’s mind about him, until Gabriel came to him and said: “Peace be upon you, Abū Ibrāhīm.”

It is said that al-Muqawqis sent a eunuch with her, and that he used to stay with her.

Aḥmad ibn Saʿīd al-Fihrī related to us: Marwān ibn Yaḥyā al-Ḥāṭibī related to us: Ibrāhīm ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Adʿaj related to me: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam related to me, from his father (who said: Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥāṭib related to me, from his father), from his grandfather Ḥāṭib ibn Abī Baltaʿa, who said: The Messenger of God ﷺ sent me to al-Muqawqis, the ruler of Alexandria. I brought him the Messenger of God’s letter ﷺ. He put me up in a house, and I stayed with him for several nights. Then he sent for me, having gathered his patriarchs, and said: “I am going to speak to you, and I would like you to understand what I say.” I said: “Go ahead.” He said: “Tell me about your master. Is he not a prophet?” I said: “Yes, he is the Messenger of God.” He said: “Then why, when that was so, did he not pray against his people when they expelled him from his homeland to another?” I said to him: “And you testify that Jesus son of Mary is a Messenger of God. So why, when his people seized him and wanted to crucify him, did he not pray against them, that God might destroy them, before God raised him up to Himself in the lowest heaven?” He said: “You are a wise man who has come from a wise man. These are gifts that I shall send with you to Muḥammad, and I shall send with you an escort to escort you to your place of safety.”1 He gave the Messenger of God ﷺ three slave girls, among them Umm Ibrāhīm; one whom the Messenger of God ﷺ gave to Abū Jahm ibn Ḥudhayfa al-ʿAbdarī; and one whom he gave to Ḥassān ibn Thābit. He also sent him garments and some of their wares. Māriya bore the Messenger of God ﷺ Ibrāhīm, who was among the people dearest to him, until he died; and the Messenger of God ﷺ grieved deeply for him.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: Ḥafṣ ibn Sulaymān related to us, from Kathīr ibn Shinẓīr, from Abū Naḍra, from Abū Saʿīd al-Khudrī, that the Messenger of God ﷺ prayed over his son Ibrāhīm and said four takbīrs over him. He said that water was sprinkled on his grave, as Ibn Bukayr related to us.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: Quraysh ibn Ḥayyān related to us, from Thābit al-Bunānī, from Anas ibn Mālik, who said: We entered with the Messenger of God ﷺ to visit Abū Sayf, a blacksmith in Medina, who was Ibrāhīm’s wet nurse’s husband. He brought Ibrāhīm to him, and he smelled him. Then we entered to see him while he was dying, and his eyes overflowed with tears. Ibn ʿAwf said to him, “And you too, Messenger of God?” He said: “It is compassion.” Then he added: “The eye sheds tears and the heart grieves, but we say nothing that displeases our Lord.”

My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, related to us: Muslim ibn Khālid al-Zanjī related to us, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUthmān ibn Khuthaym, from Shahr ibn Ḥawshab, from Asmāʾ daughter of Yazīd, who said that she had been told: When Ibrāhīm died, the Messenger of God ﷺ wept. Abū Bakr and ʿUmar said, “You are the most entitled to know what God has ordained.” He said: “The eye sheds tears and the heart grieves, but we say nothing that angers the Lord. Were it not for a true promise and an appointed gathering, and that the last of us will follow the first, we would grieve for you, Ibrāhīm, even more than we have. We are indeed sorrowful for you.”

ʿAlī ibn Maʿbad related to us: ʿĪsā ibn Yūnus related to us, from Muḥammad ibn Abī Laylā, from ʿAṭāʾ ibn Abī Rabāḥ, from Jābir ibn ʿAbd Allāh, who said: The Messenger of God ﷺ took ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf by the hand and went with him to the palm grove where his son Ibrāhīm was. He found him at his last breath, took him, and placed him in his lap. Then he wept. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said to him, “You are weeping! Were you not forbidden to weep?” He said: “No, but I was forbidden two foolish, sinful sounds: a sound at a calamity—scratching the face, tearing the clothes, and wailing, which is Satan’s voice—and a sound at a pleasure: amusement and Satan’s musical instruments. This is compassion, and whoever does not show compassion will not receive compassion. Were it not that this is a rightful decree and a true promise, and that this is a path that must be trodden, we would grieve for you with a grief greater than this. We are sorrowful for you, Ibrāhīm: the heart grieves and the eye sheds tears, but we say nothing that angers the Lord.”

Al-Naḍr ibn Salama related to us: Ibrāhīm ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Sāmī related to us: Ḥātim ibn Ismāʿīl related to us, from Usāma ibn Zayd, from al-Mundhir ibn ʿUbayd, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥassān ibn Thābit, from his mother Sīrīn, Māriya’s sister, who said: The Messenger of God ﷺ saw a gap in the grave—that is, Ibrāhīm’s grave—and ordered that it be filled. He was asked, “Messenger of God?” He said: “It neither harms nor benefits, but it pleases the living. When a servant does a thing, God loves for him to do it well.”

Duḥaym related to us: Marwān ibn Muʿāwiya related to us, from Isrāʾīl, from Ziyād ibn ʿAlāqa, from al-Mughīra ibn Shuʿba, who said: The sun was eclipsed on the day Ibrāhīm, the son of the Messenger of God ﷺ died. The Messenger of God ﷺ stood and said: “The sun and moon are two of God’s signs; they do not eclipse for anyone’s death or life. When you see them, then pray until they have cleared.”

He said: When Umm Ibrāhīm gave birth, as al-Qaʿnabī related to us, from Ḥusayn ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿAbbās, from ʿIkrima, from Ibn ʿAbbās, who said: When Māriya gave birth, the Messenger of God ﷺ said: “Her child has set her free.”

Ibrāhīm, the son of the Messenger of God ﷺ was sixteen months old when he died, according to what ʿAlī ibn Maʿbad related to us, from ʿĪsā ibn Yūnus, from al-Aʿmash, from a man whom he named, from al-Barāʾ ibn ʿĀzib. The Messenger of God ﷺ said: “He has a wet nurse in Paradise who will complete his nursing.”

Yazīd ibn Abī Salama related to us, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ziyād: al-Ḥajjāj ibn Arṭāʾ related to us, from Abū Bakr ibn ʿAmr, from Yazīd ibn al-Barāʾ, from his father, who said: When Ibrāhīm died, the Messenger of God ﷺ said: “He has a nurse in Paradise who will complete the remainder of his nursing.”

Then he returned to the account of Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb. He said: The mule and the donkey were his most beloved mounts. He named the mule Duldul and the donkey Yaʿfūr. Honey pleased him, so he prayed for blessing upon the honey of Banhā. Those garments remained until he ﷺ was shrouded in some of them.

Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us: Mūsā ibn Dāwūd related to us, from Sallām, from ʿAbd al-Malik ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, from al-Ḥasan al-ʿUranī, from Ashʿath ibn Ṭulayq, from Murra ibn al-Muṭṭalib—or al-Ṭayyib—from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: al-Qāsim ibn ʿAbd Allāh related to us, from ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿUmar, from a reliable transmitter, from Ibn Masʿūd, who said: We asked, “Messenger of God, in what shall we shroud you?” He said: “In these garments of mine, or in garments from Egypt.” In his version of the report, Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār said: “Or in garments from Egypt, or in a robe.” One of the two said: “Or in a Yemeni garment.”

Ibn Abī Maryam said: Ibn Lahīʿa said that Māriya’s sister was named Qayṣara. It is also said that her name was Sīrīn.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from al-Aʿraj, who said: Al-Muqawqis, ruler of Alexandria, sent Māriya and her sister Ḥanna. The Messenger of God ﷺ lodged her in his charitable endowment among Banū Qurayẓa.

Hānī ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb and Ibn Hubayra, that al-Ḥasan ibn ʿAlī spoke to Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān asking him to exempt all the inhabitants of the village of Umm Ibrāhīm from the poll tax, on account of her sanctity. He did so and remitted their land tax. None of them was liable for any tax. All the village’s inhabitants were her people and relatives; they dwindled away until only one household remained, with a few people still among them.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn [Maslama related to us: Ismāʿīl ibn] ʿAyyāsh related to us, from Abū Bakr ibn Abī Maryam, from Rāshid ibn Saʿd, that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: “Had Ibrāhīm lived, I would have exempted every Copt from the poll tax.”

Māriya died in Muḥarram of the year fifteen. She was buried in al-Baqīʿ, and ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb prayed over her. The Messenger sent by al-Muqawqis was, according to what ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, Ibn Jabr.

After the death of the Messenger of God ﷺ Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq, according to what ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from al-Ḥārith ibn Yazīd, from ʿAlī ibn Rabāḥ al-Lakhmī, sent Ḥāṭib to al-Muqawqis in Egypt. He passed through an area of villages in the eastern region, made a truce with them, and they gave him [tribute]. They remained in that state until ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ entered it and they fought him, whereupon that treaty was broken. ʿAbd al-Malik said: That was the first truce in Egypt.

2 Ibn Hishām said: Abū Baltaʿa’s name was ʿAmr, and Ḥāṭib was a Lakhmī. 2

Concerning this, Ḥassān ibn Thābit says, as Wuthayma ibn Mūsā related to us:

Tell the Messenger’s envoys: “Cry out to the people— Shujāʿ and Diḥya ibn Khalīfa, And ʿAmr, Ḥāṭib, and Sulayṭ, And ʿAmr, who is the head of the letter.”

In verses in which he mentioned the Messenger of God’s envoys ﷺ to the kings.

The Account of the Reason ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ Entered Egypt

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ. He said: In the year eighteen, when ʿUmar arrived at al-Jābiya, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ met privately with him and asked permission to march to Egypt. ʿAmr had entered Egypt in the pre-Islamic period, knew its routes, and had seen how much it contained. The reason ʿAmr entered it, according to what Yaḥyā ibn Khālid al-ʿAdawī related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa and Yaḥyā ibn Ayyūb, from Khālid ibn Yazīd, who said that it had reached him, was that ʿAmr came to Jerusalem on a trading expedition with a group of Quraysh. There they met a monk from among the monks of the Byzantines, from the people of Alexandria, who had come to pray in Jerusalem. He went out into some of its mountains to wander in devotion.

ʿAmr was tending his camels and those of his companions; they took turns tending them.

While ʿAmr was tending his camels, that monk passed by him. He was very thirsty on an intensely hot day. He stopped by ʿAmr and asked him for water, and ʿAmr gave him water from a waterskin he had. The monk drank until he was satisfied, then fell asleep where he was. Next to where the monk slept was a hole, from which a huge snake emerged. ʿAmr saw it, shot it with an arrow, and killed it. When the monk awoke, he saw the huge snake from which God had saved him. He said to ʿAmr, “What is this?” ʿAmr told him that he had shot it and killed it. The monk came over to ʿAmr, kissed his head, and said: “God has brought me to life through you twice: once when I was severely thirsty, and once from this snake. What brought you to this land?” He said: “I came with some companions of mine seeking profit in trade.” The monk asked him: “How much do you expect to make from your trade?” He said: “I hope to make enough to buy a camel, for I own only two camels. I hope to acquire another, so that I will have three.”

The monk said: “Tell me, what is the blood price for one of your people among you?” He said: “A hundred camels.” The monk said to him: “We are not people of camels; we are people of dinars.” He said: “It would be a thousand dinars.” The monk said to him: “I am a stranger in this land. I came only to pray in the church of Jerusalem and to wander in these mountains for a month, which I had vowed to do. I have completed that, and I wish to return to my homeland. Will you come with me to my homeland? You have God’s covenant and pledge that I will give you two blood prices, because God brought me to life through you twice.” ʿAmr said to him: “Where is your homeland?” He said: “Egypt, in a city called Alexandria.” ʿAmr said to him: “I do not know it; I have never entered it.” The monk said: “If you entered it, you would know that you had never entered a city like it.” ʿAmr said: “Will you fulfill what you say, and are you bound by that covenant and pledge?” The monk said: “Yes. I bind myself to you by God’s covenant and pledge that I will fulfill it and bring you back to your companions.” ʿAmr said: “How long will I be away?” He said: “A month. You will travel with me for ten days going, stay with us for ten days, and return in ten. I will protect you on the way there, and I will send someone with you to protect you on your return.” ʿAmr said: “Give me time to consult my companions about it.” ʿAmr went to his companions and told them of the agreement he had made with the monk. He said to them: “Stay here until I return. I pledge to give you half of that, on condition that one of you comes with me, someone whose company I know.” They agreed, and sent one of their men with him. ʿAmr and his companion set out with the monk for Egypt, until they reached Alexandria. ʿAmr saw its buildings, the great number of its inhabitants, and the wealth and prosperity it contained; he was impressed. He said: “I have never seen anything like Egypt, or the great amount of wealth it contains.” He looked at Alexandria, its buildings, the quality of its construction, the great number of its inhabitants, and the wealth it contained, and his amazement grew.

ʿAmr arrived in Alexandria during a great festival, at which their kings and nobles gathered. They had a ball of gold, crowned with jewels, which their kings tossed to one another, catching it in their sleeves. According to the test they had made of that ball, based on the practice of those who had gone before them, whoever caught the ball in his sleeve and held it there would not die before ruling them. When ʿAmr arrived in Alexandria, the monk honored him with every honor, dressed him in a garment of brocade, and put it on him. ʿAmr and the monk sat with the people at that gathering, where they tossed the ball and caught it in their sleeves. One of them threw it, and it flew until it fell into ʿAmr’s sleeve. They were astonished at this and said: “This ball has never failed us, except this time. Do you think this Bedouin will rule us? That will never happen!” The monk went among the people of Alexandria and told them that ʿAmr had saved his life twice, and that he had promised him two thousand dinars. He asked them to collect the money for him among themselves. They did so and handed it over to ʿAmr. ʿAmr and his companion set out, and the monk sent with them a guide and messenger, provisioned them, and treated them generously, until ʿAmr and his companion returned to their companions. Thus ʿAmr learned the approaches to and routes out of Egypt, and saw enough of it to know that it was the finest of lands and the richest in wealth. When ʿAmr returned to his companions, he gave them a thousand dinars among themselves and kept a thousand for himself. ʿAmr said: “That was the first wealth I acquired and accumulated.”

The Account of the Conquest of Egypt

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Abī Jaʿfar, ʿAyyāsh ibn ʿAbbās al-Qitbānī, and others—some adding to what the others related. He said: When ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb arrived at al-Jābiya, ʿAmr went to him privately and said: “Commander of the Faithful, permit me to march to Egypt.” He urged him to do so, saying: “If you conquer it, it will be a source of strength for the Muslims and an aid to them. It is the land with the greatest wealth and the least capacity for fighting and war.” ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb feared for the Muslims and disliked the idea. ʿAmr continued to magnify its importance to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, telling him about it and making its conquest seem easy, until ʿUmar was inclined to the idea. He appointed him over four thousand men, all from ʿAkk. It is also said: rather, three thousand five hundred.

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ entered Egypt with three thousand five hundred men.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, the same report, except that he said: “A third of them were from Ghāfiq.”

Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān. He said: ʿUmar said to him: “Set out, and I shall seek God’s guidance concerning your march. My letter will reach you soon, God willing. If my letter reaches you in which I order you to turn back from Egypt before you enter it, or any part of its territory, then turn back. But if you enter it before my letter reaches you, proceed on your way. Seek God’s help and His support.”

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ set out in the middle of the night, and no one among the people knew of it. ʿUmar sought God’s guidance, and it seemed that he feared for the Muslims on that expedition. He wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ ordering him to turn back with the Muslims who were with him. The letter reached ʿAmr while he was at Rafḥ. ʿAmr feared that if he took the letter and opened it, he would find in it an order to turn back, as ʿUmar had stipulated. He therefore did not take the letter from the messenger, but delayed him, and continued on as he was until he encamped at a village between Rafḥ and al-ʿArīsh. He asked about it, and was told that it was in Egypt. He called for the letter and read it aloud to the Muslims. ʿAmr said to those with him:

“Do you not know that this village is in Egypt?” They said: “Yes.” He said: “The Commander of the Faithful gave me instructions and ordered me that, if his letter reached me and I had not entered the land of Egypt, I should turn back. His letter did not reach me until we had entered the land of Egypt. So go on, and proceed with God’s blessing.”

It is also said that ʿAmr was in Palestine and advanced with his companions into Egypt without permission. A report was sent to ʿUmar about him, and ʿUmar wrote to him while he was below al-ʿArīsh. ʿAmr detained the letter and did not read it until he reached al-ʿArīsh. He read it, and it said: “From ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb to al-ʿĀṣ ibn al-ʿĀṣ. To proceed: You have marched to Egypt with those who are with you, where there are assembled forces of the Byzantines, while you have only a small band. By my life, even if they were your mother’s bereaved son, you would not have marched with them. If you have not reached Egypt, turn back.” ʿAmr said: “Praise be to God! What land is this?” They said: “It is part of Egypt.” So he advanced as he was.*)

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related this to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb.

It is also said that ʿAmr was with his troops at Caesarea, together with the forces stationed there.

It was said that it was from Egypt. He called for the letter and read it aloud to the Muslims. ʿAmr said to those with him:

“Do you not know that this village is in Egypt?” They said, “Yes.” He said, “The Commander of the Faithful entrusted me and ordered me, if his letter reached me before I had entered the land of Egypt, to turn back. His letter did not reach me until we had entered the land of Egypt. So march on, and proceed with God’s blessing.”

It is said instead that ʿAmr was in Palestine and advanced with his companions into Egypt without permission. A report about him was sent to ʿUmar, who wrote to him while he was still short of al-ʿArīsh. ʿAmr held the letter back and did not read it until he reached al-ʿArīsh. It said: “From ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb to al-ʿĀṣ ibn al-ʿĀṣ: To proceed: You have marched to Egypt with those who are with you, while there are Roman forces there and you have only a small band. By my life, even if they were your mother’s bereaved child, you would not have marched with them. If you have not yet reached Egypt, turn back.” ʿAmr said, “Praise be to God! What land is this?” They said, “Egypt.” So he advanced as he was. ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related this to us from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb.

It is said instead that ʿAmr was with his troops at Caesarea, among the Muslim forces stationed there, while ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb was then at al-Jābiya. ʿAmr wrote secretly, asking permission to go to Egypt, and ordered his companions to draw apart, as people do when they mean to move from one place to another nearby. Then he set out with them by night. When the commanders of the forces discovered he was gone, they disapproved of what he had done and thought that he had exposed them to danger. They reported this to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, who wrote to him: “To al-ʿĀṣ ibn al-ʿĀṣ: To proceed: You have exposed those with you to danger. If my letter reaches you before you enter Egypt, turn back; but if it reaches you after you have entered, then proceed, and know that I will send you reinforcements.” This is according to what ʿAbd Allāh ibn Maslama and Yaḥyā ibn Khalad related to us from al-Layth ibn Saʿd.

He said: It is also said that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ after Syria had been conquered: “Call the people to march with you to Egypt. Whoever readily joins you, march with him.” He sent this message with Shurayk ibn ʿAbda. ʿAmr called them, and they hastened to set out with him. Then ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān entered to see ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, and ʿUmar said: “I have written to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ to march from Syria to Egypt.” ʿUthmān said: “Commander of the Faithful, ʿAmr is daring, bold, and fond of command. I fear he may set out without a secure position or a force, exposing the Muslims to destruction in the hope of an opportunity that may or may not come.” ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb regretted writing to ʿAmr, concerned by what ʿUthmān had said, and wrote to him: “If my letter reaches you before you enter Egypt, return to your position; but if you have entered, proceed on your way.”

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ was, as Saʿīd ibn ʿUfair related to us from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, short, large-headed, with a prominent forehead, a wide mouth, a large beard, broad shoulders, and large hands and feet. Al-Layth said: “He fills this mosque.”)

He said: When al-Muqawqis learned that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ had arrived in Egypt, he went to al-Fusṭāṭ and began preparing armies against ʿAmr. A Roman, called al-Aʿrayj, was in charge of the fortress, and he was under al-Muqawqis’s authority. ʿAmr advanced, and when he reached Jabal al-Ḥalāl, Rāshida and tribes of Lakhm joined him. ʿAmr continued until he reached al-ʿArīsh, where the time of sacrifice came.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who said:

That day ʿAmr sacrificed a ram on behalf of his companions.

A man who had set out with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ when he marched from Syria to Egypt—according to what Hāniʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us from Abū Shurayḥ ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shurayḥ, from ʿAbd al-Karīm ibn al-Ḥārith—lost his camel. He went to ʿAmr and asked him for a mount. ʿAmr said to him: “Keep up with your companions until we reach the outskirts of the settled land.” When they reached al-ʿArīsh, the man came to him, and ʿAmr ordered that he be given two camels. Then he said: “You will continue to prosper as long as your leaders show you mercy. When they cease to show you mercy, you and they will perish.”

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ, who said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ advanced. The first place where fighting took place was al-Faramā. The Romans fought him fiercely for about a month, then God granted him victory there.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd, as Saʿīd ibn ʿUfair related to us, was on ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ’s right wing from the time he set out from Caesarea until his campaign was over.

Scholars of Egypt other than Ibn ʿUfair said: In Alexandria there was a Coptic bishop called Abū Binyāmīn. When he learned that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ had arrived in Egypt, he wrote to the Copts, telling them that the Romans would no longer hold power and that their rule had ended, and urging them to welcome ʿAmr. It is said that the Copts who were at al-Faramā were ʿAmr’s supporters that day.

In his account, ʿUthmān said: Then ʿAmr advanced, meeting no resistance except for light skirmishing, until he encamped at al-Qawāṣir.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shurayḥ told him that he heard Sharāḥīl ibn Yazīd relate from Abū al-Ḥusayn, who heard a man of Lakhm tell Kurayb ibn Abraha:

“I was grazing sheep for my family at al-Qawāṣir. ʿAmr and those with him encamped, and I drew near to their nearest camp. There I found a group of Copts nearby. One of them said to another: ‘Are you not amazed at these people, who advance against the Roman forces when they are so few?’ Another man among them answered: ‘These people never march against anyone without prevailing over them, until they kill the best of their own.’ I got up, grabbed him by the collar, and said: ‘You are the one saying this? Come with me to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ so he can hear what you said.’ His companions and others pleaded with me until they freed him from my grasp. I took the sheep back to my home, then came back and joined the people.”

In his account, ʿUthmān said: ʿAmr advanced, meeting no resistance except for light skirmishing, until he reached Bilbays. They fought him there for about a month, until God granted him victory. Then he went on, meeting no resistance except for light skirmishing, until he reached Umm Dunayn. There they fought him fiercely, and the victory was long delayed. He wrote to ʿUmar asking for reinforcements, and ʿUmar sent him four thousand, bringing the total to eight thousand, and he fought them.

Then he returned to the account of Ibn Wahb, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shurayḥ, from Sharāḥīl ibn Yazīd, from Abū al-Ḥusayn, who had heard a man of Lakhm. He said: A man came to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ and said: “Send horsemen with me so I can go around behind them during the fighting.” He sent out five hundred cavalry with him. They rode around behind the mountain until they entered Maġār Banū Wāʾil before dawn. The Romans had dug a trench, made gates in it, and scattered iron caltrops in its courtyards. When dawn broke, the two sides met, and the Lakhmī and his men came up behind the Romans. The Romans were routed and fled into the fortress.

Someone other than Ibn Wahb said: He sent out five hundred men under Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa. He said: At daybreak, the people rose, prayed the dawn prayer, and then mounted their horses. ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ went out to fight and attacked them from the front. The cavalry he had sent around behind them charged them, and they were routed. They had dug a trench around the fortress and made gates in the trench.

In his account, Ibn Wahb said, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shurayḥ: ʿAmr and those with him marched on until he encamped before the fortress and besieged them, until they asked him to allow a dozen or so households to leave and to open the fortress to him. He agreed. ʿAmr stipulated for each of his companions one dinar, a jubba, a burnous, a turban, and two leather socks. They asked him to let them prepare a banquet for him and his companions, and he agreed.

My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, related to me that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ ordered his companions to make ready. They put on their striped cloaks and then came forward.

In his account, Ibn Wahb said: When they had finished eating, ʿAmr asked them, “How much did you spend?”

They said, “Twenty thousand dinars.” ʿAmr said, “We have no need of your banquet after today. Give us twenty thousand dinars.” A group of Copts came to him and asked permission to return to their villages and families. ʿAmr said to them: “How did you find our conduct?” They said, “We have seen nothing but good.” Then the man who had said what he said the first time told them:

“You will continue to prevail over everyone you meet until you kill the best of your own.” ʿAmr grew angry and ordered that the man be seized. His companions pleaded with him and told him that the man did not know what he was saying, until they freed him. When news reached ʿAmr that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb had been killed, he sent for that Copt and found that he had died. ʿAmr was astonished by what he had said.

Someone other than Ibn Wahb said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ said: “When ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb was stabbed, I said, ‘That is what the Copt said.’ When I was told that he had been killed by Abū Luʾluʾa, a Christian man, I said, ‘That is not what he meant; he meant someone killed by the Muslims.’ When ʿUthmān was killed, I knew that what the man had said was true.”

In his account, my father said: When they had finished their banquet, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ ordered that food be prepared for them and told them to attend. He had tharīd and stew prepared for them, and ordered his companions to wear cloaks, drape them over themselves in the ṣammāʾ style, and sit on their knees. When the Romans arrived, they set out brocade chairs and sat on them, while the Arabs sat beside them. Each Arab began taking a large mouthful of tharīd and biting off pieces of the meat, which flew onto the Romans sitting next to him. The Romans were disgusted by this and said: “Where are those who came to us before?” They were told: “Those were the men of counsel; these are the men of war.”

He said: I have also heard another account of the conquest of the fortress.

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa informed us, from ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Abī Jaʿfar, ʿAyyāsh ibn ʿAbbās, and others, one adding to the account of another, that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ besieged them in the fortress called Bābalyūn for some time, fighting them fiercely morning and evening. When the conquest was long delayed, he wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb asking for reinforcements and informing him of the situation. ʿUmar sent him four thousand men, one man among them for every thousand men, and ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote to him: “I have sent you four thousand men, one man among them for every thousand, who will stand in place of a thousand: al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām, al-Miqdād ibn ʿAmr, ʿUbāda ibn al-Ṣāmit, and Maslama ibn Mukhallad.” Others said: “Rather, Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa was the fourth; they do not count Maslama.”

ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb said: “Know that you have twelve thousand with you, and twelve thousand are not defeated for want of numbers.”

ʿUthmān said: Ibn Wahb said: Al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to me: “I was told that Kisrā had men such that, if he sent one of them with an army, he would add a thousand to the army’s strength in recognition of that man’s worth in war. If he needed one of them and that man was with an army, he would hold him back because he needed him and add a thousand men to their number.”

Al-Layth said: “I understood that what ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb did when he sent al-Zubayr, al-Miqdād, and those he sent with them was like what Kisrā used to do.”

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who said: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb had been concerned for ʿAmr, so he sent al-Zubayr after him with twelve thousand men, and al-Zubayr took part with him in the conquest.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us, from ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith and Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb sent al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām with twelve thousand men.

Someone other than ʿUthmān said: They had dug a trench around their fortress, made gates in the trench, and fixed iron spikes in the courtyards at the gates. ʿAmr had come from Syria with only a small force, so he would spread out his companions to make the enemy think that they were more numerous than they were. When he reached the trench, the enemy called out to him: “We have seen what you have done. We know that you have only so many companions with you”—and they were right to the very last man. ʿAmr remained there for several days, going out at dawn and lining his companions up, armed, at the entrances to the trench. While he was doing this, news came to him of al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām. Then al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām arrived with twelve thousand men, and ʿAmr went out to meet him. They then set out together, and before long al-Zubayr mounted his horse, rode around the trench, and stationed men all around it.

Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān, from Ibn Lahīʿa, who said: When the reinforcements arrived for ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, he pressed the siege of the fortress and set up a mangonel against it. That day ʿAmr said:

A day for Hamdān, and a day for al-Ṣadaf;

The mangonel in its twisting sling;

And ʿAmr trots with the unsteady trot of a feeble old man.

They say that ʿAmr would stand only beneath the banner of Balī.

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, as a scholar of Egypt told me, had once entered the fortress and debated with its commander about some matter of their situation. ʿAmr said, “I will go out and consult my companions.” The commander of the fortress had instructed the man at the gate that, when ʿAmr passed him, he should drop a rock on him and kill him. As ʿAmr was about to leave, he passed an Arab man, who said to him: “You got in; see how you get out.” ʿAmr went back to the commander of the fortress and said: “I want to bring some of my companions to you so they can hear from you what I have heard.” The foreigner thought to himself: “It is better to kill a group than one man.” He sent word to the man he had ordered to kill ʿAmr not to harm him, in the hope that ʿAmr would bring his companions so he could kill them. Then ʿAmr left.*) This, or something to that effect.

ʿĪsā ibn Ḥammād related to us. He said: When the Muslims besieged the fortress, ʿUbāda ibn al-Ṣāmit was off to one side praying, with his horse beside him. Some Romans saw him and came out to him, wearing finery and splendid garments. When they drew near, he ended his prayer, leapt onto his horse, and charged at them. When they saw that he was not turning away from them, they fled back, and he pursued them. They began throwing down their belts and belongings to distract him from chasing them, but he paid no attention to them until they entered the fortress. They pelted ʿUbāda with stones from atop the fortress, so he turned back without touching any of the belongings they had thrown down, returning to the place where he had been and resuming the prayer. The Romans came out to gather their belongings.

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us: al-Mufaḍḍal ibn Faḍāla informed us, from ʿAyyāsh ibn ʿAbbās al-Qitbānī, from Shayīm ibn Baytān, from Shaybān ibn Umayya, from Ruwayfiʿ ibn Thābit, who said:

In the time of the Messenger of God ﷺ one of us would take his brother’s worn-out mount on the condition that he give him half of what he captured as booty, with the other half for himself. One of us might get the arrowhead and the feathers, while the other got the shaft. The Messenger of God ﷺ said: “Whoever cleans himself after relieving himself with an animal’s dung or a bone, Muḥammad disavows him.”

ʿAyyāsh ibn ʿAbbās said: Shayīm ibn Baytān also informed me, from Abū Sālim al-Jayshānī, who heard ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr, while he was stationed at the fortress of Bābalyūn, relate this ḥadīth from the Messenger of God ﷺ.

(4 In his account, ʿUthmān said: When the conquest was long delayed for ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, al-Zubayr said: “I offer myself to God, hoping that by this God will grant the Muslims victory.” He set a ladder against the side of the fortress facing the Ḥammām Market, then climbed it and ordered them to respond together when they heard his takbīr. Someone other than ʿUthmān said: They suddenly found al-Zubayr atop the fortress, shouting the takbīr, with his sword beside him, and the people pressed onto the ladder until ʿAmr stopped them, fearing it would break. Then he returned to ʿUthmān’s account, which said: When al-Zubayr stormed the fortress and those who followed him did likewise, he shouted the takbīr, and those with him shouted it too. The Muslims outside answered them, and the people in the fortress had no doubt that the Arabs had all stormed it. So they fled. Al-Zubayr and his companions made for the fortress gate and opened it, and the Muslims stormed the fortress. When al-Muqawqis feared for himself and those with him, he then asked ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ for a peace agreement and proposed that he impose on the Copts a levy of two dinars per man. ʿAmr agreed to this. 4)

Saʿīd b. ʿUfayr related to us: He said, “Muḥammad b. Maslama, Mālik b. Abī Silsila al-Sulamī, and men of Banū Ḥarām climbed the fortress with al-Zubayr. Sharḥabīl b. Ḥujayya al-Murādī set up another ladder on the side of the lane of al-Zammāmira, as it is called today, and climbed it. There was some dispute between al-Zubayr and Sharḥabīl at a gate or entrance, and it seems that Sharḥabīl said something to al-Zubayr that he disliked. This reached ʿAmr b. al-ʿĀṣ, who said to him, ‘You may exact retribution from him if you wish.’ Al-Zubayr replied, ‘Should I exact retribution for a Yemenite maggot, O son of al-Nābigha?’”

As for the description of al-Zubayr b. al-ʿAwwām, Hishām b. Isḥāq related to us—as they claim—that he was fair-complexioned, of good stature, not tall, with little beard hair and a shaggy appearance; he had abundant body hair.

According to what ʿUthmān b. Ṣāliḥ related to us, from ʿAbd Allāh b. Wahb, from al-Layth, they remained at the gate of the palace until they opened it for seven months. I have also heard another account of the conquest of the palace, differing from both reports. God knows best. ʿUthmān b. Ṣāliḥ related to us: Khālid b. Najīḥ informed us, from Yaḥyā b. Ayyūb and Khālid b. Ḥumayd, who said: Khālid b. Yazīd related to us, from a group of Successors, some of whom add to the accounts of others, that when the Muslims besieged Babylon, where there was a group of Romans and the leading notables and chiefs of the Copts, with al-Muqawqis over them , they fought the Muslims there for a month. When the people saw how resolutely the Muslims were intent on taking it, how eager they were, and how steadfast and keen they were in battle, they feared that the Muslims would overcome them. Al-Muqawqis and a group of the leading Coptic notables withdrew and left through the southern gate of the palace. A group remained behind to fight the Arabs, while al-Muqawqis and the others reached the island where the arsenal is today. They ordered the bridge cut; this was during the Nile’s flood. Some of the elders of the people of Egypt claimed that al-Aʿyaraǧ had remained behind in the fortress after al-Muqawqis. When he feared the fortress would be taken, he and the men of rank and strength boarded their ships, which had been moored against the fortress, and then joined al-Muqawqis on the island.

Then he returned to the account of Yaḥyā b. Ayyūb and Khālid b. Ḥumayd. He said: Al-Muqawqis sent word to ʿAmr b. al-ʿĀṣ: “You are a people who have entered our land and have pressed us hard in battle. You have stayed a long time in our territory, but you are only a small band. The Romans are bearing down on you, having made ready to come against you with their equipment and weapons. The Nile has hemmed you in, and you are captives in our hands. Send us men from among you, that we may hear what they have to say . Perhaps matters between us and you may reach an outcome that you and we would welcome, and this fighting may cease before the Roman forces descend upon you—at which point words will avail us nothing and we will be unable to negotiate. Perhaps you will regret it if matters turn out contrary to your demands and hopes. So send us men from among your companions , that we may make an agreement with them on terms acceptable to us and to them.”

When al-Muqawqis’s messengers reached ʿAmr b. al-ʿĀṣ, he kept them with him for two days and two nights, until al-Muqawqis became concerned for them.
