He said to his companions, “Do you think they kill messengers and detain them , and consider that permissible in their religion?” ʿAmr had done this only so that they might see the Muslims’ condition. He sent them back with his own messengers, saying: “There are only three possibilities between me and you: either you enter Islam and become our brothers, with what is ours becoming yours; or you give the poll tax willingly while humbled; or we fight you with steadfastness and battle until God decides between us, for He is the best of judges.”

When al-Muqawqis’s messengers came to him, he said, “How did you find them?” They replied, “We found a people for each of whom death is dearer than life, and humility dearer than exaltation. None of them has any desire or greed for worldly things. They sit on the ground and eat on their knees. Their commander is like one of them: among them, one cannot tell the high-ranking from the low-ranking, or the master from the slave. When prayer comes, not one of them stays away. They wash their limbs with water and humble themselves in prayer.”

At that, al-Muqawqis said: “By Him by whom one swears, if these people were to march against the mountains, they would move them. No one can fight these people. If we do not take advantage of making peace with them today, while they are hemmed in by this Nile, they will not answer us after today, when the land is open to them and they are able to leave their position.”

Al-Muqawqis sent his messengers back to them : “Send us messengers from among you, that we may negotiate with them, and that we and they may discuss whatever might prove beneficial to us and to you.”

ʿAmr b. al-ʿĀṣ sent ten men, one of whom was ʿUbāda b. al-Ṣāmit.

Saʿīd b. ʿUfayr related to us: “Ten men among the Arabs had embraced Islam whose height was ten spans; ʿUbāda b. al-Ṣāmit was one of them.”

Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān. He said: ʿAmr ordered him to be the spokesman for the group, and not to agree to anything they proposed except one of these three possibilities, for the Commander of the Faithful had instructed me in this matter and ordered me to accept nothing other than one of these three possibilities.

ʿUbāda b. al-Ṣāmit was dark-skinned. When they boarded the ships to go to al-Muqawqis and entered his presence, ʿUbāda stepped forward. Al-Muqawqis was intimidated by his dark complexion and said, “Take this dark-skinned man away from me, and put someone else forward to speak with me.” They all replied, “This man is the best of us in judgment and knowledge. He is our leader, the best of us, and the one we put forward. We all defer to his words and judgment. The commander has given him instructions apart from those he gave us, and ordered us not to oppose his judgment or his words.” He said, “How have you accepted that this dark-skinned man should be the best of you? He ought to be beneath you.” They said, “Not at all. Though he is dark-skinned, as you see, he is the foremost among us in standing, precedence, intelligence, and judgment. Dark skin is not unusual among us.”

Al-Muqawqis said to ʿUbāda, “Come forward, dark-skinned man, and speak gently with me, for your dark complexion intimidates me. If your words are harsh toward me, my apprehension will only increase.” ʿUbāda stepped forward and said: “I have heard what you said. Among the companions I have left behind are a thousand dark-skinned men, all darker than I am and more fearsome in appearance. If you saw them , you would be more intimidated by them than you are by me. I have grown old and my youth has passed; yet, praise be to God, I fear no hundred of my enemies if they were to face me all together. My companions are the same. That is because we desire and purpose only to strive in the path of God and seek His good pleasure. We did not campaign against our enemies, who have fought against God, out of desire for worldly gain or to acquire more of it—though God has made that lawful for us and made lawful for us what we take as booty. It makes no difference to any one of us whether he owns a measure of gold or nothing but a dirham. For the utmost any one of us seeks from this world is a meal to eat that will satisfy his hunger for the night and day, and a cloak to wrap himself in. If one of us owns nothing but that, it is enough for him; and if he owns a measure of gold, he spends it in obedience to God and is content with what he has in his hand, enough to sustain him in this world. For the delights of this world are no true delight, and its ease is no true ease. True delight and ease are only in the Hereafter. This is what our Lord commanded us, and what our Prophet instructed us to do. He charged us that none of us should aspire in this world to anything beyond what will satisfy his hunger and cover his nakedness, and that our purpose and occupation should be seeking our Lord’s good pleasure and striving against His enemy.”

When al-Muqawqis heard this from him, he said to those around him: “Have you ever heard speech like this man’s? His appearance intimidated me, but his words intimidate me even more than his appearance. God has brought this man and his companions forth to ruin the earth. I think their rule will prevail over the whole earth .”

Then al-Muqawqis turned to ʿUbāda b. al-Ṣāmit and said: “Good man, I have heard what you said and what you related about yourself and your companions. By my life, you have attained what you have attained only through what you described, and you have prevailed over those you prevailed over only because they loved this world and desired it. A Roman force beyond counting has set out to fight you. Its men are known for their valor and might; none of them cares whom he meets or fights. We know that you will not be strong enough to face them or withstand them, because you are weak and few. You have lived among us for months, enduring hardship and a straitened livelihood and condition. We feel compassion for you because you are weak and few, and because you have so little in your possession. We are willing to make peace with you on the terms that we allot two dinars to each of your men, one hundred dinars to your commander, and one thousand dinars to your caliph. You may receive this and return to your lands before what you cannot withstand descends upon you.”

ʿUbāda b. al-Ṣāmit said: “Man, do not deceive yourself or your companions. As for the Roman force, their number and multitude, with which you threaten us, and your claim that we cannot withstand them—by my life, this is not what frightens us…” in it, nor what will deter us from what we are about. If what you have said is true, then, by God, it will make us all the more eager to fight them and all the more determined to engage them. For that will be a stronger defense for us before our Lord when we come before Him. If we are all killed, that will better secure for us His pleasure and His Garden. Nothing is more pleasing to our eyes or dearer to us than that. In that case, one of the two finest outcomes will be ours: either a great share of this world, if we prevail over you, or a share of the Hereafter, if you prevail over us. After we have done our utmost, the latter is the dearer of the two outcomes to us. God, Mighty and Glorious, has said to us in His Book: “How many a small band has overcome a large band by God’s permission; and God is with the steadfast.”

There is not one of us who does not pray to his Lord morning and evening to grant him martyrdom, and not to send him back to his homeland, his land, or his family and children. None of us is concerned about what he has left behind: each of us has entrusted his family and children to his Lord. Our only concern is what lies ahead.

As for your saying that we are in straitened and difficult circumstances in our livelihood and condition, we are in the greatest ease. Even if the whole world were ours, we would want no more for ourselves than what we have now.

So consider what you want and make it clear to us. There is no terms between us and you that we would accept from you or agree to, except one of three terms. Choose whichever you wish, and do not let yourself hope for what is false. The commander has ordered me to say this, and the Commander of the Faithful ordered him likewise. It is a pledge from the Messenger of God ﷺ that he gave us beforehand.

Either you agree to Islam, the religion beyond which God accepts no other—the religion of His prophets, messengers, and angels. God has commanded us to fight whoever opposes it and turns away from it until he enters it. If he does so, he will have what we have and bear what we bear, and he will be our brother in God’s religion. If you and your companions accept this, you will prosper in this world and the Hereafter. We will withdraw from fighting you, and will neither deem it lawful to harm you nor interfere with you.

If you refuse, except to pay the jizya, then pay us the jizya directly, in submission. We will make an agreement with you on terms acceptable to both you and us, year after year, for as long as we and you remain. We will fight on your behalf against whoever opposes you or encroaches upon any part of your land, or threatens your lives or property, and we will take responsibility for that on your behalf, since you will be under our protection and bound by a covenant with us.

If you refuse, then between us and you there is nothing but judgment by the sword, until we are all killed or we obtain what we seek from you. This is the religion by which we worship God; in our dealings with Him, no other course is permitted to us. So consider what is best for yourselves.

Al-Muqawqis said to him, “That will never happen. All you want is to make us your slaves for as long as the world lasts.”

“Exactly so,” ʿUbāda ibn al-Ṣāmit replied. “Choose what you wish.”

Al-Muqawqis said to him, “Will you not agree to some term other than these three?”

ʿUbāda raised his hands and said, “No, by the Lord of this heaven, the Lord of this earth, and the Lord of all things: we have no term for you other than these. Choose what is best for yourselves.”

At that, al-Muqawqis turned to his companions and said, “The people have made their position clear. What do you think?” They replied:

“Would anyone accept such humiliation? As for their demand that we enter their religion, that can never be: we will not abandon the religion of Christ, son of Mary, and enter a religion we do not know. As for their wish to take us captive and make us slaves, death is easier than that. If they would accept our paying them twice what we have given them many times over, that would be easier for us.”

Al-Muqawqis said to ʿUbāda, “The people have refused. What do you think? Go back to your companion, on the understanding that this time we will give you what you asked for and that you will depart.”

ʿUbāda and his companions rose. Then al-Muqawqis said to those around him, “Obey me and agree to one of these three terms. By God, you have no power to withstand them. If you do not agree willingly, you will have to agree to something greater under duress.”

They said, “Which term should we agree to?”

He replied, “I will tell you. I do not advise you to enter a religion other than your own. As for fighting them, I know you cannot prevail over them or endure as they do. The third term is unavoidable.”

They said, “Are we to be their slaves forever?”

“Yes,” he said. “You will be slaves with authority in your own lands, secure in yourselves, your property, and your offspring. That is better for you than being killed to the last man, and becoming slaves who are sold and scattered across the lands, enslaved forever—you, your families, and your offspring.”

They said, “Death is easier for us.”

They ordered the bridge from al-Fusṭāṭ to be cut. At the Island and the Palace, a great multitude of Copts and Romans had gathered. The Muslims then pressed the fighting against those in the Palace until they overcame them and God gave them power over them. Many were killed, and some were taken prisoner. All the ships withdrew to the Island. The Muslims found themselves surrounded by water on every side, unable to proceed toward al-Ṣaʿīd or to any of the other towns and villages. Al-Muqawqis said to his companions, “Did I not tell you this and warn you of it? What are you waiting for? By God, you will agree to their terms willingly, or you will agree to something greater under duress. Obey me before you regret it.”

When they saw what they saw, and al-Muqawqis had said what he said to them, they submitted to the jizya and accepted it, on the condition that there be a treaty between them whose terms they knew. Al-Muqawqis sent word to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ: “I have always been eager to agree to one of the terms you sent me, but those Romans and Copts who are with me have prevented me. I had no right to dispose of their property without their consent. They have recognized my sincere counsel to them and my desire for their well-being, and have returned to my position. So grant me safe conduct, that you and I may meet with a group of my companions, and you with a group of yours. If we reach an agreement, it will be settled for us all; if not, we will return to the position we held before.”

ʿAmr consulted his companions about this. They said, “We should not agree to any treaty or jizya until God grants us victory and the whole land becomes ours as spoils and booty, just as the Palace and everything in it have become ours.”

ʿAmr said, “You know what the Commander of the Faithful stipulated in his instructions to me: if they agree to one of the three terms he stipulated, I am to agree to it and accept it from them. Besides, this water has come between us and the fighting we wish to do against them.”

They came to an agreement among themselves and concluded a treaty: two dinars were to be levied on every person among the Copts throughout Egypt, Upper and Lower, noble and humble alike, who had reached puberty.

There was to be no levy on the aged, the child who had not reached puberty, or women. The Muslims were to have lodging for their entire community wherever they settled. If one Muslim or more lodged with someone, that person was to provide them with hospitality for three days, as an obligation. The Copts were to retain their land and property, and no one was to interfere with any of it.

All these terms were imposed on the Copts alone. They counted specifically the Copts who had reached the age at which the jizya was due and on whom the two dinars were levied. Their headmen certified the count under solemn oaths. The total number counted in Egypt, Upper and Lower, among all the Copts, according to the figures they counted, recorded, and reported, exceeded six million people. Their levy that year therefore amounted to twelve million dinars each year.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yaḥyā ibn Maymūn al-Ḥaḍramī, who said: When ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ conquered Egypt, he concluded a treaty on behalf of all the Coptic men there who were approaching puberty and older. There were no women, elderly men, or children among them. They were counted at two dinars each, and their number reached eight.2

He said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to me; al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that al-Muqawqis concluded a treaty with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ stipulating that two dinars be levied on every Coptic man.

Then he returned to the account of Yaḥyā ibn Ayyūb and Khālid ibn Ḥumayd. He said: Al-Muqawqis stipulated for the Romans that they should be given a choice: whoever among them wished to remain under these same terms could do so, with the terms binding upon him and imposed upon him, if he remained in Alexandria or anywhere else in the land of Egypt; and whoever wished to leave it for the land of the Romans could leave. Al-Muqawqis was to have the choice concerning the Romans alone, until he wrote to the king of the Romans, informing him of what he had done. If the king accepted and approved it, it would be binding upon them; otherwise, they would all remain as they had been.

They set this down in a document, and al-Muqawqis wrote to the king of the Romans, informing him of the whole matter. The king of the Romans wrote back, condemning his judgment, reproaching him for his weakness, and repudiating what he had done. In his letter he said: “Only twelve thousand Arabs have come against you, while in Egypt there are so many Copts that their number cannot be counted. If the Copts have refused to fight, and prefer to pay tribute to the Arabs and have chosen them over us, you have in Egypt more than a hundred thousand Romans in Alexandria and with you, equipped and strong.

“The Arabs, as you have seen, are in a weak condition. Yet you have been unable to fight them, and have agreed that you and the Romans with you should be in the same condition as the Copts, humiliated. Will neither you nor the Romans with you fight them until you die or prevail over them? Given your great numbers and strength, and their small numbers and weakness, they are but a morsel among you. Engage them in battle; let that be your only course.” The king of the Romans sent a letter to the Roman community expressing the same view.

When the king of the Romans’ letter reached him, al-Muqawqis said: “By God, despite their small numbers and weakness, they are stronger and more formidable than we are, despite our great numbers and strength. One of them is worth a hundred of us. That is because they are a people for whom death is dearer than life. A man among them fights as one ready to die, hoping not to return to his family, his country, or his children. They believe they will earn a great reward for those of us whom they kill, and they say:

“‘If they are killed, they enter Paradise.’ They have no desire for this world and no pleasure in it beyond what suffices them in food and clothing. We are a people who hate death and love life and its pleasures. How, then, can we stand against them? How can we endure fighting them? Know, company of Romans, that by God I will not withdraw from what I have undertaken, nor from the peace I made with the Arabs. I know that tomorrow you will return to my view and my words, and wish you had obeyed me. For I have seen with my own eyes and learned what the king has neither seen nor witnessed nor learned. And what sound judgment he has! Is none of you content to be secure throughout his life in his person, property, and children, for two dinars a year?”

Then al-Muqawqis turned to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ and said to him: “The king has disapproved of what you did and reproached me for my weakness. He wrote to me and to the Roman community that we should not accept a peace with you, and ordered them to fight you until they prevail over you or you prevail over them. I would not withdraw from what I undertook and agreed with you. My authority extends only over myself and those who obey me. The peace with the Copts has been concluded between you and them; they have given no cause for its breach. I remain bound by it myself, and the Copts remain bound by the peace you made with them and the agreement you concluded. As for the Romans, I disavow them. I ask you to grant me three things.”

ʿAmr said to him: “What are they?” He replied: “Do not break the agreement with the Copts; include me among them and bind me to what binds them. My will and theirs are united in what I agreed with you, and they remain bound to you to do as you wish.

“As for the second: if, from this day onward, the Romans ask you to make peace with them, do not make peace with them until you make them booty and slaves, for they deserve that: I advised them, but they suspected me of treachery; I looked out for them, but they accused me. As for the third, I ask that, if I die, you order them to bury me at Abū Yaḥnis in Alexandria.”

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ granted al-Muqawqis this and agreed to what he had requested, on condition that they guarantee him both bridges and provide lodging places, hospitality, markets, and bridges for them between al-Fusṭāṭ and Alexandria. They did so.

ʿUthmān said: “The Copts thus became their auxiliaries, as stated in the report.”

It is said that al-Muqawqis made peace with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ concerning the Romans only while he was besieging Alexandria.

Yaḥyā ibn Khālid al-ʿAdawī related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, that when ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ conquered Alexandria, he besieged its people for three months and pressed them hard, until they feared him. Al-Muqawqis asked him to make peace on their behalf, as he had made peace with him concerning the Copts, on condition that he wait to learn the king’s decision.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that al-Muqawqis, the Roman who was ruler over Egypt, made peace with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ on the terms that any Romans who wished to depart could do so, while those who wished to remain would remain under terms he had specified. When this reached Heraclius, king of the Romans, he took the strongest possible exception to it and condemned it most severely. He sent armies, who shut the gates of Alexandria and declared war to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ. Al-Muqawqis went out to him and said: “I ask you for three things.” He replied:

“What are they?” Al-Muqawqis said: “Do not offer the Romans what you offered me, for I advised them, but they regarded my advice as treachery. Do not break the agreement with the Copts, for they did not cause its breach. And when I die, order them to bury me at Abū Yaḥnis.” ʿAmr said: “That is the easiest of them for us.”

Then he returned to ʿUthmān’s account. He said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ set out with the Muslims as soon as they were able to depart. A group of Coptic leaders went with him; they had repaired the roads for them and built bridges and markets. The Copts became their auxiliaries in whatever fighting they wished to undertake against the Romans. When the Romans heard of this, they made preparations and gathered reinforcements. Many ships came to them from the land of the Romans, carrying a large force of Romans equipped with arms. ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ set out from al-Fusṭāṭ toward Alexandria. He encountered no one until he reached Tarnūṭ, where he met a party of Romans. They fought him lightly, but God routed them. ʿAmr and his men continued until they met the Roman force at Kawm Shurayk. They fought there for three days, then God granted the Muslims victory, and the Romans turned their backs.

It is said, rather, that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ sent Shurayk ibn Sumayy in pursuit of them, as ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb. Shurayk caught up with them at the mound called Kawm Shurayk and fought them there, routing them.

Someone other than ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama said: Shurayk met them at Kawm Shurayk. He was at the vanguard of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, while ʿAmr was at Tarnūṭ. They drove Shurayk to the mound, where he took refuge. The Romans surrounded him. When Shurayk ibn Sumayy saw this, he ordered Abū Nāʿima Mālik ibn Nāʿima al-Ṣadafī—the owner of the chestnut horse called Ashqar Ṣadaf, which no horse could match for speed—to charge down upon them from the mound. The Romans pursued him but could not catch him before he reached ʿAmr and informed him. ʿAmr advanced toward him, and when the Romans heard of his approach, they withdrew.

The little gate in Egypt called Khawkhāt al-Ashqar was named after the chestnut horse. The horse died, and its owner buried it there, so the place was named after it.

Then he returned to the account of Yaḥyā ibn Ayyūb and Khālid ibn Ḥumayd. He said: They then met at Sulṭays and fought there fiercely, but God routed them. Then they met at al-Karyūn and fought there for a dozen-odd days. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr was at the vanguard, and the standard-bearer that day was Wardān, ʿAmr’s client.

Ṭalq ibn al-Samḥ and Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us; they said: Ḍimām ibn Ismāʿīl al-Maʿāfirī related to us, saying: Abū Qabīl related to us, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr, that he met the enemy at al-Karyūn. He was at the vanguard, and Wardān, ʿAmr’s client, carried the standard. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr suffered many wounds and said: “Wardān, if only you would fall back a little, so we might catch our breath!” Wardān replied: “You want to catch your breath? Your breath is ahead of you, not behind you.” ʿAbd Allāh advanced. Then his father sent a messenger to ask about his wounds. ʿAbd Allāh said:

“When the soul surges, I say: ‘Be patient;

Before long, you will be praised or blamed.’”

The messenger returned to ʿAmr and told him what ʿAbd Allāh had said. ʿAmr said: “He is truly my son.”

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ performed the Prayer of Fear that day.

My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, and al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us; they said: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Bakr ibn Sawāda, that a shaykh told them he performed the Prayer of Fear in Alexandria with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, with each group performing one rakʿah and two prostrations.

Then he returned to the account of Yaḥyā ibn Ayyūb and Khālid ibn Ḥumayd. He said: God then granted the Muslims victory, and the Muslims inflicted a great slaughter among them. They pursued them until they reached Alexandria, where the Romans fortified themselves. They had fortifications that could not be stormed, one fort behind another. The Muslims encamped from Ḥalwa to Qaṣr Fāris and beyond it, while the Coptic leaders with them supplied them with the food and fodder they needed.

He said: Hānīʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Bakr ibn ʿAmr al-Khawlānī, that when ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān arrived in Alexandria, he asked about its conquest. He was told: “Of those who witnessed its conquest, none remains except an old Roman.” He ordered that they bring him, and asked him what he had witnessed of the conquest of Alexandria.

The man said: “I was a young lad, and I had a companion who was the son of a patrician among the Roman patricians. He came to me and said, ‘Why don’t we go and look at these Arabs who are fighting us?’ He put on brocade clothes, a gold headband, and an ornamented sword, and mounted a fat, fleshy pony. I mounted a lean pony. We left all the fortresses until we came out onto a rise. There we saw some people in tents. Beside each tent was a tethered horse and an upright spear. We saw that they were a feeble people, and marveled at their weakness. We said, ‘How have these people achieved what they have achieved?’ As we stood watching them and wondering, a man came out of one of those tents and looked over. When he saw us, he untied his horse, rolled it in the dust, then rubbed it down. He leapt onto its bare back, took a spear in his hand, and came toward us. I said to my companion, ‘By God, that man is after us!’ When we saw him coming toward us, pursuing no one but us, we turned and fled toward the fortress. He set off after us. He caught up with my companion, because his pony was heavy and fleshy, and struck him with his spear, knocking him down. Then he thrust the spear into his belly repeatedly until he killed him.

“Then he came after me. I hurried away; my pony was lean, and I escaped him and entered the fortress. Once inside, I felt safe. I climbed onto the fortress wall and watched him. When he had given up on catching me, he turned back. He paid no attention to my companion, whom he had killed; he did not want his belongings, nor did he strip them from him. He had been wearing brocade clothes and a gold headband. The man did not seek his mount or pay attention to any of that. He went off by another route, while I watched him and heard him speaking aloud. I thought he was reciting the Arabs’ Qurʾān. Then I realized that they had prevailed as they had and conquered the lands because they sought no worldly gain and desired nothing of it. He reached his tent, dismounted, tethered his horse, planted his spear upright, and entered his tent without telling any of his companions.”

ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said: “Describe the man to me: his appearance and his condition.” He said: “Yes. He was short and ugly, not fully built in stature or in flesh; slender, dark-complexioned, and beardless.” ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz then said: “He is describing a man from Yemen.”

He said: Hānīʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us: Muḥammad ibn Yaḥyā al-Iskandarānī related to us: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ encamped at Ḥalwa and stayed there for two months. Then he moved to al-Maqs. Cavalry came out against him from the direction of the lake, concealed behind the fortress, and engaged him. That day, twelve Muslim men were killed at the Church of Gold.

Then he returned to the account of Yaḥyā ibn Ayyūb and Khālid ibn Ḥumayd. He said: The Roman king’s envoys kept traveling by ship to Alexandria, bringing Roman reinforcements. The Roman king used to say: “If the Arabs gain control of Alexandria, that will mean the end of Roman rule and their ruin, for the Romans have no churches greater than the churches of Alexandria. The Romans’ festival in Alexandria was held when the Arabs overcame Syria.” The king said: “If they overcome us in Alexandria, the Romans will be ruined and their rule will end.” He ordered his preparations and equipment for his departure to Alexandria, so that he could personally take part in fighting there, given its importance. He ordered that no Roman be left behind, and said: “How can the Romans endure after Alexandria?” When his preparations were complete, God struck him down and caused him to die, sparing the Muslims the burden of dealing with him. His death was in the year nineteen. By his death, God broke the strength of the Romans, and a great many who had been heading to Alexandria turned back.

Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, who said: Heraclius died in the year twenty, and in that year Caesarea of Syria was conquered.

He said: Then he returned to the account of Yaḥyā ibn Ayyūb and Khālid ibn Ḥumayd. He said: At that point the Arabs grew bold and pressed the people of Alexandria with fighting, and they fought them fiercely.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who said:

A force of Romans came out through a gate of Alexandria’s fortress. They charged the people, killed a man from Mahra, cut off his head, and carried it away. The men of Mahra grew angry and said: “We will never bury him without his head.” ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ said: “You are getting angry as though you were angry with someone who cares about your anger. When the people come out, charge them and kill one of them, then throw his head to them; they will throw your companion’s head back to you.” The Romans came out to meet them, and they fought. A Roman, one of their patricians, was killed. They cut off his head and threw it to the Romans, and the Romans threw the Mahrite’s head back to them. He said: “There you are now; bury your companion.”

As ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from al-Ḥārith ibn Yazīd, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ used to say: “There are three tribes in Egypt. As for Mahra, they are a people who kill and are not killed; as for Ghāfiq, they are a people who kill and are not killed; and as for Balī, most of them have had a man who accompanied the Messenger of God ﷺ and they are the finest horsemen.”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: Ḍamām ibn Ismāʿīl related to us: ʿAyyāsh ibn ʿAbbās related to us that he said: When the Muslims besieged Alexandria, the commander of the vanguard said to them: “Do not hurry until I have given you my decision.” When the gate was opened, two men went in and were killed. The commander of the vanguard wept. He was asked: “Why did you weep? They are martyrs.” He said: “If only they were martyrs! But I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: ‘No disobedient person enters Paradise.’ I had ordered them not to enter until they had received my decision, but they went in without my permission.”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Mūsā ibn ʿAlī, that a man said to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ: “If you set up a mangonel and fired it at them, you would bring down part of their wall.” ʿAmr said: “Can you keep your position in the ranks concealed?” Al-Layth said: ʿAmr was also told, “The enemy has surrounded you, and we fear for Rāʾiṭa”—meaning his wife. He said: “Then you will find plenty of silk garments.”

Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ. He said: Khālid ibn Najīḥ related to me: A trustworthy person informed me that one day ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ fought the Romans fiercely in Alexandria. When the fighting between them grew intense, a Roman came forward to duel with Maslama ibn Mukhallad. The Roman threw him down and cast him from his horse, then lunged at him to kill him, until one of his companions protected him. Maslama was a man whom none could withstand in his way,

but these are the decrees of fate. The Romans rejoiced at this, and it distressed the Muslims. ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ was angered by it. Maslama was fleshy and heavy-bodied. At that, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ said:

“What is this matter with the effeminate man, who resembles women, entering the paths of men and imitating them?”

Maslama grew angry at this and made no reply.

The fighting then grew fierce, until they forced their way into the fortress of Alexandria. The Arabs fought them inside the fortress, then the Romans surged against them and drove them all out of the fortress except for four men, who remained inside and shut the fortress gate behind them. One was ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, another was Maslama ibn Mukhallad; we did not retain the names of the other two. They were cut off from their companions, and the Romans did not know who they were. When ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ and his companions saw this, they took refuge in a vaulted chamber in one of the bathhouses. They entered it and used it as a stronghold. They ordered a Roman to speak to them in Arabic. He said to them: “You have fallen into our hands as prisoners. Surrender, and do not kill yourselves.” They refused. Then he said to them: “We have men in the hands of your companions whom they have taken prisoner. We will give you our pledges: we will exchange you for our men, and we will not kill you.” They refused.

When the Roman saw their refusal, he said to them: “Would you agree to a proposal, a compromise between us and you? You give us a pledge, and we give you the same: one man from your side will come out, and one from ours. If our man defeats yours, you will surrender to us and place yourselves in our hands; if your man defeats ours, we will let you go to your companions.” They agreed to this and pledged themselves to it. ʿAmr, Maslama, and their two companions were in the fortress, in the vaulted chamber. They called for a duel. A Roman came out, one whose valor and strength the Romans trusted. They said: “One of you, come out to face our man.” ʿAmr wanted to go out, but Maslama stopped him and said: “What is this? You make the same mistake twice: you separate yourself from your companions when you are their commander, and they depend on you, their hearts fixed upon you, not knowing what is happening to you. And then you are not content until you go out to duel and expose yourself to death. If you are killed, that will be a calamity for your companions. Stay where you are; I will suffice you, God willing.”

ʿAmr said, “Go on; perhaps God will bring relief through you.” Muslama went out to meet the Byzantine, and they fought for a while. Then God aided him against the man, and he killed him. Muslama and his companions cried “Allāhu akbar,” and the Byzantines honored the agreement they had made with them: they opened the fortress gate for them, and they came out. The Byzantines did not know that the commander of the group was among them until they learned it later. They regretted it and, in their rage at what had escaped them, bit their hands.

When they had come out, ʿAmr was ashamed of what he had said to Muslama in anger. So ʿAmr said then, “Ask forgiveness for me for what I said to you.” Muslama asked forgiveness for him. ʿAmr said, “I have never spoken offensively except three times: twice in the pre-Islamic period, and this is the third. Every time, I regretted it and felt ashamed. I have never been ashamed of any of them more than I am of what I said to you. By God, I hope I will not do so a fourth time for as long as I live.”

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who said:

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ remained besieging Alexandria for several months. When that reached ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, he said:

“They have been slow to conquer it only because of what they have introduced.”

Yaḥyā ibn Khālid related to us, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam, from his father, who said: When the conquest of Egypt was delayed for ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, he wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ:

“To proceed: I am astonished at your delay in conquering Egypt. You have been fighting them for two years. That is only because of what you have introduced and because you have come to love of this world what your enemy loves. God ﵎ grants victory to a people only through the sincerity of their intentions. I had sent you four men and informed you that, according to what I knew of them, each man was the equal of a thousand men—unless they have been changed by what has changed others. When this letter of mine reaches you, address the people and urge them to fight their enemy; encourage them to persevere and to be sincere in their intention. Put those four men at the front of the people, and command all the people to strike them with the force of a single man’s blow. Let this be at noon on Friday, for that is an hour when mercy descends and a time when prayers are answered. Let the people cry out to God and ask Him for victory over their enemy.”

When the letter reached ʿAmr, he gathered the people and read ʿUmar’s letter to them. Then he summoned those men and placed them at the head of the people. He ordered the people to purify themselves and pray two rakʿahs, then earnestly beseech God, Mighty and Exalted, and ask Him for victory. They did so, and God granted them the conquest.

It is said that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ consulted Maslama ibn Mukhlad. ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from someone who related to him, who said: “Advise me about fighting these people.” Maslama said to him, “I think you should choose a man among the Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ who has knowledge and experience, and appoint him over the people, so that he himself takes charge of the fighting and relieves you of it.” ʿAmr said, “And who is that?” He replied, “ʿUbāda ibn al-Ṣāmit.” ʿAmr summoned ʿUbāda, and he came to him riding his horse. When he drew near, he began to dismount, but ʿAmr said to him, “I adjure you not to dismount; hand me the spearhead of your lance.” He handed it to him. ʿAmr removed his turban from his head, tied it around ʿUbāda, and appointed him to fight the Byzantines. ʿUbāda advanced in his place, formed ranks opposite the Byzantines, and fought them. God granted Alexandria’s conquest at his hands that very day.

My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, related to us: When the conquest of Alexandria was delayed for ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, he lay back, then sat up and said, “I have thought about this matter, and its end can be set right only by one who set its beginning right”—meaning the Anṣār. He summoned ʿUbāda ibn al-Ṣāmit, appointed him, and God granted Alexandria’s conquest at his hands that very day.

Then he returned to the account of Yaḥyā ibn Ayyūb and Khālid ibn Ḥumayd. They said: They besieged Alexandria for nine months after Heraclius’s death, and for five months before that. It was conquered on Friday, at the beginning of Muḥarram in the year twenty.

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Bukayr ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from Busr ibn Saʿīd, from Junāda ibn Abī Umayya, who said: On the day of Alexandria, ʿUbāda ibn al-Ṣāmit summoned me. He was in charge of fighting there. The enemy raided a group of people, but he had not given them permission to fight them.

He heard me, and sent me to come between them. I went to them and separated them, then returned to him. He asked, “Was anyone killed there?” I said, “No.” He said, “Praise be to God, who kept any of them from being killed while disobedient.”

He said: ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from Mālik ibn Anas, that Egypt was conquered in the year twenty.

He said: When God ﵎ routed the Byzantines and Alexandria was conquered—as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from al-Layth—the Byzantines fled by land and sea. ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ left a thousand of his companions in Alexandria and went with those who were with him in pursuit of the Byzantines who had fled by land. Those who had fled by sea returned to Alexandria and killed the Muslims who were there, except those who escaped. This reached ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, and he turned back, conquered it, and settled there. He wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb: “God has granted us the conquest of Alexandria by force, without pact or treaty.” ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote back, rebuking his judgment and ordering him not to go beyond it.

Ibn Lahīʿa said: This was the second conquest of Alexandria. The cause of this conquest, as Ibrāhīm ibn Saʿīd al-Balawī related to us, was that a man called Ibn Bassāma was its gatekeeper. He asked ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ to grant him security for himself, his land, and his household, and in return he would open the gate for him. ʿAmr agreed, and Ibn Bassāma opened the gate for him. ʿAmr entered; this time he entered from the direction of the bridge called Solomon’s Bridge. The first time ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ entered, he came through the city gate on the side of the Church of Gold. Ibn Bassāma’s descendants remained in Alexandria up to the present day.

Hāniʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us; Ḍimām ibn Ismāʿīl al-Maʿāfirī related to us, who said: From the time of the events concerning Alexandria until it was conquered, twenty-two Muslim men were killed.

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ sent, as ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj as an envoy to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, bearing news of the conquest. Muʿāwiya said to him, “Will you not write a letter with me?”

ʿAmr said to him, “What would I do with a letter? Are you not an Arab man who can deliver the message, and who saw and witnessed what happened?”

When Muʿāwiya came to ʿUmar, he informed him of the conquest of Alexandria. ʿUmar fell down in prostration and said, “Praise be to God.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd al-Muqriʾ related to us; Mūsā ibn ʿAlī related to us, from his father, who said:

I heard Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj say: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ sent me to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb with news of the conquest of Alexandria. I arrived in Medina at midday and made my camel kneel at the mosque door. Then I entered the mosque. While I was sitting there, a young woman came out of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb’s house. She saw me, pale and wearing travel clothes, and came to me. She said, “Who are you?” I said, “I am Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj, the messenger of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ.” She turned away from me, then came hurrying back. I could hear the rustling of her garment against her leg—or legs—as she drew near. She said, “Get up and answer the Commander of the Faithful; he is calling you.” I followed her. When I entered, I found ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb taking his cloak in one hand and tightening his waist-wrapper with the other. He said, “What news do you bring?” I said, “Good news, Commander of the Faithful: God has granted the conquest of Alexandria.” He went out with me to the mosque and said to the muezzin, “Call the people to assemble for prayer.” The people gathered, and then he said to me, “Get up and tell your companions.”

I got up and informed them. Then he prayed and went into his house, faced the qibla, and said some prayers. Then he sat down and said, “Girl, is there any food?” She brought bread and oil. He said, “Eat.” I ate shyly. Then he said, “(5 Eat, for a traveler likes food. If I were to eat, I would eat with you.” I ate shyly, then he said, 5) Girl, is there any dates?” She brought dates on a plate. He said, “Eat.” I ate shyly. Then he said, “What did you say, Muʿāwiya, when you came to the mosque?” He said, “I said, ‘The Commander of the Faithful is resting.’”

He said, “How poorly you spoke—or how poorly you judged! If I sleep by day, I will neglect the people; if I sleep by night, I will neglect myself. How could I sleep with these two responsibilities, Muʿāwiya?”

After that, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, as Ibrāhīm ibn Saʿīd al-Balawī related to us:

“To proceed: I have conquered a city whose contents I cannot describe. What I found there were four thousand maniyyāt3, four thousand baths, forty thousand Jews liable to the poll tax, and four hundred places of amusement for kings.”

He said: ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ḍimām ibn Ismāʿīl related to us, from Abū Qabīl, that when ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ conquered Alexandria, he found twelve thousand greengrocers there, selling fresh vegetables.

Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us; Ibn Miqlāṣ related to us, from Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Dāwūd, who said: I think it is from Ḥaywa ibn Shurayḥ, that when ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ conquered Alexandria, he found twelve thousand greengrocers there.

Hāniʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us; Muḥammad ibn Saʿīd al-Hāshimī related to us, who said: Seventy thousand Jews left Alexandria on the night ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ entered it—or on the night they feared that ʿAmr would enter.

Hāniʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us, from Mūsā ibn Ayyūb and Rashdīn ibn Saʿd, from al-Ḥasan ibn Thawbān, from Ḥusayn ibn Shafiyy ibn ʿUbayd, who said: Among the baths counted in Alexandria were twelve dimās. The smallest dimās could hold a thousand majālis, each of which could hold a group of people. The number of Byzantine men in Alexandria was two hundred thousand. The able-bodied among them made for Byzantine territory and boarded ships. There were a hundred large ships there, which carried thirty thousand people, along with as much wealth, property, and family members as they could take. Those who remained were the captives liable to the tax; six hundred thousand were counted that day, apart from the women and children. The people disagreed with ʿAmr about dividing them, and most wanted them divided. ʿAmr said, “I cannot divide them until I write to the Commander of the Faithful.” He wrote to him, informing him of its conquest and its circumstances, and telling him that the Muslims had asked for it to be divided. ʿUmar wrote back: “Do not divide them. Leave them, so that their tax may be a fayʾ for the Muslims and a source of strength for them in waging jihād against their enemy.” So ʿAmr left them in place, counted the inhabitants, and imposed the tax on them. All of Egypt was under a peace agreement, with a fixed levy of two dinars per man; no one was charged more than two dinars as a poll tax, although he was liable for a sum proportionate to the land and cultivation he could afford to maintain. The exception was Alexandria, for its inhabitants paid the land tax and poll tax according to what their governor deemed appropriate, since Alexandria had been conquered by force without treaty or agreement, and its people had neither a peace agreement nor protected status.

Some villages in Egypt fought, as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, and their inhabitants were taken captive. Among them were a village called Balhīb, a village called al-Khays, and a village called Sulṭays. Their captives ended up in Medina and elsewhere. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb returned them to their villages and made them, along with the Copts, people under protection.

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa informed us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that ʿAmr took the inhabitants of Balhīb, Sulṭays, Qurṭasā, and Sakhā captive. They dispersed, and the first of them reached Medina when they had broken [their agreement]. Then ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote to ʿAmr ordering him to return them. He returned those he found.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote specifically concerning the people of Sulṭays: “As for those of them who are in your hands, give them the choice between Islam and their own religion. If one accepts Islam, he is one of the Muslims, with the same rights and duties as they have. If he chooses his own religion, let him return to his village.” The man from Balhīb was given the choice then, and chose Islam.

Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān, from Yaḥyā ibn Ayyūb: The people of Sulṭays, Maṣīl, and Balhīb had aided the Byzantines against the Muslims with a force of their own. When the Muslims prevailed over them, they deemed them lawful to take and said, “These are ours as fayʾ, along with Alexandria.” ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb about this. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote back ordering him to make Alexandria and these three villages protected communities under the Muslims, and to levy the land tax on them. Their tax, together with what the Copts had agreed to pay, was to be a source of strength for the Muslims against their enemy. They were not to be made fayʾ or slaves. They did so.

It is said that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb returned them because an agreement had previously been made with them.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa and Ibn Wahb related to us, from ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from ʿAwf ibn Ḥaṭṭān, that there was an agreement with certain villages in Egypt, including Umm Dūnayn and Balhīb. When ʿUmar heard of this, he wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, ordering him to give them the choice: if they entered Islam, that was settled; if they were unwilling, he was to return them to their villages.

He said: Among the descendants of the women of Sulṭays were ʿImrān ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Jaʿfar ibn Rabīʿa; the mother of ʿIyāḍ ibn ʿUqba; Abū ʿUbayda ibn ʿUqba; the mother of ʿAwn ibn Khārija al-Qurashī, then al-ʿAdawī; the mother of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj; and, later, clients of noblemen who came into the possession of Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam, among them Abān, his paternal uncle Abū ʿIyāḍ, and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Balhībī.

Mention of those who said that Egypt was conquered by treaty

He said: Then he returned to the account of Mūsā ibn Ayyūb and Rashdīn ibn Saʿd, from al-Ḥasan ibn Thawbān, from Ḥusayn ibn Shafiyy: When ʿAmr conquered Alexandria, six hundred thousand captives liable to the tax remained there and were counted that day, apart from the women and children.

The people disagreed with ʿAmr about dividing them, and most of the Muslims wanted them divided. ʿAmr said, “I cannot divide them until I write to the Commander of the Faithful.” He wrote to him, informing him of its conquest and its circumstances, and that the Muslims had asked for it to be divided. ʿUmar wrote back: “Do not divide them. Leave them, so that their tax may be a fayʾ for the Muslims and a source of strength for them in waging jihād against their enemy.” So ʿAmr left them in place, counted the inhabitants, and imposed the tax on them. All of Egypt was under a peace agreement, with a fixed levy of two dinars per man; no one was charged more than two dinars as a poll tax, although he was liable for a sum proportionate to the land and cultivation he could afford to maintain. The exception was Alexandria, for its inhabitants paid the land tax and poll tax according to what their governor deemed appropriate, since Alexandria had been conquered by force without treaty or agreement, and its people had neither a peace agreement nor protected status.

ʿUthmān related to us; al-Layth informed us, who said: Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb used to say, “All of Egypt was conquered by treaty except Alexandria, which was conquered by force.”

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from Bakr ibn Muḍar, from ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Abī Jaʿfar, who said:

A man who had met ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ related to me: “The Copts have an agreement with so-and-so and an agreement with so-and-so,” and he named three men.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; Yaḥyā ibn Ayyūb related to us, from ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Abī Jaʿfar, from a leading veteran of the army, that the agreement of the people of Egypt was held by their leading men.

Hishām ibn Isḥāq al-ʿĀmirī related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, from ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Abī Jaʿfar, who said: I asked an old man about the conquest of Egypt. He said, “I emigrated to Medina in the days of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb when I had reached puberty, and I witnessed the conquest of Egypt.” I said to him, “Some people say that they had no agreement.” He replied, “It would not matter if a man who says they had no agreement stopped praying.” I asked, “Did they have a written document?” He said, “Yes, three documents: one held by Ṭalmā, the ruler of Ikhnā; one held by Qazmān, the ruler of Rashīd; and one held by Yaḥannas, the ruler of al-Burullus.” I asked, “What were the terms of their peace?” He said:

“Two dinars per person as poll tax, and provisions for the Muslims.” I asked, “Do you know what the conditions were?” He said:

“Yes. Six conditions: they were not to be driven from their homes; their women were not to be taken; nor their villages; nor their lands; and no increase was to be imposed on them.”

Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who related to him, from Abū Jumʿa, the client of ʿUqba, who said: ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir wrote to Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān asking him for land near the village of ʿUqba where he could establish a residence. Muʿāwiya granted him an area of one thousand cubits by one thousand cubits. A client of his who was with him said, “Look for suitable land, may God set you right.” ʿUqba replied, “We have no right to do that. Their agreement contains six conditions: nothing is to be taken from their persons, their women, or their children; no additional burden is to be imposed on them; and they are to be defended against their enemy at places where they are in danger. I bear witness on their behalf to this.”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us, from Abū Shurayḥ ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shurayḥ, from ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Abī Jaʿfar, from Abū Jumʿah Ḥabīb ibn Wahb, who said: ʿUqbah ibn ʿĀmir wrote to Muʿāwiyah asking him for a plot of land in a village in which he could build houses and dwellings. Muʿāwiyah granted him a thousand cubits by a thousand cubits. His clients and those present with him said, “Look for land that pleases you, mark it out there, and build.” He said, “We are not entitled to do that. They have six conditions in their covenant, including that nothing be taken from their land, that nothing be added to their obligations, that they not be charged with more than they can bear, that their children not be taken, and that their enemy be fought off from behind them.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Yaḥyā ibn Ayyūb related to us, from ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Abī Jaʿfar, from a man among the senior officers, who said: Muʿāwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān wrote to Wardān, ordering him to add one qīrāṭ to the assessment of each of them. Wardān wrote to Muʿāwiyah: “How can you increase their assessment when their covenant stipulates that nothing be added to it?” So Muʿāwiyah dismissed Wardān.

It is said that Muʿāwiyah dismissed Wardān for the reason reported to us by Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr: ʿUtbah ibn Abī Sufyān arrived to visit Muʿāwiyah with a group of Egyptians. Muʿāwiyah had appointed ʿUtbah over the army, Wardān over the tax revenue, and Ḥuwayt ibn Zayd over the register. Muʿāwiyah asked the delegation about ʿUtbah. ʿUbādah ibn Ṣumayl al-Maʿāfirī said, “A whale of the sea, Commander of the Faithful, and an ibex of the land.” Muʿāwiyah said to ʿUtbah, “Listen to what your subjects say of you.” He replied, “They speak truly, Commander of the Faithful. You have kept me away from the tax revenue, though they have rights over me. I dislike sitting down to ask for it, then failing to act, and so becoming miserly.” Muʿāwiyah therefore assigned the tax revenue to him as well.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb; and Ibn Wahb, from ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from ʿAwf ibn Ḥaṭṭān, who said: Some of the villages of Egypt, including Umm Dunayn and Balhīb, had a covenant. When ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵁ heard of this, he wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ ordering him to give them a choice: if they entered Islam, that was settled; if they disliked it, he was to return them to their villages.

He said: ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from Yaḥyā ibn Maymūn al-Ḥaḍramī, who said: When ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ conquered Egypt, a treaty was made with all the Coptic men in it who had reached near adulthood or were older. No woman, boy, or old man was included. The assessment was two dinars apiece. They were counted for this purpose, and their number came to eight million.

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us, saying: I heard Ḥaywah ibn Shurayḥ say: I heard al-Ḥasan ibn Thawbān al-Hamdānī say: Hishām ibn Abī Ruqayyah al-Lakhmī related to me that when ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ conquered Egypt, he said to the Copts of Egypt, “If anyone conceals a treasure from me and I discover it, I will kill him.” A Nabataean from Upper Egypt, named Buṭrus, told ʿAmr that he had a treasure. ʿAmr sent for him and questioned him, but he denied it and disavowed any knowledge of it. ʿAmr imprisoned him and asked about him: “Do you hear him asking about anyone?” They said, “No. We have only heard him ask about a monk on Mount Sinai.” ʿAmr sent for Buṭrus, removed the ring from his hand, and then wrote to that monk, “Send me what you have,” sealing the letter with his ring. The monk’s messenger came to him with a small Syrian jar sealed with lead. ʿAmr opened it and found a sheet inside, on which was written: “What you have is beneath the great cistern.” ʿAmr sent men to the cistern and drained the water from it. Then they pulled up the paving beneath it and found fifty-two irdabbs of minted gold. ʿAmr struck off Buṭrus’s head at the mosque gate. Ibn Abī Ruqayyah related that the Copts brought out their treasures for fear that someone among them might be oppressed and killed, as Buṭrus had been.

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ declared the property of a Copt of Egypt forfeit because it had been established that he was informing the Byzantines of the Muslims’ vulnerabilities and writing to them about them. He extracted from him fifty-odd irdabbs of dinars.

He said: Then he returned to the account of Yaḥyā ibn Ayyūb and Khālid ibn Ḥumayd. He said: God granted the Muslims the conquest of all the land of Egypt by treaty, except Alexandria and three villages that had sided with the Byzantines against the Muslims:

(3 Sultays, Maṣīl, and Balhīb. The Byzantines had assembled forces, and these villages sided with them against the Muslims. 3) When the Muslims prevailed over them, they declared them forfeit and said, “These are booty for us, along with Alexandria.” ʿAmr wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb about this, and ʿUmar wrote back to him: “Make Alexandria and these three villages protected communities under Muslim rule, and levy the land tax upon them. Their tax revenue, together with all that the Copts have agreed to pay, is to be a resource for the Muslims; do not make them booty or slaves.” They did so, and this remains the case to this day.

Mention of those who said that Egypt was conquered by force

Others said: Rather, Egypt was conquered by force, without covenant or treaty.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah and ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; they said: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from someone who heard ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Mughīrah ibn Abī Burdah say: I heard Sufyān ibn Wahb al-Khawlānī say: “When we conquered Egypt without a covenant, al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām stood up and said, ‘Divide it up, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ.’ ʿAmr said, ‘By God, I will not divide it up.’ Al-Zubayr said, ‘By God, you will divide it up, just as the Messenger of God ﷺ divided Khaybar.’ ʿAmr said, ‘By God, I will not divide it up until I write to the Commander of the Faithful.’ He wrote to him, and ʿUmar replied: ‘Leave it as it is, so that generations yet unborn may campaign from it.’”

Ibn Lahīʿah said: Yaḥyā ibn Maymūn related to me, from ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Mughīrah, from Sufyān ibn Wahb, with this account, except that he said: ʿAmr replied, “I would not take any action concerning them until I write to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb.” He wrote to him, and ʿUmar wrote back with this reply.

ʿAbd al-Malik said in his version of the report: Al-Zubayr was reconciled with a payment that satisfied him.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah and ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; they said: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Hubayrah, that Egypt was conquered by force.

ʿAbd al-Malik related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ziyād ibn Anʿum, who said:

I heard our elders say that Egypt was conquered by force, without covenant or treaty. Ibn Anʿum said: Among them was my father, who related to us from his father, one of those who had witnessed the conquest of Egypt.

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us, from Ibn Anʿum, who said: I heard our elders say that Egypt was conquered by force, without covenant or treaty.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Abū al-Aswad, from ʿUrwah, that Egypt was conquered by force.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Abū Qinān Ayyūb ibn Abī al-ʿĀliyah, from his father. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah also informed us, from Ibn Wahb, from Dāwūd ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Ḥaḍramī, that Abū Qinān related to him from his father, who heard ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ say: “I have sat in this very place, and I have no covenant or treaty with any of the Copts of Egypt, except the people of Anṭābulus, for they have a covenant that must be honored.”

In Ibn Lahīʿah’s account, he said: “If you wish, you may kill them; if you wish, you may take one-fifth; and if you wish, you may sell them.”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us, from ʿIyāḍ ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Fihrī, from Rabīʿah ibn Abī ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ conquered Egypt without treaty or covenant, and that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb held back its yield and reserves so that nothing would be taken from it, in consideration of Islam and its people.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shurayḥ, from Yaʿqūb ibn Mujāhid, from Zayd ibn Aslam, who said: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb had a chest containing every covenant he had made with anyone. No covenant with the people of Egypt was found in it.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shurayḥ said: I do not know whether Zayd related this, or whether it was something he himself said. Whoever among them embraced Islam was part of the community; whoever remained was a protected subject.

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār and ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us; they said: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Janādah, the scribe of Ḥayyān ibn Surayj, an Egyptian and a client of Quraysh. He said: Ḥayyān wrote to ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, asking him to make the living Copts pay the poll tax of the dead Copts. ʿUmar asked ʿArāk ibn Mālik, who said, “I have heard of no covenant or treaty made with them. They were taken by force, like slaves.” ʿUmar therefore wrote to Ḥayyān ibn Surayj ordering him to make the living Copts pay the poll tax of the dead.

I heard Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr say: Abū Salamah ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān set out for Alexandria by boat. He needed someone to propel it, so he compelled a Copt to do so. He was challenged over this and said, “They are like slaves, if we need them.”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿah, from al-Ṣalt ibn Abī ʿĀṣim, that he read the letter of ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz to Ḥayyān ibn Surayj, which said that Egypt was conquered by force, without covenant or treaty.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shurayḥ, from ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Abī Jaʿfar, that the scribe of Ḥayyān related to him: There was a need for timber for the shipyard. Ḥayyān wrote to ʿUmar about it, explaining that he had found timber in the possession of some protected subjects, but had been reluctant to take it from them before informing him. ʿUmar wrote back: “Take it from them at a fair price, for I have found no covenant made with the people of Egypt that I must honor.”

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān related to us: ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who said: ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz wrote to Ḥayyān ibn Surayj: “Egypt was conquered by force, without covenant or treaty.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Yaḥyā ibn Ayyūb related to us, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Kaʿb ibn Abī Lubābah, that ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said to Sālim ibn ʿAbd Allāh, “You say that the people of Egypt have no covenant?” He replied, “Yes.”

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from ʿAmr ibn Shuʿayb, from his father, from his grandfather, that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb concerning monks who had taken up the monastic life in Egypt, one of whom died without an heir. ʿUmar wrote back: “If any of them has descendants, give his inheritance to them. If he has no descendants, place his property in the treasury of the Muslims, for his bond of clientage belongs to the Muslims.”

(1 Yaḥyā ibn Khalad related to us, from Rushdayn ibn Saʿd, from ʿAqīl ibn Khālid, from Ibn Shihāb, who said: Some parts of Egypt were conquered by covenant and protected status, and some by force. ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵁ made all of it protected territory and imposed that status upon them; this has continued among them to this day. 1)

Mention of the settlement plans

He said: ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that when ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ conquered Alexandria and saw that its houses and buildings were complete, he considered settling there and said, “These are dwellings already prepared for us.” He wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb seeking permission to do so. ʿUmar asked the messenger, “Will water come between me and the Muslims?” He replied, “Yes, Commander of the Faithful, when the Nile rises.” ʿUmar wrote to ʿAmr: “I do not want you to settle the Muslims in a place where water will come between me and them, in winter or summer.” ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ therefore moved from Alexandria to al-Fusṭāṭ.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb. ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us, from al-Layth, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote to Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, who was encamped at the cities of the Persian kings; to his governor in al-Baṣrah; and to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, who was encamped at Alexandria: “Do not let water come between me and you. Whenever I wish to ride my mount to you and come to you, I should be able to do so.” Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ moved from the cities of the Persian kings to al-Kūfah, the governor of al-Baṣrah moved from his former location and settled in al-Baṣrah, and ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ moved from Alexandria to al-Fusṭāṭ.

(2 He said: It was called al-Fusṭāṭ for the reason related to us by my father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, and Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr: When ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ intended to set out for Alexandria to fight the Byzantines there, he ordered that his tent be taken down. In it was a dove that had nested and laid eggs. ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ said, “It has taken shelter with us under the protection of one who has taken shelter,” and ordered that the tent be left just as it was. He entrusted it to the owner of the palace. When the Muslims returned from Alexandria, they said, “Where shall we settle?” They were told, “At the tent”—that is, ʿAmr’s tent, which he had left behind. It had been pitched on the site of the house now known as Dār al-Ḥaṣā, beside what is today called the small house of ʿAmr. 2)

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ built the mosque, as ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd. Its surroundings were gardens and vineyards. They set up ropes until they had aligned it, then set to work; ʿAmr remained standing until they had set the direction of prayer. It was ʿAmr and the Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ who set it.

He had a pulpit made for it, as ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿah, from Abū Tamīm al-Jayshānī, who said: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote to him: “To proceed: I have heard that you have had a pulpit made, from which you climb above the necks of the Muslims. Is it not enough for you to stand upright while the Muslims are beneath your heels? I charge you to break it.”

(5 ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from Abū al-Khayr, that Abū Muslim al-Ghāfiqī, a Companion of the Messenger of God ﷺ used to call the adhān for ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ. I saw him fumigating the mosque with incense. 5)

He said: The people’s properties became intermingled. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: Ibn Wahb informed us, from Yaḥyā ibn Azhar, from al-Ḥajjāj ibn Shaddād, from Abū Ṣāliḥ al-Ghifārī, who said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb: “We have laid out a house for you beside the congregational mosque.” ʿUmar wrote back: “How can a man living in the Ḥijāz have a house in Egypt?” He ordered him to make it a market for the Muslims.

Ibn Lahīʿah said: It was Dār al-Barakah, and it was made into a market where enslaved people were sold. This is what Ibn Lahīʿah said.

As for al-Layth ibn Saʿd, ʿAbd al-Malik related to us from him that Dār al-Barakah was a plot belonging to ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān asked him for it, and he gave it to him without receiving anything in return.

Aḥmad ibn ʿAmr related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us, from Yūnus ibn Yazīd, from Ibn Shihāb, from Sālim ibn ʿAbd Allāh, who said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar witnessed the conquest of Egypt and laid out a plot there for Dār al-Barakah, the house of the enslaved people. He said, “I gave it to Muʿāwiyah in the hope that he would compensate me for it. He did not, until he died, and so I release him from the obligation.”

Among those remembered as having witnessed the conquest of Egypt from among the Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ whether from Quraysh or others, and among those who were not Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ—as ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah and others besides him related to us, and others have also mentioned some of them—were: al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām; Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ; ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, who was commander of the army; ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr; Khārijah ibn Ḥudhāfah al-ʿAdawī; ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb; Qays ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ al-Sahmī; al-Miqdād ibn al-Aswad; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Sarḥ al-ʿĀmirī; Nāfiʿ ibn ʿAbd al-Qays al-Fihrī. (2 It is also said that he was rather ʿUqbah ibn Nāfiʿ; and Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Yazīd ibn Anīs al-Fihrī. 2) And Abū Rāfiʿ, the client of the Messenger of God ﷺ; Ibn ʿAbdah; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān and Rabīʿah, the sons of Sharāḥīl ibn Ḥasanah; and Wardān, the client of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, who carried ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ’s standard.

There is disagreement about Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ. It is said that he entered Egypt only after the conquest. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, that Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ came to Egypt.

Those from the Anṣār who witnessed the conquest included: ʿUbādah ibn al-Ṣāmit, who had witnessed Badr and the Pledge of al-ʿAqabah; Muḥammad ibn Maslamah al-Anṣārī, who had witnessed Badr and was the one ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb sent to Egypt to divide ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ’s property with him—he was one of those who climbed the fortress with al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām; and Maslamah ibn Mukhallad al-Anṣārī, who is said to have been a Companion.

They related to us, from Wakīʿ: Mūsā ibn ʿAlī related to us, from his father, who said: I heard Maslamah ibn Mukhlid says: “I was born when the Prophet ﷺ arrived in Medina. The Messenger of God ﷺ died when I was ten years old.”

He had governed the province in the days of Muʿāwiya and for part of Yazīd’s caliphate. Maslama died in Egypt in the year sixty-two. Abū Ayyūb al-Anṣārī, whose name was Khālid ibn Zayd, had fought at Badr and died at Constantinople in the year fifty. Abū al-Dardāʾ, whose name was ʿUwaymir.

Ibn Hishām said: “ʿUwaymir ibn ʿĀmir”; it is also said: “ʿUwaymir ibn Zayd”.

Among men of various tribes were Abū Baṣra al-Ghifārī, whose name was Ḥumayl ibn Baṣra, and Abū Dharr al-Ghifārī, whose name was Jundub ibn Junāda. It is also said that it was Barīr. (2 Ibn Hishām said: “I heard more than one scholar say that Abū Dharr was Jundub ibn Junāda.” 2)

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama told us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who said: Abū Dharr was among those who witnessed the conquest with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ. Also among them was Hubayb ibn Mughfil. There is only one report from him, namely the report of Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that Aslam Abū ʿImrān told him, from Hubayb ibn Mughfil, that he said: “I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: ‘Whoever trails [his garment] out of pride will tread in the Fire’”.

Wādī Hubayb in the Maghrib is named after him. And [there was] ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Juzʾ al-Zubaydī, whose name had been al-ʿĀṣ, but the Messenger of God ﷺ named him ʿAbd Allāh.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ and Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr told us; they said: al-Layth ibn Saʿd told us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Juzʾ al-Zubaydī, who said: A man who had come to the Prophet and become Muslim died. The Messenger of God ﷺ was at the grave and said: “What is your name?”

I said: “Al-ʿĀṣ.” (4 He said to Ibn ʿAmr: “What is your name?” He replied: “Al-ʿĀṣ.” And he said to al-ʿĀṣ ibn al-ʿĀṣ: “What is your name?” He replied: “Al-ʿĀṣ.” 4) The Messenger of God ﷺ said: “Al-ʿĀṣ? You are ʿAbd Allāh. Go down.” He said:

“So we buried our companion, then came out of the grave with our names changed.” And [there was] Kaʿb ibn Ḍanna al-ʿAbsī, also said to be Kaʿb ibn Yasār ibn Ḍanna. And ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir al-Juhanī, whose kunya was Abū Ḥammād. He was the messenger whom ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb sent to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ when he wrote to him, ordering him to return if he had not entered the land of Egypt. And Abū Zumʿa al-Balawī. And Baraḥ ibn Ḥaskal, who had come to the Messenger of God ﷺ from Mahra, witnessed the conquest with ʿAmr, and established an allotment. This is how Ibn ʿUfayr said it: Baraḥ ibn Ḥaskal. The Mahriyyūn say: Baraḥ ibn ʿAskal. And Junāda ibn Abī Umayya al-Azdī. And Sufyān ibn Wahb al-Khawlānī, who was a Companion.

ʿAmr ibn Sawwād told us; Ibn Wahb told us; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shurayḥ told me, saying:

I heard Saʿīd ibn Abī Shamir al-Sabāʾī say: I heard Sufyān ibn Wahb al-Khawlānī say: I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: “The hundred will not come upon anyone with a living person still upon its back”. He said: I related this to Ibn Ḥujayra, who got up and went in to ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān. He said: Sufyān, who was a very old man, was carried in until he was brought before ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān. The latter asked him about the report, and he related it to him. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said:

“Perhaps he means that no one who was with him will remain alive until the turn of the hundred.” Sufyān replied: “This is how I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say it.”

And Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj al-Kindī, who was the messenger sent by ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb with news of the conquest of Alexandria. There is disagreement about Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj. Some said that he was a Companion, and cited as evidence a report related to us by my father ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, Shuʿayb ibn al-Layth, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from Suwayd ibn Qays, from Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj, that the Messenger of God ﷺ prayed one day, gave the salutation, then turned away, although one rakʿa of the prayer remained. A man caught up with him and said: “One rakʿa of the prayer remains.” He returned, entered the mosque, and prayed one rakʿa with the people. I told the people about this, and they said: “Do you know the man?” I said: “No, but I would recognize him if I saw him.”

Others said that he was not a Companion, citing as evidence a report related to us by Yūsuf ibn ʿAdī, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from al-Ḥārith ibn Yazīd, from ʿAlī ibn Rabāḥ, who said:

I heard Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj say: “We emigrated in the time of Abū Bakr ﵀. While we were with him, he appeared at the pulpit, praised and extolled God, then said: ‘A head has arrived here, that of Yannaq the patrician, though we had no need of it. This is only the custom of the non-Arabs.’ Then he said: ‘Stand up, ʿUqba.’ A man called ʿUqba stood up. He said: ‘I do not mean you; I mean ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir. Stand up, ʿUqba.’ A man stood up, eloquent and skilled in recitation, and began reciting Sūrat al-Baqara. Then he spoke of their fighting and of what God had granted them to conquer. From that day on, I never ceased to love him.”

And ʿĀmir, the client of Jamal, who was called ʿĀmir Jamal, witnessed the conquest while he was enslaved. He was called ʿĀmir Jamal because he was with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ in the presence of Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān, and ʿĀmir said to ʿAmr: “Speak, for I am behind you.” Muʿāwiya said to him: “And who are you?” He replied: “I am ʿĀmir, the client of Jamal.” Muʿāwiya said to him: “Rather, you are ʿĀmir Jamal.” He was called ʿĀmir Jamal because Muʿāwiya said this.

Among them were six men who had fought at Badr: al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām, Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, al-Miqdād ibn al-Aswad, ʿUbāda ibn al-Ṣāmit, Abū Ayyūb al-Anṣārī, and Muḥammad ibn Maslama. ʿAmmār ibn Yāsir had entered Egypt, but he entered it after the conquest, in the days of ʿUthmān.

ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd ibn al-Walīd told us; Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān told us, from Mujālid, from al-Shaʿbī, that ʿAmmār ibn Yāsir entered Egypt in the days of ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, who sent him there on one of his affairs. There is only one report from him.

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār told us; Ibn Lahīʿa told us, from Abū ʿAshāna, who said:

I heard Abū al-Yaqẓān ʿAmmār ibn Yāsir say: “Rejoice, for by God, you love the Messenger of God ﷺ more than most of those who saw him.”

He said: Some of them established allotments in the town, and we have mentioned their allotments; for some of them, no allotment is mentioned. God knows best how the matter stood.

He said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ established his residence, which is his to this day, by the mosque gate, with the road between them. His other residence adjoined it. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ was buried there, according to what some of the town’s elders claimed, because of an incident that had occurred in the town at that time.

Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr told us, saying: ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ died on his estate at al-Sabʿ in Palestine. It is also said that he died in Mecca. God knows best.
