His kunya was Abū Muḥammad, and he died in the year seventy-three. The people of Egypt have nearly a hundred reports from him, from the Prophet ﷺ.

And [there is] the bathhouse called the Bathhouse of the Mouse. It was called the Bathhouse of the Mouse because the baths of the Byzantines were large bathhouses. When this bathhouse was built and they saw how small it was, they said: “Who would go into this? This is a bathhouse for a mouse”.

And ʿAmr’s residence is there. It is also said that ʿAmr established an allotment for himself at the site of the residence of Ibn Abī al-Razzām.

His son ʿAbd Allāh established this large residence by the congregational mosque. He was the one who built it in this form and built a palace in it, square like the first Kaʿba.

Those who claimed that this large residence by the mosque was ʿAmr’s own allotment cited the report of Ibn Lahīʿa, from Ibn Hubayra, from Abū Tamīm al-Jayshānī, who heard ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ say: “A man among the Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ told me that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: ‘God has added a prayer for you, so pray it between the evening prayer and the dawn prayer: the witr, the witr’”. “Indeed, it was Abū Baṣra al-Ghifārī.”

Abū Tamīm al-Jayshānī said: Abū Dharr and I were sitting together, and Abū Dharr took my hand and we went to Abū Baṣra. We found him at the gate that leads to ʿAmr’s residence. Abū Dharr said: “Abū Baṣra, did you hear the Messenger of God ﷺ say: ‘God has added a prayer for you, so pray it between the evening prayer and dawn: the witr, the witr’?” He said: “Yes.” He asked: “Did you hear it from him?” He said: “Yes.”

Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr told us, from Ibn Hubayra. ʿAmr ibn Sawwād also related it to us, from Ibn Wahb, from Ibn Lahīʿa. Ṭalq ibn al-Samḥ also related part of it to me, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Ibn Hubayra, from Abū Tamīm al-Jayshānī.

They have several reports from ʿAmr, from the Prophet ﷺ. Among them is the report of Mūsā ibn ʿAlī, from his father, from Abū Qays, the client of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, that the Prophet ﷺ said:

“The distinction between our fasting and the fasting of the People of the Book is the pre-dawn meal”. My father related it to us, from al-Layth, from Mūsā ibn ʿAlī. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ also related it to us, from Mūsā ibn ʿAlī himself.

Among them is the report of Nāfiʿ ibn Yazīd, from al-Ḥārith ibn Saʿīd al-ʿAtqī, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Munayn—of Banū ʿAbd Kulāl—from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, who said: “The Messenger of God ﷺ taught me fifteen prostrations in the Qurʾān: three in the short sūras, and two in Sūrat al-Ḥajj.” Saʿīd ibn Abī Maryam related it to us.

Those who established allotments around the congregational mosque with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ

Quraysh, the Anṣār, Aslam, Ghifār, Juhayna, and those in the banner whose clans had not been numerous at the conquest with ʿAmr established allotments around ʿAmr and the mosque.

Wardan, the client of ʿAmr, established an allotment for the palace known as the Palace of ʿUmar ibn Marwān. It was attributed to ʿUmar ibn Marwān because Antanās, commander of the army and administrator of the revenues of Maslama, asked Muʿāwiya to provide him with a residence near the dīwān. Muʿāwiya wrote to Maslama ibn Mukhlad, ordering him to buy Wardan’s residence and mark out an allotment for Wardan wherever he wished. He did so. Antanās took possession of the residence, and Maslama sent Wardan with al-Samṭ, the client of Maslama, instructing him to allot Wardan a bowshot’s distance. He went out with him until they stopped at a place where camels were made to kneel. It was an open space in which the Muslims had room to spread out between themselves and the sea. Al-Samṭ said to Wardan: “Today we shall show the superiority of the Persians’ shooting distance over that of the Byzantines.” Al-Samṭ was Persian, and Wardan was Byzantine. Al-Samṭ drew his bow and shot an arrow for him, and Wardan marked out the allotment. When Antanās died, it was granted to ʿUmar ibn Marwān. Wardan’s kunya was Abū ʿUbayd.

It is said that the Palace of ʿUmar ibn Marwān was on an allotment of the Azd. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān bought it and gave it to his brother ʿUmar ibn Marwān. This was because the alley running from the Palace of ʿUmar ibn Marwān to the stable, and the stable itself, were on an allotment of the Azd.

Qays ibn Saʿd ibn ʿUbāda established an allotment south of the congregational mosque: the residence of al-Filfil. It had been an open space, and he built on it when he governed the province. ʿAlī ibn Abī Ṭālib had appointed him to it, then removed him. People used to say that it belonged to him, until this was mentioned to him. He said: “What residence do I have in Egypt?” They told him of it, and he said: “I built that with the Muslims’ money; I have no claim to it.”

It is said that when Qays ibn Saʿd was on his deathbed, he said: “I built a residence in Egypt when I was its governor, and I used the Muslims’ assistance in building it. It belongs to the Muslims; their governors may reside in it”.

They have two reports from Qays, from the Prophet ﷺ. One is that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: “The owner of a mount has the greatest right to its front”. Abū al-Aswad related it to us; Ibn Lahīʿa told us, from ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Milayl, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abī Umayya, from Qays ibn Saʿd.

It is also said that the residence of al-Filfil, and the adjoining residence of al-Zalābiyya, belonged to Nāfiʿ ibn ʿAbd al-Qays al-Fihrī. It is also said that it was ʿUqba ibn Nāfiʿ. Qays ibn Saʿd took it from him and compensated him with the residence of the Fihrīs in the alley of the Lanterns. It is also said that that residence was the allotment of ʿUqba ibn Nāfiʿ. It is also said that the residence of al-Filfil belonged to Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ, who gave it as an endowment to the Muslims and kept only his residence at al-Mawqif. God knows best. It is said that his residence at al-Mawqif, known as the Inn, was not Saʿd’s allotment; rather, it belonged to a client of Saʿd, who died, and Saʿd’s family inherited it from him. The residence of al-Filfil was so named because Usāma ibn Zayd al-Tanūkhī, when he was in charge of Egypt’s tax revenues, bought pepper from Mūsā ibn Wardan for twenty thousand dinars. Al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik had written about it, intending it as a gift for the ruler of the Byzantines, and Usāma stored it there. Mūsā ibn Wardan complained about this to ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz when he became caliph, and ʿUmar wrote that it should be handed over to him.

Ṭalq ibn al-Samḥ told us; Ḍimām ibn Ismāʿīl told us; Mūsā ibn Wardan told me, saying: I went in to see ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz and related to him reports from those Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ whom I had met. I was treated as one of his household: I could enter whenever I wished and leave whenever I wished. I used to relate to him reports from the Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ whom I had met. I asked him to write to Ḥayyān ibn Surayj concerning twenty thousand dinars that I was to collect as the price of pepper, so that he would write to him to pay them to me. He said: “And to whom do the twenty thousand dinars belong?” I said: “They belong to me.” He said: “How did they come to belong to you?” I said to him: “I was a merchant.” He struck with his short staff, then said: “The merchant is a sinner, and the sinner is in the Fire.” Then he said: “Write to Ḥayyān ibn Surayj”. I never went in to see him again, and he ordered his doorkeeper not to admit me.

The residence of al-Zalābiyya passed to al-Ḥakam ibn Abī Bakr. It is also said that the residence of al-Zalābiyya was the allotment of ʿAbda ibn ʿAbda.

Maslama ibn Mukhlad established the residence of al-Raml. Abū Rāfiʿ, the client of the Messenger of God ﷺ established an allotment there with Maslama, and ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir al-Juhanī established one with them. When Maslama ibn Mukhlad became governor, Muʿāwiya asked him for his residence, and he gave it to him. Muʿāwiya marked out for him, in the open space, the residence with the bathhouse in the market of Wardan. Then it passed to the descendants of Abū Bakr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, and the descendants of al-ʿAbbās took possession of it along with the other property seized from the descendants of Marwān.

Ibn Shāfiʿ praised Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAlī, and Ṣāliḥ granted it to him.

It came to the descendants of Abū Bakr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz because Maslama ibn Mukhlad died without leaving a son. His daughter, Umm Sahl, daughter of Maslama, inherited from him. The estate of Umm Sahl is named after her, together with his two wives and his agnatic kin, the descendants of Abū Dujāna. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz married Maslama’s two wives after his death, and paid the twenty thousand dinars that Maslama owed. Abū Bakr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz married his daughter, Umm Sahl daughter of Maslama.

What came to them of Maslama’s estates came to them by inheritance from the women they had inherited.

Maslama’s residence extended from the Kaʿk Mill to the bathhouse in Wardan’s market. It passed to ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz and Abū Bakr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz. Abū Bakr inherited from his wife Umm Sahl what he received of the estate of Umm Sahl.

The other property of this kind in the hands of people besides them had belonged to Ibn al-Ashtar al-Ṣadafī, the descendants of Wardan, Ḥammāda daughter of Muḥammad, and Mūsā ibn ʿAlī. It consisted of rights belonging to Maslama’s agnatic kin, which Yaḥyā ibn Saʿīd al-Anṣārī sold; the agnatic kin had appointed him to do so. It was for this reason that Yaḥyā ibn Saʿīd came to Egypt.

The residence known as the Residence of the Spindles, in al-Ḥamrāʾ, was also among the properties sold by Yaḥyā ibn Saʿīd. Ibn Wardan and Ibn Miskīn bought it from him.

As Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, I think that Maslama ibn Mukhlad was in charge of the mills in ʿAmr’s days. Muʿāwiya also bought the residence of ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir and marked out an allotment for him in the open space, opposite the road leading to the residence of Maḥfūẓ ibn Sulaymān. It extended from the main allotment to the sea.

It is also said that Maslama ibn Mukhlad granted it to ʿUqba on behalf of his daughter, Umm Kulthūm daughter of ʿUqba. It may be that Maslama granted it to ʿUqba at Muʿāwiya’s order, as compensation for the residence that had been taken from him.

The residence of Abū Rāfiʿ had passed to his client al-Sāʾib, the client of Abū Rāfiʿ. Muʿāwiya bought it from him and granted al-Sāʾib an allotment in the open space near Ḥayyiz al-Wazz.

It is also said that al-Miqdād ibn al-Aswad established a residence beside the residence of al-Raml, and it was beside the residence of ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir.

Adjacent to it was a house belonging to ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir, his allotted plot. ʿUqba bought the house of al-Miqdād ibn al-Aswad and demolished it; he demolished his own house as well, then built the two together into a house for Ramla, daughter of Muʿāwiya. Muʿāwiya wrote to him: “We have no need of it ; make it for the Muslims.”

The Dār al-Raml was named after Ramla , for people used to call it “Ramla’s House,” but the common people corrupted this and called it “the Sand House.” It is also said that it was called the Dār al-Raml because sand was carried there for the mint.

I heard Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr say this, as I recall, and I do not know that I heard it from anyone else.

Al-Miqdād’s kunya was Abū Maʿbad.

Yaʿqūb ibn Isḥāq ibn Abī ʿAbbād related to us: Ḥammād ibn Shuʿayb related to us, from Manṣūr, from Hilāl ibn Yasāf , who said: “The Messenger of God ﷺ appointed al-Miqdād over an expedition. When he returned, the Messenger of God ﷺ said to him: ‘How did you find command, Abū Maʿbad?’ He replied: ‘I set out, Messenger of God, thinking I had no advantage over any of the men in the group. I returned and it was as if they were my slaves.’ He said: ‘That is what command is like, Abū Maʿbad, except for one whom God protects from its evil.’ He replied: ‘By Him who sent you with the truth, I shall never take charge of any task again.’”

It is also said that when Muʿāwiya became caliph, he wrote to ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir asking him to hand it over to Yazīd because it was close to the mosque, and promising him something better in exchange. ʿUqba did so. Muʿāwiya allotted him his house in the Sūq Wardan and built it for him; he built the lower story of the Dār al-Raml for Yazīd. Muʿāwiya also allotted Yazīd a village among the villages of the Fayyūm. The people considered this a grave matter and spoke out against it. When word of this reached Muʿāwiya, he disliked what people were saying, so he returned that village to the land tax, as it had been for the Muslims, and made the Dār al-Raml a house for the Muslims, where their governors would reside. He had built only its lower story, until al-Qāsim ibn ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Ḥabḥāb built its upper story.

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Abū Qubayl, from Faḍāla ibn ʿUbayd, who said: “One day we were with Muʿāwiya, and Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj was with him. He was like a ṭannī camel , putting one foot forward and drawing the other back. He would cast out a word: if the Arabs went along with it, he carried it through; if they rejected it, he did not. One day he said: ‘I do not know in which book of God you find this provision and stipend. If only we withheld it!’ Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj struck him repeatedly between the shoulders, until we thought he felt the pain. Then he said: ‘No, by Him in whose hand my soul is, O Ibn Abī Sufyān! We shall seize you by their spearpoints; then you shall stand at their anvils, and no dinar or dirham will make its way out from them to me.’ Muʿāwiya fell silent.”

Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān’s kunya was Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, and Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj’s was Abū Nuʿaym.

In Muʿāwiya’s time, the dīwān, as Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us from Ibn Lahīʿa, numbered forty thousand, of whom four thousand received two hundred each.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Razīn ibn ʿAbd Allāh, a report like it. He added: “The only surplus that was brought to Muʿāwiya was six hundred thousand, over and above the troops’ stipends.”

Hāniʾ related to us: Ḍimām related to us, from Abū Qubayl, who said: “Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān had appointed a man over each of the Arab tribes. In the Maʿāfir tribe, the man was called al-Ḥasan. Every morning he would go around the gathering places and ask: ‘Has a child been born among you tonight? Has anyone come to settle among you?’ They would say: ‘A boy has been born to so-and-so, and a girl to so-and-so.’ He would say: ‘Name them,’ and write it down. It is also said that a man from Yemen came to settle there with his family; they named him and his family. When he had finished with all the tribes, he went to the dīwān.”

In Muʿāwiya’s time, the dīwān, as Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us from Ibn Lahīʿa, numbered forty thousand, of whom four thousand received two hundred each.

Ibn ʿUfayr said in his report from Ibn Lahīʿa: “Muslama ibn Mukhallad gave the people of the dīwān their stipends, and gave their families their stipends and provisions, their extraordinary expenses, and the extraordinary expenses of the districts, including the bridges; he paid the scribes and sent loads of wheat to the Ḥijāz. He sent Muʿāwiya a surplus of six hundred thousand dinars.”

Ibn ʿUfayr said: “The camels set out, and Bariḥ ibn Ḥaskal met them. He said: ‘What is this? Why is what belongs to us leaving our land? Take it back!’ It was taken back. When he reached the mosque, he said: ‘Have you received your stipends , your provisions, your families’ stipends, and your extraordinary expenses?’ They said: ‘Yes.’ He said: ‘May God not bless them.’”

He said: “Bariḥ ibn Ḥaskal’s allotted plot was by Dār Zanīn, in the lane known as ‘behind al-Qammāḥ’ .”

Qays ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ al-Sahmī allotted the house that was by Dār Ibn Rummāna. Dār Ibn Rummāna lay between it and the mosque, and part of it was incorporated into the mosque when ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṭāhir enlarged it. ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ had appointed Qays judge.

Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, who said: “Qays ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ was in Egypt. ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ appointed him judge.”

Next to Qays ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ, toward Zuqāq al-Balāṭ, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Juzʾ al-Zubaydī allotted Dār Ibn Rummāna and the adjoining property. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān bought it. When Ibn Rummāna came to him, he gave him what he had built; what remained belonged to al-Aṣbagh ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz. ʿAbd Allāh’s house adjoined the mosque, and south of its door today is the treasury’s latrine.

Ibn Rummāna had been at school with ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz had given Ibn Rummāna a ring of his. When ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz attained the position he did, Ibn Rummāna came to him from the Ḥijāz on a camel, with nothing on him but a fur cloak . He said to the doorkeeper: “Ask the amīr to admit me.” The doorkeeper seemed reluctant, so Ibn Rummāna said to him: “Admit me today, and I will admit you tomorrow.” The doorkeeper went in to ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz and told him what he had said. He replied: “Bring him in.” When Ibn Rummāna entered and spoke with him, he brought out the ring. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz recognized it and removed his own ring from his finger and gave it to Ibn Rummāna. He built him his house and planted for him the palm trees that they have today in the vicinity of Ḥulwān.

It was also ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz who planted for ʿUmayr ibn Midrak the palm trees at al-Jīza known as Jannān ʿUmayr. The reason for this, as my father ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam related to us, was that ʿUmayr ibn Midrak had planted varieties of fruit trees. When they had matured, he asked ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz to come and see them. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz went there with him, and when he saw them, he said to him: “Give them to me.” ʿUmayr gave them to him. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz sent for the governor of the Island and said to him: “If Friday comes and there is still a single tree standing there, I will cut off your hand.” On the Island there were five hundred laborers, kept ready in case a fire broke out in the city or a building had to be demolished. The governor of the Island brought them, and they cut down the trees with their produce while ʿUmayr watched in grief. When they had finished, ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ordered that palm offshoots be brought to him from Ḥulwān, and planted them as palm trees. When they had matured, ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz went out to see them, taking a camel with him. He said to ʿUmayr: “How does this compare with what was there before?” ʿUmayr replied: “How could I attain what the amīr has attained?” He said: “It is yours,” and endowed it for your children . It remains theirs to this day.

Next to ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥārith, Thawbān, the freedman of the Messenger of God ﷺ allotted a plot. It is also said that it was ʿAjlān, the freedman of Qays ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ. This is the house that Salama, the freedman of Ṣāliḥ ibn ʿAlī, added to the mosque.

ʿUbāda ibn al-Ṣāmit allotted a plot next to Ibn Rummāna, in the direction of the Ḥammām market. This is the house where Jūjū the muezzin lived, and the house beside it. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān bought one of them, and it became his; the other went to the Banū Miskīn .

Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa allotted a plot west of the mosque, between it and Thawbān’s house, opposite the old ablution place, extending to the henna sellers and the sawiq sellers, with the road between it and the mosque.

Al-Rabīʿ ibn Khārija was an orphan in ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz’s care. When he came of age, he bought his house from him for ten thousand dinars for al-Aṣbagh ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz. When ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz became ruler, he rode to him and produced the document confirming the house’s endowment . He returned it to him after requiring him to pay its price. The man asked him to give him its rent. He said: “As for the rent, no; rent goes with liability.” He returned it to him and did not grant him the rent.

Al-Layth ibn Saʿd said: “I saw al-Rabīʿ there when I was still a boy.” Then al-Aṣbagh disputed the matter before him. Ibn Shihāb was his judge at the time, and Ibn Shihāb ruled that Ibn Khārija should have the house. He took possession of it, holding that a guardian may not buy from someone whose affairs he oversees. Then he brought suit before Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik after ʿUmar, and Yazīd ruled that the rent be paid to him. The sons of al-Aṣbagh handed it over to him until Yazīd died. Then they brought the matter before Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, who ruled that they owed no rent, and returned the rent to the sons of al-Aṣbagh.

Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa, as Shuʿayb ibn al-Layth and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us,—4

He brought out for him the deed endowing the house, and returned it to him after he paid him its price. He asked him to pay its rent. He said, “As for the rent, no: rent is due with liability.” He returned it to him and did not order that rent be paid to him.

Al-Layth ibn Saʿd said: “I saw al-Rabīʿ there, and at that time I was a boy.” Then al-Aṣbagh brought a suit concerning it before him, and Ibn Shihāb was his judge at that time. Ibn Shihāb ruled that the house belonged to Ibn Khārija, who took possession of it, on the grounds that it is not permissible for a guardian to buy from someone whose affairs he oversees. Then he brought a suit before Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, after ʿUmar, who ruled in his favor concerning the rent. The Banū al-Aṣbagh handed it over to him until Yazīd died. Then they brought the matter before Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, who ruled that they owed no rent, and the rent was returned to the Banū al-Aṣbagh.

As Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa was described to us by Shuʿayb ibn al-Layth and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ, from al-Layth, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, he was the first to build an upper chamber in Egypt. This reached ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, who wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ: “Peace. To proceed: I have learned that Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa has built an upper chamber. Khārija must have intended to look into the private affairs of his neighbors. When this letter of mine reaches you, demolish it, God willing. Peace.”

The people of Egypt have one hadith from Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa, from the Prophet ﷺ—and no other hadith from him from the Prophet. It is the hadith of al-Layth ibn Saʿd, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Rāshid al-Zawfī, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Murra al-Zawfī, from Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa, who said: “The Messenger of God ﷺ—came out to us and said: ‘God has provided you with a prayer better for you than red camels: the witr prayer. He has appointed it for you between the evening prayer and the rising of dawn.’” My father, Shuʿayb ibn al-Layth, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related this to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd.

They also have anecdotes about Khārija himself. Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa was in charge of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ’s police, during the time of ʿAmr and during the time of Muʿāwiya, until a Khārijite killed him. This happened because ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ had developed some trouble in his stomach and stayed at home. Khārija was serving the people their evening meal when the Ḥarūrī struck him, thinking he was ʿAmr. When he learned that he was not ʿAmr, he said, “I wanted ʿAmr, but God wanted Khārija.” ʿAmr used to say, “My stomach was of no use to me except on that day.”

Muʿāwiya ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Yaḥyā ibn Maʿīn related to us, from Wahb ibn Jarīr, from his father, who said: A Ḥarūrī went to kill ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ in Egypt. When he arrived there, he saw a man sitting and eating lunch, who had been put in charge of ʿAmr’s police. He thought the man was ʿAmr, sprang upon him, and killed him. When the man was brought in before ʿAmr, he said, “By God, I wanted no one but you.” ʿAmr replied, “But God did not want me,” and the man was killed.

It has also been said that Khārija was killed in Syria. God knows best.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: al-Hayqal ibn Ziyād related to us, from Muʿāwiya ibn Yaḥyā al-Ṣadafī, who said: al-Zuhrī related to me: Three men from Iraq made a pact at the Kaʿba to kill Muʿāwiya, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, and Ḥabīb ibn Maslama. After Muʿāwiya had been pledged allegiance as caliph, they set out and arrived at Īliyāʾ. They prayed in the mosque before dawn for as long as they could, then went out and asked some Syrians who had been in the mosque, “At what time can we meet the Commander of the Faithful when he is alone? We are a group of Iraqis whose stipends have incurred a shortfall, and we want to speak with him while he is free.” The man said to them, “Wait until he has mounted his animal, then go up to him and speak with him. He will stop for you until you have finished talking.”

They hastened to do so. When Muʿāwiya came out for the dawn prayer, he said the opening takbīr. When he prostrated for the first time, one of them threw himself upon the back of the guard who was prostrating between him and Muʿāwiya, and stabbed Muʿāwiya in the buttock with a dagger, aiming for his thigh. Muʿāwiya withdrew and said to the people, “Complete your prayer.” The man was seized and bound, and a doctor was summoned for Muʿāwiya. The doctor said, “Unless this dagger is poisoned, you are in no danger.” The doctor prepared medicines to be taken in case it was poisoned. Then he instructed one of his attendants who knew about them to give them to Muʿāwiya if his tongue became numb, and to have him lick the dagger. He then tested it and found it was not poisoned. He said the takbīr, and those around him also said it.

Then Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa, one of the Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb, came out from Muʿāwiya to the people and said, “This is a grave matter, but there is no harm to the Commander of the Faithful, praise be to God.” He began exhorting the people, when one of the two remaining Ḥarūrīs rushed at him, thinking he was ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, and struck him with a sword on the forelock and killed him. The people threw garments over the attacker and joined forces to seize him and bind him. The third drew his sword and charged the people in the mosque. Saʿīd ibn Mālik ibn Shihāb stood firm against him. Saʿīd was wearing a rain cloak, with his sword beneath it, fastened at its hilt. He reached out and thrust his hand through the cloak into the sword’s fastening, and had not freed it before the Ḥarūrī came upon him and struck him at the shoulder, with a blow that reached his vitals. Saʿīd then drew his sword, and he and the Ḥarūrī exchanged two blows. The Ḥarūrī struck him in the eye, taking away his left eye; Saʿīd struck the Ḥarūrī and severed his right hand with the sword, then struck him with the sword until he killed him. Saʿīd bled heavily and was carried away, but he soon died. He said, as he told those who came in to see him, “By God, had I wished, I could have escaped along with the people. But I thought it improper to turn my back to him while I had the sword with me.” A man from Kalb came in and said, “Was this the one who stabbed Muʿāwiya?”

They said, “Yes.” He drew his sword and struck off the man’s head. The Kalbī was seized and imprisoned, and he was told, “You have brought suspicion upon yourself.” He said, “I killed him only out of anger for God.” When he was investigated, he was found innocent, so he was released.

The killer of Khārija was handed over to his heirs among the Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb. They cut off his hands and feet, then carried him until they brought him to Iraq. He lived like that for a time, then married a woman who bore him a son. When they heard that he had been blessed with a son, they said, “We have failed, since we have let the killer of Khārija father sons.” They spoke to Muʿāwiya, who gave them permission to kill him, and they killed him.

The Ḥarūrī who killed Khārija said, “By God, I wanted none but ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ.” When ʿAmr heard this, he said, “But God wanted Khārija.”

After Khārija was killed, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ appointed al-Sāʾib ibn Hishām ibn ʿAmr, one of the Banū Mālik ibn Ḥisl, to command his police. Hishām ibn ʿAmr was the man who had taken a stand to annul the document Quraysh had drawn up against the Banū Hāshim: that they were not to marry them, give them women in marriage, or buy anything from them until they surrendered the Messenger of God ﷺ.

(1. In this connection, Ḥassān ibn Thābit says:

Will the Umayyads fulfill a pact, a pledge, as Hishām fulfilled his protection?

From a people who do not betray their neighbor— for al-Ḥārith ibn Ḥabīb ibn Sukhām.

And when the Banū Ḥisl granted protection under a pact, they fulfilled it and sent their neighbor on his way in safety.

Ibn Hishām: Sukhām.)

Ibn Hishām differed from other authorities on poetry and said: “It is only Suḥām”.

Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa al-Qurashī, of the Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb, had built an upper chamber during the time of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, and it overlooked the neighboring houses. His neighbors complained about it to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb. He wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ: “Set up a bed on the side about which the complaint was made, then station upon it a man who is neither stout nor short. If the chamber overlooks him, close it up.”

Yazīd was asked, “Who told you this report?” He replied, “The elders of the army.”

He said: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAdīs al-Balawī laid out al-Dār al-Bayḍāʾ, the White House. It is said, however, that the White House had been an open court between the mosque and the house of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, a place where the Muslims’ horses were kept at the mosque gate. Then Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam came to Egypt in the year sixty-five and built it for himself as a house. He said, “A caliph should not be in a city where he has no house.” It was built for him in two months.

Ibn ʿAdīs was among those who pledged allegiance beneath the Tree. The people of Egypt have one hadith from him from the Prophet—no other hadith from him from the Prophet is known to them. It is the hadith of Ibn Lahīʿah, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from Ibn Shamāsah, that a man told him, on the authority of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAdīs, that he said: “I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: ‘People will emerge who will pass out of the religion as an arrow passes through its target. God will kill them at Mount Lebanon and al-Jalīl ,’” or “at al-Jalīl and Mount Lebanon .”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAdīs, the brother of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAdīs, laid out a plot beside the Dome , the house of the Maʿāfirī.

The house of the Banū Jumāḥ had been a pool where water collected. ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ said, “Lay out a plot for my cousin beside me”—meaning Wahb ibn ʿUmayr al-Jumaḥī, who had been present at the conquest. The pool was filled in, and a plot was laid out for him.

It is said instead that he was ʿUmayr ibn Wahb ibn ʿUmayr. It is also said that it was a grant from Muʿāwiyah. ʿUmayr had come to Egypt in the days of Muʿāwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān, who wrote that a house should be built for him. The area there was open land, with no one’s house upon it; it was a place where water drained. This is evidence for the claim that the area around the mosque was open ground used as a place for the Muslims’ horses, as when ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, after some of the Banū Sahm who had not been present at the conquest came to him, built for them the House of the Chain, west of the mosque.

Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, who said: Wahb ibn ʿUmayr was commander of the people of Egypt in the campaign against ʿAmmūriyyah in the year 23. The commander of the people of Syria was Abū al-Aʿwar al-Sulamī.

Ibn al-Ḥuwayrith al-Sahmī laid out a plot beside the house of the Banū Jumāḥ and south of the house of Zakariyyāʾ ibn al-Jahm al-ʿAbdarī.

Thaqīf laid out plots at the mosque’s eastern corner, as far as the saddlers. The house of Abū ʿArābah was the plot of Ḥabīb ibn Aws al-Thaqafī, who had hosted Yūsuf ibn al-Ḥakam ibn Abī ʿAqīl, along with his son al-Ḥajjāj ibn Yūsuf, when they came to Egypt ahead of Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam. Thaqīf also had the land adjoining Abū ʿArābah’s house, extending to the lane that leads to the house of Faraj.

Zakariyyāʾ ibn al-Jahm al-ʿAbdarī laid out his house in the Lane of the Lamps. It is today the house of ʿAbbās ibn Shuraḥbīl, the one with the niche.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān and Rabīʿah, the sons of Shuraḥbīl ibn Ḥasanah, laid out the other houses of ʿAbbās ibn Shuraḥbīl beside it , and the house of Salamah ibn ʿAbd al-Malik al-Ṭaḥāwī.

Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us; Ibn Lahīʿah related to us. He said: Rabīʿah ibn Shuraḥbīl ibn Ḥasanah was in charge of the customs levy.

He said: Abū Dharr al-Ghifārī laid out the House of the Columns, which has a bathhouse. The scribe Barakah ibn Manṣūr took its well. One of its doors is in the Lane of the Lamps, and its other door faces the house of Barakah. From there, turning back toward the Market of the Berbers as far as the Palace of Ibn Jabr, the plot on your side belongs to Ghifār. Ibn Jabr had been an ally of Ghifār.

This Ibn Jabr was the envoy of al-Muqawqis to the Messenger of God ﷺ bringing Māriyah and her sister, and the gifts sent with them. The Copts claim that one of their men accompanied the Messenger of God, meaning Ibn Jabr. Abū Dharr was the man to whom the Messenger of God had entrusted what he entrusted concerning Egypt.

My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, related to us; Rashdīn ibn Saʿd related to us. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us; Ibn Wahb related to us, from Ḥarملah ibn ʿImrān, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shamāsah al-Mahrī, who said: I heard Abū Dharr say: The Messenger of God said: “You will conquer a land where the qīrāṭ is mentioned. So treat its people well, for they have a covenant and kinship. When you see two brothers fighting over the space of a brick, leave .” He passed by ʿAbd al-Raḥmān and Rabīʿah, the sons of Shuraḥbīl ibn Ḥasanah, while they were quarrelling over the space of a brick, and he left it. Ibn Wahb said: I heard al-Layth say: “I think the Prophet ﷺ said that to him only because of what happened concerning the people of Egypt under ʿUthmān.” Iyās ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Qārī laid out a plot west of the house of the Banū Shuraḥbīl ibn Ḥasanah.

The Anṣārīs Ruwayfiʿ ibn Thābit and ʿUqbah ibn Karīm laid out plots alongside Rabīʿah and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, the sons of Shuraḥbīl ibn Ḥasanah.

The Anṣārī Ruwayfiʿ ibn Thābit also laid out the house that came to belong to the Banū al-Ṣimmah. Ruwayfiʿ ibn Thābit died at Barqah, which he had governed.

Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth, who said: Ruwayfiʿ ibn Thābit governed Anṭābulus in the year 43.

Abū Fāṭimah al-Azdī laid out the Dawsī house, and the house where the men with the pendant cords are today.

The people of Egypt have one hadith from him from the Prophet ﷺ. It is the report of Ibn Lahīʿah, from al-Ḥārith ibn Yazīd, who said: Kathīr al-Aʿraj al-Ṣadafī told me: I heard Abū Fāṭimah say, while he was with us at Dhū al-Ṣawārī:

The Messenger of God said to me: “Abū Fāṭimah, prostrate often, for no Muslim prostrates to God in prostration except that God raises him by it one degree .”

Abū al-Aswad and Saʿīd ibn Abī Maryam related it to us, from Ibn Lahīʿah. Others besides the people of Egypt have also transmitted it from him .

He said: The house where ʿAmr ibn Khālid lived was the plot of a man from Banū Tamīm.

The people of the sawīq also had a plot belonging to a man of Banū Tamīm, one of those who had been present at the conquest. ʿAmr ibn Suhayl later bought it from him.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Sarḥ laid out his house adjoining the Palace of the Byzantines, known as the House with the Niche. The house called the House of the Banana, and not this large palace of his , which is known as the Palace of the Jinn, was a plot. Rather, he built that palace later, during the caliphate of ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān. He ordered it built when he set out west to raid Ifrīqiyah.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us; Ibn Lahīʿah related to us that he heard Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb mention that al-Miqdād had raided Ifrīqiyah with ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd. When they returned, ʿAbd Allāh said to al-Miqdād, about a house he had built, “What do you think of the construction of this house?” Al-Miqdād replied, “If it was built with the wealth of God, you have been extravagant; if with your own wealth, you have squandered it.” ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd said, “Were it not for someone saying that I had squandered it twice, I would tear it down.” ʿAbd Allāh was kunyah-named Abū Yaḥyā.

The people of Egypt have one hadith from him from the Prophet ﷺ—no other hadith from him from the Prophet is known to them. It is the hadith of Ibn Lahīʿah, from ʿAyyāsh ibn ʿAbbās al-Qitbānī, from al-Haytham ibn Shafī, Abū al-Ḥuṣayn, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Sarḥ, who said: While the Messenger of God ﷺ and ten of his Companions were with him—Abū Bakr, ʿUmar, ʿUthmān, ʿAlī, al-Zubayr, and others—on a mountain, the mountain moved beneath them. The Messenger of God said: “Be still, Ḥirāʾ, for upon you there is only a prophet, a truthful one, or a martyr .”

They have accounts about him personally, transmitted only by the people of Egypt.

Kaʿb ibn Ḍannah—or, it is said, Kaʿb ibn Yasār ibn Ḍannah al-ʿAbsī—laid out the house at the end of the Lane of the Lamps facing the Market of the Berbers, known as the House of the Palm Tree. Kaʿb was the son of the daughter of Khālid ibn Sinān al-ʿAbsī—or of his sister’s daughter. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: “I am uncertain.”

Khālid ibn Sinān is the man whom Qays claim was a prophet during the interval between the Prophet and Jesus, blessings be upon them both.

There is a lengthy hadith about Khālid ibn Sinān.

Al-Muqriʾ ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd related to us; Ḥaywah ibn Shurayḥ related to us; al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Shurāḥbīl al-Ghāfiqī related to us, that ʿAmmār ibn Saʿd al-Tujībī informed them that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ ordering him to appoint Kaʿb ibn Ḍannah as judge. ʿAmr sent for him and read him the Commander of the Faithful’s letter. Kaʿb said, “No, by God! God will not save a man from the Age of Ignorance and the ruin it brought, only for him to return to it after God has saved him from it .” He refused to accept the judgeship, so ʿAmr left him to it .

Ibn ʿUfayr said: Kaʿb ibn Ḍannah had been an arbitrator in the Age of Ignorance.

Qays also had the house known as the House of al-Zīr, which today belongs to the Banū Wardān. The Lane of the Lamps used to be called the Lane of the Nobles because ʿAmr’s house stood at one end, toward the congregational mosque, and Kaʿb ibn Ḍannah’s at the other, toward the Market of the Berbers. Between them were the house of ʿIyāḍ ibn Juraybah al-Kalbī, which ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān gave him; the house of Ibn Mudhaylifah al-Kalbī; the house of Ibn Firās al-Kinānī; the house of Nāfiʿ ibn ʿAbd al-Qays al-Fihrī—or, it is said, ʿUqbah ibn Nāfiʿ; the house of Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Kinānī; the house of Abū Dharr al-Ghifārī; the houses of Rabīʿah and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, the sons of Shuraḥbīl ibn Ḥasanah, whose clients are served by Bakr ibn Muḍar; the house of Zakariyyāʾ ibn al-Jahm al-ʿAbdarī; the house of Iyās ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Qārī; and the house of Abū Ḥakīm, the client of ʿUtbah ibn Abī Sufyān, which Muʿāwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān built for him.

Ibn ʿAbdah laid out his house in the Saddlers’ quarter, where the ʿAqābīn are today. It came to belong to the Banū Miskīn.

The house of Naṣr belonged to a man from Quraysh. He died, and ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān bought it and gave it to al-Aṣbagh.

The House of Sahl, where the saddlers and the Bathhouse of Sahl are, belonged to ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ. He bought it and gave it to his daughter Umm ʿAbd Allāh, daughter of ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān married her, and she bore him Sahl and Suhayl, who inherited it from their mother .

The palace called the Palace of Māriyah was a plot belonging to Ibn Rifāʿah al-Fahmī. He gave it to ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān, who built it for a Roman umm walad of his, named Māriyah; it was named after her. It is said that he compensated him for it with its site at al-Ḥamrāʾ.

It is said instead that it was their plot, and that ʿĪsā ibn Yazīd al-Jalūdī, when he entered Egypt with ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṭāhir, demolished it and made it a prison. It is the prison beside the guard post of Banānah , near the residence of ʿAmr ibn Suwwād al-Sarḥī. Banānah had been a nurse or wet nurse to some of the Banū Marwān, and the guard post was named after her.

Māriyah was the mother of Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, who left no descendants.

As Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿah, from Ibn Hubayrah, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ had summoned Khālid ibn Thābit al-Fahmī, the ancestor of the Banū Rifāʿah, intending to put him in charge of the customs levy. Khālid asked to be excused. ʿAmr said, “What do you dislike about it?” He replied, “Kaʿb said: ‘Do not approach the customs levy, for its collector is in the Fire.’”

Jahm ibn al-Ṣalt al-Muṭṭalibī laid out, toward the oil merchants, the house opposite the Bathhouse of Bisr.

Ibn Muljam laid out, at al-Rāyah, in the oil merchants’ quarter, the house whose façade is built of stone.

Iyās ibn al-Bukayr and his son Tamīm ibn Iyās laid out the house beside the house of Ibn Abraha—the house where the men with the pegs are, extending to the market. He was Iyās ibn al-Bukayr ibn ʿAbd Yalīl ibn Nāshib ibn Ghayrah ibn Saʿd ibn Layth ibn Bakr ibn ʿAbd Manāh ibn Kinānah, allies of the Banū ʿAdī ibn Kaʿb.

Mujāhid ibn Jabr, the client of Bint Ghazwān, laid out his house in the Coppersmiths’ quarter, which came to belong to Ṣāliḥ, the market overseer.

Abū Shamir ibn Abraha laid out a plot beside the house of Shuyaym al-Laythī.

Ibn Waʿlah laid out a plot beside him. They and those with them acquired the area up to the Bathhouse Market and the houses that had belonged to the Banū Marwān.

Ḥumayd ibn Hishām al-Ḥimyarī informed me, saying: Ibn Abraha had no plot in Fusṭāṭ Egypt.

Their plots were only at al-Jīzah. The houses they came to own in Fusṭāṭ were inherited from al-Waʿliyyah, for they had married into Ibn Waʿlah’s family, and the houses came to them by inheritance.

The sons of Abraha were four: Kurayb ibn Abraha, Abū Rashdīn; Abū Shamir ibn Abraha; Maʿdī Karib ibn Abraha; and Yaksūm ibn Abraha.

Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us; Ibn Lahīʿah related to us. He said: Kurayb ibn Abraha and his brother Abū Shamir ibn Abraha migrated during the caliphate of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb.

Hārūn ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Zuhrī related to us; Muḥammad ibn ʿUmar related to us; ʿAbd al-Ḥamīd ibn Jaʿfar informed me, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān asked Kurayb ibn Abraha ibn al-Ṣabāḥ about ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb’s sermon at al-Jābiyah: “Were you present for it?” He replied, “I was present as a boy, wearing a waistcloth. I heard it but did not understand it. But I can direct you to someone who heard it as a grown man.” He asked, “Who?” Kurayb replied, “Sufyān ibn Wahb al-Khawlānī.” ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz sent for him and asked him, “Were you present with ʿUmar at al-Jābiyah?” He replied, “Yes.” Then he related the hadith.

Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us; Maymūn ibn Yaḥyā related to us, from Makhramah ibn Bukayr, from Yaʿqūb ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ashajj, who said: I came to Egypt in the days of ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān and saw Kurayb ibn Abraha coming out from ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, with five hundred men of Ḥimyar beneath his stirrup.

Kaʿb ibn ʿAdī al-ʿAbādī laid out a plot in the qaysāriyyah. When ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz wanted to build it, he bought it from them and laid out their house for them in the Banū Wāʾil quarter.

The bathhouse known today as the Bathhouse of Abū Murrah was a plot belonging to a man of Tanūkh, the ancestor—or father—of Ibn ʿAlqamah. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān asked him for it, and he gave it to him. He built it as a bathhouse for Zabān ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz; it was known by Zabān’s name. About it the poet says:

Whoever holds white women in esteem,

let him visit the fair one at Zabān’s bathhouse.

There is no soul in him, nor a blade to turn him,

but he is an idol in the form of a human being.

In verses of his . There was in it a marble statue shaped like a woman, astonishingly strange, until it was broken in the year in which Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ordered the idols to be broken. He had ordered them broken in the year 102. In compensation for it, ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz planted for him the palm grove at al-Jīzah that is known today as the Garden of Kaʿb.

Al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām laid out his house, which is today in the Market of Wardān; the plot belongs to Balī. In it is the staircase that al-Zubayr set up and climbed to reach the fortress. According to some shaykhs, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr would stay there whenever he came to Egypt. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān had once taken it for himself, but Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik returned it to them. Yazīd ibn al-Walīd then took it from them, and it remained in their hands until the rule of the Commander of the Faithful Abū Jaʿfar. Hishām ibn ʿUrwah interceded with him about it. Hishām had influence with Abū Jaʿfar, so he ordered it returned to them, saying: “A man like Abū ʿAbd Allāh”—meaning al-Zubayr—“should not have anything taken from him.”

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām laid out a plot in Fusṭāṭ.

Abū Baṣrah al-Ghifārī laid out a plot beside the house of al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām. ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ retained the palace, did not divide it, and set it aside.

The people of Egypt have hadiths from Abū Baṣrah from the Prophet ﷺ. Among them is the following: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Khālid ibn Yazīd, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from Abū al-Khayr, from Abū Baṣrah, that the Messenger of God said: “Tomorrow we are going to ride out to the Jews. When they greet you, say: ‘And upon you’ .”

Another is the hadith of al-Layth ibn Saʿd, from Khayr ibn Nuʿaym, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Hubayrah, from Abū Tamīm al-Jayshānī, from Abū Baṣrah al-Ghifārī, that the Messenger of God ﷺ prayed the afternoon prayer one day at al-Mukhammis—one of their valleys—then turned away and said: “This prayer was prescribed for those before you, but they neglected it and abandoned it. Whoever among you prays it, God will record for him twice the reward. There is no prayer after it until the witness rises .”

This was related to us by ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ. It was also related to us by Idrīs ibn Yaḥyā al-Khawlānī, from Ibn ʿAyyāsh al-Qitbānī, from Ibn Hubayra.

Among these is also a report from al-Layth, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from Kulayb ibn Dhuhl al-Ḥaḍramī, from ʿUbayd ibn Jabr: he travelled with Abū Baṣra al-Ghifārī during Ramaḍān. When they set out from al-Fusṭāṭ, Abū Baṣra called for food while we were looking toward al-Fusṭāṭ. I said to him, “We can eat, even if we wish to look toward al-Fusṭāṭ; we have looked.” He said, “Would we turn away from the practice of the Messenger of God ﷺ and his Companions?” So we broke our fast.

Among these is a report from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Mūsā ibn Wardān, from Abū al-Haytham, from Abū Baṣra: the Messenger of God ﷺ said, “The unbeliever eats from seven intestines, and the believer eats from one intestine.” Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related this to us.

He said: Aslam established their allotment next to the house of Abū Dharr. Among their properties is the house of al-Ṣabbāḥ. The lane containing the house of Ibn Balāda, on its eastern side, belongs to Aslam. They also have land from the palace of Ibn Jabr to the barbers at the Bārbār market.

Some of the elders of the people of Egypt claim that Khuzāʿa have two houses. The house known by the name of Ibn Nīzak belonged to a man of theirs called al-Ḥārith ibn So-and-so—or So-and-so ibn al-Ḥārith. The house beside it is the one next to the judges.

The Laythīs who were with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ established their allotment by the paper sellers; they were the family of ʿUrwa ibn Shiyam. Behind them, Busr ibn Abī Arṭāʾ established his allotment.

The Banū Muʿādh of Mudlij have two houses: one is in the lane of ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama and belonged to Ashhab the jurist; the other is on the rise at the Bārbār market, in the lane containing the house of Muṣʿab al-Zuhrī.

The ʿAnaza of Rabīʿa have houses gathered together, about ten of them, and a mosque at the foot of the rise by the house of Ibn Ṣāmit.

Balī established their allotment behind Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa. Then they proceeded along their allotment from the house of ʿAmr ibn Yazīd to the house of Salama and the house of Wāḍiḥ, until they encompassed the house of Mujāhid ibn Jabr as far as Darb al-Zujāj. Then they went on until they reached the people who sold oil; then they went on, reaching the qibla side of the Wardan market, until they came to the Mosque of al-Qurūn. Then the lane turned inward toward the mosque of the Banū ʿAwf of Balī. This is the mosque in the lane, and the house of Ibn Yabūla, at the Wardan market, belongs to Balī, as a share extending to the presses.

Balī used to stand only to the right of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ’s banner, because the mother of al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil was from Balī.

4) ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Hishām related to us: Ziyād ibn ʿAbd Allāh related to us, from Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq, that the mother of al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil was a woman from Balī. (4

Balī became numerous in Egypt for the reason related to us by al-ʿAbbās ibn Ṭālib, from ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Ziyād, from ʿĀṣim al-Aḥwal, from Abū ʿUthmān al-Nahdī. He said: A man of Balī, a clan of Quḍāʿa in Syria, called out, “O people of Quḍāʿa!” This reached ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, who wrote to the governor of Syria, ordering him to send a third of Quḍāʿa to Egypt. They investigated and found that Balī were a third of Quḍāʿa, so they sent them to Egypt.

He said: Then the Banū Baḥr established their allotment beside Balī. They are a people of al-Azd among Lakhm; then they reached the sea.

After them, al-Ḥamrāʾ established their allotment. I shall mention their report in its proper place, if God wills.

Then a group of Salāmān reached the sea; after them, a group of Fahm and Kināna—Fahm, then al-Ḥamrāʾ as well—reached the bridge.

The first of the tribes were Balī, the people of the banner, beside Balī ibn ʿAmr; the banner belonged to Quraysh and those with them.

It was called “the Banner” because of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ’s banner. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, saying, “The Banner belonged to Quraysh; ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ’s banner was with them.” It is said that it was called the Banner because groups of Arabs from various tribes had taken part in the conquest with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, but their own tribes did not have enough men for them to stand under their tribes’ banners. They disliked standing under someone else’s banner, so ʿAmr said to them, “I shall make a banner that I will not attribute to anyone in particular, beneath which you can stand.” They accepted this. Thus, everyone whose tribe did not have enough men stood beneath it, and it was called the Banner for that reason. God knows best.

The Ḥijr of al-Azd extended from the Mosque of al-ʿAytham until one reaches the lane of al-Sammī, then Yarfaʿ, then Shajāʿa, then Tharād. Then Hudhayl and Fahm met them. Hudhayl then came between them and Salāmān, until Hudhayl reached Suwayqat ʿAdwān, the little market by the lane of al-Makkī. The house of Sabra and the lane where Ibn al-Aghlab used to stay, as far as this little market, belong to Hudhayl; and the lane from the school of Ismāʿīl to the residence of Banāna belongs to Fahm.

The Mosque of al-ʿAytham was built by al-Ḥakam ibn Abī Bakr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān. It had been a stable, and the stable belonged to al-Azd. Al-Ḥakam bought it from them and built the mosque there. He assigned three dinars each month from the rent of the stable to the person who recited from the codex they had placed in the mosque, which is called the Codex of Asmāʾ. When their property was seized and added to the property of God, and the stable was seized among the other property, a letter was written about the codex, by order of the Commander of the Faithful, Abū al-ʿAbbās. He wrote that they should leave their codex in their mosque as it was, and pay the person who recited from it three dinars each month from the property of God.

The story of the codex, as Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr and others related to us—with some adding to what others reported—is that al-Ḥajjāj ibn Yūsuf had codices written and sent them to the provinces, and dispatched one of them to Egypt. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān was angered by this and said, “He sends a codex to a garrison over which I am in command?” So he ordered this codex, which is in the congregational mosque today, to be written for him. When it was finished, he said, “Whoever finds an erroneous letter in it shall receive a red-headed slave and thirty dinars.” The reciters passed it around, and a man from al-Ḥamrāʾ came looked through it, then came to ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz and said, “I have found an erroneous letter in the codex.” He said, “In my codex?” The man said, “Yes.” They looked, and found the words, “Indeed, this is my brother; he has ninety-nine ewes”.

There it was written najʿa, with the jīm placed before the ʿayn. He ordered that the codex be corrected, and then gave the man thirty dinars and a red-headed slave.

Then ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz died, and Abū Bakr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz bought the codex from his estate for a thousand dinars. Then Abū Bakr died, and it was sold from his estate. Asmāʾ, daughter of Abū Bakr ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, bought it for seven hundred dinars. She made it available to the people and publicized it, and it was named after her. Then Asmāʾ died, and al-Ḥakam ibn Abī Bakr bought it and placed it in the mosque. He assigned the person who recited from it three dinars each month from the rent of the stable. Al-Ḥakam ibn Abī Bakr was the one who built the mosque known today as the Dome of the Wardan Market.

He said: Then there were ʿAdwān, extending as far as the market, and then Salāmān met them. The house of Ibn Abī al-Kanūd faces Suwayqat ʿAdwān; the lane of al-Makkī is the allotment of Dāris, and a group of Yarfaʿ. Then Salāmān continued until they reached the sea by Jannān Ḥawwāʾ. Then Kināna of Fahm came between them; their territory extends from the lane of Ibn Rifāʿa until it reaches the sea. Then, facing Jannān Ḥawwāʾ, the Banū Yashkur of Lakhm met Salāmān. Jannān Ḥawwāʾ and the western slope of the mountain belong to Yashkur ibn Jazīla of Lakhm. There is also an allotment of ʿAlī ibn Rabāḥ al-Lakhmī in al-Ḥamrāʾ, at Jannān Ḥawwāʾ, on your left as you go toward the bridge.

He said: When Mahra first arrived, they established their allotment at Dār al-Khayl and its environs, on the slope of the mountain called Mount Yashkur, from the side of the trench toward the eastern part of the army camp, as far as today’s Jannān Banī Miskīn. The Mahra mosque is there, a black-domed building, until Ṭarīf the eunuch incorporated it into the stables when he built them.

Today’s Jannān Banī Miskīn was the allotment of a man from Mahra called al-Jarrāḥ. He died without leaving descendants, so Shurayḥ ibn Maymūn al-Mahrī came, inherited it, married his wife, and made a treaty for him on the sea. No madadī in his time, it was said, achieved the distinction he did—except that Tawba ibn Nimr al-Ḥaḍramī was a madadī and held the judgeship.

Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth, who said: “African ships arrived in the year ninety-eight, with Ibn Abī Burda in command. They and the people of Egypt, under the command of Shurayḥ ibn Maymūn, raided, and he wintered them out. The first ships were commanded by ʿAmr ibn Hubayra, while Abū ʿUbayda was in charge of the people of the city at al-Bunṭus.”

The homes of Mahra were south of the Banner, beside the homes of Ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Sarḥ, on land they had enclosed. When they went to Friday prayer, they tied up their horses. Later, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ moved them and attached them to himself, and they abandoned their homes there. Mahra’s allotment then extended until it met Ghāfiq at the market, and met al-Ṣadaf and Ghant in the western area.

Lakhm established their allotment. They established it south of Thaqīf, beside the saddlers: the house that later became ʿAyyāsh ibn ʿUqba’s belonged to them, as did the house of al-Zallābiyya. They proceeded along their allotment to the rise of Mahra and the lane of Abū Ḥakīm. With them were a group of Judhām. Then they descended into the lane of Wardan, the client of Ibn Abī Sarḥ.

There is the allotment of Abū Ruqayya al-Lakhmī, and his residence there still stands as it was, unchanged, facing the mosque by the houses of the Banū Wardan.

Then they descended to the mosque of ʿAbd Allāh. Whatever is on your right as you head toward the congregational mosque, along the road to the houses of the Wardānīs from the mosque of ʿAbd Allāh, belongs to Lakhm; whatever is on your left belongs to Ghāfiq. Then Lakhm’s allotment passed on to the houses of Maṭar at the Bārbār market, where al-Azd met them at the houses of Abū Maryam. The rest of their allotment belongs to Ḥijr and Ḥāʾ. The mosque of Ḥāʾ is the mosque by the house of Isḥāq ibn Mutawakkil, with the minaret. The mosque on the road toward the outpost of Abū Ḥabīb was a meeting place where they used to sit. When the prayer was called, they would come out of three small gates opening onto the road; when they had prayed, they would return to their meeting place.

Then they meet Khuthaym and Māzin of al-Azd, beside the house of Ibn Fulayḥ. Then they meet Tanūkh, beside the house of al-Barāʾ ibn ʿUthmān ibn Ḥunayf. Then they meet Ghant of al-Azd, beside the house of Ibn Barmak, where the agents used to stay. That lane and courtyard, and what extends from the house of Ibn al-Haytham al-Aylī into the mosque of ʿAbd Allāh between the two, belong to Ghant of al-Azd, as far as the residence of Ashhab. If you take Ashhab’s lane, whatever is on your right as you head toward the stopping place belongs to Ghāfiq, and whatever is on your left belongs to al-Azd, as far as the stopping place.

The stopping place belonged to the daughter of Maslama ibn Mukhallad, who gave it in charity to the Muslims. The house of Abū Qudāma was also among the properties she gave in charity, as was the house of Ibrāhīm ibn Ṣāliḥ, the house of the Banū ʿAbd al-Jabbār of Ghāfiq.

Then al-Azd continued until they took in what reaches into the little market opposite the house of Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr, and the lane of the roasters, as far as the house of Ḥawwāʾ and the house of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Hāshim.

Then, beside the little market, they meet the clients, who are few. The Mosque of the Clients there is well known. The clients’ territory extends from the house of Ziyād the Chamberlain down to the farrier of Bilāl and the market.

Zubayd ibn al-Ḥārith al-Ḥijrī, of Ḥijr of Ḥimyar, was counted among the clients and was their ʿarīf. Saʿīd ibn al-Jahm used to say to ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Qāsim, “You are one of us,” which troubled him. That is, Zubayd ibn al-Ḥārith was from Ḥijr and was their client.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Qāsim was in charge of the clients.

If you come from the little market heading toward the congregational mosque, whatever is on your right belongs to al-Azd, and whatever is on your left, beside the outpost of Abū Ḥabīb, belongs to them.

Then Shajāʿa meets them at the covered passage of the spinners; Fahm meets them at the school of Ismāʿīl; and the Banū Shabāba of al-Azd meet them at the house of Ḥawwāʾ. Whatever lies along the main route, once you reach the lane of the house of Ḥawwāʾ, leave it, and head toward the army camp: it belongs to Fahm as far as the camp. That is the allotment of the Banū Shabāba of Fahm.

The Banū Shabāba also have the mosque with the minaret that leads you to the covered passage of Turkī, as well as the mosque in the courtyard of al-Sūsī.

If you descend from the seaward lane of Ḥawwāʾ, you come to Hudhayl. Whatever is on your right as you head toward the trench belongs to Hudhayl, and whatever is on your left belongs to Dahna of al-Azd, until you meet Yashkur of Lakhm on Mount Yashkur.

Then Ghāfiq established their allotment between Mahra and Lakhm. They continued along their allotment until they came out into the open country, beside the stopping place. Facing the direction from which the north wind blows, they met Lakhm and Ghant; toward the qibla they met al-Ṣadaf and Mahra.

They established their allotment, which expanded because they were numerous. As Ibn Lahīʿa related to us from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, Ghāfiq made up a third of the people in ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ’s entry into Egypt.

Ghāfiq’s territory extends from Darb al-Sarrājīn to the houses of the Banū Wardan. Whatever is on your right belongs to Ghāfiq as far as the Mosque of Fahm at al-Jamarāt; then it extends to al-Ṣafā, to the two mosques of Ḥidhrān. Ḥidhrān is a branch of Ghāfiq. It extends to the Mosque of Aḥdab and to the Mosque of al-Zimām.

They say that Muḥammad ibn Abī Bakr al-Ṣiddīq was buried at the site of the Mosque of al-Zimām.

Then return to the bathhouse of Sahl. Whatever is on your left as you head toward Mahra belongs to Ghāfiq. There is also the lane of Ḥamd of Ghāfiq, opposite the women’s bathhouse of Sahl. In it is the mosque of Abū Mūsā al-Ghāfiqī; there is no other mosque in the lane.

Abū Mūsā had been a Companion of the Messenger of God ﷺ. Abū Mūsā’s name was ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mālik. Two reports from him, from the Messenger of God ﷺ are known.

Muḥammad ibn Yaḥyā al-Ṣadafī related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us: ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith related to us that Yaḥyā ibn Maymūn al-Ḥaḍramī told him, from Wadāʿa al-Ḥamdī, who told him that he heard Abū Mūsā al-Ghāfiqī say: The Messenger of God ﷺ said, “Whoever knowingly attributes a lie to me, let him take his place in a house—or a seat—in the Fire”.

Asad ibn Mūsā and Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us; they said: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sulaymān, from Thaʿlaba Abū al-Kanūd, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mālik, who said that he heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say:

“When I am in a state of major ritual impurity, if I perform ablution I may eat and drink, but I do not pray or recite until I have performed the ritual bath.”

Then the route continues to the lane of al-Mawza. Once you pass the lane of al-Mawza, you reach the Mosque of Sībān, the domed mosque by the house of Khālid ibn ʿAbd al-Salām al-Ṣadafī. Sībān is of Mahra. Whatever is on your left as you head toward the covered passage of Jawād belongs to Ghāfiq; whatever is on your right belongs to al-Ṣadaf, as far as the Mosque of Aḥdab and beyond it, as far as the lane that takes you out into the open country. The exception is the house of Ibn Sābūr, the house that became the property of Ismāʿīl ibn Asbāṭ: it is the allotment of a man of Ḥimyar.

The Rabbānīs of Ghāfiq also have territory extending from the house of Maṭar; whatever is on your right as you head toward the Mosque of ʿAbd Allāh belongs to them. The ʿAbd Allāh for whom the mosque is named is ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān. ʿAbd al-Malik appointed him governor of Egypt after the death of ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān. His governorship began in Jumādā of the year eighty-six, as Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us from al-Layth ibn Saʿd. He was young. The people of Egypt called him Mukayyis. He was the first to transfer the registers into Arabic, for they had been in a foreign language; and he was the first to forbid people to wear barānis.

Then comes the house of Ibn Hajala al-Ghāfiqī. Once you reach the house of Ibn Hajala, everything on your right and left belongs to Ghāfiq. Muḥammad ibn Abī Bakr was hiding in the house of Ibn Hajala al-Ghāfiqī when ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ entered Egypt in the year of the embankment. As Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, the embankment was built in Ṣafar of the year thirty-eight.

Al-Ghāfiqī had a weak sister. When Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj and his men came seeking those who had killed ʿUthmān, al-Ghāfiqī’s sister said, “Whom are you seeking?” They said, “Muḥammad ibn Abī Bakr?” She said, “I will lead you to him, but do not kill my brother.” So she led them to him. When he was seized, he said, “Honor Abū Bakr in my person.” Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj said, “I have killed seventy of my people over ʿUthmān, and should I spare you, when you were his killer?” Then he killed him.

It is the house adjoining the Mosque of the Zanj; sandals from Sind are made at its door, and inside it are millstones.

Ghāfiq’s territory extends from the Mosque of Bādī to the house of Ibrāhīm ibn Ṣāliḥ, and then to the Mosque of Ibrāhīm al-Qarrāṭ. That is Dahna of Ghāfiq.

Ghāfiq’s allotment extends farther than what we have mentioned, but these are its main parts.

Al-Ṣadaf established their allotment south of Mahra. They continued along their allotment until one part of it emerged into the open country, where they met Ḥaḍramawt before the open country. Toward the qibla they met the Banū Saʿd of Tujīb, and they met the family of Īdaʿān ibn— Saʿd. At one end of it they met Salham of Madhḥij; then Ḥaḍramawt came upon them and stood between them and the desert.

The banner of al-Ajdūm was the entrance by which ʿAmr came, with Ḥayyān—or Ḥibbān—ibn Yūsuf. When Ṣadaf had settled there, ʿImrān ibn Rabīʿa was appointed over them as their ʿarīf. He remained in that post for years, then his son was appointed in his place. Some of them of noble standing and generosity continued to live in the town; among them was Ibn Sulayk al-Ṣadafī.

Ḥaḍramawt and a branch of Yaḥṣib settled in the place where they live today, in the time of ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān—all except ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Mutahallil. Among those from Ḥaḍramawt who entered Fusṭāṭ with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ was ʿAbd Allāh ibn Kulayb of al-Ashbāʾ; his allotted quarter was among Āl Aydʿān, by the house of Ibn al-Rawwāgh. There was also Mālik ibn ʿAmr ibn al-Ajdaʿ of al-Ḥārith. His house was the house of Hubayra ibn Abyaḍ. And there were al-Malāmis ibn Judhayma ibn Sarīʿ, whose quarter was at al-Ṣafāʾ, by the house of al-Faraj ibn Jaʿfar; Namr ibn Zurʿa ibn Namr ibn Shājī al-Bassī ; al-Aʿyan ibn Namr ibn Mālik ibn Sarīʿ; and Abū al-ʿĀliya, their client, who was the grandfather of Abū Qinān.

They had been with their maternal uncles among Tujīb. Then their reinforcements arrived in the days of ʿUthmān, and they settled east of Salham and Ṣadaf, extending out toward the open country. Those among them who had been with Tujīb and wished to move joined them.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Kulayb of al-Ashbāʾ had settled in their place, his quarter among Banū Aydʿān by the house of Ibn al-Rawwāgh. His brother Qays ibn Kulayb was among ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ’s chamberlains in Muʿāwiya’s time. He was a handsome young man, and Muʿāwiya saw him with ʿAmr and asked, “Who is this young man?” ʿAmr replied, “One of my chamberlains.”

Muʿāwiya said, “A man like this is not fit to serve as a chamberlain.”

Later, Qays served as chamberlain to ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān. In a poem in which he satirized the notables of Egypt, Abū al-Muṣʿab al-Balawī said of Qays ibn Kulayb:

All night I called to the wretched Qays,

So that he might let me in , though supper was ready.

Qays is not of noble grandmothers,

But of lowly Ḥaḍramī women.

And Yarbūʿ turned away a breath of favor from me,

Though he had raised the banner.

He gestured with his right hand, though it was

A left hand, unfit to give a gift.

I speak to ʿĀʾidh, but he turns away from me,

Pride keeping him from greeting me.

A bank whose two sides have collapsed—

That is Kuraib, the weary old beast.

As for al-Qaḥzamī, he is a mule,

Made worse by the mange and the rough ground.

And that short fellow from Tujīb—

If he could, he would not even shake off the dung.

It is also related: “Made worse by the mange and castration.”

He said: Whenever Muʿāwiya received someone from Egypt, he would ask him, “Do you know Abū al-Muṣʿab’s poem by heart?” These verses are from one of his poems. By Yazīd he means Yazīd ibn Sharāḥbīl ibn Ḥasana; Qays is Qays ibn Kulayb, the chamberlain; and ʿĀʾidh is ʿĀʾidh ibn Thaʿlaba al-Balawī. ʿĀʾidh was killed at al-Barullus in the year fifty-three, along with Wardān, the client of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, and Abū Ruqayya al-Lakhmī . I shall relate their story in its proper place, God willing. Al-Qaḥzamī was ʿAmr ibn Qaḥzam; Kuraib was Kuraib ibn Abraha; and the short man from Tujīb was Ziyād ibn Ḥanāṭa al-Tujībī, then al-Khalāwī. He was the owner of the Palace of Ibn Ḥanāṭa in Tujīb.

Al-Malāmis ibn Judhayma continued to be the ʿarīf of Ḥaḍramawt, with al-Ashbāʾ and al-Ḥārith claiming him as their representative, until the time of Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān. A dispute then arose between Muslima ibn Mukhallad and al-Malāmis. Al-Malāmis asked Muʿāwiya for permission to move to Palestine with the people of Ḥaḍramawt. Muʿāwiya granted permission and wrote to Muslima accordingly, but Muslima disliked the arrangement. A man of Ḥaḍramawt, called So-and-so ibn Muslim, said to him, “I will go between them and make them reluctant to leave.” And he did so. When al-Malāmis had secured this from Muslima, Muslima told him, “If your people agree.” Then he gathered them and told them what al-Malāmis had said. One of them said, “We will not leave our homeland.” Muslima asked him, “Who are you?” He replied, “I am Ibn Umayya.” “And who are your people?” “Banū ʿAwf.” Then the others spoke in agreement with him. Muslima registered them and appointed them as representatives.

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from ʿUtba ibn Abī Ḥakīm, from Ibn Shihāb, that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: “Ḥaḍramawt is better than Banū al-Ḥārith” .

Abū al-Aswad related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from al-Ḥārith ibn Yazīd, that Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān wrote to Muslima ibn Mukhallad, who was then in charge of Egypt: “Appoint no one to office under you except a man of Azd or Ḥaḍramawt, for they are people of trustworthiness.”

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from al-Ḥārith ibn Yazīd, from Tabīʿ, who said: “No one from Ḥaḍramawt will encounter the Antichrist.”

He said: Then Tujīb settled there. Banū ʿĀmir took the area east of the fortress, south of the house of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Sarḥ. They extended their quarter until they met Mahra and Ṣadaf on the north-facing side, Salham to the east, and Waʿlān of Madhḥij and a branch of Khawlān to the south. Then they met Banū Ghuṭayf and clans of Madhḥij, while Salham stood between them and the desert.

The quarter of Kināna ibn Bishr ibn Salmān al-Aydʿī was the house of Hubayra, and his mosque was there. Later it passed to ʿUthmān ibn Yūnus Abū al-Samḥ, the maternal grandfather of Ibn Dahqān. Kināna had a sword called al-Muqallad, which passed to Saʿīd ibn ʿUbayd. Saʿīd used to say, “Tujīb has only two swords: ʿArīḍ of Banū Ḥudayj and al-Muqallad. Now al-Muqallad has come to me.”

He said: Khawlān settled east of the fortress, southward and toward the south-facing side. They extended their quarter until they met Banū Wāʾil and the Persians on the plain, Tujīb and Raʿīn in the mountain, and Banū Ghuṭayf and Banū Waʿlān of Madhḥij in the east; they met Tujīb on the north-facing side. Ghuṭayf passed beyond them and settled between them and their quarter.

Rāʾim ibn Thaʿlaba al-Khawlānī, of al-Ḥiyāwiyya, was said to be a man of Kināna, with known lineage among them. Ibn Jadhil al-Ṭaʿʿān said of him:

Who will carry a message from me to Khawlān,

A message borne along by the sons of Firās ibn Mālik?

That our brother Rāʾim, the good, is among you,

Dwelling without guilt in the desolate depths;

He traces his lineage to Mālik, when his ancestry is counted—

Kināna, people of noble deeds and lordly rank.

A man of Khawlān answered him, saying:

Who will carry a message from me to Firās?

We belong to Khawlān ibn ʿAmr ibn Mālik.

My lineage and stock go back to the kings of Sabaʾ;

My grandfather tells me so, and he is no liar.

He said: Madhḥij settled between Khawlān and Tujīb. Waʿlān settled toward the palace, then they and Banū Ghuṭayf advanced to encamp opposite Khawlān and Tujīb.

Then Murād advanced with its quarter until it met the clans of Nāfiʿ and Raʿīn, among whom were Banū ʿAbs ibn Zūf. They extended their quarter until they met Banū Mawhib of al-Maʿāfir, and met al-Salaf and Sabaʾ, standing between them and the desert.

Some people have been mistaken about Banū ʿAbs ibn Zūf and the alley attributed to Banū ʿAbs. They have said that these were ʿAbs of Qays, but that is not as they said.

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from ʿUtba ibn Abī Ḥakīm, that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: “The tribe most numerous in Paradise is Madhḥij.”

The clans affiliated with Sabaʾ settled east of Janb, toward Murād. Among them was Ibn Dhī Hijrān, and al-Salaf were with them. They extended their quarter between al-Maʿāfir and Ḥaḍramawt until they reached the open country.

Ḥimyar settled south of Khawlān and to its east, and east of Badīʿa of Madhḥij. Yaḥṣib lay south of al-Maʿāfir, and they continued until they crossed the mountains.

Yāfiʿ and Raʿīn settled east of Khawlān , then met the clans of al-Kalāʿ. They extended their quarter between the clans of Sabaʾ and al-Maʿāfir, and between the stable of Qurra ibn Sharīk, until they reached the open country.

Al-Maʿāfir settled there, including the Ashʿarīs and al-Sakāsik, east of al-Kalāʿ. Al-Akānūʿ, who were among the Ashʿarīs, were assigned authority over them there. Banū Mawhib, then al-Sakāsik, then al-Maʿāfir lived there, mixed together. Then they extended their quarter until they reached the open country, where they faced Ḥimyar and a group of Khawlān. Ḥimyar and al-Maʿāfir occupied the mountain, facing the clans of Muḍar. No tribes lived on this mountain except these, although Juhayna had once settled at the ravine of Yanna .

Al-Maʿāfir had settled beside ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, where mosquitoes troubled them, and the Nile was in flood. They complained of this to ʿAmr and asked him to move them. He said, “I can find no people more able to bear it for me than my companions.” So he moved Quraysh to their place and moved al-Maʿāfir to the place where they are today. ʿAmr said to his companions, “Make the most of it. It is as if I can see the mosque and the area around it filled with people who desire it, while your place has become deserted.” And it happened as he said.

Hānī ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us: Ḍimām ibn Ismāʿīl related to us, from Abū Qabīl, from Shafī ibn Mātiʿ, who said: When people were alarmed, they went out with their banners, and each people had a station. The station of al-Maʿāfir was beneath the mound, meaning in Alexandria.

The Palace of Fahd in al-Maʿāfir, and the mosque of Sabaʾ whose site he laid out, belonged to Fahd ibn Kathīr ibn Fahd . He was in charge of Barqa during the first period of Usāma ibn Zayd’s rule, and had also been in charge of the Island of the Arsenal. It is the palace by the Mosque of al-Zīna. A report concerning the Ashʿarīs and al-Sakāsik follows.

Abū Jābir Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Malik related to us: al-Rukn ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd related to us, from Makḥūl, from Muʿādh, that when the Prophet sent him to Yemen, he put him on a she-camel and said, “Muʿādh, go until you reach al-Jund. Wherever this she-camel kneels , give the call to prayer, pray, and build a mosque there.” Muʿādh set out. When he reached al-Jund, his she-camel circled around him and refused to kneel. He asked, “Is there another al-Jund?” They said, “Yes, Jund Rukhāma.” When he reached it, the she-camel circled around and knelt. Muʿādh dismounted, called the people to prayer, and then stood and prayed. Ibn Yukhāmir al-Saksakī came out to him and asked, “Who are you?” He replied, “I am the Messenger of the Lord of the worlds’ messenger.” “What do you want?” he asked. He replied, “I wish to fight those who oppose the Messenger of God ﷺ.” When Muʿādh had told him what the Messenger of God ﷺ had instructed him to do, Ibn Yukhāmir said to him, “Welcome to the one from whom you have come, and welcome to you. Give me your hand.” He pledged allegiance to him, and a band of the Ashʿarīs came forward to join him, as did al-Amlūk, the Amlūk of Radmān. Ibn Yukhāmir said, “The plot of land on which you built the mosque belongs to me.” Muʿādh said:

“Take its price.”

He said, “No, it is for God and the Messenger.”

With the band of Ashʿarīs and al-Amlūk, the Amlūk of Radmān, Muʿādh fought those who opposed the Messenger of God ﷺ until they responded to him. He wrote to the Messenger of God ﷺ: “I fought until the people of Yemen responded to me, with a band of Ashʿarīs, al-Sakāsik, and al-Amlūk, the Amlūk of Radmān.” The Messenger of God ﷺ said: “O God, forgive al-Sakāsik, al-Amlūk, the Amlūk of Radmān, and a band of Ashʿarīs.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to me, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that it had reached him that the Messenger of God ﷺ said: “Shall I tell you which tribes are best?” They said, “Yes.” He said, “Al-Amlūk, the Amlūk of Radmān; groups of Ashʿarīs; groups of Khawlān; al-Sakāsik; and al-Sakūn.”

He said: Banū Wāʾil settled on the north-facing side. They extended their quarter, running along the Nile until they met Rāshida of Lakhm toward the stable. Between one group of them and Yaḥṣib, who lived in the mountain, were the Persians, who were few in number.

Then a group of Lakhm moved down behind Banū Wāʾil and settled along the Nile. They extended their quarter, facing Yaḥṣib, who lived in the mountain, until they emerged onto cultivated land and fields. There was open space between each tribe and the next. When reinforcements arrived in the time of ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān and afterward, and the population grew, each people made room for their kinsmen until the buildings spread and joined together.

The Settlement Plan of al-Jīza

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb and Ibn Hubayra—one of the two added to the other—who said: Hamdān and those allied with them preferred al-Jīza. ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, informing him of what God had done for the Muslims, what He had granted them in conquest , what they had done in laying out their quarters, and how Hamdān and their allies preferred to settle at al-Jīza. ʿUmar wrote back, praising God for what had happened, and saying: “How could you agree to have your companions separated from you? You ought not to have agreed that any of your companions should have a river between them and you. You do not know what might suddenly befall them; perhaps you would be unable to come to their aid before what you fear overtakes them. Bring them together with you. If they refuse and prefer their location, build them a fortress from the Muslims’ spoils.”

ʿAmr presented this to them, but they refused. They preferred their location at al-Jīza, as did those of their kinsmen who were allied with them: Yāfiʿ and others. They liked the place there, so ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ built for them the fortress at al-Jīza in the year twenty-one and completed its construction in the year twenty-two.

Masters from among the people of Egypt other than Ibn Lahīʿa said: When ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ asked the people of al-Jīza to join Fusṭāṭ, they said, “We have already sent forth one who preceded us in the path of God. We would not leave him and move elsewhere.” Yāfiʿ settled at al-Jīza, including Mibraḥ ibn Shihāb; as did Hamdān, Dhū Aṣbaḥ—including Abū Shamir ibn Abraha—and a group of Ḥijr, including ʿAlqama ibn Janāda, one of Banū Mālik ibn Ḥijr .

A group of them had settled in Fusṭāṭ, below the pass of Tanūkh; I have explained this at the beginning of my book.

He said: A group of foreigners, called the Ḥamrāʾ and the Persians, had entered with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ. The Ḥamrāʾ were a people of the Byzantines, including Banū Yanna, Banū al-Azraq, and Banū Rūbīl.
