The Persians were a people of Persia. They included, they claimed, some Persians who had been in Ṣanʿāʾ . Their standard-bearer was Ibn Yanna, after whom the portico of Ibn Yanna in the Ḥamrāʾ quarter of Fusṭāṭ, Egypt, was named.

The Byzantines and the Persians said, “They are Arabs, and we do not trust them; we fear treachery from them.” They were asked, “What do you advise?” They said, “We will settle at one end, and you at the other. If they betray us, they will be between us.” One of them said, “If they betray us, we will be between the jaws of the lion, and we will have taken the surest course.”

The Byzantines settled in al-Ḥamrāʾ by the bridge, and the Persians settled in the area of Banū Wāʾil. The Mosque of the Persians there is well known.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from a shaykh among the clients of Fahm, from ʿAlī ibn Rabāḥ, who said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ brought the Ḥamrāʾ and the Persians from Syria. Ibn Lahīʿa said: He called them the Ḥamrāʾ because they were foreigners.

The Occupied Residences of Alexandria

He said: As for Alexandria, it had no planned quarters. However, Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām laid out a quarter in Alexandria.

Rather, these were occupied residences: whoever took a house settled in it with his kinsmen. When ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ conquered Alexandria, he and ʿUbāda ibn al-Ṣāmit came forward and climbed the mound on which the Mosque of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ stands. Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj said, “Let us settle,” and ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ settled in the palace that later belonged to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Sarḥ. It is said that ʿAmr gave it to him when he took charge of the town.

Abū Dharr al-Ghifārī settled in a residence west of the place of prayer by the Mosque of ʿAmr, toward the sea; it has since fallen into ruin. Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj settled where his house stands above this hill. ʿUbāda ibn al-Ṣāmit built a structure and remained there until he left Alexandria. It is said that Abū al-Dardāʾ was with him, but God knows best.

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb and Ibn Hubayra in their account. They said: When the lands had been secured for them, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ detached a quarter of his men to garrison Alexandria, a quarter to garrison the coastal regions, and kept half with him . In summer, for about six months, he would station in Alexandria only that quarter. After them, another contingent would serve there in winter for six months. Each ʿarīf had a palace where he and his companions lodged, and they established occupied residences there.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us: Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb related to us that when the Muslims settled there for their garrison service, then returned, then went on campaign, they hastened to [occupy the houses]. A man would come to the house where his companion had been before and hasten to settle in it. When they went on campaign, ʿAmr said, “I fear that you will ruin the houses if you keep taking turns in them.” When it was time for al-Karyūn5 he said to them, “Go with God’s blessing. Whoever among you plants his spear in a house, it belongs to him and his father’s sons.” A man would enter a house and plant his spear in one of its rooms; then another would come and plant his spear in one of the house’s other rooms. Thus a house might belong to two or three tribes. They would live in them, and when they returned [from campaign], the Byzantines would live in them and maintain them.

Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb used to say: “Nothing of their rent is lawful, nor may they be sold or inherited; nothing from them may be inherited. They were only for the Muslims to live in during their garrison service.”

Expansion of the congregational mosque

Then Maslama ibn Mukhallad al-Anṣārī enlarged the congregational mosque after ʿAmr had built it. Maslama was the one who compelled the people of Egypt to build minarets for the mosques. He compelled them to do so in the year 53; the minarets were built, and his name was inscribed on them.

Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, saying: “Maslama ibn Mukhallad compelled the people to build minarets for the mosques, but exempted Khawlān from this, because he had married into their tribe; he waived the obligation for them.”

Then ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān demolished the mosque in the year 77 and rebuilt it. Then, during his caliphate, al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik wrote to Qurra ibn Sharīk al-ʿAbsī, who was then his governor over the people of Egypt.

Qurra ibn Sharīk’s governorship of Egypt began in the year 90. He arrived on Monday, thirteen nights having passed of the month of Rabīʿ al-Awwal, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Malik was dismissed. The poet says of this:

How astonishing, what astonished me when it reached us:

That Qurra ibn Sharīk had been appointed over us,

And the blessed young man had been dismissed from us—

Then your father’s judgment in the matter proved mistaken.

He demolished the whole mosque and built it in this form, decorated it, and gilded the tops of the columns in the halls of Qays. There is no column in the mosque with a gilded top except in the halls of Qays. When Qurra demolished the mosque, he moved the pulpit to the honey market, so people performed the prayers there and held the Friday congregational prayers there until the building was completed. The qibla in the market remains there to this day. The dome that stood in the middle of the island between the two bridges was in the congregational mosque. Then Mūsā ibn ʿĪsā al-Hāshimī enlarged its rear section in the year 175. Later, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṭāhir enlarged it across its width, with al-Maʾmūn’s written permission, in the year 213. He incorporated all of Dār al-Raml into it, except for what remained of it belonging to the mint. Dār Ibn Rummāna also came within it, along with other parts of the districts we have mentioned.

As Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us, al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik’s officials wrote to him: “The treasuries are too cramped for the funds from the fifth.” He wrote back to them, “Build mosques.”

The first mosque built in Fusṭāṭ Egypt was the mosque at the base of the Roman fortress, by the Bāb al-Rayḥān, opposite the place known as al-Qālūs. It is known as the Mosque of the Citadel.

Ḥumayd ibn Hishām al-Ḥimyarī related to us, saying: “Any mosque in Fusṭāṭ Egypt that has marble columns is not of the Khattī type.”

The first church built in Fusṭāṭ Egypt, as ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from one of the elders of the people of Egypt, was the church behind the bridge, in the days of Maslama ibn Mukhallad. The troops objected to this on Maslama and said to him, “Are you permitting them to build churches?” Matters nearly came to blows between them and him. That day Maslama argued with them, saying, “It is not in your military settlement; it is outside it, on their land.” At that they fell silent. These, then, are the districts of the people of Egypt.

An account of the fiefs

He said: When the Muslims laid out their settlement, they had left open space between themselves, the sea, and the fortress, so that they could make their mounts sweat and train them. Matters remained so until Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān became governor. He bought Maslama ibn Mukhallad’s plot from him and granted him his house in the market of Wardān. Then he bought ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir’s plot and granted him his house in the open space by the people of the straw. It is today in the possession of Faraj. He bought Abū Rāfiʿ’s house, which had passed to al-Sāʾib, his freedman, and granted al-Sāʾib the house by the Goose Quarter.

Then ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz built the guesthouse, which was for ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz’s guests. Muʿāwiya also granted Sāriya, the freedman of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, a place in the alley known as the Goose Quarter. His descendants sold it off in parcels.

ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz granted Khālid ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Hishām Makh­rama’s house in the open space, which had belonged to him, and the house of Mūsā ibn ʿĪsā al-Nawsharī by the stopping place.

He said: Khālid and ʿUmar, the sons of ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Hishām, had been with ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr. Abū Bakr ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān was a brother of ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān and his contemporary. When ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān prevailed, he said, “There is no way to do what ʿUmar and Khālid would dislike while Abū Bakr is here; but I bind myself before God that they shall not live in the Ḥijāz.” He wrote to al-Ḥajjāj to let them choose whichever of the provincial capitals they wished, and go to it. Khālid joined ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān, who granted him Makh­rama’s house in the open space, which had belonged to him, and the house of Mūsā ibn ʿĪsā by the stopping place. As for ʿUmar, he joined Bishr ibn Marwān in Iraq, where he has many traces at Wāsiṭ.

He granted ʿUmāra ibn al-Walīd ibn ʿUqba ibn Abī Muʿayṭ the houses lying south of the people of the straw.

Abū Muʿayṭ was called Abān. Muḥammad ibn Idrīs al-Rāzī related this to me. Ḍirār ibn al-Khaṭṭāb says of him:

Eye, weep for ʿUqba ibn Abān,

Scion of Fihr, horseman among horsemen.

One of the poets says of him:

Whoever delights in rich, still meat,

Let him come to ʿUqba ibn Abān’s bowl.

He said: ʿAbd al-Aʿlā ibn Abī ʿAmra, a freedman of Banū Shaybān, was married to a sister of Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr and held a position with ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz laid out for him his house with the bathhouse called the Straw Bath. When ʿAbd al-Aʿlā ibn Abī ʿAmra returned from the court of Alyūn, the ruler of the Byzantines, he said to ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz:

“You have worn the Muslims out by sending me on your behalf, but I have served well and given good counsel. Grant me four columns from the ruins of Alexandria.” He ordered them given to him; they stand by the basin of his great bathhouse.

ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz used to send him with brocade to Ibn ʿUmar.

Abū al-Aswad related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Mughīra, from ʿAbd al-Aʿlā ibn Abī ʿAmra, that ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān sent him with a thousand dinars to Ibn ʿUmar, who accepted them.

He said: ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān granted ʿUmar ibn ʿAlī al-Fihrī, one of the Banū Muḥārib, his house with the bathhouse that Mūsā ibn ʿĪsā had bought, beside the people of the earthenware vessels. This was because when ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān killed ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd, ʿUmar ibn ʿAlī had served valiantly alongside him and was among his companions. He entered the private chamber where ʿAmr ibn Saʿīd lay slain, and ʿAbd al-Malik consulted them about killing him. They all dreaded killing him and did not consider it right. ʿUmar ibn ʿAlī said, “Kill him—may God kill him.” “He will remain opposed for as long as he lives.”

ʿAbd al-Malik said, “There he is; he has spoken.” He said, “Throw his head to the people and let them plunder the treasury; they will scatter away from you.” He did so, and the people scattered. ʿAbd al-Malik sent him to search ʿAmr’s house. He found there letters bearing the names of those who had pledged allegiance to him, and burned them. When ʿAbd al-Malik heard of this, he said to him, “What made you do that?”

He replied, “Had you read them, no Syrian heart would have remained loyal to you, nor would obedience have held firm, once he knew you had learned of his opposition.” ʿAbd al-Malik approved of his judgment and praised him, and granted him his house with the bathhouse that Mūsā ibn ʿĪsā had bought, beside the people of the earthenware vessels.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama said: “It was a grant from ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz to the Fihrī, but he did not name him.”

However, Ibn ʿUfayr named him. He said: “ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama [said that] ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz granted it to the Fihrī, a freedman of Ibn Rummāna, when he came to him. Yazīd ibn Rummāna built it for him. It is the house known today as Dār al-Silsila.”

The family of Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān Yazīd ibn Anīs deny this, and they know the matter better. They say: “It was a plot belonging to Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Fihrī. He laid it out in the year Egypt was conquered, but built nothing of it except its wall. Then he went to Syria and was martyred there. After that, his sons al-ʿAlāʾ and ʿAlī came. Al-ʿAlāʾ was the elder of the two, and he had seen the Messenger of God ﷺ. They came to Egypt and made that structure like a great, round platform, but set only one dwelling in it. They settled there a freedman of theirs called Yaḥannas. Then al-ʿAlāʾ went to Medina, where he was killed in the year of al-Ḥarra, leaving behind al-Ḥārith ibn al-ʿAlāʾ. ʿAlī went to Syria, where he died, leaving behind ʿUmar ibn ʿAlī, who attained a position with ʿAbd al-Malik.”

He sent for Ibn Rummāna, sent him money, and asked him to build his grandfather’s house as skillfully as he could, to put a bathhouse in it, and to make a small door in the house so that he could enter it whenever he wished. He said, “This was mentioned to you and your elder.” This stirred Ibn Rummāna to action. He built it, making its wall more than two cubits thick by the builder’s cubit. He made it revolve around marble columns and gave it a circular base, but built nothing above it.

Then ʿUmar ibn ʿAlī came to Egypt, after Ibn Rummāna had finished it. ʿUmar said to him, “You did the work well, except that you did not make a mosque for it.”

He built the mosque known today as the Mosque of the Qūrūn. He built it like a large platform, set it apart from the house, and left a space between it and the house. He used to sit in it. After him, Abū ʿAwn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Yazīd built it; then al-Muṭṭalib ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Khuzāʿī enlarged it. Then it burned down, and al-Sarī ibn al-Ḥakam rebuilt it in its present form. Afterward ʿUmar ibn ʿAlī died, and al-Ḥārith ibn al-ʿAlāʾ—his nephew—inherited everything he left. He made the house a charitable endowment for the nearest male descendants of al-Ḥārith ibn al-ʿAlāʾ, generation after generation, excluding women, with each generation taking precedence over those below it. “When the men are extinct, it is for the women, all those who trace their descent from him in the direct line”. When the women are extinct, the house, its bathhouse, and its mound known as Abū Qashāsh are to be divided into thirds: one third for the cause of God, one third for the poor and needy, and one third for his freedmen, his children’s freedmen, and their descendants, generation after generation, after its upkeep and the provision of a steward’s stipend if it has one. When the freedmen are extinct and none of them remain, it is for the poor and needy in Fusṭāṭ Egypt and the City of the Messenger ﷺ as its administrator sees fit for its maintenance.

Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān’s name was Yazīd ibn Anīs ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥabīb ibn ʿAmr ibn Shaybān ibn Muḥārib ibn Fihr. ʿAmr ibn Ḥabīb was the Eater of the Foal. His mother was al-Sawdāʾ, daughter of Zuhra ibn Kilāb. The poet says of him:

The sons of the Eater of the Foal, who are like

Stars shining in the horizons of the sky.

By Dār al-Silsila there was a marble basin. I do not know whether it was this house or another.

On the feast days it would be filled with wine, vessels would be set upon it, and people would drink. Matters remained so until ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz became governor and stopped it.

In Fusṭāṭ, besides the Fihrī house, there is more than one house called Dār al-Silsila: the al-Sahmī house in the shoemakers’ quarter, and the house in which Aṣbagh the jurist lived, in the Alley of the Lanterns.

He said: ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān built the markets: the honey market, the rope market, and the rams’ market, which is in the district of a group from Baliyy called al-Waḥāwiḥa; and the market where brocade is sold, known as the Market of ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz. He incorporated into it parts of the district of al-Rāya. In it was the house of Kaʿb ibn ʿAdī al-ʿIbādī, for which he compensated him with his house among Banū Wāʾil.

He said: Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik built his market, known as the Market of Hishām, where Fusṭāṭ brocade was sold, in the open space between the palace and the sea. After that, some open space remained between Banū Wāʾil and the sea, and the Banū al-ʿAbbās granted it to people.

When ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ became governor, he granted his freedman Wardān the land behind the bridge, from its western side, where Abū Ḥumayd stood, as far as the church of the Byzantines there. All the land from your right, starting at the head of the old bridge as far as the Ram’s Bath—that is the bath known today as the Market Bath—and the other stretch as far as the shore of Marīs, all belonged to al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik. Al-Walīd also owned what lay to your left as you left for al-Jīza, and the shops adjoining the island of the shipyard.

ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵁ had granted Ibn Sandar the estate of al-Aṣbagh. Ibn Sandar took a thousand faddāns for himself from it, as Yaḥyā ibn Khālid related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd. We have no report that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb granted any of the people anything from the land of Egypt except Ibn Sandar: he granted him the land of the estate of al-Aṣbagh. It remained his until he died; then al-Aṣbagh ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz bought it from his heirs. In Egypt there is no grant older or better than this.

The reason ʿUmar made this grant, as ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa (from ʿAmr ibn Shuʿayb, from his father, from his grandfather), was that Zunbāʿ al-Judhāmī had a slave called Sandar. He found him kissing one of his slave girls, so he castrated him and cut off his ears and nose. Sandar went to the Messenger of God ﷺ who sent for Zunbāʿ and said, “Do not burden them with what they cannot bear. Feed them what you eat, and clothe them with what you wear. If you are content, keep them; if you dislike them, sell them. Do not torment God’s creation. Whoever mutilates someone or burns him with fire is free, and is a client of God and His Messenger.” Sandar was set free. He said, “Messenger of God, give me your recommendation.” He said, “I recommend you to every Muslim.” When the Messenger of God ﷺ died, Sandar came to Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq ﵁ and said, “Keep the Messenger of God’s recommendation concerning me.” Abū Bakr supported him until he died. Then Sandar went to ʿUmar and said, “Keep the Prophet’s recommendation concerning me.” He said, “Yes. If you are content to stay with me, I will provide for you what Abū Bakr used to provide. Otherwise, tell me where you would like me to assign you a place.” Sandar said, “Egypt, for it is a land of fertile countryside.” So ʿUmar wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ: “Keep the Messenger of God’s recommendation concerning him.” When Sandar came to ʿAmr, he assigned him a broad tract of land and a house. Sandar lived there, and when he died, it was taken into the public treasury.*”

ʿAmr ibn Shuʿayb said: “Then ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān granted it to al-Aṣbagh. It is among the best of their properties.”

Ibn Wahb related from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from Rabīʿa ibn Laqīṭ al-Tujībī, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Sandar, from his father, that he had been a slave of Zunbāʿ ibn Salāma al-Judhāmī. Zunbāʿ took offense at him, castrated him, and mutilated him. Sandar went to the Messenger of God ﷺ and told him. He spoke harshly to Zunbāʿ and freed Sandar from him. Sandar said:

“Messenger of God, give me your recommendation.” He said, “I recommend you to every Muslim.” Yazīd said: “Sandar was an unbeliever.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that a slave of Zunbāʿ al-Judhāmī accused him, and Zunbāʿ ordered that he be castrated and that his nose and ears be cut off. The slave went to the Messenger of God ﷺ who freed him and said, “Any slave who is mutilated is free and is a client of God and His Messenger.” He stayed in Medina with the Messenger of God ﷺ who treated him kindly. When the Messenger of God ﷺ became gravely ill, Ibn Sandar said to him, “Messenger of God, as you can see, what will become of us after you?” The Messenger of God ﷺ said, “I recommend you to every believer.”

When Abū Bakr ﵁ took office, he continued his allowance until he died. When ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb took office, Ibn Sandar came to him and said, “Keep the Messenger of God’s recommendation concerning me.” He said to him, “Choose whichever of the Muslim armies you wish to join, and I will order that you be given what is sufficient for you.” Ibn Sandar said, “I will join Egypt.” So ʿUmar wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, ordering him to assign Ibn Sandar enough land for his needs. Ibn Sandar continued to have enough in Egypt.

It is said “Sandar” and “Ibn Sandar”; God knows best which is correct.

The people of Egypt transmit two Prophetic reports from him. This is one; the other is as follows. Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr and ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; both said: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from Abū al-Khayr, from Ibn Sandar, who said that the Messenger of God ﷺ said, “Aslam—may God grant it peace; Ghifār—may God forgive it; and Tujīb—may God answer it and His Messenger”.

In his version of the report, Ibn Bukayr said: “I said, ‘Abū al-Aswad, did you hear the Messenger of God ﷺ mention Tujīb?’ He said, ‘Yes.’ I said, ‘May I relate this from you to the people?’ He said, ‘Yes.’”

ʿAmr’s departure to the countryside

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shurayḥ, from Abū Qabīl, who said: When the people returned [from campaign], they used to gather in Fusṭāṭ. When the countryside’s provisions were at hand, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ addressed the people and said, “The provisions of your countryside have arrived, so go back. When the milk has turned sour, the wood has grown firm, and the flies have multiplied, then come back to your Fusṭāṭ. Let me not hear that any one of you has come back having fattened himself and worn out his horse.” Aḥmad ibn ʿAmr related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who said: ʿAmr used to say to the people when they returned from their campaigns, “Spring has arrived. Whoever among you wants to take his horse out to graze in the spring may do so. Let me not hear that a man has come back having fattened himself and worn out his horse. When the milk has turned sour, the flies have multiplied, and the wood has bent, return to your military settlement.”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ told the people when they returned: “Go out to your rural districts . When the flies begin to buzz, the milk turns sour, and the wood bends, then hasten to your camp.”

The sermon of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ

Saʿīd ibn Maysara related to us, from Isḥāq ibn al-Furāt, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from al-Aswad ibn Mālik al-Ḥimyarī, from Buḥayr ibn Dhākhir al-Maʿāfirī, who said: I went with my father to the Friday prayer early, at the end of winter—I think it was a few days after the Christians’ Ḥamīm.6 We prolonged our bowing when men arrived, carrying whips and driving the people along. I was alarmed and said, “Father, who are these?” He said, “My son, these are the police.” The callers to prayer then established the prayer. ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ stood on the pulpit, and I saw a man of medium height, upright in stature and broad-headed, with deep-black eyes and a broad, bright forehead. He wore embroidered clothes, as though adorned with pure gold; a robe, turban, and tunic gleamed upon him. He praised God and extolled Him briefly, invoked blessings upon the Prophet ﷺ—admonished the people, and commanded and forbade them. I heard him exhort them to pay the alms and maintain ties of kinship, command moderation, and forbid extravagance and having many dependents. He said on that matter:

“O people, beware of four traits: they bring toil after ease, hardship after abundance, and humiliation after honor. Beware of having many dependents, reduced means, squandering wealth, and talk followed by more talk, without achievement or benefit.

“A person must have some free time in which to give his body rest, attend to his affairs, and let himself pursue his desires. Whoever comes to that should be moderate and take the lesser share. Let no one, in his free time, deprive himself of his share of knowledge, lest he return without any good and heedless of what God has made lawful and unlawful.

“O people, al-Jawzāʾ has descended, al-Shiʿrā has grown bright , the rains have ceased, disease has lifted, the dew has diminished, the pastures are good, the pregnant have given birth, and the young animals are growing. A shepherd must take proper care of his flock. So go, with God’s blessing, to your countryside. Take from its produce, milk, lambs, and game. Pasture your horses, fatten them, look after them, and treat them well, for they are your protection from your enemy, and through them you gain your spoils and carry your burdens . Treat well the Copts among those who live beside you. Beware of fragrant substances and sweetened foods, for they corrupt religion and weaken resolve.

“ʿUmar, Commander of the Faithful, related to me that he heard the Messenger of God ﷺ—say: ‘After me, God will open Egypt to you, so treat its Copts well, for you have with them ties of kinship and a covenant .’

“Guard your hands and your private parts, and lower your eyes. Let me never hear that a man has come having fattened his own body and made his horse thin. Know that I inspect horses as I inspect men. If a man makes his horse thin without cause, I will reduce his stipend by that amount. And know that you are on garrison duty until the Day of Resurrection, because of the many enemies around you , whose hearts yearn for you and for your land, the source of crops, wealth, abundant good, and increasing blessing.

“ʿUmar, Commander of the Faithful, also related to me that he heard the Messenger of God ﷺ—say: ‘When God opens Egypt to you, station there a numerous army, for that army is the best of the armies of the earth.’ Abū Bakr said to him, ‘Why, Messenger of God?’ He replied, ‘Because they and their wives will be on garrison duty until the Day of Resurrection.’

“So give thanks to God, people , for what He has bestowed upon you . Enjoy your countryside as long as it pleases you. When the wood dries, the pole grows hot, the flies multiply, the milk turns sour, the greens wither , and the blossoms fall from the trees, then hasten to your camp, with God’s blessing . Let no man with dependents return to them without bringing them a gift, according to what his effort or hardship allows. This is what I say; I entrust you to God’s care.”

He said: I remembered that from him. After we returned home and I had told my father the sermon, he said, “My son, when people return to him, he urges them to garrison duty just as he urged them to the countryside and repose.”*) and its lambs and game. Pasture your horses, fatten them, look after them, and treat them well, for they are your protection from your enemy, and through them you gain your spoils and carry your burdens . Treat well the Copts among those who live beside you. Beware of fragrant substances and sweetened foods, for they corrupt religion and weaken resolve.

ʿUmar, Commander of the Faithful, related to me that he heard the Messenger of God ﷺ—say: “After me, God will open Egypt to you, so treat its Copts well, for you have with them ties of kinship and a covenant .”

Guard your hands and your private parts, and lower your eyes. Let me never hear that a man has come having fattened his own body and made his horse thin. Know that I inspect horses as I inspect men. If a man makes his horse thin without cause, I will reduce his stipend by that amount. And know that you are on garrison duty until the Day of Resurrection, because of the many enemies around you , whose hearts yearn for you and for your land, the source of crops, wealth, abundant good, and increasing blessing.

ʿUmar, Commander of the Faithful, also related to me that he heard the Messenger of God ﷺ—say: “When God opens Egypt to you, station there a numerous army, for that army is the best of the armies of the earth.” Abū Bakr said to him, “Why, Messenger of God?” He replied, “Because they and their wives will be on garrison duty until the Day of Resurrection.” So give thanks to God, people , for what He has bestowed upon you . Enjoy your countryside as long as it pleases you. When the wood dries, the pole grows hot, the flies multiply, the milk turns sour, the greens wither , and the blossoms fall from the trees, then hasten to your camp, with God’s blessing . Let no man with dependents return to them without bringing them a gift, according to what his effort or hardship allows. This is what I say; I entrust you to God’s care.

He said: I remembered that from him. After we returned home and I had told my father the sermon, he said, “My son, when people return to him, he urges them to garrison duty just as he urged them to the countryside and repose.”

Account of the army’s spring pastures

He said: When spring and the milk season came, he would write for each group to go to its spring pasture and milk country wherever it wished. The villages where most of them took up residence were Minūf, Dasbandas , Ahnās, and Ṭalḥā.

The people of the banners were scattered. The families of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd would take up residence in Minf and Wasīm.

Hudhayl would take up residence in Banā and Būṣīr. ʿAdwān would take up residence in Būṣīr. The villages of ʿAkk where most of them took up residence were Būṣīr, Minūf, Dasbandas, and Atrīb. Balī would take up residence in Minf and Ṭarābiya. Fahm would take up residence in Atrīb, ʿAyn Shams, and Minūf. Mahra would take up residence in Tata and Tammī. Ṣadaf would take up residence in al-Fayyūm. Tujīb would take up residence in Tammī, Basṭa, and Wasīm.

Lakhm would take up residence in al-Fayyūm, Ṭarābiya, and Qirbīṭ. Judhām would take up residence in Ṭarābiya and Qirbīṭ.

Ḥaḍramawt would take up residence in Bibā, ʿAyn Shams, and Atrīb. Murād would take up residence in Minf and al-Fayyūm, together with ʿAbs ibn Zūf. Ḥimyar would take up residence in Būṣīr and the villages of Ahnās. Khawlān would take up residence in the villages of Ahnās, al-Bahnasā, and al-Qays. The family of Waʿla would take up residence in Safṭ in Būṣīr.

The family of Abraha would take up residence in Minf. Ghifār and Aslam would take up residence with Wāʾil of Judhām and Saʿd in Basṭa, Qirbīṭ, and Ṭarābiya. The family of Yasār ibn Ḍanna would take up residence in Atrīb. Maʿāfir would take up residence in Atrīb, Sakha, and Minūf. A group of Tujīb and Murād would take up residence in al-Yadqūn.

Some of these tribes would sometimes go beyond the areas of others in spring. This is not known of any group except the bulk of the tribes. They would take up residence in the places we have described ; a spring pasture was assigned to them, and they remained wherever they found milk.

Ghifār and Layth also had a spring pasture at Atrīb.

He said: Mudlij settled at Kharabtā and made it their home. With them were a number of men from Ḥimyar, from Dhabḥān and elsewhere, who had allied themselves with them there; those were their homes.

Khushayn, together with a group of Lakhm and Judhām, returned and settled around Ṣān, Iblīl, and Ṭarābiya; no record of them has been preserved.

Qays was not in the eastern Ḥawf in former times. It was Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb who settled them there. He had gone as a delegate to Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, who assigned him a military levy of five thousand men—or three thousand men; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān was uncertain . Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb assigned the levy to Qays and brought them, settling them in Egypt’s eastern Ḥawf.

Account of the horses of Egypt

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said : When the people had settled and felt secure in their homes, they would go out and train their horses on the racecourse.

Aḥmad ibn ʿAmr related to us: Ibn Wahb related to us, from ʿAmr ibn al-Ḥārith, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shumāsa al-Mahrī, from Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj, that he passed by a man at the racecourse with a horse, holding its reins on a mound. He sent his boy to see who the man was, and it turned out to be Abū Dharr. Ibn Ḥudayj went over to him and said, “Abū Dharr, I see that this horse is troubling you, yet I see nothing here with it.” Abū Dharr said, “This is a horse whose prayer has been answered.” Ibn Ḥudayj said, “What prayer could a beast among beasts offer?” Abū Dharr replied, “There is no horse that does not pray to God at every dawn: ‘O God, You have entrusted me to one of Your servants and placed my provision in his hands. O God, make me dearer to him than his children, his family, and his wealth.’”

My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, and Shuʿayb ibn al-Layth related to us; they said: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from Ibn Shumāsa, that Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj told him that he passed by Abū Dharr, standing beside one of his horses, was asked what he was doing with his horse. He replied, “I think this horse’s prayer has been answered.” Then he mentioned a report like that of Ibn Wahb.

Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Qays ibn al-Ḥajjāj, who said: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj passed by us while we were sitting with Ḥanash ibn ʿAbd Allāh near Ṣafā Mahra. He failed to greet us, so Ḥanash called out to him, “Son of Muʿāwiya, you pass by without greeting us? By God, I remember pleading with your father to have stirrups put on your saddle so that you could put your foot in one.”

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: The sons of Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj had no stirrups on their saddles; they simply leapt onto their horses.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: Some of the stock of horses in Egypt came from horses that Ibn ʿUfayr named. Among them was Ashqar Ṣadaf, which belonged to Abū Nāʿima Mālik ibn Nāʿima al-Ṣadafī. The small gate of Ashqar in Fusṭāṭ, Egypt, was named after it. The reason was that Ashqar died, and its owner disliked throwing it onto the heaps, as the carcasses of beasts are thrown. So he dug a grave for it and buried it there; the place was then named after it.

My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, related to us: When the Muslims conquered the fortress, a Roman used to come from the direction of al-Quṣayr on a grey horse while the Muslims were performing the dawn prayer. He would kill and stab, and the Muslims’ cavalry pursued him but could not catch him. The owner of Ashqar was away. When he returned, he was told about it, and he lay in ambush for the man in a certain place. The foreigner came and did as he used to do. The owner of Ashqar pursued him and caught up with him. He said, “I was occupied with killing the foreigner, and Ashqar charged the hybrid horse and killed it.”

Among them was Dhū al-Rīsh, the horse of al-ʿAwwām ibn Ḥabīb al-Yaḥṣubī; al-Khaṭṭār, the horse of Labīd ibn ʿUqba al-Sūmī; al-Dhaʿlūq, the horse of Ḥumayr ibn Wāʾil al-Sūmī; and ʿAjlā, a horse that belonged to ʿAkk. The poet says of her:

ʿAjlā outstripped the peoples; she outstripped them while pregnant.

ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Isḥāq related to us: Marwān ibn Muʿāwiya related to us, from Abū Ḥayyān al-Taymī, from Abū Zurʿa, from Abū Hurayra, that the Messenger of God ﷺ—called the female horse a faras.

He said: ʿAjlā, the one mentioned by ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj, was brought up in jest to Namir ibn Ayfaʿ al-ʿAkkī, who was asked, “What has become of ʿAjlā?” He replied, “Well, she has two shares in your mother.”

The Lakhm tribe also had a horse called Ablāq Lakhm. Al-Jawn belonged to ʿUqba ibn Kulayb al-Ḥaḍramī.

ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān had sought al-Khaṭṭār from Labīd ibn ʿUqba, but Labīd refused him. So ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz sent him on a campaign to Ifrīqiya, where he died. When Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr was in office, he presented ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān with horses, among them al-Khaṭṭār. He said that the horse was well known and had a long tail. When the horses came to them, they could find no one who knew al-Khaṭṭār. They said, “Labīd’s daughter.” ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz sent the horse to her. She said to the person who brought it, “I am a woman, so leave me until I can look at it.” They did so. She came out, looked at it, and recognized it. She said, “By God, no one shall ride you after my father.” Then she cut off the horse’s ears and docked its tail, and said, “There it is. Take it; may God not bless you in it.” It became the property of ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān, who used it as a breeding stallion. From it came al-Dhāʾid; then from al-Dhāʾid came al-Farqad, the progenitor of the Farqad horses. No horse of Farqad blood among the horses of Egypt ever failed to come in first.

When the people of Egypt learned that Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam had reached al-Qāṣira, they sent ʿUqba ibn Shurayḥ ibn Kulayb al-Maʿāfirī and Muṭayr ibn Yazīd al-Tujībī ahead as scouts. Muṭayr was then riding al-Khaṭṭār, the horse of Labīd ibn ʿUqba al-Sūmī. They entered Marwān’s camp and rode around in it. Then an old man from the camp noticed them and found their appearance suspicious. He said, “By God, I recognize the look of these two horses, and their riders do not look travel-worn.” They turned back toward Fusṭāṭ and passed a Ṣarṣarāniyyah camel belonging to Bishr ibn Marwān on the edge of the camp. They drove it off. When the cavalry caught up with them, Muṭayr said to ʿUqba, “Drive the camel on; I’ll hold them off for you.” Muṭayr turned and fought them until they turned away from him, then caught up with his companion. The cavalry caught up with them again, and he did the same until they reached Fusṭāṭ. They were asked about the news, and they said, “First slaughter the camel and eat its meat; it is the first spoil.” So the camel was slaughtered and its meat eaten. Then they told them what had happened, and said that they were stronger than a single man.

ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote, as Shuʿayb ibn al-Layth, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ, Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr, and ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ: “Look among those under your authority who pledged allegiance beneath the tree, and bring their stipends up to two hundred. Bring yours up as well, since I appointed you to office; and bring up Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa’s, for his bravery, and ʿUthmān ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ’s, for his hospitality.”

ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb’s Sharing Out of the Officials’ Wealth

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: Then ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb sent Muḥammad ibn Maslama, as Muʿāwiya ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from Muḥammad ibn Samāʿa al-Ramlī, who said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, a reliable elder, related to me from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, and wrote to him: “To proceed: You officials have taken your seats over the sources of wealth, so you have collected unlawful wealth, consumed unlawful wealth, and passed on unlawful wealth. I have sent you Muḥammad ibn Maslama al-Anṣārī to divide your property with you. Present your property to him. Peace.”

When Muḥammad ibn Maslama arrived in Egypt, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ offered him a gift, which he returned. ʿAmr grew angry and said, “Muḥammad, why did you return my gift, when I offered a gift to the Messenger of God ﷺ—upon my return from the expedition of Dhāt al-Salāsil, and he accepted it?” Muḥammad replied, “The Messenger of God ﷺ—accepted what revelation allowed him to accept and declined what it allowed him to decline. Had a brother offered a gift to his brother, I would have accepted it; but this is a gift from a wicked ruler who came after him.” ʿAmr said, “May God curse the day I became governor for ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb! I have seen ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil wearing brocade fastened with gold, while al-Khaṭṭāb ibn Nufayl carried firewood on a donkey in Mecca.” Muḥammad ibn Maslama said to him, “Your father and his father are in the Fire, and ʿUmar is better than you. Had it not been for the day on which you have become a man of reproach, you would have been found holding a she-goat, pleased by her abundant milk and distressed when it failed.” ʿAmr said, “That was an angry man’s outburst; what I said is in your trust.” Then he presented his property to him, and Muḥammad divided it with him and returned.

He said: The reason ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb divided the officials’ wealth was, as Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār and ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from Khālid ibn al-Ṣaʿq, who composed these verses and sent them to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb:

Convey a message to the Commander of the Faithful: You are God’s trustee over wealth and authority.

Do not let the people of the rural districts and the jizya freely consume God’s wealth on abundant leather goods.

Send for al-Nuʿmān and check his accounts; send for Juzʾ, and send for Bishr.

Do not forget either of the two Nāfiʿs, nor your wealthy kinsman by marriage from Banū Ghazwān.

Do not summon me to testify; I am absent, but I see the age’s marvels:

Horses like gazelles, and women white as dolls, and what cannot be forgotten of brocade and curtains;

and a folded rayṭa, kept in its protective covering, and folds of red curtains dyed with safflower;

when the Indian merchant comes with a musk pod, its scent runs through the partings in their hair.

We sell when they sell, and raid when they raid;

How, then, should they have wealth, when we have no abundance?

So divide it among them; may my soul be your ransom, for they

Will be content if you divide half of your wealth among them.

ʿUmar divided their wealth in half. Al-Nuʿmān was al-Nuʿmān ibn Bashīr, who governed Ḥimṣ; and the son-in-law of Banū Ghazwān was Abū Hurayra, who was in charge of al-Baḥrayn.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: It is also said that the speaker of these verses was—as Muʿāwiya ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from Yaḥyā ibn Maʿīn, from Wahb ibn Jarīr, from his father, from al-Zubayr ibn al-Khirrīt—Abū al-Mukhtār al-Numayrī, who said:

Convey a message to the Commander of the Faithful,

For you are God’s trustee on land and sea.

Send for al-Nuʿmān and reckon his account;

Send for Jazʾ, and send for Bashīr.

Do not leave out either of the two beneficiaries,

Nor that man in the market, the client of Banū Badr.

ʿĀṣim is not among them with empty saddlebags,

Nor Ibn Ghallāb, one of the nobles of Banū Naṣr.

We sell when they sell, and raid when they raid;

How, then, should they have wealth, when we have no abundance?

You see the sleek horses like [al-khazzān (uncertain)], and the white steeds like statues,

And countless hangings and curtains,

And a folded mantle in its protective cover,

And layers of red, rounded curtains.

When the Indian merchant brings a musk pod,

Its scent spreads through their partings.

Here is God’s wealth before you; do not leave it,

For they will be content if you divide half of your wealth among them.

Do not summon me to testify, for I

Am absent, yet I see the age’s marvels.

ʿUmar said: “We have excused him from giving testimony, and we shall take half their wealth.” He took half; ʿUmar had appointed these men to office.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Jaʿfar ibn Rabīʿa, from his father, That his grandfather had bequeathed that half his property be given to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb; ʿUmar had appointed him over one of his offices.

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us: Sulaymān ibn Abī Sulaymān related to us, from Muḥammad ibn Sīrīn, who said: Abū Hurayra said: “When I returned from al-Baḥrayn, ʿUmar said to me, ‘Enemy of God and enemy of Islam, you have betrayed the property of God.’ I said, ‘I am not an enemy of God or an enemy of Islam, but I am an enemy of whoever is hostile to them. I have not betrayed the property of God; rather, it is the proceeds from horses of mine that bred and shares that had accrued.’ He said, ‘Enemy of God and enemy of Islam, you have betrayed the property of God.’ I said, ‘I am not an enemy of God or an enemy of Islam, but I am an enemy of whoever is hostile to them. I have not betrayed the property of God; rather, it is the proceeds from horses of mine that bred and shares that had accrued.’ He said that three times: ʿUmar said this, and Abū Hurayra answered him with these words. He said, ‘He made me pay twelve thousand.’ I stood for the dawn prayer and said, ‘O God, forgive the Commander of the Faithful.’ Then he asked me to take up the office again, but I said, ‘No.’ He said:

“‘Was not Joseph better than you, when he asked for the office?’ I said, ‘Joseph was a prophet, son of a prophet, whereas I am the son of Umayma, and I fear three things and two.’ He said, ‘Why not say five?’ I said, ‘No.’ He said, ‘Enough!’ I said, ‘I fear that I might speak without forbearance, judge without knowledge, have my back beaten, my honor reviled, or my property seized.’”

Account of the Nile

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Wāhib ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Maʿāfirī, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, who said: “The Nile of Egypt is the master of rivers. God subjected to it every river between east and west. When God wills the Nile of Egypt to flow, He commands every river to replenish it, and the rivers replenish it with their water. God causes springs to burst forth from the earth for it. When its flow reaches the point God wills, God reveals to every water that it return to its source.

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān asked Kaʿb al-Aḥbār, ‘Do you find any account of this Nile in the Book of God?’ He said, ‘Yes indeed, by Him…’

He split the sea for Moses. I find in the Book of God that God reveals to it twice each year. He reveals to it when it flows: “God commands you to flow,” and it flows as far as God has decreed for it. Then, after that, He reveals to it:

“O Nile, [غر: uncertain] praiseworthy.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yūsuf related to us; ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar related to us, from Ḥabīb ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, from Ḥafṣ ibn ʿĀṣim, from Abū Hurayrah, that the Messenger of God ﷺ—said: “The Nile, Sayḥān, Jayḥān, and the Euphrates are among the rivers of Paradise.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; al-Layth related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from Abū al-Khayr, from Kaʿb al-Aḥbār, who used to say: “Four rivers of Paradise did God place in this world: the Nile is the river of honey in Paradise; the Euphrates is the river of wine in Paradise; Sayḥān is the river of water in Paradise; and Jayḥān is the river of milk in Paradise.”

Saʿīd ibn Abī Maryam related to us; al-Layth ibn Saʿd and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Lahīʿah related to us—both said: Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb related to us, from Abū al-Khayr, from Abū Janādah al-Kinānī, who heard Kaʿb say: “In the Hereafter, the Nile is honey, the most abundant of the rivers God named; the Tigris, in the Hereafter, is milk, the most abundant of the rivers God named; the Euphrates is wine, the most abundant of the rivers God named; and Jayḥān is water, the most abundant of the rivers God named.”

He said: When ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ conquered Egypt, as ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿah, from Qays ibn al-Ḥajjāj, from someone who had related to him: its people came to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ when he entered Būʾūnah, one of the non-Arab months, and said to him: “Commander, this Nile of ours has a custom without which it does not flow.” He asked them, “What is that?” They said: “When twelve nights of this month remain, we take a maiden, a virgin between her parents, secure her parents’ consent, adorn her with the finest jewelry and clothes, and then cast her into this Nile.” ʿAmr said to them: “This has no place in Islam; Islam effaces what came before it.” So Būʾūnah, Abīb, and Masrā passed, and it did not flow, little or much, until they were on the verge of leaving. When ʿAmr saw this, he wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb about it. ʿUmar wrote back to him: “You have acted rightly. If …”)

“Islam effaces what came before it. I have sent you a written slip; when my letter reaches you, cast it into the Nile.”

When the letter reached ʿAmr, he opened the slip and found written on it: “From ʿAbd Allāh ʿUmar, Commander of the Faithful, to the Nile of the people of Egypt. To proceed: If you flow of your own accord, then do not flow; but if it is God, the One, the Overpowering, who makes you flow, then we ask God, the One, the Overpowering, to make you flow.”

ʿAmr cast the slip into the Nile one day before the Day of the Cross. The people of Egypt had prepared to evacuate and leave, for only the Nile could provide for their needs there. When they awoke on the Day of the Cross, God had made it flow sixteen cubits in a single night, and He put an end to that evil custom among the people of Egypt.

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that Moses ﵇—prayed against Pharaoh’s people, and God withheld the Nile from them until they were about to evacuate. They then asked Moses to pray to God, and he prayed to God, hoping that they would believe. When they awoke, God had made it flow sixteen cubits that night. God, by His gracious favor, answered ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb just as He had answered His prophet Moses ﵇.

On the jizya

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: ʿAmr used to send the jizya to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb after withholding what he needed. The obligation of Egypt, as ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, was to dig its canals, maintain its embankments, build its bridges, and cut through its islands: one hundred and twenty thousand men, equipped with axes, mattocks, and tools. They took turns at this work, and did not neglect it in winter or summer.

Then ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote—as ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from al-Qāsim ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Dīnār, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar—that seals should be placed around the necks of the protected people.

[They were to be branded] on their necks with lead, display their zunnārs (waist-belts), shave their forelocks, and ride sideways on saddles [the word translated as “saddles” is uncertain]. The jizya was to be imposed only on those upon whom the razors had passed ; it was not to be imposed on women or children , nor were they to be allowed to imitate the Muslims in their clothing.

Shuʿayb ibn al-Layth told us; my father told us, from Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAnj , that Nāfiʿ related to them. And ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama told us; Ibn Wahb told us; ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿUmar and ʿUmar ibn Muḥammad told me that Nāfiʿ related to them, from Aslam, ʿUmar’s client, who told him that ʿUmar wrote to the commanders of the armies that they must not impose the jizya except on those upon whom the razors had passed.

Their jizya was forty dirhams for those among them who possessed silver, and four dinars for those who possessed gold. They also owed provisions for the Muslims: two mudd of wheat and three qist of oil each month for every person from among the people of Syria and the Jazīra, as well as fat and honey—I do not know how much.

As for those from among the people of Egypt, they owed one irdabb each month for every person. I do not know how much fat and honey; they also owed textiles and clothing of the kind with which the Commander of the Faithful clothed the people. They were to host any Muslims who came to stay with them for three nights .

The people of Iraq owed fifteen ṣāʿ for each person. I do not know how much fat they owed.

He did not impose the jizya on women and children, and he used to place a seal around the necks of the men liable for the jizya.

He said: “The wība of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, as ʿAbd al-Malik related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, during the governorship of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, was six mudd measures.”

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us; he said: Sufyān ibn ʿUyayna related to us, from Abū Isḥāq, from Ḥāritha ibn Muḍarrib, that ʿUmar said: “I have imposed upon the people of al-Sawād hospitality for a day and a night. Whoever is prevented by rain must spend from his own wealth.”

He said: When the matter had been firmly established for ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, he left its Copts to collect taxes according to the Roman system. Their tax assessment was adjusted according to circumstances: when a village prospered and its population grew, the tax imposed upon it was increased; when its population dwindled and it fell into ruin, the tax was reduced. The village headmen, its mārūṭa [a local official; term uncertain], and the chiefs of its people would gather and discuss prosperity and ruin. Once they had agreed on the increased assessment, they would take that allotment to the districts. Then they would gather with the village chiefs and distribute it according to the villages’ capacity and the extent of their farms. Each village would then return with its allotment; they would collect their allotments and the tax of each village, together with all the cultivated land in it, and then [the word is uncertain] set aside from the land faddāns for their churches, baths, and ferry crossings, out of the land as a whole. Then they would set aside the hospitality allotment for the Muslims and the lodging of the ruler. When they had finished, they would consider the craftsmen and laborers in each village and apportion the tax among them according to their capacity. If there were a jāliya among them, they apportioned it among them according to their capacity. Rarely was it anyone other than a man who came and went or was married. Then they would consider the tax that remained and divide it among them according to the amount of land. They would then divide it among those who wished to cultivate, according to their means. If anyone was unable to cultivate his land and complained of being too weak to do so, they would distribute what he could not manage among those able to bear it. If anyone among them wanted an increase, he would be given what the weak among them could not manage. If they disputed over it, they divided it according to their number. Their allotments were reckoned in qīrāṭs: the dīnār was four and twenty qīrāṭs; they divided the land accordingly. The same was related from the Prophet ﷺ: “You will indeed conquer a land in which the qīrāṭ is mentioned, so treat its people well”. He imposed upon them, for each faddān, half an ardabb of wheat and two wībahs of barley, except for the qīrāṭ, on which no levy was imposed; the wība in those days was six mudd-s .

ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, as ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah reported to us, from Ibn Wahb, from Yūnus, from Ibn Shihāb, used to take from the treaty people with whom he had made terms what they had stipulated for themselves; he would neither reduce any of it nor increase it. As for those among them who accepted the jizya without stipulating any amount to pay, ʿUmar would consider their circumstances: when they were in need, he would ease their burden; and when they were prosperous, he would increase it in proportion to their prosperity.

He said: Ḥaywah ibn Shurayḥ related, saying: al-Ḥasan ibn Thawbān told me that Hishām ibn Abī Ruqayyah al-Lakhmī told him that the chief of Ikhnā came to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ and said to him, “Tell us what each of us owes in jizya, so that he may bear it.” ʿAmr said, pointing toward a pillar of a church, “If you gave me everything from the ground to the ceiling, I would not tell you what you owe. You are merely a treasury for us: if our burden increases, we increase yours; if it is eased for us, we ease yours.” Whoever adopts this report holds that Egypt was conquered by force.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah reported to us; Ibn Lahīʿah reported to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who said: ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said, “Any protected non-Muslim who embraces Islam—his Islam secures his person and property for him; as for any land he holds, it belongs to God’s fayʾ, for the Muslims.”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah reported to us; al-Layth ibn Saʿd reported to us that ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said:

“Any people who make a settlement to pay a jizya: whoever among them embraces Islam, his land and house belong to the rest of them.”

Al-Layth said: Yaḥyā ibn Saʿīd wrote to me that whatever the Copts sold in payment of their jizya, and whatever they are made to pay of the due incumbent upon them, whether a male slave, a slave-girl, a camel, a cow, or a riding beast, is valid as payment from them.

It is lawful for whoever purchases it from them, and it is not to be returned to them if they become solvent. Whatever land they lease out, its lease is lawful, unless it harms the jizya incumbent upon them. Perhaps the land should then be restored to them if it harms their ability to pay the jizya. But if there is a surplus after the jizya, we consider it lawful for whoever leases it from them to do so.

Yaḥyā said: We hold that there are two kinds of jizya: a jizya levied on the persons of men, and a lump sum levied on the people of a village and collected from them. If someone from a village upon which a specified jizya is levied as a village—not on the persons of its men—dies, we hold that if the deceased has neither a child nor an heir, his land reverts to his village, as part of the jizya collectively incumbent upon them. But if someone dies whose jizya was levied on the persons of men and leaves no heir, his land belongs to the Muslims.

Al-Layth said: ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said: “The jizya is levied on persons, not on lands”—meaning the protected non-Muslims.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama told us; Ibn Lahiʿa told us, from ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Janāda, that ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz wrote to Ḥayyān ibn Surayj, instructing him to impose the jizya of the deceased Copts upon those of them who remained alive.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: This report of ʿAbd al-Malik indicates that ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz held that Egypt had been conquered by force, and that the jizya was imposed only on the villages. Thus, when someone among the people of the villages died, that jizya remained incumbent upon them, and the death of whoever among them died did not reduce their jizya at all.

He said: It is possible that Egypt was conquered under a treaty, in which case that treaty remained binding upon those of them who survived, and the death of whoever among them died did not reduce anything of what they had agreed to. God knows best.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama told us; Ibn Wahb told us, from Muḥammad ibn ʿAmr, from Ibn Jurayj, that a man embraced Islam in the time of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb and said, “Remove the jizya from my land.” ʿUmar replied, “No. Your land was conquered by force.”

ʿAbd al-Malik said: Mālik ibn Anas said: “Whatever the people under treaty sell of their land is lawful.”

As for them, whatever was conquered by force, none of them may buy it, nor is it permissible for them to sell anything of the land in their possession. For those who had made a treaty, whoever among them became Muslim had the stronger claim to his land and property. But as for those conquered by force, whoever among them became Muslim thereby secured himself, while his land belonged to the Muslims, since the people conquered by force had been overcome in their lands, which thus became spoils for the Muslims. The people who made a treaty, by contrast, were people who held out and defended their lands until they made a treaty concerning them; they owe nothing beyond what they made the treaty to pay. I do not think that anything should be added to what they owe, or taken from them beyond what ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb imposed, for ʿUmar addressed the people and said:

“I have prescribed the obligations for you, established the practices for you, and left you upon the clear path.”

He said: As for the land tax, I have no knowledge of it, nor do I know what ʿUmar did concerning it. He did, however, leave the land in place and did not divide it among the people who had conquered it. If this matter arose for anyone, I would consider it right to ask the people of the region who possess knowledge and integrity how the matter stood. If he found knowledge that gave a satisfactory answer, then well and good; otherwise, he and the Muslims present with him should exercise their judgment.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us that ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz remitted the poll tax from those among the protected people of Egypt who became Muslim, and enrolled in the register those of them who had made a treaty and became Muslim, among the clans of those through whom they had become Muslim.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: Someone other than ʿAbd al-Malik said: Before that, it had been collected from those who became Muslim. The first to collect the poll tax from protected people who became Muslim was, as ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Razīn ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Murādī, al-Ḥajjāj ibn Yūsuf. Then ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān wrote to ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān, instructing him to impose the poll tax on protected people who became Muslim. Ibn Ḥujayra spoke to him about this and said: “I seek God’s protection for you, O Commander, from being the first to establish this practice in Egypt. By God, the protected people bear the poll tax of those among them who become monks; how, then, can you impose it on those among them who have become Muslim?” So he left them as they were.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz wrote to Ḥayyān ibn Surayj, instructing him to remit the poll tax from those among the protected people who became Muslim. For God ﵎ said: “But if they repent, establish prayer, and give alms, then let them go their way. Indeed, God is forgiving, merciful.”

And He said: “Fight those who do not believe in God or the Last Day, do not forbid what God and His Messenger have forbidden, and do not follow the true religion—among those who were given the Scripture—until they pay the poll tax willingly, while humbled.”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us and said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd had Christian clients and freed them, but they remained liable for the land tax. Al-Layth said: We encountered some of them, and they were still paying the land tax.

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; they said: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us and said:

When Ibn Rifāʿa was appointed governor of Egypt, he set out to count its inhabitants and determine the assessment of the land tax upon them. He spent six months at this task in Upper Egypt, as far as Aswān, accompanied by a group of assistants and scribes who diligently and energetically took care of the work for him; he spent three months in Lower Egypt. They counted more than ten thousand villages. In the smallest of them, they counted no fewer than five hundred male heads liable for the poll tax.

Mention of al-Muqaṭṭam

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us and said: Al-Muqawqis asked ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ to sell him the slope of al-Muqaṭṭam for seventy thousand dinars. ʿAmr was astonished by this and said:

“I shall write about this to the Commander of the Faithful.”

He wrote to ʿUmar about it, and ʿUmar wrote back: “Ask him why he has offered you what he has, when it cannot be cultivated, no water can be drawn from it, and it is of no use.” So he asked him.

He said: “We find its description in the books: that the Garden has its plantings there.” He wrote this to ʿUmar, who wrote back: “We know of no planting of the Garden except for the believers. Bury there those Muslims who die before you, and do not sell it for anything.” The first person buried there was a man of the Maʿāfir, named ʿĀmir; it was said, “It has been made flourishing.”

Al-Muqawqis said to ʿAmr, as ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from Ibn Wahb, from ʿUmāra ibn ʿĪsā: “That is not what you promised us, nor is this what you agreed with us.” So he set for them the boundary that lay between the cemetery and them.

Hānīʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, that al-Muqawqis said to ʿAmr: “We find in our book that between this mountain and the place where you have settled, trees of the Garden will grow.” He wrote his statement to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, who said: “He speaks truly; make it a cemetery for the Muslims.”

Someone other than ʿUmāra ibn ʿĪsā said that those of the Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ who were known were buried there, as ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from someone who told him: five men—ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ al-Sahmī, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ḥudhāfa al-Sahmī, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Juzʾ al-Zubaydī, Abū Baṣra al-Ghifārī, and ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir al-Juhanī.

Someone other than ʿUthmān said: “And Maslama ibn Makhlad al-Anṣārī.” Ibn Lahīʿa said: “Al-Muqaṭṭam extends from al-Quṣayr to the stone quarry; beyond that is al-Yaḥmūm.”

There are differing accounts concerning al-Quṣayr. ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ informed us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, who said: “It is not the Quṣayr of the prophet Moses ﵇ but rather the Quṣayr of the sorcerer Moses.”

Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr and ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbbād related to us; they said: al-Mufaḍḍal ibn Faḍāla related to us, from his father, who said: We entered upon Kaʿb al-Aḥbār, and he said to us: “Where are you from?” We said: “From Egypt.” He said: “What do you say about al-Quṣayr?” We said: “The Quṣayr of Moses.” He said: “It is not the Quṣayr of Moses, but the Quṣayr of the Mighty One of Egypt. When the Nile rose, he would take refuge there. Moreover, it is sacred from the mountain to the sea.”

He said: It is also said that it was a beacon where a fire was lit for Pharaoh when he rode from Memphis to Heliopolis. There was another beacon on al-Muqaṭṭam; when they saw the fire, they knew he was riding and prepared for him what he wanted. The same was done when he rode back from Heliopolis. God knows best.

Hānīʾ ibn al-Mutawakkil related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa and Rashdīn ibn Saʿd, from al-Ḥasan ibn Thawbān, from Ḥusayn ibn Shafiyy al-Aṣbaḥī, from his father Shafiyy ibn ʿUbayd: When he arrived in Egypt—the people of Egypt had established a place of prayer opposite Abū ʿAwn’s waterwheel, which is by the encampment—he said: “Why have they placed their place of prayer on the accursed mountain and left the sacred mountain?” Al-Ḥasan ibn Thawbān said: So they moved their place of prayer to the site where it stands today.

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us: Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Abū Qabīl, that a man asked Kaʿb about the mountain of Egypt. He said: “It is sacred from al-Quṣayr to al-Yaḥmūm.”

Mention of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb’s impatience with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ over the land tax

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: When ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb became impatient with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ over the land tax, as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, he wrote to him:

“In the name of God, Most Gracious, Most Merciful. From ʿAbd Allāh ʿUmar, Commander of the Faithful, to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ. Peace be upon you. I praise God to you, other than whom there is no god. To proceed: By my life, ʿAmr, your land is vast, broad, and fertile. God has granted its people numbers, endurance, and strength, on land and sea. The Pharaohs cultivated it and carried out well-executed works there, despite their great arrogance With God’s leave, I have a remedy that will cure what I ask you about. Do not be distressed, Abū ʿAbd Allāh, that the due is taken from you and given to you; for pressure brings forth pearls, the truth is clear, and leave to me what you have been mumbling about, for the matter has long been laid bare. Peace.”

He said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ wrote back to him:

“In the name of God, Most Gracious, Most Merciful. To ʿAbd Allāh ʿUmar, Commander of the Faithful, from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ. Peace be upon you. I praise God to you, other than whom there is no god. To proceed: The Commander of the Faithful’s letter has reached me concerning the delay with which I have sent the land tax, and his remarks about the Pharaohs’ works before me and his astonishment at the land tax they collected, and at how it has diminished since Islam came. By my life, the land tax was greater and more abundant then, and the land was more fully cultivated, because, despite their unbelief and arrogance, they were more eager to cultivate their land than we have been since Islam came. You said that pressure brings forth pearls. I milked it so thoroughly that its milk dried up. You wrote at length, reproached me, insinuated, and censured; I knew that there was something behind this which you were concealing from one who had no information. So, by my life, you came out with dreadful and cutting accusations. Yet your words on the matter included sound, decisive, eloquent, and truthful points. We served the Messenger of God ﷺ and those who came after him. By God’s praise, we fulfilled our trusts and safeguarded what God had magnified of the rights of our leaders. We considered anything else reprehensible and its practice evil; this is acknowledged of us, and our account of ourselves is believed.

God forbid such ill-gotten gains and such vile traits, and the audacity to commit every sin. Take back your appointment; for God has kept me clear of those low gains and any desire for them, as shown by your letter, in which you spared no one’s honor and showed no respect to a brother. By God, son of al-Khaṭṭāb, when such a thing is demanded of me, I am fiercest in anger on my own behalf, and most protective and respectful of myself. I have done no deed for which I see any hold over me; rather, I have preserved what you failed to preserve. Were I one of the Jews of Yathrib, you could have asked no more—may God forgive you and us. I kept silent about things I knew, though my tongue was ready to speak of them. But God has magnified your rights in a way that cannot be ignored. Peace.”

ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote back to him, as I found in a book that Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr gave me, from ʿUbayd Allāh ibn Abī Jaʿfar, from Abū Marzūq al-Tujībī, from Abū Qays, the client of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ:

“From ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ. Peace be upon you. I praise God to you, other than whom there is no god. To proceed: I am astonished at how often I have written to you about your delay in sending the land tax, and at your writing to me with little evasions. You know that I will accept nothing from you but the manifest truth. I did not appoint you to Egypt to make it a source of provision for you or your people. Rather, I sent you there in the hope that you would increase the land tax and govern well. When this letter of mine reaches you, send the land tax, for it is the spoils of the Muslims, and I have here people you know, kept under watch. Peace.”

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ wrote back to him:

“In the name of God, Most Gracious, Most Merciful. To ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ. Peace be upon you. I praise God to you, other than whom there is no god. To proceed: The Commander of the Faithful’s letter has reached me, expressing impatience with me over the land tax, and claiming that I am stubborn against the truth and turning from the right path. By God, I have no wish to abandon what you know to be right. But the people of the land asked me to give them time until their harvest came in, and I considered the Muslims’ interests. Treating them gently was better than pressing them harshly, which would drive them to sell what they cannot do without. Peace.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, that ʿAmr collected twelve million.

Someone other than al-Layth said: The year before, al-Muqawqis had collected twenty million. It was then that ʿUmar wrote to him the letter he wrote.

Al-Layth said: When ʿUthmān appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd over Egypt, he collected fourteen million. ʿUthmān said to ʿAmr: “Abū ʿAbd Allāh, the milch camel has yielded more than it did before.” ʿAmr said:

“You have harmed her young.”

Someone other than al-Layth said: ʿAmr said to him: “That is, if the foal does not die.”

Hishām ibn Isḥāq al-ʿĀmirī related to us and said: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, instructing him to ask al-Muqawqis about Egypt: “What brings about its prosperity and its ruin?” ʿAmr asked him, and al-Muqawqis said: “Its prosperity and ruin come from five things: that its land tax be collected in a single season, when its people have finished sowing their crops; that its land tax be levied in a single season, when its people have finished pressing their grapes; that its canals be dug each year; that its irrigation channels and embankments be repaired; and that its people not be allowed to suffer injustice”—meaning oppression. “If these things are done there, it will prosper; if things are done there contrary to this, it will be ruined.”

He said: In the book that Ibn Bukayr gave me, from Ibn Zayd ibn Aslam, from his father, it says:

When ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb became impatient with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ over the land tax, he wrote to him: “Send me a man from among the people of Egypt.” He sent him an old man from among the Copts. ʿUmar asked him about Egypt and its land tax before Islam. He said: “Commander of the Faithful, nothing was taken from it until after it had been cultivated, whereas your governor does not attend to cultivation; he takes only what is visible to him, as though he wants it for one year only.” ʿUmar understood what he said and accepted what ʿAmr had been offering as his excuse.

Mention of the prohibition against the troops farming

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: Then ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, as ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from Ibn Wahb, from Ḥaywa ibn Shurayḥ, from Bakr ibn ʿAmr, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Hubayra, ordered his herald to go out to the commanders of the armies, instructing them to direct the subjects that their stipends were being paid and their dependents’ provisions were flowing, so they should neither farm nor hire others to farm. Ibn Wahb said: Sharīk ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Murādī informed me: We were told that Sharīk ibn Sumayy al-Ghuṭayfī came to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ and said: “You do not give us enough to sustain us. Will you permit me to farm?” ʿAmr said to him: “I cannot do that.” Sharīk farmed without ʿAmr’s permission. When ʿAmr heard of it, he wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, informing him that Sharīk ibn Sumayy al-Ghuṭayfī had plowed land in Egypt. ʿUmar wrote back: “Send him to me.” When ʿUmar’s letter reached ʿAmr, he read it to Sharīk. Sharīk said to ʿAmr: “You have killed me, ʿAmr.” ʿAmr said: “I have not killed you; you did this to yourself.” He said to him: “Since this is your view, permit me to go to him without a letter, and I pledge to you by God that I will place my hand in his.” He permitted him to go. When he stood before ʿUmar, he said: “Will you grant me safety, Commander of the Faithful?” He said: “Which army are you from?” He replied: “From the army of Egypt.” ʿUmar said: “Perhaps you are Sharīk ibn Sumayy al-Ghuṭayfī?” He replied: “Yes, Commander of the Faithful.” ʿUmar said: “I shall make you a warning to those who come after you.” He said: “Or will you accept from me what God has accepted from His servants?” ʿUmar said: “And will you do so?” He replied: “Yes.” So ʿUmar wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ: “Sharīk ibn Sumayy came to me in repentance, and I accepted it from him.”

Account of the Digging of the Canal of the Commander of the Faithful

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ—or someone else—related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, that the people of Medina were struck by severe hardship during the caliphate of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, in the Year of the Ashes. He wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, who was in Egypt: “From ʿAbd Allāh ʿUmar, Commander of the Faithful, to al-ʿĀṣ ibn al-ʿĀṣ: Peace. To proceed: By my life, ʿAmr, you care nothing, so long as you and those with you are sated, that I and those with me should perish. So, succor! And then, succor!” He repeated these words.

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ wrote back to him: “To ʿAbd Allāh ʿUmar, Commander of the Faithful, from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ. To proceed: At your service, and then again, at your service! I have sent you a caravan of camels, the first of which is with you and the last of which is with me. Peace be upon you, and the mercy of God.”

He sent him a vast caravan of camels. Its first camels were in Medina and its last in Egypt, following one another. When it arrived to ʿUmar, he used it to provide generously for the people, giving each household in Medina and its environs a camel with its load of food. He sent ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf, al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām, and Saʿd ibn Abī Waqqāṣ to distribute them among the people. They gave each household a camel with its load of food, so that they might eat the food, slaughter the camel and eat its meat, use its fat as a condiment, make sandals from its hide, and put to whatever use they wished the container in which the food had been carried, as bedding or otherwise. By this means God provided generously for the people.

When ʿUmar saw this, he praised God and wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, summoning him to come with a group of Egyptians. They came to him, and ʿUmar said: “ʿAmr, God has opened Egypt to the Muslims. It is abundant in good things and food. It has occurred to me—for I wish to show kindness to the people of the Two Sanctuaries and provide generously for them, now that God has opened Egypt to them and made it a source of strength for them and for all the Muslims—that I should dig a canal from its Nile so that it may flow into the sea. This would make it easier to transport food to Medina and Mecca, for carrying it overland is arduous, and we cannot bring as much of it as we need. Go, then, you and your companions, and consult one another about this until you reach a sound judgment.”

ʿAmr went and told the Egyptians who were with him about this. It weighed heavily upon them, and they said:⟦uncertain: passage continues beyond this page⟧ “We fear that harm may come to Egypt from this. We think you should make much of the matter to the Commander of the Faithful and tell him: ‘This is a matter that is neither feasible nor possible, and we can find no way to accomplish it.’

ʿAmr returned to ʿUmar with this reply. When ʿUmar saw him, he laughed and said: “By Him in whose hand is my soul, ʿAmr, it is as though I can see you and your companions when you told them what we had ordered concerning the digging of the canal. It weighed heavily on them, and they said: ‘This will bring harm upon the people of Egypt. We think you should make much of the matter to the Commander of the Faithful and tell him: “This matter is neither feasible nor possible, and we can find no way to accomplish it.”’”

ʿAmr marveled at ʿUmar’s words and said: “By God, you have spoken the truth, Commander of the Faithful! It happened just as you described.” ʿUmar said to him: “Go, ʿAmr, with my firm command, and press on with this undertaking. Before a year has passed, you must have finished it, God willing.” ʿAmr departed and gathered enough laborers for the task to accomplish what he wanted. Then he dug the canal along the edge of al-Fusṭāṭ, which is called the Canal of the Commander of the Faithful, and led it from the Nile to al-Qulzum. Before a year had passed, ships were sailing on it, carrying whatever food he wished to al-Madīna and Mecca. By this, God benefited the people of the Two Sanctuaries, and it was named the Canal of the Commander of the Faithful.

Food continued to be carried along it until after ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz. Then the governors neglected it; it was abandoned and the sand overwhelmed it, so it was cut off. Its farthest point came to be at the tip of the crocodile, in the direction of Ṭaḥā al-Qulzum.
