He said: It is also said that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb said to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ when he came to him, as my brother ʿAbd al-Ḥakam ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam related to us: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Wahb related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān—he said: “I think it was from ʿUrwa”—that ʿUmar said: “ʿAmr, the Arabs have come to regard me as an ill omen, and they have nearly perished at my hands. I know what has befallen them. Of all the armies, yours is the one through which I most hope God will bring relief to the people of the Ḥijāz. If you can devise a way to help them, so that God may bring them relief!” ʿAmr said: “Whatever you wish, Commander of the Faithful. You know that before Islam, ships carrying merchants from the people of Egypt used to come to us. But when…”

“When we conquered Egypt, that canal was cut off and silted up, and the merchants abandoned it. If you wish, we can dig it out and set boats upon it to carry food to the Ḥijāz; I will do so!” ʿUmar said to him, “Yes, do so!” When ʿAmr left ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, he mentioned this to the chiefs of the people of his land among the Copts of Egypt. They said to him, “What have you brought upon us—may God set the amīr right? You mean to go and take the food and fertility of your land to the Ḥijāz, and ruin this land! If you can, consider that too onerous.”

When ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb was bidding him farewell, he said to him, “ʿAmr, look to that canal; do not forget to dig it.” He replied, “Commander of the Faithful, it has become blocked, and digging it will entail great expenses.” ʿUmar said to him, “By Him in whose hand is my soul, I think that when you left me, you spoke of this to the people of your land. They made it seem a great burden to you and disliked it. I charge you: you must dig it and put boats upon it.” ʿAmr said, “Commander of the Faithful, if the people of the Ḥijāz can obtain Egypt’s food and abundance alongside the well-being of the Ḥijāz, they will not hasten forth to jihad.” He said, “Then I shall make it so that nothing is carried on this sea except the provision of the people of Medina and the people of Mecca.” ʿAmr dug the canal, worked on it, and put boats upon it.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: It is also said that, as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, from Hishām ibn Saʿd, from Zayd ibn Aslam, from his father, ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ: “To al-ʿĀṣ ibn al-ʿĀṣ: By my life, you do not care, when you and those with you grow fat, that I and those under my authority grow emaciated. Help! Help!” ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ wrote back to him: “As for what follows: Here I am, and again, here I am! A caravan has come to you, its first at your place and its last with me, although I …”

“I hope to find a way to bring provisions to you by sea.” Then ʿAmr regretted having written about transporting provisions to Medina by sea, and said [the Arabic reads «وقل», apparently corrupt]: “If I enable ʿUmar to do this, he will ruin Egypt and transfer it to Medina.” So he wrote to him: “I have considered the matter of the sea, and found it difficult, impossible to put in order, and beyond what can be managed.” ʿUmar wrote back to him: “To al-ʿĀṣ ibn al-ʿĀṣ: Your letter has reached me, in which you make excuses about the matter of the sea that you had written to me about. By God, you will do it, or I shall pull you by your ear, or send someone who will do it.” ʿAmr knew that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb was in earnest, so he did it. ʿUmar then sent him word: “Do not leave in Egypt any of its food, clothing, onions, lentils, or vinegar without sending us some of it.”

He said: It is also said that a man from the Copts of Egypt directed ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ to the canal. My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, narrated to us; Sufyān ibn ʿUyaynah narrated to us, from Ibn Abī Najīḥ, from his father, that a man from among the Copts of Egypt came to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ and said: “What do you think: if I show you a place where ships can sail, until they reach Mecca and Medina, will you remit the poll tax from me and my household?” He replied: “Yes.” He wrote to ʿUmar, and ʿUmar wrote back to him: “Do it.” When the ships arrived at al-Jār, ʿUmar set out on pilgrimage or lesser pilgrimage. He said to the people: “Come with us to see the ships that God has sent us from the land of Pharaoh, until they reached us.” A man of Banū Ḍamrah said: “The journey placed me in his company, with seven men. Night overtook us at a Bedouin tent, and there was a pot covered over the fire. ʿUmar asked: ‘Is there any food?’ They said: ‘No, except for a gazelle we caught yesterday.’ They brought it near, and he ate some of it while in iḥrām.”

Asad ibn Mūsā narrated to us; Wakīʿ ibn al-Jarrāḥ narrated to us, from Hishām ibn Saʿd, from Zayd ibn Aslam, from ʿAmr ibn Saʿd al-Jārī, that ʿUmar came to al-Jār, then called for towels and said: “Bathe with seawater, for it is blessed.”

Someone other than Asad said: When the ships arrived at al-Jār carrying food, ʿUmar issued the people certificates for that food, and the merchants traded the certificates among themselves before taking possession of it.

He said: My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, told me; Ibn Lahīʿah informed us, from Abū al-Aswad, from ʿUrwah ibn al-Zubayr, who said: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb met al-ʿAlāʾ ibn al-Aswad and asked: “How much…”

Ḥakīm ibn Ḥizām’s profit? He said: “I bought vouchers from al-Jār for one hundred thousand dirhams and made a profit of one hundred thousand on them.” ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb met him and said: “Ḥakīm, how much did you profit?” He told him the same as al-ʿAlāʾ had told him. ʿUmar said: “Did you sell them before taking possession of them?” He replied: “Yes.” ʿUmar said: “This is an invalid sale. Return them.” Ḥakīm said: “I did not know this was invalid, and I am unable to return them.” ʿUmar said: “There is no way around it.” Ḥakīm said: “By God, I am unable to do that; they have already been dispersed and gone. But my capital and my profit are charity.”

My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, related to us: Mālik ibn Anas related to us, from Nāfiʿ, that Ḥakīm ibn Ḥizām bought food that ʿUmar had ordered for the people. Ḥakīm sold the food before taking full possession of it. ʿUmar heard of this and rescinded the sale against him. He said: “Do not sell food you have bought until you have taken full possession of it.”

Mālik said: “I was told that, in the time of Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam, vouchers for food from al-Jār were issued to the people, and they traded those vouchers among themselves before taking full possession of them. Zayd ibn Thābit and a man among the Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ—went in to Marwān. They said to him: ‘Do you permit usury sales, Marwān?’ He said: ‘I seek refuge in God! What do you mean?’ They said: ‘These vouchers: people trade them among themselves, then sell them before taking full possession of them.’ Marwān sent the guards to pursue them, seize them from the people’s hands, and return them to their owners.”

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us: Mahdī ibn Maymūn related to us: Saʿīd al-Jarīrī related to us, from Abū Naḍrah, from Abū Firās, that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb addressed the people. He praised and glorified God, then said:

“People, a time has passed over me when I thought that whoever recited the Qurʾān did so only seeking God and what is with Him. Toward the end of that time, it seemed to me that some people had recited it seeking this world and seeking people. So seek God through your deeds, and seek Him through your recitation. We used to know you when revelation was descending, when the Messenger of God ﷺ—was among us, and when God was informing us of your affairs. Revelation has now ceased, and the Prophet ﷺ—has departed. Now we know you only by what we say to you: whoever we see doing good, we think well of him and love him for it; and whoever we see doing evil, we think ill of him and hate him for it. Your innermost affairs are between you and your Lord. I send only…”

I send my governors to teach you your religion and teach you your practices; I do not send them to beat your backs or take your property. So whoever suffers any such thing, let him bring it to me. By Him in whose hand is ʿUmar’s soul, I shall exact retaliation from him.

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ stood and said, “What do you think, Commander of the Faithful? If one of your governors takes offense at one of his subjects and disciplines a man among his subjects, will you exact retaliation from him?” He said, “Yes. By Him in whose hand is ʿUmar’s soul, I shall exact retaliation from him. Shall I not exact retaliation, when I saw the Messenger of God ﷺ—allow retaliation against himself? Do not beat the Muslims and so humiliate them; do not withhold their rights and so make them disbelievers; do not keep them too long on campaign and so tempt them; and do not station them in the thickets and so ruin them.”

A man from among the people of Egypt came to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, as we were told from Abū ʿAbdah, from Thābit al-Bunānī and Ḥumayd, from Anas. He said, “Commander of the Faithful, I seek your protection from injustice.” He said, “You have sought refuge with one who will protect you.” The man said, “I raced against the son of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ and beat him. He began striking me with a whip, saying, ‘I am the son of the noblest of men.’” ʿUmar wrote to ʿAmr, ordering him to come to him and to bring his son with him. He came, and ʿUmar said, “Where is the Egyptian? Take the whip and strike him.” The man began striking him with the whip, while ʿUmar said, “Strike the son of the lowliest!” Anas said, “He struck him, and by God, he struck him while we were glad to see him struck. He did not stop until we wished he would hold back. Then ʿUmar said to the Egyptian, ‘Lay it on ʿAmr’s side.’” The man said, “Commander of the Faithful, it was his son who struck me, and I have taken my satisfaction from him.” ʿUmar said to ʿAmr, “Since when have you enslaved people, when their mothers gave birth to them free?” He said, “Commander of the Faithful, I did not know, and he did not come to me.”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to me; al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to me, from Nāfiʿ, the client of Ibn ʿUmar, that Ṣabīgh al-ʿIrāqī began asking about things in the Qurʾān among the Muslim garrisons until he came to Egypt. ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ sent him to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb. When the messenger brought ʿUmar the letter and he read it, he said:

“Where is the man?” He replied, “He is with the baggage.” ʿUmar said, “Look carefully to see whether he has gone, lest punishment from me befall you.” the painful [blows]. He brought him to him, and ʿUmar said to him, “What are you asking about?” He told him. ʿUmar sent for fresh palm fronds and beat him with them until his back was covered with sores. Then he left him until he recovered, and then did the same to him again. Then he left him until he recovered. Then he summoned him to do it again. Ṣubaygh said, “Commander of the Faithful, if you intend to kill me, then kill me cleanly; but if you intend to treat me, then, by God, I have recovered.” He permitted him to return to his homeland and wrote to Abū Mūsā al-Ashʿarī: “Let no one among the Muslims sit with him.” This weighed heavily on the man, so Abū Mūsā wrote to ʿUmar, “His condition has improved.” ʿUmar wrote back, “Permit people to sit with him.”

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us: Muḥammad ibn Khāzim related to us, from al-Ḥajjāj, from ʿAmr ibn Shuʿayb, from his father, from his grandfather, who said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb asking him about a man who became Muslim, then apostatized, then became Muslim, then apostatized, and did this many times: should his Islam be accepted? ʿUmar wrote back to him, “Accept it from him. Invite him to Islam; if he accepts, leave him alone, and if not, strike off his head.”

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us: Muḥammad ibn Khāzim related to us, from al-Ḥajjāj, from ʿAmr ibn Shuʿayb, from his father, from his grandfather, who said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb asking him about a slave who had found a jar of gold buried in the ground. ʿUmar wrote back to him, “Give him a small portion of it, for that is more likely to make them report what they find.”

The Conquest of al-Fayyūm

*(Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr and others related to us. They said: When the conquest had been completed for the Muslims, ʿAmr sent cavalry detachments to the villages around it. Al-Fayyūm remained for a year unknown to the Muslims, until a man came to them and told them of it. ʿAmr sent with him Rabīʿa ibn Ḥubaysh ibn ʿArfaṭah al-Ṣadafī. When they travelled through al-Mujābah, they saw nothing and were about to turn back. They said, “Do not hurry; keep going. If he has lied, you can still do what you intended!” They had gone only a little farther when the dark expanse of al-Fayyūm appeared before them. They fell upon it; there was no fighting there, and its people surrendered.

It is also said that Mālik ibn Nāʿimah al-Ṣadafī—the owner of al-Ashqar—set out on his horse … [the text breaks off mid-word: ل]

He was scouring al-Mujābah, with no knowledge of what lay beyond it of al-Fayyūm. When he saw its dark expanse, he returned to ʿAmr and informed him.

He said: It is also said that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ sent Qays ibn al-Ḥārith to al-Ṣaʿīd. He traveled until he came to al-Qays, where he settled; it was named al-Qays after him. When news of him was delayed from reaching ʿAmr, Rabīʿah ibn Ḥubaysh said, “I shall take care of it.” He mounted his horse and crossed the sea on it—it was a mare—and brought him the news. It is also said that he crossed from the direction of al-Sharqiyyah until he reached al-Fayyūm.* His horse was called al-Aʿmā. God knows best.

He said: And ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ sent Nāfiʿ ibn ʿAbd al-Qays al-Fihrī—Nāfiʿ was the maternal brother of al-ʿĀṣ ibn Wāʾil. Their cavalry entered the land of al-Nūbah on summer expeditions, like the summer expeditions of the Byzantines. Matters continued thus until ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ was dismissed from Egypt and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Sarḥ was appointed; then he made peace with them. I shall relate that in its proper place, God willing.

The Conquest of Barqah

(2 He said: The Berbers were in Palestine, and their king was Jālūt. When David ﵇ killed him, the Berbers set out toward the Maghrib, until they reached Lūbiyah and Marāqiyah—two districts among the districts of western Egypt, which receive their water from the sky and are not reached by the Nile. They dispersed there. Zanātah and Maghīlah went on toward the Maghrib and settled in the mountains. Lawātah went on and settled in the land of Anṭābulus, which is Barqah, and dispersed throughout this Maghrib, spreading across it 2), until they reached al-Sūs. Hawwārah settled in the city of Labdah, and Nafūsah settled in the city of Sibrat. The Byzantines who had been there departed on account of this, while the Afāriq—who had been servants of the Byzantines—remained under a peace agreement, paying tribute to whoever gained control of their lands.

(3 ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ marched with the cavalry until he arrived at Barqah. He made peace with its people for thirteen thousand dinars, which they would pay him as jizyah, on the condition that they sell whichever of their sons they wished toward their jizyah 3).

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, saying: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ wrote concerning the Lawātah, among the Berbers, in the terms he imposed upon them: “You are obliged to sell your sons and daughters toward the jizyah due from you.”

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us that Anṭābulus was conquered under a treaty made by ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Ḥaḍramī, that when Ibn Diyās assumed authority over Anṭābulus, he brought him their treaty document.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Yazīd ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Ḥaḍramī, from Abū Qanān Ayyūb ibn Abī al-ʿĀliya al-Ḥaḍramī, from his father, who said: I heard ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ say from the pulpit, “The people of Anṭābulus have a treaty, which is to be fulfilled for them.”

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ and others. He said: At that time, no collector of land tax entered Barqa; rather, they would send the tribute when its time came.

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ dispatched ʿUqba ibn Nāfiʿ, who went as far as Zawīla, and the territory between Barqa and Zawīla became the Muslims’.

The Account of Aṭrābulus

He said: Then ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ marched until he encamped at Aṭrābulus in the year twenty-two. Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, who said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ raided Ṭarābulus in the year twenty-three.

Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān. He encamped at the dome that stood on the high ground on its eastern side. He besieged it for a month, but could accomplish nothing against them. One day, a man from Banū Mudlij went out from ʿAmr’s camp to hunt, accompanied by seven men. They went west of the city, traveling far from the camp, then turned back. The heat overcame them, so they took the seashore. The sea lay close against the city wall, and there was no wall between the city and the sea. Roman ships lay at anchor in their harbor, extending toward their houses. The Mudlijī and his companions looked and saw that the sea had receded on the city’s side. They found a way into it where the sea had receded, and entered by that route until they reached the area by the church. They shouted “God is greatest!” The Romans had no refuge except their ships. ʿAmr and his companions saw the salla inside the city7 ʿAmr advanced with his army and entered upon them. The Romans escaped only with whatever vessels they could manage to get away in. ʿAmr took as booty what was in the city.

The people of Sabrāt were fortified there (its name is Nabāra; Sabrāt was the old market, and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb moved it to Nabāra in the year thirty-one). When they heard that ʿAmr was besieging the city of Aṭrābulus, that he had accomplished nothing there, and that he had no power against its people, they felt secure. But when ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ captured the city of Aṭrābulus, he sent out a large body of cavalry that very night and ordered them to ride swiftly. At dawn his cavalry reached the city of Sabrāt, whose people were off their guard. They had opened their gates to let their livestock out to pasture, so the cavalry entered, and not one of them escaped. ʿAmr took possession of everything in it, and they returned to him.

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from al-Ḥārith ibn Yazīd, who heard Abū Tamīm al-Jayshānī say: We raided with ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ in the expedition to Aṭrābulus. We gathered in an assembly, which included Hubayb ibn Mughfal, and discussed making up missed Ramadan fasts. Hubayb ibn Mughfal said, “They should not be divided.” ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ said, “There is no harm in dividing them up, once you have counted the number.”

The Account of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ Asking ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb’s Permission to Raid Ifrīqiya

ʿAmr wanted to send an expedition toward the west, so he wrote to ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb, as ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Ibn Hubayra, from Abū Tamīm al-Jayshānī: “God has granted us the conquest of Aṭrābulus, and it is only nine days from there to Ifrīqiya. If the Commander of the Faithful thinks it right for us to raid it and for God to grant its conquest at his hands, then let him do so.” ʿUmar wrote back to him: “No. That is not Ifrīqiya; it is the treacherous land that is itself betrayed. No one is to raid it as long as I remain alive.”

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Abū Qabīl, from Murra ibn Layshara al-Maʿāfirī, who said: I heard ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb say: “Ifrīqiya is treacherous and betrayed”—three times—“I will send no one there as long as my eyes hold water.”

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from al-Ḥārith ibn Yazīd, from ʿAlī ibn Rabāḥ, from Masʿūd ibn al-Aswad, a Companion of the Messenger of God ﷺ—who had pledged allegiance beneath the tree: he asked ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb for permission to raid Ifrīqiya. ʿUmar said, “No. Ifrīqiya is treacherous and betrayed.”

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ and others. He said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ received a letter from al-Muqawqis, in which he told him that the Byzantines intended to break the treaty and violate what had been agreed between them. ʿAmr had made a pact with al-Muqawqis not to conceal from him any new development. So ʿAmr turned back at once when the letter reached him. ʿAmr had been sending out cavalry detachments, which would seize booty and then return.

The Account of ʿAmr’s Removal from Egypt

He said: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān related to us that ʿUmar ﵀—died while Egypt had two governors:

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ in Lower Egypt, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Sarḥ in Upper Egypt. He said: ʿUmar died, as Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, at the departure of the pilgrims, in the year twenty-three.

Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us. He said: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb had appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd over Upper Egypt, from al-Fayyūm. When ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān became caliph, as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ—or someone else—related to us, from al-Layth, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ became eager, when he saw ʿUthmān’s position, to have ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd removed from Upper Egypt. He went to him and spoke with him about it. ʿUthmān said to him, “ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb appointed him over Upper Egypt. There is no kinship or special tie between him and ʿUmar, and you know that he is my foster brother. How can I remove him from what someone other than I appointed him to?”

According to what Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us, ʿUthmān also said to him: “You are unaware of what his mother used to do for me. She would hide a piece of meat for me in the sleeve of her garment until I came.”

Then he returned to the account of al-Layth ibn Saʿd. He said: ʿAmr became angry and said, “I will not return unless that is done.” ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān wrote to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd, appointing him governor over all Egypt. The letter reached him at al-Fayyūm. Ibn ʿUfayr said: It was in a village there called Damūsha.

Al-Layth said in his account: He gave the people of Aṭwāb a payment in return for bringing him to al-Fusṭāṭ before dawn in their boat. As the family of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd claim, the payment he gave them was five dinars. Al-Layth said:

They brought him to al-Fusṭāṭ before dawn. He sent for the muezzin, who commenced the prayer when dawn broke. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr was waiting for the muezzin to call him to prayer, since he was his father’s deputy, and he found the commencement of the prayer strange.

He was told, “ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd has led the people in prayer.”

The family of ʿAbd Allāh claim that ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd came from the west side of the mosque with a candle before him, while ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr came from the direction of his house with a candle before him. The two candles met at the qibla.

Al-Layth said in his account: ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr came up and stood before ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd. He said, “This is your desire and your intrigue.” ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd said, “I did nothing. You and your father used to envy me for Upper Egypt. Come, and I will appoint you over Upper Egypt and appoint your father over Lower Egypt, and I will not envy you over it.” ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd remained governor there, held in good regard. He conducted three campaigns there, all of them notable: Ifrīqiya, al-Asāwid, and the Day of Dhū al-Ṣawārī. I shall recount these in their proper place, if God wills.

He said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ was removed from Egypt, as Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd was appointed in the year twenty-five.

The Account of the Revolt of Alexandria

He said: ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said that Alexandria had revolted, as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb. The Byzantines came against it, with the eunuch Manūʾīl, in ships, and anchored at Alexandria. The Byzantines who were there joined them, but al-Muqawqis had taken no action and had not broken the treaty.

ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān had removed ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ and appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd. When the Byzantines landed at Alexandria, the people of Egypt asked ʿUthmān to keep ʿAmr in office until he had finished fighting the Byzantines, since he was experienced in warfare and feared by the enemy. He agreed. Alexandria had its wall around it. ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ swore that if God gave him victory over them, he would demolish its wall, so that it would be like an adulteress’s house, entered from every direction. ʿAmr went out against them by land and sea.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: Others besides al-Layth reported that those among the Copts who obeyed al-Muqawqis rallied to him, but none of the Byzantines obeyed him. Khārija ibn Ḥudhāfa said to ʿAmr, “Attack them before their reinforcements grow, and before all Egypt revolts.” ʿAmr said, “No. I will leave them until they march against me. They will plunder the places they pass through, and God will disgrace some of them through others.”

They left Alexandria with those people from the villages who had broken the treaty. They began to enter villages, drink their wine, eat their food, and plunder whatever they passed. ʿAmr did not confront them until they reached Niqyūs. He met them on land and sea. The Byzantines and Copts attacked first, shooting arrows into the water in a fierce volley. That day, an arrow struck ʿAmr’s horse in its chest while he was on land, and the horse stumbled; ʿAmr dismounted. Then the Byzantines came out of the sea and joined those on land. They showered the Muslims with arrows, and the Muslims drew back a little. The Byzantines charged the Muslims, and the Muslims turned away before them. Sharīk ibn Sumayy fled with his cavalry.

The Byzantines had arranged their forces in ranks behind ranks. That day, a commander from the Byzantine lands who had come with them rode out on a horse, wearing gilded armor, and called for single combat. A man from Zabīd named Ḥawmal, known by the kunya Abū Madhjaḥ, rode out against him. They fought for a long time with spears, riding back and forth. Then the commander cast aside his spear and took up his sword, and Ḥawmal cast aside his spear and took up his sword. Ḥawmal was known for his courage. He kept calling, “Abū Madhjaḥ!” and the other answered, “At your service!” The people were on the bank of the Nile, on land, arrayed in their ranks. The two fought with their swords for a while. Then the commander charged Ḥawmal and bore him down, for Ḥawmal was slight. But Ḥawmal drew a dagger that he had at his belt or on his arm, struck the foreigner in the neck, sapping his strength, and brought him down. He took his equipment. Ḥawmal died a few days later ﵀. ʿAmr was seen carrying his bier between the two poles of the funeral litter until he buried him at al-Muqaṭṭam.

Then the Muslims charged them, and the Byzantines were routed. The Muslims pursued them until they drove them back to Alexandria. God granted the Muslims victory over them, and they killed the eunuch Manūʾīl.

Al-Haytham ibn Ziyād related to us that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ kept killing them until he had penetrated deep into their city. He was reproached for this, so he ordered the sword to be lifted from them. A mosque was built in that place where he had ordered the sword lifted. It is the mosque in Alexandria known as the Mosque of Mercy; it was called the Mosque of Mercy because ʿAmr lifted the sword there. He demolished the entire city wall.

ʿAmr gathered up what he had taken from them. The people of those villages who had not broken the treaty came to him and said, “We had remained faithful to our treaty, but these brigands passed through our land and took our goods and our animals, which are now in your possession.” ʿAmr returned to them whatever property of theirs they recognized and could prove was theirs. One of them said to ʿAmr, “What you did to us was not lawful for you. You were obliged to fight on our behalf, because we were under your protection and had not broken the treaty. As for those who broke it, may God drive them far away.” ʿAmr regretted what he had done and said, “Would that I had met them when they came out of Alexandria!”

The cause of Alexandria’s revolt, as Ḥaywa ibn Shurayḥ related to us, from al-Ḥasan ibn Thawbān, from Hishām ibn Abī Ruqayya, was as follows: the governor of Ikhnā came to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ and said:

“Tell us what the poll tax on each of us will be, so that we may bear it.”

ʿAmr said, pointing to a corner of a church, “Even if you gave me everything from that corner to the ceiling, I would not tell you. You are merely a treasury for us: if our revenues increase, we increase your burden; if our burden is eased, we ease yours.”

The governor of Ikhnā was angered, and went to the Byzantines and brought them back. God defeated them, and the Nabataean was captured and brought to ʿAmr. The people said to him, “Kill him.” He replied, “No. Go, and bring us another army.” Saʿīd ibn Sābiq related to us. He said: His name was Ṭalmā. When ʿAmr brought him in, he put a bracelet on him, crowned him, and dressed him in a purple burnus. Then he said to him, “Bring us others like these.” He agreed to pay the poll tax. Ṭalmā was asked, “Why not go to the Byzantine emperor?” He replied, “If I went to him, he would kill me and say, ‘You killed your companions.’”

The Account of the Ruin of Kharibat Wardān

He said: When ʿAmr set out for Alexandria, he destroyed the village now known as Kharibat Wardān. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: There are differing accounts among us as to why it was destroyed. Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us that when ʿAmr set out for Niqyūs to fight the Byzantines, Wardān turned aside at dawn to relieve himself. The people of the village seized and hid him. ʿAmr noticed he was missing, asked about him, and followed his tracks. They found him in one of their houses. ʿAmr ordered that the village be destroyed and its people driven out.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us. He said: All the people of the village were monks. They treacherously killed some of the men at the rear of ʿAmr’s army after ʿAmr had reached al-Karyūn. ʿAmr halted and sent Wardān against them; he killed them and destroyed the village. It has remained in ruins to this day.

My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, related to us. He said: The people of the village were given to rebellion and wickedness.

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ sent to their land and had a sack filled with its soil. Then he summoned them and spoke with them, but they would not agree to anything. He ordered them to be expelled. Then he had the soil spread beneath the place where he prayed, sat upon it, and summoned them again. This time they agreed to what he wanted. He then ordered the soil to be removed and summoned them once more, but they would not agree to anything. He repeated this several times. When ʿAmr saw this, he said, “This is a town that can only be trodden down.” He ordered it destroyed. God knows best.

Mention of Some Accounts of the Second Conquest of Alexandria

Then he returned to the account of Ibn Lahīʿa, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb. He said: When God routed the Byzantines, ʿUthmān wanted ʿAmr to command the army and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd to oversee the revenues. ʿAmr said, “Then I would be like someone holding a cow by its horns while another milks it.” ʿAmr refused.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd al-Muqriʾ related to us; Ḥarmala ibn ʿImrān related to us, from Tamīm ibn Faraʿ al-Mahrī, who said: I witnessed the second conquest of Alexandria, but no share of the booty was assigned to me. A dispute between my people and Quraysh nearly broke out over this. Some of the people said, “Send for Abū Baṣra al-Ghifārī and ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir al-Juhanī, for they are Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ—and ask them about this.” They sent for them and asked them. The two said, “Look: if he has reached puberty, assign him a share.” Some of the people looked at me and found that I had reached puberty, so they assigned me a share.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Wahb related to us, from Mūsā ibn ʿAlī, from his father, from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, that he conquered Alexandria in the final conquest by force and compulsion, during the caliphate of ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān, after the death of ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb ﵃ all.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us. He said: The first conquest of Alexandria took place in the year twenty-one, and the second in the year twenty-five, with four years between them.

Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, who said: The first conquest of Alexandria took place in the year twenty-two, and the second in the year twenty-five.

Someone other than Ibn Lahīʿa said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ remained for a month after the conquest of Alexandria. Then ʿUthmān removed him and appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd.

Someone other than Ibn Lahīʿa said in his report from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb: The Khays of al-Baymā continued fighting the people for seven years after Egypt was conquered, using those waters and marshes to make raids against them.8

The arrival of ʿAmr before ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, who said: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb lived for three years after the conquest of Egypt. During that time, ʿAmr came before him twice. Ibn ʿUfayr said: On one of those occasions, he appointed Zakariyyāʾ ibn al-Jahm al-ʿAbdarī over the troops, and Mujāhid ibn Jabr, client of Banū Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf, over the land tax. He was the grandfather of Muʿādh ibn Mūsā al-Naffāṭ, father of Isḥāq ibn Muʿādh the poet. ʿUmar asked him, “Whom did you appoint in your place?” He named Mujāhid ibn Jabr. ʿUmar said to him, “The client of the daughter of Ghazwān?”

He replied, “Yes. He is a scribe.” ʿUmar said, “The pen raises its wielder.” This daughter of Ghazwān was the sister of ʿUtba ibn Ghazwān, and ʿUtba had been present at Badr. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Hishām related to us. He said: Ziyād ibn ʿAbd Allāh related to us, from Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq, who said: ʿUtba ibn Ghazwān ibn Jābir ibn Wahb ibn Nasīb ibn Mālik ibn al-Ḥārith ibn Māzin ibn Manṣūr ibn ʿIkrima ibn Khaṣafa ibn Qays ibn ʿAylān was an ally of Banū Nawfal ibn ʿAbd Manāf. He said: Mujāhid ibn Jabr’s allotment was the house of Ṣāliḥ, the market inspector.

He said: Then he returned to Ibn ʿUfayr’s report and said: On the second visit, he appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr in his place. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; they said: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ entered upon ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb while he was at his table, kneeling, and all his companions were in the same posture. There was no room in the serving bowl for anyone else to sit. ʿAmr greeted ʿUmar, who returned his greeting. ʿUmar said, “Is that ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ?”

He said, “Yes.” ʿUmar put his hand into the tharīd, filled it with tharīd, and then handed it to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, saying:

“Take this.” ʿAmr sat down, held the tharīd in his left hand, and ate with his right. The delegation from Egypt watched him. When they had left, the delegates said to ʿAmr, “What was that you did?” ʿAmr replied, “By God, he knows that I have no need of the tharīd he gave me, given what I brought with me from Egypt. But he wanted to test me. Had I not accepted it, I would have met with trouble from him.”

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Abū Qabīl, who said:

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ entered upon ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb with his head and beard dyed black. ʿUmar said, “Who are you?” He replied, “I am ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ.” ʿUmar said, “I remember you as an old man, but today you are a young man. I charge you: go out and wash this off.” ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who said:

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ once came from Egypt to ʿUmar. He arrived while ʿUmar was on the pulpit on Friday, and ʿUmar said, “Here is ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, who has come to you. It is not fitting for ʿAmr to walk upon the earth except as an emir.” Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, from Mishraḥ ibn ʿĀhān, from ʿUqba ibn ʿĀmir, that ʿUmar ﵁ said, “It is not fitting for ʿAmr to walk upon the earth except as an emir.” Al-Layth said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ said, “There was nothing in which I was more adept at making a profit than warfare.”

The death of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ

He said: Then ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ died in the year 43. Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, who said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ died in the year 43. In that year, ʿUtba ibn Abī Sufyān was appointed over the people of Egypt, and in that year Sharīk ibn Sammī went on campaign to the Maghrib.

He said: Asad ibn Mūsā and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; they said: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from Ibn Shamāsa, who informed him that when death came upon ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, his eyes filled with tears. ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr said, “Abū ʿAbd Allāh, is fear of death making you do this?” He said:

“No, but what comes after death.” ʿAbd Allāh reminded him of his experiences with the Messenger of God ﷺ and of the conquests in Syria. When ʿAbd Allāh had finished, he said, “I have passed through three stages. Had I died in any one of them, I know what people would have said. When God sent Muḥammad ﷺ I was the person most opposed to what he brought. I wished I could kill him. Had I died in that state, people would have said: ‘ʿAmr died as a polytheist, an enemy of God and His Messenger, one of the people of Hell.’ Then God cast Islam into my heart, and I came to the Messenger of God ﷺ. He extended his hand to me so that I might pledge allegiance to him, but I drew back my hand. Then I said, ‘I pledge allegiance to you on condition that my previous sin be forgiven.’ At that time, I thought I would commit no sin in Islam. The Messenger of God ﷺ said, ‘ʿAmr, Islam wipes away what came before it, and the emigration wipes away what lies between it and Islam.’ Had I died in that state, people would have said: ‘ʿAmr embraced Islam and strove with the Messenger of God ﷺ. We hope that ʿAmr has much good with God.’ Then I was given governorships, and there were civil strifes; so I am apprehensive about this stage.”

“When you carry me out, hasten with me, and let no woman eulogize me, nor any fire [be carried before me]. Bind my waist-wrapper tightly around me, for I am in dispute. Cast earth upon me gently, for my right side is no more entitled to the earth than my left. Do not put any piece of wood or brick into the grave. Then, when you have buried me, remain beside me for as long as it takes to slaughter a camel and cut it up, so that I may find comfort in your presence.”

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us; Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb related to us, from Suwayd ibn Qays, from Qays ibn Sammī, with a similar report.

He said: ʿAmr said, “By God, I was the person most bashful before the Messenger of God ﷺ. I never filled my eyes with looking at him, nor did I ever address him with what I wanted to say, out of bashfulness before him, until he went to God.”

The testament of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ at his death

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Muḥammad related to us, from Muḥammad ibn Ṭalḥa, from Ismāʿīl, that when death came upon ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, he said, “Call ʿAbd Allāh for me.” He said, “My son, when I die, wash me an odd number of times, and put some camphor in the last water with which you wash me. When you have finished, hasten”

“with me. When you have placed me in my grave, cast earth upon me gently. Know that you are leaving me alone and afraid. O God, I make no excuse, but I seek Your forgiveness. O God, You commanded us to do things, but we neglected them; You forbade us things, but we did them. I have no innocence to plead, nor any power to prevail. But there is no god but You, there is no god but You”—three times. Then he died.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Yaʿqūb ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān related to us, from his father, that when death came upon ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, his eyes welled up and he wept. ʿAbd Allāh said to him, “Father, I did not think that when God’s decree came upon you, you would do anything but endure it patiently.” He said to him, “My son, three things have come upon your father: the first is that his deeds have ceased; the second is the terror of what is revealed; the third is separation from loved ones—and that is the easiest of them. O God, You commanded, but I was remiss; You forbade, but I disobeyed. O God, among Your qualities are pardon and forbearance.” Wahb Allāh ibn Rāshid related to us; Yūnus ibn Yazīd informed us, from Ibn Shihāb, from Ḥumayd ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr, that when death came upon ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, he said:

“My son, when I die, shroud me in three garments, then wrap one of them around me. Dig the earth for me and scatter earth upon me gently, for I am in dispute.” Then he said, “O God, You commanded us to do things and forbade us other things, but we neglected much of what You commanded and fell into much of what You forbade. O God, there is no god but You.” He continued repeating it until he breathed his last.

Al-Muqriʾ ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd related to us; Ḥarmala ibn ʿImrān al-Tujībī related to us; Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb related to me, from Abū Firās, client of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, that when death came upon ʿAmr, he said to his son ʿAbd Allāh: “When I die, wash me and shroud me, and bind my waist-wrapper tightly around me, for I am in dispute. When you carry me, walk quickly. When you set me down in the place of prayer—that is, on a festival day—look toward the mouths of the roads. When no one remains and the people have gathered, begin by praying over me, then pray the festival prayer. When you place me in my niche, heap earth upon me, for my right side is no more entitled to the earth than my left. When you have levelled it over me, sit beside my grave for as long as it takes to slaughter a camel and cut it up, so that I may find comfort in your presence.”

When ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr stepped forward to pray over his father, as ʿAbd al-Ghaffār ibn Dāwūd and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, from Rabīʿa ibn Laqīṭ, he said: “By God, I did not…”

I would have liked, in place of my father, to have an Arab man as a father. I would not wish God to know that my eye had shed a tear for him out of grief, even if I had the finest red camels. Then he said, “Allāhu akbar.”

Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us: He was buried at al-Muqaṭṭam, in the direction of al-Fajj. The road to the Ḥijāz ran past there in those days, and he wanted those who passed by to pray for him. About this, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr says:

Have you not seen how the vicissitudes of time have laid low ʿAmr al-Sahmī, for whom Egypt pays tribute?

He became like wine left out in the open, while his stratagems and abundant wealth availed him nothing;

Neither his retinue nor his resourcefulness availed him, nor his cunning, when time caught up with him.

The Conquest of Ifrīqiya

Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān and others. He said: When ʿUthmān removed ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ from Egypt and appointed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Sarḥ, the latter sent out Muslim raiding parties on horseback, as they had done in the days of ʿAmr. They would raid the outskirts of Ifrīqiya and take booty. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd wrote to ʿUthmān about this, informing him how close they were to the Muslims’ stronghold and seeking his permission to invade it.

After consulting about the matter, ʿUthmān called the people to join the expedition. When they had gathered, ʿUthmān appointed al-Ḥārith ibn al-Ḥakam to command them until they reached ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd in Egypt; the command would then pass to him.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd set out for it. At that time the seat of power in Ifrīqiya was in a city called Carthage. Its ruler was a man called Jirjīr, whom Heraclius had appointed as his successor. He had rebelled against Heraclius and struck dinars bearing his own image. His realm stretched from Tripoli to Tangier.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, saying: Heraclius had appointed Jirjīr as his successor, but Jirjīr rebelled against him.

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ and others. Jirjīr met him and fought him, and God killed Jirjīr.

The man who killed him, they claim, was ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr. Jirjīr’s army fled. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd sent out detachments and scattered them, and they seized much booty. When the leading men of Ifrīqiya saw this, they asked ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd to accept money from them in return for withdrawing from their land. He accepted their offer and returned to Egypt. He appointed no one over them and established no garrison town there.

The Muslims’ booty in those days was as follows. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Abū al-Aswad, from Abū Uways, who said: Abū al-Aswad was a client of ours. He said: We raided Ifrīqiya with ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd. After the fifth was taken out, he divided the booty among us. A cavalryman’s share amounted to three thousand dinars: two thousand for his horse and one thousand for its rider; a foot soldier received one thousand dinars. A man in the army who died at Dhāt al-Ḥammām was allotted a share, and after his death one thousand dinars were delivered to his family.

Yūsuf ibn ʿAdī related to us; Ibn al-Mubārak related to us, from Ḥaywa ibn Shurayḥ, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Abī Hilāl, from Abū al-Aswad: Abū Aws, an old client of theirs, told him that a man went out on the raid into Ifrīqiya and died at Dhāt al-Ḥammām. A share was allotted to him; his share at that time was one thousand dinars.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from several people, that ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd raided Ifrīqiya and killed Jirjīr. A cavalryman received three thousand dinars at that time, and a foot soldier one thousand dinars. Others among the elders of Egypt, besides al-Layth, said: For every dinar there was an additional dinar and a quarter.

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ and others. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd’s army numbered twenty thousand.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, who said: In ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd’s raid, the Mahra alone numbered six hundred men. Ghath from the Azd numbered seven hundred men. Maydaʿān numbered seven hundred, and Maydaʿān were from the Azd.9

The person in charge of dividing the booty was, according to what Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa, from al-Ḥārith ibn Yazīd, from Azhar ibn Yazīd al-Ghuṭayfī, [the name here continues:] Sharīk ibn Sumayy. Ibn Zurāra al-Madīnī sold gold dust for gold, some of it of better quality than the rest. Then al-Miqdād ibn al-Aswad met him and mentioned it to him. Al-Miqdād said:

“This is not permissible.” Ibn Zurāra said to him, “The surplus is a gift to you.” Sharīk said, “I would not like to have what you acquire; I shall return with it.”

As my father ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam and Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us, Jirjīr’s daughter had fallen as a share to a man of the Anṣār. He set off homeward with her, having put her on one of his camels, and began to chant:

Daughter of Jirjīr, climb the hill ahead of you; Your mistress is in the Ḥijāz; You will surely carry your water skin from Qubāʾ.

She said, “What is that dog saying?” When she was told, she threw herself off the camel she was riding, broke her neck, and died.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us, saying: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd was the one who conquered Ifrīqiya—and it is transmitted that he was the one who first broke into Ifrīqiya. They used to pile up heaps of silver before him and ask the Ifrīqiyans, “Where did you get this?” One of them began to walk around as though searching for something. When he found an olive, he brought it to him and said, “This is what the silver comes from.” “How so?” he asked. The man said, “The Byzantines have no olive trees. They used to come to us and buy oil, and we would take this silver from them.”

The Ifrīqiyans were so called because, as ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa and others, they were descended from Fāriq ibn Bayṣar. Fāriq had claimed for himself the land stretching from Barqa to Ifrīqiya, and Ifrīqiya was named for the Ifrīqiyans.

My father ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam related to us; Bakr ibn Muḍar related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from Qays ibn Abī Yazīd, from al-Jallās ibn ʿĀmir, from ʿAbd Allāh ibn Abī Rabīʿa, who said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd led the people in the sunset prayer in Ifrīqiya. When he had prayed two units, he heard a commotion in the mosque. They were alarmed and thought the enemy was upon them, so he interrupted the prayer. When he saw that there was nothing there, he addressed the people and said, “This prayer has been cut short.” Then he ordered his muezzin to give the call to commence the prayer, and he repeated it.

He said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd sent ʿUqba ibn Nāfiʿ with the news of the conquest, as ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us, from Ibn Lahīʿa. It is also said that it was ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr; that is more accurate. They claim that ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr rode his mount from Ifrīqiya to Medina in twenty nights.

Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us; al-Mundhir ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Ḥizāmī related to me, from Hishām ibn ʿUrwa, that ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd sent ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr to ʿUthmān with news of the conquest of Ifrīqiya. He entered upon ʿUthmān and began telling him of their encounter with the enemy and what had taken place on that expedition. ʿUthmān was impressed and said to him, “Can you tell the people something like this?” “Yes,” he replied. ʿUthmān took him by the hand and led him to the pulpit, then said, “Tell them what you told me.” ʿAbd Allāh hesitated at first. Al-Zubayr picked up a handful of pebbles and was about to pelt him with them. Then ʿAbd Allāh spoke, and his words impressed them. Al-Zubayr used to say, “When one of you wishes to marry a woman, let him look at her father and her brother; before long, he will see a sprig of her at his door,” because he saw how much ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr resembled Abū Bakr.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, saying: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd sent ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr, who was in the army, with news of the conquest. He came to ʿUthmān ibn ʿAffān and went to him before going to his father, al-Zubayr ibn al-ʿAwwām. ʿUthmān went out to the mosque with Ibn al-Zubayr. He praised and glorified God, then mentioned what God had brought about for the Muslims at the hands of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd. Then he said, “Stand up, ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr, and tell the people what you witnessed.”

Al-Zubayr said: I felt resentment toward ʿUthmān and said to myself, “He is putting forward a mere boy, who cannot attain what is required of him or what befits him.” He stood up and spoke, and spoke both eloquently and aptly. He had scarcely finished before he had filled them with amazement. Then ʿUthmān came down, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Zubayr went to his father. His father took him by the hand and said, “When you wish to marry a woman, look at her father and her brother before you marry her”—as though comparing him, for his eloquence, to his grandfather Abū Bakr al-Ṣiddīq.

He said: Ibn Lahīʿa related this to me, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb. It has also been said that ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd had sent Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam from Ifrīqiya to ʿUthmān. I do not know whether this was during the conquest or afterward. God knows best.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Maʿshar al-Aylī related to us: Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam came from Ifrīqiya, sent by ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd, who had sent with him a man from the Arabs, either of Lakhm or Judhām—ʿAbd al-Raḥmān was uncertain. He said: We travelled until, when we were at some point along the road and night was near, my companion said to me: “Would you like to visit a friend of mine here?” I said: “As you wish.” He turned aside with me from the road until he came to a monastery, where a chain was hanging. He took the chain and shook it. He was more knowledgeable than I was. A man looked out at us, and when he saw us he opened the door. We entered, and he said nothing until he had laid out one mattress for me and one for my companion. Then he turned to my companion and spoke with him in his language, conversing with him until I grew suspicious. Then he turned to me and said: “What is your kinship to their caliph?” I said: “I am his cousin.” He said: “Is anyone closer to him than you?” I said: “No, unless it be his child.” He said: “Are you the master of the Holy Land?” I said: “No.” He said: “Then if you can be that man, do so.” Then he said: “I want to tell you something, but I fear you may not be able to bear it.” I said: “Are you saying this to me? I am what I am.” Then he turned to my companion and conversed with him in his language. Then he turned to me and asked me the same sort of questions, and I gave him the same answers. He said: “Your companion is to be killed, and we find that the master of the Holy Land will rule this affair after him. So if you can be that man, do so.” I was deeply troubled by this.

He said to me: “I told you I feared you might not be able to bear it.” I said: “How could I not be affected?”—or words to that effect—“when the death of the leader of the Muslims and Commander of the Faithful has been foretold to me?”

He said: Then I arrived in Medina and stayed for a month without telling ʿUthmān any of this. Then I went in to see him at a house of his. He was on a couch, holding a fan in his hand. I told him about it. When I got to the part about the killing, I wept and stopped. ʿUthmān said to me: “Speak—may you never speak [again]!” So I told him. He took the edge of the fan and bit it, I think; ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: “He lay back on his back, took the tip of his heel, and rubbed it until I regretted having told him.” Then he said to me: “He spoke truly, and I shall tell you about that. When the Messenger of God ﷺ went on the expedition to Tabūk, he gave his Companions one share, and gave me two shares. I thought the Messenger of God ﷺ had given me that only because of what I had spent at Tabūk. So I went to the Messenger of God ﷺ and said: ‘You gave me two shares, while you gave each of my companions one share. I thought that was because of what I had spent.’ The Messenger of God ﷺ said: ‘No. Rather, I wanted the people to see your place with me, or your standing with me.’”

I turned away, and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf caught up with me and said: “What did you say to the Messenger of God ﷺ? He kept watching you as you went.” I thought that what I had said had displeased the Messenger of God ﷺ. So I waited until he went out to pray, then went to him and said: “Messenger of God, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAwf told me such-and-such, and I repent to God”—or words to that effect. He said: “No, but you are to be killed, or you are the killer; so be the one who is killed. God knows best.”

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: The conquest of Ifrīqiya took place, as Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, in the year 27.

In that same year, as ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us from Mālik ibn Anas, Ḥafṣa, wife of the Prophet ﷺ died.

The Nubians

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd campaigned against the Blacks, that is, the Nubians, as Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us: this was in the year 31.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who said:

In the year 31, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Sarḥ was ʿUthmān’s governor over Egypt, and the Nubians fought against him.

Ibn Lahīʿa said: al-Ḥārith ibn Yazīd related to me: They fought a fierce battle. On that day the eye of Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj was struck, as were the eyes of Abū Shamir ibn Abraha and Ḥuwaywil ibn Nāshira. From that day they were called “the shooters of the eyeballs.” ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd made a truce with them when he found himself unable to overcome them. The poet said:

My eye has never seen a day like Dumqula, while the palm groves are traversed, weighed down in coats of mail. In his report, Ibn Abī Ḥabīb said: ʿAbd Allāh made peace with them on the terms of a truce between them: that they would not raid the Muslims, nor would the Nubians raid them; that the Nubians would deliver every year to the Muslims such-and-such a number of captive heads, and that the Muslims would deliver to them such-and-such quantities of wheat and lentils each year. Ibn Abī Ḥabīb said: There is no treaty or pact between them and the people of Egypt; it is only a truce, a guarantee of security between us.

Ibn Lahīʿa said: There is no harm in buying their enslaved people from them or from others. Abū Ḥabīb—the father of Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, whose name was Suwayd—was one of them.

Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us: I heard Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb say:

My father was among the captives of Dumqula, a client of a man from Banū ʿĀmir, one of the people of Medina, named Sharīk ibn Ṭufayl. He said: As for the terms on which the Nubians made peace, some of the elders of the people of Egypt said they were three hundred and sixty heads each year. It is also said that they were four hundred heads each year: three hundred and sixty heads as spoils for the Muslims, and forty heads for the governor of the district.

He said: Some of the elders claimed that among them were seventeen nursing women. Then ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd withdrew from them.

According to what some of the earlier elders related, one of them looked through some registers in Fusṭāṭ and read one before it had fallen to pieces . He found in it this text, which he remembered: “We have made a pact and agreement with you that you will deliver to us every year three hundred and sixty heads, and that you will enter our land as passers-through, not as residents. We shall likewise enter your land. If you kill a Muslim, your truce is dissolved; if you give shelter to a Muslim’s slave, your truce is dissolved. You must return runaway Muslims, and any protected people who seek refuge with you.”

He said: Others of the elders claimed that the Nubians owed the Muslims no annual payment, and that the first year they sent the baqṭ, they presented ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ with forty heads. He was unwilling to accept them, so he returned them to a leading man among the Copts, named Nastaqūs, who was their steward in this matter. That man sold them and bought supplies for the Nubians. They cited this as evidence that ʿAmr sent them wheat and vinegar : for they had been warned against wheat and horses, and when they tested this in earlier times, they were afflicted. This is their story.

Then the account resumes. As ʿAbd Allāh was withdrawing, the Beja gathered against him on the bank of the Nile. He asked about them and was told where they were . He thought little of their affair, proceeded on his way, and left them alone. They had no pact or peace treaty. The first to make peace with them was ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Ḥabḥāb.

Some of the elders claim that one of them read Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb’s letter, which said: “Three hundred virgins every year, provided that they come down to the countryside as passing merchants, not as residents; that they kill neither a Muslim nor a protected person; that, if they do kill one, they have no treaty; that they give no shelter to Muslim slaves; and that they return their runaways if they are found. I witnessed this being enforced in their days. For each sheep taken by a Bejawi, he must pay four dinars; for a cow, ten dinars. Their agent remained in the countryside as a hostage in the hands of the Muslims.”

The Battle of Dhāt al-Ṣawārī

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: Then ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd ibn Abī Sarḥ campaigned at Dhāt al-Ṣawārī, as Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us from al-Layth ibn Saʿd. This was in the year 34.

The account of this campaign, as ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, is that when ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd arrived at Dhāt al-Ṣawārī, he sent half the men with Busr ibn Abī Arṭāʾ on a detachment by land. After they had gone, someone came to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd and said: “What you would do if Heraclius were to descend upon you with a thousand ships, do now.”

Someone other than al-Layth said: It was Heraclius’s son, for Heraclius had died in the year 19, while the Muslims were besieging Alexandria.

Then the report returns to al-Layth’s account from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb. He said: The Muslims had just over two hundred ships that day. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd stood among the men and said: “I have been told that Heraclius is coming against you with a thousand ships. Advise me.” Not a single Muslim spoke to him. He sat for a short while, to give their hearts time to recover, then stood up and addressed them a second time. No one spoke to him, so he sat down. Then he stood up a third time and said: “There is no remaining course. Advise me.”

A man from Medina, who had volunteered to serve with ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd, stood up and said: “Commander, God—glorified be His praise—says: ‘How many a small company has overcome a large company by God’s permission, and God is with the steadfast.’”

ʿAbd Allāh said: “Embark, in the name of God.” They embarked. Each ship was only half loaded, for the other half of the men had gone ashore with Busr. They met and fought with arrows and bolts. Heraclius held back so that he would not suffer defeat, and the small boats kept going back and forth to him with reports. He said: “What have they done?” They said: “They have fought with arrows and bolts.” He said: “The Romans have won.” Then they came to him again, and he said: “What have they done?” They said: “The arrows and bolts have run out, and they are throwing stones at each other.” He said: “The Romans have won.” Then they came to him again and said: “The stones have run out, and they have tied the ships to one another and are fighting with swords.” He said: “The Romans have won.”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who said:

At that time, ships were fastened together with chains during battle. On that day ʿAbd Allāh’s ship, on which he was the commander, was chained to one of the enemy’s ships. The enemy ship nearly dragged ʿAbd Allāh’s ship over to them. ʿAlqama ibn Yazīd al-Ghaṭīfī, who was aboard with ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd, stood up and cut the chain with his sword.

Afterward ʿAbd Allāh asked his wife Basīsa, daughter of Ḥamza ibn Layšaraḥ, who had been with ʿAbd Allāh that day—and men used to go on campaigns with their women aboard the ships—“Who did you see fighting most fiercely?” She said: “ʿAlqama, the man of the chain.” ʿAbd Allāh had proposed to Basīsa’s father, who said to him: “ʿAlqama has proposed to her, and I have made him a promise concerning her. If he lets her go, I will [give her to you].” ʿAbd Allāh spoke with ʿAlqama, and he let her go. ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd married her. Then ʿAbd Allāh died, and ʿAlqama ibn Yazīd married her after him. Then ʿAlqama died, and Kurayb ibn Abraha married her after him. She died while married to him, in the year Marwān killed al-Akdar ibn Ḥammām.

Someone other than Ibn Lahīʿa said: Marwān killed al-Akdar ibn Ḥammām on the very day Basīsa died. The news reached Kurayb, who said: “Let me finish burying this dead woman.” He did not turn back before he himself was killed. That day people blamed Kurayb ibn Abraha. The story of al-Akdar ibn Ḥammām and his killing is longer than this.

Someone other than Ibn Lahīʿa said: In the year 35 the Romans went to Constantine, son of Heraclius, and said: “You are leaving Alexandria in the hands of the Arabs, though it is our greatest city!” He said: “What can I do with you? You cannot hold out even for an hour when you meet the Arabs.” They said: “Then come out with us, on the understanding that we will die.”

They pledged one another to that and set out in a thousand ships, intending for Alexandria. They sailed during days of strong wind , but God sent a wind against them and drowned them all except Constantine, who escaped in his ship. The wind cast him onto Sicily. They asked him what had happened, and he told them . They said: “Christianity has been put to shame, and you have destroyed its men. If the Arabs came in on us, we would find no one to repel them.” He said: “We set out with our strength, and this happened to us.” They prepared a bath for him, and went in to see him. He said: “Woe to you! Your men are gone, and you kill your king.” They said: “It is as though he drowned along with them.” Then they killed him and released those who had been with him in the ships.

The Alexandria Garrison

ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us; Ibn Bahlīʿa related to us from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Hubayra—the two adding to each other’s accounts. They said: When the lands were settled and God had granted the Muslims the conquest of Alexandria, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ assigned a quarter of his men specifically to garrison Alexandria. That quarter would remain for six months, then the winter contingent would relieve them for six months; a quarter would be on the coasts, while the other half would remain with him.

Someone other than these two said: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb used to send a force from the people of Medina every year to garrison Alexandria. He wrote to the governors: “Do not neglect it; strengthen its garrison and do not feel secure about it against the Romans.”

ʿUthmān wrote to ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd: “You know the concern the Commander of the Faithful has had for Alexandria. The Romans have broken their agreement twice. Therefore keep Alexandria’s garrison in place, then pay them their stipends and rotate them every six months.”

Ṭalq ibn al-Samḥ related to us; Ḍimām ibn Ismāʿīl al-Maʿāfirī related to us; Abū Qabīl related to us: ʿUtba ibn Abī Sufyān appointed ʿAlqama ibn Yazīd al-Ghaṭīfī over Alexandria and sent twelve thousand men with him. ʿAlqama wrote to Muʿāwiya complaining about ʿUtba, who had put him and his men in danger. Muʿāwiya wrote to him:

“I have reinforced you with ten thousand men from Syria and five thousand from Medina,” bringing their number to twenty-seven thousand.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us that ʿAlqama ibn Yazīd was in charge of Alexandria with twelve thousand men. He wrote to Muʿāwiya: “You left me in Alexandria with only twelve thousand men; because we are so few, some of us can hardly see the others.” Muʿāwiya wrote to him: “I have reinforced you with ʿAbd Allāh ibn Muṭīʿ and four thousand men from Medina. I have ordered Maʿn ibn Yazīd al-Sulamī to be stationed at al-Ramla with four thousand men, holding the reins of their horses, so that as soon as news of alarm reaches them from you, they can cross over to you.”

Ibn Lahīʿa said: ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ used to say: “The governorship of Egypt is comprehensive; it is equal to the caliphate.”

Those Who Went on Campaign to the Maghrib after ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ and His Conquests

Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj. He said: After ʿAbd Allāh ibn Saʿd, Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj al-Tujībī went on campaign to the Maghrib in the year 34. Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam was with his army that year. He conquered fortresses and took great booty . He established an encampment at al-Qarn and remained there until he set out for Egypt. A group of the Emigrants and the Helpers were with him on this campaign.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us. Yūsuf ibn ʿAdī related to us; ʿAbd Allāh ibn al-Mubārak related to us with a similar report from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Bukayr ibn ʿAbd Allāh, from Sulaymān ibn Yasār, who said: We campaigned in Ifrīqiya with Ibn Ḥudayj, and a great many of the Emigrants and Helpers were with us. Ibn Ḥudayj awarded us half after the fifth had been set aside. I saw no one object to that except Jābala ibn ʿAmr al-Anṣārī.

Yūsuf ibn ʿAdī related to us; Ibn al-Mubārak related to us from Ibn Lahīʿa, from Khālid ibn Abī ʿImrān, who said: I asked Sulaymān ibn Yasār about the extra award on campaign. He said: I have seen no one do it except Ibn Ḥudayj. He awarded us half of Ifrīqiya’s booty after the fifth had been set aside. Many people from among the first Emigrants, Companions of the Messenger of God ﷺ were with us, but Jābala ibn ʿAmr al-Anṣārī refused to take anything from it.

Then the account returns to ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ and the others. He reached Qūniya, which is the site of the city of Qayrawān , then went on to a mountain called al-Qarn, and made camp beside it . He sent Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam to a city called Jalūlāʾ with a thousand men. Marwān besieged it for several days but achieved nothing and turned back. He had not gone far when he saw a great cloud of dust at the rear of the army. He thought the enemy was pursuing them. A group of men turned back because of this, while those who remained held their battle lines, and the rash ones among the people hurried on. Then it became clear that the wall of Jalūlāʾ had been breached. The Muslims entered it and took all that was in it. Marwān returned to Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj.

The people disagreed about the booty, so Muʿāwiya ibn Abī Sufyān was sent a letter about it. He replied: “The army is a support for the detachment,” and he divided it among them. Each man received two hundred dinars for himself; the horse was allotted two shares and its rider one. Marwān said: “I received six hundred dinars for my horse and myself, and bought a slave girl with them.”

He said: It is also said that Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj himself campaigned against it and besieged its people but could not overcome them. He turned back, having despaired of taking it; most of his companions had been wounded, and some had been killed. God granted victory over it after he had departed, without horsemen or men. He and those with him returned to it. The captives were still there, and no one had taken them away. They took the booty and set out from there, returning to Egypt.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama related to us; Ibn Lahīʿa related to us from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who said: Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj campaigned in Ifrīqiya three times. The first was in the year 34, before ʿUthmān was killed; on that campaign ʿUthmān gave Marwān the fifth. Few people know of that campaign. The second was in the year 40, and the third in the year 50.

ʿUqba ibn Nāfiʿ. He said: Then, after Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj, ʿUqba ibn Nāfiʿ al-Fihrī set out for the Maghrib in the year forty-six, accompanied by Busr ibn Abī Arṭāʾ and Shurayk ibn Sumayy al-Murādī. He proceeded until he encamped at Mughmadāsh, in Surt. Busr had previously marched toward it, as Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from Surt in the year twenty-six. Winter caught up with him, and he was weakened. He was informed that the people of Waddān had broken their treaty and withheld what Busr ibn Abī Arṭāʾ had assessed upon them.

ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ had previously sent Busr there while he was besieging the people of Atrābulus, and Busr conquered it. ʿUqba ibn Nāfiʿ left his army there, appointing ʿUmar ibn ʿAlī al-Qurashī and Zuhayr ibn Qays al-Balawī over them. Then he himself set out with those of his men who could travel light: four hundred cavalrymen, four hundred camels, and eight hundred waterskins. He reached Waddān and conquered it, taking their ruler and cutting off his ear. The ruler said, “Why did you do this to me, when I had made a treaty with you?” ʿUqba replied, “I did this to discipline you: whenever you touch your ear, you will remember it and will not fight the Arabs.” He extracted from them what Busr had assessed upon them: three hundred and sixty persons.

Then ʿUqba asked them, “Is there anyone beyond you?” He was told, “Jarma”—the greatest city of Fazzān. He marched there from Waddān for eight nights. When he drew near, he sent a messenger to summon them to Islam, and they accepted. He encamped six miles from it. Their ruler came out, intending to meet ʿUqba, and ʿUqba sent cavalry that came between the ruler and his retinue. They made him walk until he reached ʿUqba, exhausted. He had been accustomed to comfort, and began spitting blood. He said to him, “Why did you do this to me, when I came to you willingly?” ʿUqba replied, “To discipline you, so that when you remember it, you will not fight the Arabs.” He assessed upon him three hundred and sixty slaves. That very day ʿUqba dispatched the raḥḥāl eastward. [The word raḥḥāl is uncertain.]

Then he immediately continued in that direction to the forts of Fazzān, conquering them one by one, until he reached the farthest. He asked them, “Is there anyone beyond you?” They said, “Yes: the people of Khāwār. It is a great fort at the edge of the wilderness, in rugged terrain, atop a mountain; it is the principal town of Kawār.”

He traveled to them for fifteen nights. When he arrived, they fortified themselves, and he besieged them for a month, but could do nothing against them. He went on ahead to the fortresses of Kuwār and conquered them, one after another, until he reached the farthest, where their king was. He seized him and cut off one of his fingers. The king said, “Why did you do this to me?” He replied, “To discipline you: whenever you look at your finger, you will not fight the Arabs.” He imposed on him three hundred and sixty slaves.

He asked them, “Is there anyone beyond you?” The guide said, “I have no knowledge or information about that.” ʿUqba turned back. He passed by the fortress of Khāwār, but did not attack it or stop among its people. He traveled for three days, and they felt secure and opened their city. ʿUqba camped at a place now called Māʾ Fars, though there was no water there. They were overcome by severe thirst, and ʿUqba and his companions nearly died from it. ʿUqba prayed two cycles of prayer and called upon God.

ʿUqba’s horse began pawing at the ground until it uncovered a slab of rock, from beneath which water burst forth. The horse began to suck up the water. ʿUqba saw it and called out to the people, “Dig!” They dug seventy wells, drank, and drew water. For this reason, the place was named Māʾ Fars.

Then ʿUqba returned to Khāwār by a route different from the one by which he had come. Its people did not notice him until he fell upon them at night. He found them secure, settled in their burrows, and seized all the families and property in the city, killing their fighting men.

Then he turned back and traveled until he camped at the place now called Zawīla. He then set out and reached his camp after five months, his horses and mounts having recovered. He traveled toward the Maghrib, keeping to the side of the main road, and made for the land of Mazāta. He conquered every fortress there, then went on to Ṣufr and conquered its citadels and fortresses. Next, he sent cavalry to Ghādāmis, and Ghādāmis was conquered. When his cavalry returned to him, he went to Qafṣa and conquered it, and conquered Qasṭīliyya.

Then he returned to al-Qayrawān, but he did not approve of the al-Qayrawān that Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj had built before him, so he rode out with the people accompanying him until he came to the site of present-day al-Qayrawān. It was a valley with many trees and abundant fruit, where wild beasts, predators, and vermin took shelter. Then he called out at the top of his voice: “People of the valley, depart, may God have mercy on you, for we are settling here.” He called out thus for three days. Not one of the predators, wild beasts, or vermin remained; they all came out. He ordered the people to clear the ground and lay out the plots, and moved them from the place where Muʿāwiyah ibn Ḥudayj had settled to the site of present-day al-Qayrawān. He planted his lance and said: “This is your qayrawān.”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us that ʿUqbah ibn Nāfiʿ raided Ifrīqiyah and came to the valley of al-Qayrawān. He and his companions spent the night there. When morning came, he stood at the head of the valley and said: “People of the valley, move away, for we are settling here.” He said this three times. Then snakes began to slither away, along with scorpions and other creatures unknown to them, making their way out. They stood watching them from morning until the sun began to hurt them, and until they could see none of them. At that point they descended into the valley. Al-Layth said: Ziyād ibn al-ʿAjlān related to me that the people of Ifrīqiyah remained there for forty years afterward; if one had searched for a snake or scorpion, offering a thousand dinars for it, one would not have found one.

Abū al-Muhājir. He said: Then ʿUqbah ibn Nāfiʿ was removed from office in the year fifty-one. Maslamah ibn Mukhallad al-Anṣārī removed him; at that time, he was governor of the province on behalf of Muʿāwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān. Maslamah ibn Mukhallad was the first to be given authority over both Egypt and the Maghrib.

Maslamah ibn Mukhallad’s governorship, as Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, was in the year forty-seven. He appointed Abū al-Muhājir Dīnār, a client of the Anṣār, and when appointing him, instructed him to remove ʿUqbah honorably. Abū al-Muhājir disobeyed him: he removed him harshly, imprisoned him, and loaded him with chains, until the caliph’s letter arrived ordering that he be released and brought to him. ʿUqbah set out until he came to Qaṣr al-Māʾ. He prayed, then supplicated, saying: “O God, do not let me die until You grant me power over Abū al-Muhājir Dīnār ibn Umm—”

Dinār . This reached Abū al-Muhājir, and from the time the supplication reached him he remained fearful. When ʿUqbah arrived in Egypt, Maslamah ibn Mukhallad rode out to meet him and swore to him by God that Abū al-Muhājir’s actions had been contrary to his instructions, and that he had specifically charged him to treat ʿUqbah well.

It had been said to Maslamah, “If only you would keep ʿUqbah in office, for he has distinction and merit.” Maslamah replied, “Abū al-Muhājir stood by us when he held no office and had received no great reward, so we wish to repay him.”

When Abū al-Muhājir arrived in Ifrīqiyah, he disliked settling at the place that ʿUqbah ibn Nāfiʿ had laid out. He went on until he had left it two miles behind him, then built and settled there.

Before Abū al-Muhājir, people, as ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us, on the authority of Ibn Lahīʿah—

and Aḥmad ibn ʿAmr, on the authority of Ibn Wahb, on the authority of Ibn Lahīʿah, on the authority of Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb—used to raid Ifrīqiyah and then return from it to al-Fusṭāṭ. The first to remain there after raiding it was Abū al-Muhājir, the client of the Anṣār: he stayed there through winter and summer and made it his home. Maslamah ibn Mukhallad had appointed him over the army that went out with him there. They remained there until Ibn al-Zubayr was killed, then left.

Then ʿUqbah came before Muʿāwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān and said to him, “I conquered the lands, built settlements and the congregational mosque, and they submitted to me .
