Then you sent the client of the Anṣār, who treated me badly when dismissing me.” Muʿāwiyah excused himself to him, saying, “I know the standing of Maslamah ibn Mukhallad with the wronged caliph, his preference for him, his seeking vengeance for his blood, and his offering his life. I have restored you to your office.”

It is said that it was not Muʿāwiyah who reinstated ʿUqbah ibn Nāfiʿ; rather, after his father’s death, ʿUqbah came before Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiyah, who reinstated him as governor of Ifrīqiyah. This is more accurate, because Muʿāwiyah died in the year sixty.

Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, on the authority of al-Layth ibn Saʿd, who said: Muʿāwiyah ibn Abī Sufyān died in the year sixty.

The Killing of ʿUqbah ibn Nāfiʿ: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān and others, and said: ʿUqbah set out swiftly, in anger against Abū al-Muhājir, until he reached Ifrīqiya. He bound Abū al-Muhājir in tight fetters, treated him harshly in dismissing him, and took him along on a campaign to al-Sūs, still in chains.

The people of al-Sūs are a Berber tribe called Anbiyyah. He roamed through their lands, where no one confronted or fought him. He returned to Ifrīqiya, and when he drew near its frontier, he ordered his companions to disperse from him and gave them leave, until he was left with only a small number. He made for a place called Tahūdhah, where Kusāylah ibn Lamzam confronted him with a large force of Romans and Berbers. Kusāylah had learned that the men had dispersed from ʿUqbah. They fought fiercely, and ʿUqbah and those with him were killed. Abū al-Muhājir was also killed, still bound in chains. Then Kusāylah and his men marched until they came to the place that ʿUqbah had laid out as a settlement. Kusāylah stayed there, subdued those nearby, including Bāb Qābis and the surrounding area, and began sending his men in every direction.

It is also said that ʿUqbah ibn Nāfiʿ set out for al-Sūs and appointed ʿUmar ibn ʿAlī al-Qurashī and Zuhayr ibn Qays al-Balawī as his deputies in al-Qayrawān. Ifrīqiya was then called Mazāq. ʿUqbah advanced toward al-Sūs, while a foreigner with thirty thousand men went against ʿUmar ibn ʿAlī and Zuhayr ibn Qays, who had six thousand men; God routed him.

The Berber son of al-Kāhinah set out in pursuit of ʿUqbah. Every time ʿUqbah departed from a watering place, the son of al-Kāhinah buried it [the wording is unclear]. This continued until ʿUqbah reached al-Sūs, unaware of what the Berber had done. When ʿUqbah reached the sea, he drove his horse into it until the water reached its neck, then said: “O God, I call You to witness that there is no crossing; had I found a crossing, I would have crossed.”

He turned back, but the water sources had been rendered inaccessible [the wording is uncertain], and the Berbers joined forces against him. He continued fighting, Abū al-Muhājir was with him in chains. When the fighting grew fierce, ʿUqbah ordered that his chains be removed, but Abū al-Muhājir refused and said, “I shall meet God in my chains.” ʿUqbah, Abū al-Muhājir, and those with them were killed.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us that ʿUqbah ibn Nāfiʿ set out from Yazīd ibn Muʿāwiyah with an army to campaign in the Maghrib. He passed by ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr, who was in Egypt. ʿAbd Allāh said to him, “ʿUqbah, perhaps you are among the army who will enter Paradise with their mounts.” He went on with his army until he fought the Berbers, who were unbelievers, and they were all killed.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us: Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Buḥayr ibn Dhākhir al-Muʿāfirī, who said: I was with ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ when ʿUqbah ibn Nāfiʿ ibn ʿAbd al-Qays al-Fihrī entered to see him. ʿAbd Allāh said, “What brings you here, ʿUqbah? I know that you love command.” He replied, “The Commander of the Faithful, Yazīd, has appointed me over an army bound for Ifrīqiyah.” ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAmr said to him, “Beware of becoming the curse of the widows of Egypt, for I have continually heard that a man of Quraysh will set out on this expedition and perish there.”

He arrived in Ifrīqiyah, pursued the tracks of Abū al-Muhājir, constrained him, and put him in chains. Then he went out to fight the Berbers. They were five thousand men from Egypt, and he took Abū al-Muhājir with him in chains. He was killed, his companions were killed, and Abū al-Muhājir was killed with them.

The killing of ʿUqbah ibn Nāfiʿ and his companions took place, as Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, in the year sixty-three.

He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān and others. He said: Then Ibn al-Kāhinah marched on al-Qayrawān, intending to attack ʿUmar ibn ʿAlī and Zuhayr ibn Qays. They fought him fiercely, Ibn al-Kāhinah was defeated, and his companions were killed. ʿUmar ibn ʿAlī and Zuhayr ibn Qays set out for Egypt with the army because the Berber host had assembled. They left at Ṭarābulus their weaker followers and those of the clients of Ifrīqiyah who had gone out with them.

It is said that when ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān became governor of Egypt, he wrote to Zuhayr ibn Qays, who was then in Barqa, ordering him to raid Ifrīqiya. Zuhayr set out with a large force. When he drew near to Qūniya, where the army of Kusayla ibn Lamzam was encamped, Zuhayr drew up his men for battle. Kusayla came out to meet him, and they fought. Kusayla and those with him were killed; then Zuhayr turned back and returned to Barqa.

It is also said, however, that it was Ḥassān ibn al-Nuʿmān who had dispatched Zuhayr ibn Qays. God knows best.

Kusayla was killed, as Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, in the year 64.

Ḥassān ibn al-Nuʿmān

Ḥassān ibn al-Nuʿmān then arrived as governor of the Maghrib. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān appointed him over it in the year 73. He set out with a large army and halted at Ṭarābulus. Those who had left Ifrīqiya and Ṭarābulus gathered to him there. He sent Muḥammad ibn Abī Bakr, Hilāl ibn Tharwān al-Lawātī, and Zuhayr ibn Qays at the head of his army. They conquered the country and seized much booty.

He went on to the city of Qarṭājanna, where the Byzantines were. There he encountered only a few of their weaker men.

He turned back and raided the Kāhina, who was then queen of the Berbers and had gained control over most of Ifrīqiya. He met her at a river now called the River of Affliction. They fought fiercely, and she defeated him, killing some of his men and taking 80 of them prisoner. Ḥassān escaped and made his way from there to Anṭābulus. He halted at fortresses in the district of Barqa, which were therefore called the Fortresses of Ḥassān. He appointed Abū Ṣāliḥ as his deputy over Ifrīqiya. Anṭābulus, Lūbiya, and Murāqiya, as far as the boundary of Ajdābiya, were under Ḥassān’s jurisdiction.

The Kāhina treated the prisoners from among his men well and released them all except one, a man of Banū ʿAbs called Khālid ibn Yazīd. She adopted him, and he remained with her. Ḥassān sent a man to Khālid. When the man reached him, he said, “Ḥassān asks you: What prevents you from writing to us with news of the Kāhina?” Khālid ibn Yazīd wrote a letter to Ḥassān and placed it inside a loaf baked in hot ashes, then handed it to the messenger, so that the letter would be concealed inside it and anyone who saw the loaf would think it was the man’s provisions. The Kāhina came out, saying, “My sons, your destruction lies in something that people eat.” She repeated this.

The messenger continued on until he reached Ḥassān with the letter, which contained the information he needed. Then he also wrote him another letter and placed it inside a pommel he had hollowed out. He put the letter in it and sealed it over until it was smooth and its hiding place concealed. The Kahina came out again, saying, “My sons, your ruin lies in something dead from among the plants of the earth.” She repeated this.

The messenger went on until he reached Ḥassān. Ḥassān then called his companions to arms and raided her territory. When he set out against her, she came forth with her hair loose and said, “My sons, look: what do you see in the sky?” They said, “We see something like a red cloud.” She said, “No, by my God! It is the dust raised by the horses of the Arabs.” Then she said to Khālid ibn Yazīd, “I adopted you only for a day such as this. I am to be killed, and I charge you to treat these two brothers of yours well.” Khālid said, “I fear that if what you say is true, they will not be spared.” She said, “Indeed, one of them will attain greater standing among the Arabs than he has today. Go, then, and secure safe-conduct for them.” Khālid went and met Ḥassān, told him what she had said, and obtained safe-conduct for her two sons.

Ḥassān had with him a group of Berbers from the Butr. He appointed the elder of the Kahina’s two sons over them and brought him close to himself. Ḥassān and his men went on and encountered the Kahina at the foot of a mountain. She was killed there, along with most of those who were with her, and the place was named Biʾr al-Kāhina. Ḥassān then withdrew and encamped at the site of the present-day city of Qayrawān in Ifrīqiya; it was the Kahina’s resting place. He said: Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān and others. He said: He built the congregational mosque of her community, established the registers, and imposed the land tax on the non-Arabs of Ifrīqiya and on those Berbers who remained Christian alongside them; most of these were Barānis, with only a few Butr. Ḥassān remained at his post until the land was brought under control. Then he set out for ʿAbd al-Malik with his spoils in Jumādā II, in the year seventy-six.

He said: Ibn Bukayr related to us; al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us; he said: Ḥassān ibn al-Nuʿmān returned from Ifrīqiya in the year seventy-eight.

[In the text, “she said” is written as “he said” in the exchange about the red cloud. The name is written “Khād” in one occurrence; it is rendered Khālid, as elsewhere in the passage.]

When Ḥassān passed through Barqa, he appointed Ibrāhīm the Christian over its tax revenues. Then he continued on, passed by ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān, who was in Egypt, and proceeded to ʿAbd al-Malik. ʿAbd al-Malik was delighted by the conquests and spoils Ḥassān brought him.

It is said, however, that ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz took from him all the captives he had with him. He had brought with him some young Berber slave women whose beauty was without equal. Naṣīb al-Shāʿir used to say: “I was present at the distribution of the captives ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz had taken from Ḥassān: two hundred slave girls, some of whom were valued at a thousand dinars.”

The killing of Zuhayr ibn Qays: He said: After Ḥassān, the Byzantines raided Anṭābulus. Ibrāhīm ibn al-Naṣrānī fled and abandoned the people of Anṭābulus and its protected inhabitants in the hands of the Byzantines. They took charge of it for forty nights, until they quickly spread corruption there. This reached ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān, who sent for Zuhayr ibn Qays. Zuhayr had gone out with Ḥassān, but when he reached Egypt, he remained there. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ordered him to march against the Byzantines. Zuhayr had only seventy men from among his companions assembled to him. A Ṣadafī notable named Jandal ibn Ṣakhr was also there; he was harsh and stern. Zuhayr said to ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān: “Since you have ordered me to go out, do not send Jandal, a Ṣadafī notable, with me, lest his severity and harshness hold the people back from me.” ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz was displeased with Zuhayr ibn Qays, because Zuhayr had fought him when his father Marwān ibn al-Ḥakam sent him from the direction of Aylah, before he entered Egypt. He said to him: “I have known you, Zuhayr, only as a boor and a lout.” Zuhayr replied: “I would not have thought, Ibn Laylā, that a man who had gathered what God revealed to Muḥammad ﷺ—before your parents had even come together could be a boor and a lout. He is neither boorish nor a lout. I am setting out; may God not bring me back to you.”

He set out, and when he reached Darnah of Ṭabarqah, in the land of Anṭābulus, he encountered the Byzantines with only seventy men. He held back so that the people could catch up with him. A young man who was with him said: “You have grown cowardly, Zuhayr.” He replied: “I have not grown cowardly, nephew, but you have killed me and yourself.” He then met them in battle. Zuhayr and all his companions were martyred; their graves are there, known to this day.

The killing of Zuhayr and his companions took place, as Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us from al-Layth, in the year 76.

He said: At Amlas, in the wilderness of Anṭābulus, there was a man of Madhḥij named ʿAṭiyyah ibn Yarbuʿ.

He went out with a son of his, fleeing the plague. There was a group of Muslims in that wilderness. He called on them for help and rallied the people around him. Seven hundred men gathered to him, and he advanced with them against the Byzantines. They fought them and defeated them; the Byzantines took refuge in their ships, and the rest of them fled.

This reached ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān, who sent there a retainer named Tulayd and dispatched with him some prominent men of Egypt, and he secured the place.

Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us from al-Layth ibn Saʿd: He said: When Zuhayr was killed, Ṭāriq was appointed over Anṭābulus. The people found it burdensome to have Tulayd lead them in prayer because he was a slave. This reached ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān, who sent Tulayd his manumission. Tulayd remained in Anṭābulus.

Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr: Ḥassān ibn al-Nuʿmān came from ʿAbd al-Malik, heading toward the Maghrib. When he reached Egypt, he said to ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz: “Write to your retainer ordering him to withdraw from Anṭābulus.” ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said to him: “I would not do that, now that I have lost it and the Byzantines have taken control of it.” Ḥassān said: “Then I shall return to the Commander of the Faithful.” ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said: “Return, then.” So Ḥassān turned back to ʿAbd al-Malik, leaving his baggage in Egypt. He arrived before ʿAbd al-Malik while the latter was ill. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz sent Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr to the Maghrib. Ḥassān told ʿAbd al-Malik of this, and ʿAbd al-Malik fell prostrate, saying: “Praise be to God, who has enabled me to deal with Mūsā, for my grief over him was so great.”

Mūsā had been ʿAbd al-Malik’s governor in Iraq alongside Bishr ibn Marwān. ʿAbd al-Malik was displeased with him and wanted to kill him, but ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ransomed him with money, recognizing Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr’s intelligence and sound judgment; Mūsā was then with him in Egypt. Ḥassān ibn al-Nuʿmān survived only a short while before he died, and Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr arrived in the Maghrib in the year 78.

Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us; al-Layth related to us: Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr was appointed over Ifrīqiyah in the year 79.

He dismissed Abū Ṣāliḥ and conquered most of the Maghrib. He sent reports of his successive conquests to ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Marwān, and sent him the spoils. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz reported them to ʿAbd al-Malik, which eased some of ʿAbd al-Malik’s resentment toward Mūsā.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us; al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us that when Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr raided the Maghrib, he sent his son Marwān with an army, and he captured one hundred thousand people as captives. He sent his brother’s son with another army, and he captured one hundred thousand. Al-Layth ibn Saʿd was asked, “Who were they?” He replied, “The Berbers.” When his letter reporting this arrived, people said: “By God, Ibn Nuṣayr is a fool! Where could he get twenty thousand to send to the Commander of the Faithful as the fifth?” This reached Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr, and he said: “Let them send someone to collect their twenty thousand.”

Then ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Marwān died. His death occurred, as Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, on Thursday, when fourteen nights of Shawwāl had passed, in the year 86. Al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik succeeded him. Conquests in the Maghrib kept arriving before al-Walīd from Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr, and Mūsā’s standing with him rose greatly, as did his admiration for him.

The account of the conquest of al-Andalus

He said: Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr sent his son Marwān ibn Mūsā to Ṭanjah to guard the coast. He and his companions were hard pressed, so he withdrew, leaving Ṭāriq ibn ʿAmr in command of his army. They numbered 1,700. It is said instead that Ṭāriq had twelve thousand Berbers with him, with only sixteen Arabs; this is not sound. It is also said that Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr set out from Ifrīqiyah on a raid to Ṭanjah, and that he was the first governor to settle there. There were Berber clans there, from both the Butr and the Barānis, who had not entered into obedience. When he drew near Ṭanjah, he sent out raiding parties. His cavalry reached the Lower Sūs, raided them, and took captives. They submitted to him, and he appointed over them a governor who treated them well. He sent Bisr ibn Abī Arṭāh to a fortress belonging to a town three days’ journey from al-Qayrawān. Bisr conquered it, took the children captive, and seized its wealth. He said: “It was named Bisr’s Fortress; to this day it is known by no other name.”

Then Mūsā dismissed the man he had appointed over Ṭanjah and appointed Ṭāriq ibn Ziyād in his place. He then returned to al-Qayrawān. Ṭāriq had gone out with a slave girl of his named Umm Ḥakīm. Ṭāriq remained there for some time, guarding the frontier. This was in the year 92.

The crossing between him and the people of al-Andalus was controlled by a non-Arab man named Yulyān, lord of Sabtah. He was in charge of a town on the crossing to al-Andalus called al-Khaḍrāʾ and al-Khaḍrāʾ lies toward Ṭanjah. Yulyān rendered obedience to Lughḏrīq, lord of al-Andalus, who lived in Ṭulayṭulah. Ṭāriq corresponded with Yulyān and dealt gently with him until they exchanged gifts. Yulyān had sent a daughter of his to Lughḏrīq, lord of al-Andalus, to be educated and taught, but Lughḏrīq impregnated her. When Yulyān learned of this, he said: “I see no punishment or recompense for him except that I bring the Arabs in against him.” He sent word to Ṭāriq: “I shall bring you into al-Andalus.” Ṭāriq was then at Tilimsān, while Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr was at al-Qayrawān. Ṭāriq said: “I shall not trust you until you send me a hostage.” So Yulyān sent him his two daughters; he had no children other than them. Ṭāriq kept them at Tilimsān and secured them.

Then Ṭāriq went out to Yulyān, who was at Sabtah on the crossing. Yulyān was glad when he arrived and said to him: “I shall bring you into al-Andalus.” Between the two crossings was a mountain now called Jabal Ṭāriq, between Sabtah and al-Andalus. At nightfall, Yulyān brought him the boats and carried him in them across the strait. He concealed him there during the day. At nightfall, he sent the boats back to the rest of his companions, who were carried across to him until not one of them remained behind. The people of al-Andalus were unaware of them and thought only that the boats were going back and forth as they had before, in the course of their usual business.

Ṭāriq was in the last group to board. He crossed over to his companions, while Yulyān and the merchants who were with him remained behind at al-Khaḍrāʾ, so as to put his companions and the people of his town more at ease.

News of Ṭāriq and his men, and of the place where they were, reached the people of al-Andalus. Ṭāriq set out, leading his companions over a mountain bridge to a village called Qartājannah, and advanced toward Qurṭubah. He passed by an island in the sea and left there a slave girl of his named Umm Ḥakīm, along with a group of his soldiers. From that day onward, the island was called the Island of Umm Ḥakīm.

When the Muslims had landed on the island, they found some vine growers there, and no one else. They seized them, then deliberately took one of the vine growers, slaughtered him, bit into him, and cooked him, while the rest of the men watched. They had also cooked meat in other pots. When it was done, they discarded the meat of the man they had cooked, without the others knowing that they had thrown it away, and ate the meat they had cooked. The remaining vine growers watched them and became convinced that they had eaten their companion’s flesh. Then the Muslims released the survivors, who told the people of al-Andalus that the Muslims ate human flesh and told them what had been done to the vine grower.

He said: In al-Andalus, as my father ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam and Hishām ibn Isḥāq related to us, there was a house with locks on it. Each king who succeeded would add a lock of his own, until the king who was ruling when the Muslims entered came to it. They urged him to put a lock on it, as the kings before him had done, but he refused, saying: “I would not put anything on it until I know what is inside.” He ordered it opened, and inside were images of Arabs and an inscription that said: “When this door is opened, these people will enter this land.”

Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān and others: When Ṭāriq crossed over, the troops of Qurṭubah met him and grew bold against him because of the small number of his men whom they saw. They fought, and the battle grew fierce. Then they were defeated. Ṭāriq kept killing them until they reached the city of Qurṭubah.

This reached Lughḏrīq, who advanced against them from Ṭulayṭulah. They met at a place called Shadhūnah, beside a river now called the River of Umm Ḥakīm. They fought a fierce battle, and God—Mighty and Glorious—is the one who slew Lughḏrīq and those with him.

Muʿtab al-Rūmī, a retainer of al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, commanded Ṭāriq’s cavalry. Muʿtab al-Rūmī advanced toward Qurṭubah, while Ṭāriq proceeded to Ṭulayṭulah and entered it. He asked about the table, for nothing else occupied his mind. It was the table of Sulaymān ibn Dāwūd, as the People of the Book claim.

Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us; al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us: Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr conquered al-Andalus and took from it the table of Sulaymān ibn Dāwūd ﵇ and the crown. Ṭāriq was told: “The table is in a fortress called Firās, two days’ journey from Ṭulayṭulah. A nephew of Lughḏrīq is in command of the fortress.” Ṭāriq sent him a guarantee of safety for himself and his household. The man came down to him; Ṭāriq granted him safety and kept his word. Ṭāriq said to him: “Hand over the table.” He handed it over, and it bore gold and jewels the like of which had never been seen.

Ṭāriq removed one of its legs, with its jewels and gold, and made another leg for it in its place. The table was valued at two hundred thousand dinars for its jewels. Ṭāriq took whatever jewels, weapons, gold, silver, and vessels were in the man’s possession. He also acquired wealth the like of which had never been seen. He gathered all of it together, then returned to Qurṭubah and stayed there. He wrote to Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr, informing him of the conquest of al-Andalus and the spoils he had acquired. Mūsā wrote to al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik to inform him of this, and claimed the conquest for himself. Mūsā wrote to Ṭāriq, ordering him not to go beyond Qurṭubah until Mūsā arrived, and rebuked him harshly.

Then Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr set out for al-Andalus in Rajab of the year 93, with the leading Arabs, the clients, and the Berber officers, until he entered al-Andalus. He was enraged with Ṭāriq. Ḥabīb ibn Abī ʿUbaydah al-Fihrī went out with him. Mūsā appointed his son ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mūsā, the eldest of his children, as his deputy over al-Qayrawān. He crossed over from al-Khaḍrāʾ, then proceeded to Qurṭubah. Ṭāriq met him and sought to appease him, saying: “I am only your client, and this conquest is yours.” Mūsā gathered such wealth that it cannot be described, and Ṭāriq handed over to him all that he had captured.

He said: It is also said that Lughḏrīq advanced against Ṭāriq while Ṭāriq was in the mountain. When Lughḏrīq reached him, Ṭāriq went out to meet him. Lughḏrīq was then on his royal throne, which was carried between two mules. He wore his crown and his gauntlets, and all the ornaments worn by kings before him.

Ṭāriq and his companions went out against him on foot, none of them mounted. They fought from sunrise until sunset, thinking it would be their destruction. God slew Lughḏrīq and those with him, and granted the Muslims victory. Never in the Maghrib had there been a slaughter greater than this. The Muslims did not stop striking them with the sword for three days. Then the people set out for Qurṭubah.

It is also said that Mūsā was the one who sent Ṭāriq, after his entry into al-Andalus, to Ṭulayṭulah, which lies halfway between Qurṭubah and Arbūnah; Arbūnah is the farthest frontier of al-Andalus. ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz’s letters reached Arbūnah. Then the people of unbelief overcame it, and it is in their hands today. It is said that Ṭāriq acquired the table there.

Lughḏrīq ruled over two thousand miles, from the coast and beyond it. The people acquired abundant spoils of gold and silver.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us; al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us: He said: A carpet might be found woven with rods of gold, with a chain of gold strung with pearls, rubies, and emeralds. The Berbers would sometimes find one and be unable to carry it, so they would fetch an axe and strike it in the middle. One would take half and the other the other half for themselves, while a group of them moved on and the people were occupied with other things.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us; al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us: When al-Andalus was conquered, a man came to Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr and said: “Send men with me; I will show you a treasure.” He sent men with him. The man said to them: “Dig here.” So they dug. He said: A stream of emeralds and rubies poured over them, unlike anything they had ever seen. When they saw it, they were overawed and said: “Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr will not believe us.” So they sent for him, and he came and saw it.

ʿAbd al-Malik related to us; al-Layth related to us that when Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr conquered al-Andalus, he wrote to [al-Walīd ibn] ʿAbd al-Malik: “These are not conquests; this is the Resurrection.”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us; Mālik ibn Anas related to us, from Yaḥyā ibn Saʿīd, who said: When al-Andalus was conquered, the people acquired spoils there, and they embezzled much of it. They carried it aboard ships and set sail in them. When they were in the middle of the sea, they heard a caller say: “O God, drown them!” They prayed to God and hung Qurʾāns around their necks. He said: It was not long before a violent wind struck them, and the ships crashed against one another until they broke apart and sank with those aboard.

The people of Egypt reject this account and say: Those who drowned were not the people of al-Andalus, but the people of Sardinia. The people of Sardinia, as Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us, when the Muslims set out against them, went to a harbor of theirs in the sea, sealed it off, and drained the water from it. Then they threw their gold and silver vessels into it and restored the water to its former state. They also went to a church of theirs, made a roof beneath its roof, and placed whatever wealth they had between the two roofs.

A Muslim man went down to bathe in the place they had sealed off. They restored the water over it, and his foot touched something. He pulled it out and found a silver platter. He dived again and brought something else out. When the Muslims learned of this, they held the water back from the place and took all those vessels. A Muslim entered the church with a crossbow and looked toward a pigeon. He shot at it, missed it, and struck a wooden panel, breaking it. The wealth came pouring down upon them, and the Muslims embezzled much that day. A man might take a cat, slaughter it, throw away what was inside its belly, then stuff it with what he had embezzled, sew it up, and throw it on the road, so that anyone who saw it would think it dead. Then, when he went out, he would take it. A man might also pull out his sword blade, throw it away, fill the scabbard with embezzled goods, and place the sword’s hilt on the scabbard.

When they boarded the ships and set out, they heard a caller crying: “O God, drown them!” They hung Qurʾāns around their necks, and they all drowned except Abū ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Ḥablī and Ḥanash ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Sabāʾī, for neither of them had been involved in any embezzlement.

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us; Ibn Lahīʿah related to us: He said: I heard Abū al-Aswad say:

I heard ʿAmr ibn Aws say: Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr sent me to search the men of ʿAṭāʾ ibn Rāfiʿ, the client of Hudhayl, after their ships were wrecked. Sometimes I would find a man who had hidden dinars in a rag, in a place between his testicles. He said: A man leaning on a reed cane passed by me. I went to search him, but he resisted me. I grew angry, seized the cane, and struck him with it. It broke, and the dinars spilled out of it, so I began gathering them.

ʿAbd al-Malik related to us; al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us. He said: It reached me that a man on the expedition of ʿAṭāʾ ibn Rāfiʿ, or another expedition in the Maghrib, had stolen something. He carried it along until he had put it in pitch. At the time of his death, he kept crying out, “The pitch! The pitch!”

He said: Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr seized Ṭāriq ibn ʿAmr, bound him tightly, and imprisoned him, intending to kill him. Muʿattab al-Rūmī was a servant of al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik. Ṭāriq sent him a message: “If you bring my case to al-Walīd, and tell him that the conquest of al-Andalus was at my hands and that Mūsā has imprisoned me and intends to kill me, I will give you one hundred slaves.” He made a pledge to him to that effect. When Muʿattab was about to depart, he took leave of Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr and said to him:

“Do not be hasty with Ṭāriq. You have enemies, and the Commander of the Faithful has heard of his case. I fear his anger against you.”

Muʿattab departed, while Mūsā was in al-Andalus.

When Muʿattab arrived before al-Walīd, he told him what had happened regarding the conquest of al-Andalus at Ṭāriq’s hands, Mūsā’s imprisonment of him, and Mūsā’s intention to kill him. Al-Walīd wrote to Mūsā, swearing to him by God: “If you strike him, I will strike you; and if you kill him, I will kill your son in his place.” He sent the letter with Muʿattab al-Rūmī. Muʿattab brought it to Mūsā in al-Andalus. When Mūsā read it, he released Ṭāriq and let him go. Ṭāriq fulfilled his promise to Muʿattab, giving him the one hundred slaves he had pledged.

Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr left al-Andalus with his spoils, jewels, and the table. He appointed his son ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Mūsā over al-Andalus. Mūsā had stayed in al-Andalus during the years ninety-three and ninety-four, and for some months of the year ninety-five. When Mūsā arrived in Ifrīqiya, al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik wrote to him ordering him to come to him. Mūsā departed, appointing his son ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mūsā over Ifrīqiya. He traveled with those spoils and gifts until he reached Egypt. Al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik fell ill and kept writing to Mūsā, urging him to hurry, while Sulaymān wrote to him to remain and stay, so that al-Walīd would die and what Mūsā had with him would pass to Sulaymān. Mūsā set out, and when he was at Ṭabariyya, news reached him of al-Walīd’s death. He arrived before Sulaymān with those gifts, and Sulaymān was pleased by them.

It is said that when Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr returned from al-Andalus, he did not alight at al-Qayrawān. He left it behind and alighted at Qaṣr al-Māʾ. He sacrificed there, then set out, and Ṭāriq set out with him.

Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, who said: Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr returned as a delegate to the Commander of the Faithful in the year ninety-six, and entered al-Fusṭāṭ on Thursday, with six nights remaining in Rabīʿ al-Awwal.

Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān ibn Ṣāliḥ and others, and said: While Sulaymān was turning over those gifts, a man from among the companions of Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr, called ʿĪsā ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Ṭawīl, who was from the people of Medina and had been in charge of the spoils, came forward and said: “Commander of the Faithful, God has enriched you with what is lawful, leaving you no need of what is unlawful. I am the one responsible for these shares, and Mūsā did not take out a fifth from everything he brought you.” Sulaymān became angry and rose from his throne. He went inside his house, then came out to the people and said: “Indeed, God has enriched me with what is lawful, leaving me no need of what is unlawful.” He ordered that the goods be brought into the treasury. Sulaymān had ordered Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr to submit his requests and those of his companions, then return to the Maghrib.

He said: It is also said that Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr came before al-Walīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik when al-Walīd was ill. Mūsā presented him with the table. Ṭāriq said, “I found it,” but Mūsā denied it. Ṭāriq said to al-Walīd, “Have the table brought, and see whether anything is missing from it.” Al-Walīd had it brought, and looked. One of its legs was unlike the others. Ṭāriq said to him, “Ask him about the leg, Commander of the Faithful. If he tells you something by which you can judge that he is truthful, then he is telling the truth.” Al-Walīd asked Mūsā about the leg, and Mūsā said, “That is how I found it.” Ṭāriq brought out the leg he had taken from the table when he found it, and said, “The Commander of the Faithful can use this to judge the truth of what I told him: that I found it.” Al-Walīd believed Ṭāriq, accepted his account, and greatly increased his reward.

Then he returned to the account of ʿUthmān and others, and said: After ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Mūsā’s father departed he married a Christian woman, the daughter of a king from among the people of al-Andalus. It is said that she was the daughter of Roderic, king of al-Andalus, whom Ṭāriq had killed. She brought him a great deal of worldly wealth beyond description. When she came to him, she said, “Why do I not see the people of your kingdom honoring you and prostrating themselves before you as the people of my father’s kingdom used to honor him and prostrate themselves before him?” He did not know what to say to her. He ordered a doorway to be cut for him in one side of his palace and made it low. He would admit people, and anyone entering would come through the doorway with his head bowed because it was low. She watched the people from a place where she could see them. When she saw this, she said to ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz, “Now your kingdom is strong.”

People heard that he had cut the doorway for this reason, and some claimed that she had made him a Christian. Ḥabīb ibn Abī ʿUbayda al-Fihrī, Ziyād ibn al-Nābigha al-Tamīmī, and companions of theirs from the Arab tribes rose against him. They agreed to kill ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz because of what they had heard about his affairs. They went to his muezzin and said, “Make the call to prayer at night, so that we may go out to pray.” The muezzin called to prayer, then repeated the summons to prayer. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz came out and said to his muezzin:

“You have hurried and called to prayer at night.”

Then he set out toward the mosque. Those men and others who had come to pray had gathered there. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz stepped forward and began reciting, “When the Event comes to pass—there is no denying its coming—abasing, exalting”. Ḥabīb placed his sword on ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz’s head. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz fled and ran until he entered his house. He went into a garden of his and hid beneath a tree. Ḥabīb ibn Abī ʿUbayda and his companions fled, while Ziyād ibn al-Nābigha pursued him. He entered after him and found him beneath the tree. ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz said to him, “Ibn al-Nābigha, save me, and you shall have whatever you ask.” He replied, “You will not taste life after this.” He finished him off and cut off his head. When Ḥabīb and his companions heard of it, they returned.

Then they set out with ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz’s head to Sulaymān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik. They appointed Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr’s sister’s son, Ayyūb, over al-Andalus. They passed through al-Qayrawān, where ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr was in charge, but he did not oppose them. They continued until they came before Sulaymān with the head of ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Mūsā. They placed it before him, and Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr was present. Sulaymān said to him, “Do you recognize this?” He said, “Yes. I knew him as one who fasted much and prayed through the night. May God’s curse be upon the one who killed him, if the killer is better than he was.”

The killing of ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn Mūsā took place, as Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, in the year ninety-seven.

He said: Sulaymān was displeased with Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr and handed him over to Ḥabīb ibn Abī ʿUbayda and his companions to take him out to Ifrīqiya. He sought protection from Ayyūb ibn Sulaymān, who granted it to him and interceded for him with his father.

It is said that Sulaymān seized Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr, imposed a fine of one hundred thousand dinars on him, and made him liable for it. He took everything Mūsā owned. Mūsā sought the protection of Yazīd ibn al-Muhallab, who asked Sulaymān to grant him to him. Sulaymān granted him and his property to Yazīd, restored the property to him, and imposed nothing on him.

For years thereafter, the people of al-Andalus had no governor to unite them.

Sulaymān resolved to make the pilgrimage and sent Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr out with his saddle raised. Mūsā set out, and when he was at al-Marr, he died. His death was in the year ninety-seven, according to what Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd.

Then Muḥammad ibn Yazīd al-Qurashī governed Ifrīqiya. Sulaymān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik appointed him on the advice of Rajāʾ ibn Ḥaywa, and dismissed ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mūsā in the year ninety-six.

Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth, who said: Muḥammad ibn Yazīd was appointed over Ifrīqiya in the year ninety-seven. Muḥammad ibn Yazīd remained governor until Sulaymān ibn ʿAbd al-Malik died.

His death occurred, as Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, on Friday, with ten nights remaining in Ṣafar of the year ninety-nine. He was dismissed, and Ismāʿīl ibn ʿUbayd Allāh was appointed in his place in al-Muḥarram of the year one hundred, in charge of its military affairs, land tax, and alms. He was a governor of good conduct. At that time, there was not a single Berber in his province who had not become Muslim. He remained its governor until ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz died.

His death occurred, as Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, on Friday, with ten nights remaining in Rajab of the year one hundred and one. He was dismissed, and Yazīd ibn Abī Muslim, al-Ḥajjāj’s secretary, was appointed in his place. Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik appointed him in the year one hundred and one.

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr was then in the East. He came to Ifrīqiya with Yazīd ibn Abī Muslim. When they were close to it, people came out to meet them. When Yazīd entered al-Qayrawān, he ordered ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr to return to his home. ʿAbd Allāh went to his house, and Yazīd ordered the people to follow him until they thought that he was his associate. When ʿAbd Allāh had gone, Yazīd sent a messenger after him with the order: “Prepare from your own wealth five years’ pay for the army.”

Then Yazīd ibn Abī Muslim seized the clients of Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr from among the Berbers, branded their hands, and divided them into five groups. He took a census of their property and children, then made them his guards and intimates. He seized Muḥammad ibn Yazīd al-Qurashī, tortured him, and flogged him painfully. Muḥammad asked him for water, and he gave him ashes to drink. Muḥammad ibn Yazīd had been in charge of torturing Yazīd ibn Abī Muslim in the East in al-Ḥajjāj’s time. Yazīd said to him, “When morning comes, I will torture you until you die, or until I die before you.” He had built a narrow room for him in the prison and put him in it. He dressed him in a thick woolen robe and sealed it with a lead signet.

When Yazīd ibn Abī Muslim had finished his evening meal, grapes were brought to him as the last course. He took a bunch, and a man from his guard named Ḥurayz sprang at him with a sword and struck him until he killed him. He cut off his head and threw it into the mosque at night. A servant of Muḥammad ibn Yazīd came, entered the prison, and said to him, “Rejoice, for Yazīd has been killed.” Muḥammad said to him, “You are lying,” thinking that the message had been planted on him. Then another of his servants came to him, then another, until seven of them had gathered. When Muḥammad was certain that Yazīd was dead, he set the slaves free.

It is also said that when Yazīd ibn Abī Muslim arrived, his guard consisted only of Barānis. They had been the governors’ guards before him, exclusively Barānis; there was not one Zanāta among them. Yazīd ibn Abī Muslim addressed the people and said, “If I come to hold authority properly, I will brand the hands of my guards as the Byzantines do: I will brand a man’s right hand with his name and his left with ‘guard,’ so that they can be recognized as distinct from others.” They resented this, and some of them secretly plotted with others to kill him. That night, he went out to the mosque for the sunset prayer, and they killed him at his place of prayer. His killing took place, as Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, in the year one hundred and two.

When Yazīd ibn Abī Muslim was killed, the people gathered and considered who should take charge of their affairs until the decision of Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik arrived. They agreed upon al-Mughīra ibn Abī Burda al-Qurashī, one of the Banū ʿAbd al-Dār. His son ʿAbd Allāh said to him, “Sheikh, this man was killed in your presence. If you take charge after him, I cannot be sure that the Commander of the Faithful will not hold you responsible for his killing.” The sheikh accepted this. The people of Ifrīqiya then agreed upon Muḥammad ibn Aws al-Anṣārī, who was in Tunis, leading its naval campaign. They sent for him and put him in charge of their affairs. He wrote to Yazīd, informing him of what had happened. Yazīd sent Khālid ibn Abī ʿImrān, who was from the people of Tunis, about this. He came before Yazīd, who accepted what they had done and pardoned them for their lapse.

Khālid ibn Abī ʿImrān said: Yazīd summoned me privately and said, “What sort of man is Muḥammad ibn Aws?”

I said, “A man of religion and virtue, known for his jurisprudence.” He said, “Was there no Qurashī there?” I said, “Yes: al-Mughīra ibn Abī Burda.” He said, “I know him. Why did he not take charge?” I said, “He refused and preferred to keep to himself.” He fell silent.

People suspected that ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr had been the one who arranged the killing of Yazīd ibn Abī Muslim. Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik appointed Bishr ibn Ṣafwān al-Kalbī over Ifrīqiya in the year one hundred and two. Bishr had been his governor in Egypt. He set out for Ifrīqiya, leaving his brother Ḥanẓala as his deputy in Egypt. When he entered Ifrīqiya, he was told that ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mūsā was the one who had instigated the killing of Yazīd ibn Abī Muslim. Khālid ibn Abī Ḥabīb al-Qurashī and others testified to this. Bishr wrote to Yazīd ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, who wrote to Bishr ibn Ṣafwān ordering him to kill ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr. Bishr intended to delay him for some days, but Khālid ibn Abī Ḥabīb and Muḥammad ibn Abī Bakr said to Bishr ibn Ṣafwān, “Hurry and kill him before the pardon from the Commander of the Faithful reaches him.”

ʿAbd Allāh’s mother, the daughter of Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr, was married to al-Rabīʿ, the holder of Yazīd’s signet. He spoke to Yazīd, who ordered that ʿAbd Allāh be pardoned. His sister promised the messenger three thousand dinars if he could reach him in time. Bishr ordered the killing of ʿAbd Allāh ibn Mūsā, and he was killed. The messenger arrived with the pardon after ʿAbd Allāh had been killed that same day. Bishr sent his head with Sulaymān ibn Waʿla al-Tamīmī to Yazīd, who had it displayed.

Then Bishr ibn Ṣafwān went as a delegate to Yazīd with gifts he had prepared for him. When he was partway along the road, news reached him of Yazīd’s death. His death occurred, as Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, on the night of al-Jumla, with four nights remaining in Shaʿbān of the year one hundred and five.

Bishr brought those gifts to Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik, who sent him back to Ifrīqiya. Bishr arrived there, sought out the wealth of Mūsā ibn Nuṣayr, tortured his officials, and appointed ʿAnbasa ibn Suḥaym al-Kalbī over al-Andalus in place of al-Ḥurr ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Qaysī. Bishr had raided by sea from Ifrīqiya, but a great terror befell them, and many of his troops perished as a result. Bishr ibn Ṣafwān then died of an illness called al-dubayla, in Shawwāl of the year one hundred and nine.

Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, who said: Bishr ibn Ṣafwān was removed from Ifrīqiya in the year one hundred and five, returned to it in the year one hundred and six, and died in the year one hundred and nine.

When Bishr ibn Ṣafwān died, he appointed Nughāsh ibn Qurṭ al-Kalbī as his deputy over Ifrīqiya. Hishām dismissed him and appointed ʿUbayda ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Qaysī over Ifrīqiya in Ṣafar of the year one hundred and ten.

Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth, who said: ʿUbayda ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān took charge of Ifrīqiya in al-Muḥarram of the year one hundred and ten. When ʿUbayda arrived in Ifrīqiya, he sent al-Mustanīr ibn al-Ḥabḥāb al-Ḥarashī on a raid against Sicily. A wind struck them and drowned them. The vessel on which al-Mustanīr was sailing reached the coast of Tripoli. ʿUbayda ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān wrote to his governor in Tripoli, Yazīd ibn Muslim al-Kindī, ordering him to bind al-Mustanīr tightly and send him with a trustworthy escort. He sent him in chains. When al-Mustanīr arrived before ʿUbayda, ʿUbayda flogged him painfully, paraded him around al-Qayrawān on a donkey, and then had him flogged once every Friday, until he had got hold of him. This was because al-Mustanīr had stayed in Byzantine territory until winter set in and the sea’s waves and storms grew fierce; he had remained imprisoned with him.

ʿUbayda had appointed ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-ʿAkkī over al-Andalus. He was a righteous man. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān raided the Franks, the farthest enemy of al-Andalus, and took abundant booty. He defeated them, and among what he seized was a gold statue studded with pearls, rubies, and emeralds. He ordered it broken, then took out the fifth and distributed all the rest among the Muslims who were with him. When ʿUbayda heard of this, he became very angry and wrote him a threatening letter. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān wrote back to him: “If the heavens and the earth were closed up, the All-Merciful would make a way out of them for the God-fearing.” Then he went out raiding them again, and was martyred along with most of his companions. His killing took place, as Yaḥyā related to us from al-Layth, in the year one hundred and fifteen.

After him, ʿUbayda appointed ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Qaṭan over al-Andalus. Then ʿUbayda set out for Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik with gifts. This was in Ramaḍān of the year one hundred and fourteen.

Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, who said: ʿUbayda ibn ʿAbd al-Raḥmān arrived from Ifrīqiya in the year one hundred and fifteen, and in that year he appointed Ibn Qaṭan over al-Andalus.

Among what he took with him were male and female slaves and seven hundred selected slave girls, as well as other eunuchs, horses, beasts of burden, gold, silver, and vessels.

When ʿUbayda left, he appointed ʿUqba ibn Qudāma al-Tujībī as his deputy over Ifrīqiya. ʿUqba arrived before Hishām with his gifts and asked to be relieved of office. Hishām relieved him and wrote to ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Ḥabḥāb, his governor in Egypt, ordering him to proceed to Ifrīqiya and appointing him governor there. This was in Rabīʿ al-Ākhir of the year one hundred and sixteen.

ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Ḥabḥāb arrived in Ifrīqiya, released al-Mustanīr from prison, and appointed him over Tunis. He appointed his son Ismāʿīl ibn ʿUbayd Allāh over the Sūs, appointed his son al-Qāsim ibn ʿUbayd Allāh as his deputy in Egypt, and appointed ʿUqba ibn al-Ḥajjāj over al-Andalus, dismissing ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Qaṭan.

It is said, however, that the governor of al-Andalus at that time was ʿAnbasa ibn Suḥaym al-Kalbī. Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb dismissed him and appointed ʿUqba ibn al-Ḥajjāj. ʿUqba ibn al-Ḥajjāj died in al-Andalus, so ʿUbayd Allāh restored ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Qaṭan to the governorship there.

ʿUbayd Allāh sent Ḥabīb ibn Abī ʿUbayda al-Fihrī on a raid against the Sūs and the land of the Blacks.

He won a victory such as none had ever seen, and took as much gold as he wished. Among what he captured were one or two slave girls of a kind the Berbers call Ijjān, each of whom had only one breast. Then he also raided by sea, and afterward withdrew.

The Berbers at Tangier rebelled against ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Ḥabḥāb and killed his governor, ʿUmar ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Murādī. The man who led this was Maysara al-Faqīr al-Barbarī, then al-Madgharī. He was the one who took charge of the Berbers, claimed the caliphate, took that title, and received the oath of allegiance. Maysara then appointed ʿAbd al-Aʿlā ibn Jurayj al-Ifrīqī over Tangier. He was originally a Roman and was a client of Ibn Nuṣayr.

Then he marched to the Sūs, where Ismāʿīl ibn ʿUbayd Allāh was in charge, and killed him. This was the beginning of the Berber uprising in the land of Ifrīqiya.

ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Ḥabḥāb sent Khālid ibn Abī Ḥabīb al-Fihrī against the Berbers at Tangier. With him were the leading men of Ifrīqiya, from Quraysh, the Anṣār, and others. Khālid and his companions were killed; not one escaped. That campaign was therefore called the Campaign of the Nobles. It is said that Khālid encountered Maysara outside Tangier and was killed along with those who were with him.

Maysara then returned to Tangier. The Berbers disapproved of his conduct and of his changing from what they had pledged allegiance to him to do, so they killed him and placed ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Qaṭan al-Muḥāribī in charge of their affairs.

Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, who said: “There was a dispute between Maysara al-Faqīr and the Berbers of Ifrīqiya .... Ismāʿīl ibn ʿUbayd Allāh and Khālid ibn Abī Ḥabīb were killed in the year 123.”

Ibn al-Ḥabḥāb sent Ḥabīb ibn Abī ʿUbayda against them. When he reached Tilimsān, he seized Mūsā ibn Abī Khālid, a client of Muʿāwiya ibn Ḥudayj, who was in charge of Tilimsān. Those who remained loyal had gathered around him. Ḥabīb suspected that he might have sympathies of his own or might have instigated the revolt, so he cut off his hand and foot. He had been stationed at Tilimsān with his army. ʿUbayd Allāh ibn al-Ḥabḥāb returned to Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik in Jumādā I of the year 123.

Then Hishām appointed Kulthūm ibn ʿIyāḍ al-Qushayrī over Ifrīqiya in Jumādā II of the year 123, and sent Balj ibn Bishr ahead of him. When Kulthūm arrived in Ifrīqiya, he ordered its people to make preparations and go out with him against the Berbers. He requisitioned a contingent from the people of Tripoli, and a great force set out. He appointed ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUqba al-Ghifārī as his deputy in al-Qayrawān and Maslama ibn Sawāda al-Qurashī over the army. After Kulthūm had set out toward the Berbers of Tangier, ʿAkkāsha ibn Ayyūb al-Fazārī, a Ṣufrī, rebelled against him from the direction of Qābis. He sent a brother of his, who came to Sabrat. There he gathered Zanāta and besieged the people of the market of Sabrat in their mosque. Ḥabīb ibn Maymūn was in charge of them.

The news reached Ṣafwān ibn Abī Mālik, who was governor of Tripoli. He went out with his men and came upon the Fazārī’s brother, who was besieging the people of Sabrat. He fought them, and the Fazārī was routed; his companions from Zanāta and others were killed. He fled to his brother at Qābis.

Maslama ibn Sawāda went out with the people of al-Qayrawān against ʿAkkāsha ibn Ayyūb at Qābis and fought him. Maslama was routed, and most of those who went out with him were killed. He reached al-Qayrawān, and most of the people of al-Qayrawān who had been with Maslama took refuge there, with Saʿīd ibn Bajra al-Ghassānī in charge of them.

It is said that when Kulthūm ibn ʿIyāḍ came from Hishām, he left al-Qayrawān without stopping there or entering it. He camped at Sabība, which is a day’s journey from the city of al-Qayrawān. He broke his fast there and wrote to Ḥabīb ibn Abī ʿUbayda, ordering him not to leave his camp until he arrived. Kulthūm then set out on campaign until he reached Ḥabīb, and the two of them then marched together, with their men, toward Tangier.

When Kulthūm went out against the Berbers, he had appointed Balj ibn Bishr al-Qaysī to lead his cavalry vanguard. When Balj arrived at Ḥabīb’s camp, Ḥabīb rejected him and treated his position with contempt. Then Kulthūm arrived. Ḥabīb received him, but also treated him disdainfully. Kulthūm then addressed the people from his watchtower, attacking Ḥabīb and insulting him and his household. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb was with his father, Ḥabīb.

Then Kulthūm and Ḥabīb advanced. When they reached their destination in the land of Tangier, the Berbers met them in force, led by Khālid ibn Ḥumayd al-Zanātī, then al-Hatūrī. They were naked and stripped down, wearing nothing but trousers. They were Ṣufrīs and came without baggage. Ḥabīb ibn Abī ʿUbayda advised Kulthūm to have the foot soldiers fight the foot soldiers and the cavalry fight the cavalry. Kulthūm said to him, “We have no need of your advice, son of Umm Ḥabīb.”

He sent Balj ibn Bishr with the cavalry to trample them underfoot, for Kulthūm had more confidence in the cavalry than in the foot soldiers. Balj rode through the night until he engaged them at daybreak. They met him naked and stripped down. The cavalry charged them, and they shouted, turned in flight, and threw al-awṣāf10 Balj was routed, wounded. The horses collapsed upon Kulthūm, who had made ready and arrayed his companions. He sent to Ḥabīb ibn Abī ʿUbayda and said, “The Commander of the Faithful ordered me to put you in charge of the fighting and to appoint you over the people.” Ḥabīb said, “The moment has passed.”

The Berber foot soldiers advanced in pursuit of the cavalry until they closed in on Kulthūm and his companions. Ḥabīb swore his son ʿAbd al-Raḥmān not to dismount, but to stay with Balj and remain with him, out of grief for Balj, saying, “For I am doomed.” Kulthūm, Ḥabīb, and those with them perished, and the people fled to Ifrīqiya. Kulthūm was killed in the year 123.

Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, who said: “Kulthūm was killed in the year 124. Maysara killed them. Balj ibn Bishr, Thaʿlaba al-Judhamī, and the rest of the people of Syria were routed to al-Andalus. Abū Yūsuf al-Hawwārī, a tyrant among the Berber tyrants, pursued them and caught up with them. He fought them, and Abū Yūsuf was killed; his companions were routed, while Balj and Thaʿlaba went on to al-Andalus.

“Kulthūm had written to the people of al-Andalus, where ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Qaṭan al-Fihrī was in charge, ordering them to send reinforcements and go out to join him. Balj arrived as they had reached the crossing at al-Khaḍrāʾ. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb went ahead of Balj to al-Andalus, arrived there, and ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Qaṭan ordered that Balj should neither be listened to nor obeyed. Balj then arrived and stayed at al-Jazīra. He wrote to ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Qaṭan, informing him that he was”

Kulthūm’s successor. Thaʿlaba al-Judhamī and his companions testified to this on his behalf. The judge of al-Andalus carried messages between them. ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Qaṭan handed the governorship over to Balj, though ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb opposed this. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān left Cordoba, unwilling to accept Balj’s rule.

Then, when Balj arrived in Cordoba, he imprisoned ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Qaṭan. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb and Umayya ibn ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Qaṭan rose up, and the two of them gathered forces to fight Balj. Balj brought ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Qaṭan out of prison and said to him, “Stand in the mosque and tell the people that Kulthūm wrote to you that I am his successor.” ʿAbd al-Malik stood up and said, “People, I am Kulthūm’s governor, and I am imprisoned without right.” Balj struck off his head.

Then ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb arrived with forces. Balj and the people of Syria with him went out to meet him. A river lay between them. When night fell, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān crossed over to Cordoba. Balj’s deputy there was the judge, who had been suspected of involvement in ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Qaṭan’s death. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb seized him, gouged out his eyes, cut off his hands and feet, struck off his head, and crucified him on a tree. He placed a pig’s head on his corpse. Balj knew nothing of this. Then he left Cordoba and Balj fought him, routing ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān gathered another force and killed Balj and those with him.

It is said that Balj was not killed, but died a natural death.

Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, who said: “Balj died in the year 125, a month after killing Ibn Qaṭan.

“The people of al-Andalus then split among four commanders, until Ḥanẓala ibn Ṣafwān al-Kalbī sent Abū al-Khaṭṭār al-Kalbī to them, who brought them together. I shall recount that in its proper place, God willing.”

Kulthūm ibn ʿIyāḍ had written to his governor in Tripoli, Ṣafwān ibn Abī Mālik, asking him for reinforcements. Ṣafwān went out with the people of Tripoli and reached Qābis. When news of what had happened to Kulthūm and those with him reached him, he turned back. Saʿīd ibn Bajra and those of Maslama ibn Sawāda al-Judhamī’s companions who had taken refuge with him had gone out against Ṣafwān. The Fazārī withdrew to a river called al-Jamma, twelve miles from Qābis. When Ṣafwān ibn Abī Mālik returned, Saʿīd ibn Bajra and his companions took refuge in Qābis. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUqba al-Ghifārī went out from al-Qayrawān with its people against the Fazārī. He met him between Qābis and al-Qayrawān; the Fazārī was routed, and most of his companions were killed.

Then Hishām ibn ʿAbd al-Malik appointed Ḥanẓala ibn Ṣafwān in Ṣafar of the year 124.

He had been Hishām’s governor in Egypt. When he arrived in Ifrīqiya, the people of al-Andalus, the people of Syria, and others wrote to him asking him to send them a governor. He sent Abū al-Khaṭṭār. When Abū al-Khaṭṭār arrived there, they rendered him their allegiance; he took charge of the province, and it submitted to him. He dispersed the forces of Balj ibn Bishr and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb. He sent Thaʿlaba ibn Salāma away to Ifrīqiya in a ship, and afterward sent ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb away. He sent the people of Syria with Thaʿlaba, and they were at al-Qayrawān with Ḥanẓala.

Then Ḥanẓala ibn Ṣafwān sent ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUqba al-Ghifārī against ʿAkkāsha ibn Ayyūb al-Fazārī, who had gathered forces after his defeat at Qābis. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān met him with his men, and the Fazārī was routed; most of his companions were killed. The Fazārī gathered forces again, and ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUqba met him and defeated him. He gathered another force, and ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Yazīd al-Hawwārī, then al-Madhamī, came to him. He was a Ṣufrī and had joined with the Fazārī to fight Ḥanẓala ibn Ṣafwān. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUqba went out against the two of them with the people of Ifrīqiya. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUqba and his companions were killed. According to what Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth, ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿUqba was killed in the year 124.

Then ʿAbd al-Wāḥid ibn Yazīd went on, took Tunis, and gained control of it. He was hailed as caliph. Then he advanced toward al-Qayrawān, while the Fazārī withdrew with his army to one side. Both were heading for al-Qayrawān, racing to see which would reach it first and plunder it. When Ḥanẓala saw the Berber forces gathering around them, alongside the Fazārī and ʿAbd al-Wāḥid, he dug a trench around al-Qayrawān. ʿAbd al-Wāḥid advanced toward them and wrote to Ḥanẓala, ordering him to surrender al-Qayrawān and its people to him. They were at their wits’ end, and thought they would be taken captive; indeed, Ḥanẓala would send a messenger from among them to bring him news, but the messenger would not go out as far as three miles unless he was given fifty dinars.

When ʿAbd al-Wāḥid came upon him, he was about a stage’s journey from al-Qayrawān, at a place called al-Aṣnām. The Fazārī was camped six miles from al-Qayrawān. Abū Qurra al-ʿUqaylī was with ʿAbd al-Wāḥid and commanded his vanguard. Ḥanẓala wrote the Fazārī a letter, expressing sympathy for him and making him promises, in the hope that the two would not join forces against him, since he could not withstand them both. He feared that they would unite.

ʿAkkāsha was closer to Ḥanẓala. At dawn, ʿAbd al-Wāḥid attacked al-Aṣnām with his forces, and Ḥanẓala advanced against the Fazārī, who was near him. He went out with the people of al-Qayrawān. Some men who had despaired of survival went out, because they feared the capture of children, the loss of women and property. He appointed Muḥammad ibn ʿAmr ibn ʿUqba over them. They met ʿAbd al-Wāḥid at al-Aṣnām. God routed ʿAbd al-Wāḥid and his force. He and those with him were killed in such numbers that none could tell what had become of them; some of them fled.

When victory opened the way for Ḥanẓala, he attacked ʿAkkāsha al-Fazārī that very night and fought him at al-Qarn. ʿAkkāsha had not yet heard of ʿAbd al-Wāḥid’s defeat. God routed him and his companions, and ʿAkkāsha fled until he reached one of the districts of Ifrīqiya. A group of Berbers took him prisoner and brought him to Ḥanẓala, who killed him.

ʿAbd al-Wāḥid and those with him were Ṣufrīs who considered it lawful to take women captive.

According to what Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth, ʿAkkāsha and ʿAbd al-Wāḥid were killed in the year 125.

When ʿAbd al-Wāḥid had settled at al-Aṣnām and ʿAkkāsha at al-Qarn, and both were near al-Qayrawān, Ḥanẓala had written to his governor in Tripoli, Muʿāwiya ibn Ṣafwān, ordering him to go out to him with the people of Tripoli. Muʿāwiya went out and reached Qābis. News reached him of the defeat of ʿAbd al-Wāḥid and ʿAkkāsha. Ḥanẓala then wrote to him about Berbers who had risen in Nafzāwa and taken the people of its protected community captive, ordering him to march against them. He went out against them with his men and fought them. Muʿāwiya ibn Ṣafwān was killed, as were the Ṣufrīs, and the people of the protected community who had been taken were rescued. Ḥanẓala sent Zayd ibn ʿAmr al-Kalbī to Muʿāwiya’s army, and Zayd returned with them to Tripoli.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb was in Tunis, and Thaʿlaba ibn Salāma al-Judhamī was with Ḥanẓala. When the people of Syria in Ifrīqiya heard that al-Walīd ibn Yazīd had been killed, most of their commanders set out, and Thaʿlaba ibn Salāma went east.

According to what Yaḥyā ibn Bukayr related to us, from al-Layth ibn Saʿd, al-Walīd was killed on Thursday, with three nights remaining in Jumādā II of the year 126.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb set out from Tunis and gathered forces to kill Ḥanẓala ibn Ṣafwān and expel him from Ifrīqiya. When Ḥanẓala heard of this, he sent the leading men of Ifrīqiya to ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, urging him to keep the peace and refrain from fomenting discord. They set out, and when they were partway along the road, news reached them that Marwān ibn Muḥammad had become caliph. They wanted to turn back. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān heard that Ḥanẓala had sent him messengers, who numbered fifty men, and that they intended to turn back. He sent cavalry to them and had them brought to him. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān was displeased with them for having set out to meet him. They had previously corresponded with him in secret, without Ḥanẓala’s knowledge, but when news reached them that Marwān had become caliph, they withdrew from that. He sent them to Tunis in chains.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān wrote to Ḥanẓala, ordering him to surrender al-Qayrawān and leave it. He gave him three days. He wrote to the treasurer, ordering him to give Ḥanẓala neither a dinar nor a dirham, except for the stipends to which he was entitled. When Ḥanẓala read the letter, he considered fighting him, but his scrupulous piety restrained him, for he was a devout man. He left with whichever of his companions from Syria could travel light. This was in Jumādā I of the year 127. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb entered al-Qayrawān in Jumādā II of the year 126.

Then ʿAbd al-Raḥmān appointed his brother Ibn Ḥabīb governor of Tripoli. Ibn Ḥabīb seized ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd al-Tujībī, who was an Ibāḍī and a leader among them, and struck off his head. The Ibāḍīs gathered in Tripoli, so ʿAbd al-Raḥmān dismissed his brother and appointed Ḥumayd ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-ʿAkkī.

When the Ibāḍīs gathered, ʿAbd al-Jabbār ibn Qays al-Murādī was in charge of them, with al-Ḥārith ibn Tulayd al-Ḥaḍramī. They besieged Ḥumayd ibn ʿAbd Allāh in one of the villages of Tripoli. An epidemic broke out among his companions, and he went out under a pact and guarantee of safety. When they came out, ʿAbd al-Jabbār ibn Qays seized Nuṣayr ibn Rāshid, a client of the Anṣār, and killed him. He had been one of Ḥumayd’s companions. They were seeking vengeance for ʿAbd Allāh ibn Masʿūd al-Tujībī, who had been killed. ʿAbd al-Jabbār took control of Zanāta and its territory.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb wrote to Yazīd ibn Ṣafwān al-Muʿāfirī, appointing him governor of Tripoli, and sent Mujāhid ibn Muslim al-Hawwārī to win the people over and turn Hawwāra and others against ʿAbd al-Jabbār.

Mujāhid remained among Hawwāra for several months, then they drove him out. He joined Yazīd ibn Ṣafwān in Tripoli. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb sent Muḥammad ibn Mafrūq with cavalry and wrote to Yazīd ibn Ṣafwān, ordering him to go out with him. They set out, and ʿAbd al-Jabbār ibn Qays and al-Ḥārith ibn Tulayd met them in Hawwāra territory. Yazīd ibn Ṣafwān and Muḥammad ibn Mafrūq were killed, and Mujāhid ibn Muslim fled to Hawwāra territory.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb returned and many men gathered around him. He marched with them against ʿAbd al-Jabbār and al-Ḥārith ibn Tulayd. They met in Zanāta territory, and ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān and his companions were routed. ʿAbd al-Jabbār and al-Ḥārith took control of all Tripoli.

Then ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān went out to Daghūghā with Mujāhid ibn Muslim. Al-Ḥārith pursued him. ʿAmr left Daghūghā for the desert region, and al-Ḥārith caught up with him. ʿAmr continued on to Surt, where al-Ḥārith’s cavalry caught him. They killed some of his companions, but ʿAmr escaped on his horse, wounded. Al-Ḥārith seized his camp. The power of ʿAbd al-Jabbār and al-Ḥārith grew strong. Then their relations turned sour, and the conflict between them intensified. They fought, and both ʿAbd al-Jabbār and al-Ḥārith were killed.

The Berbers appointed Ismāʿīl ibn Ziyād al-Nafūsī over themselves. His standing grew great, and the number pledging allegiance to him increased. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Ḥabīb went out against him. When he reached Qābis, he sent his cousin Shuʿayb ibn ʿUthmān ahead with cavalry. Shuʿayb met Ismāʿīl, and Ismāʿīl and his companions were killed; many Berbers were taken prisoner.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān remained in his camp and did not take part in the battle. When victory opened the way for him, he advanced to the market of Tripoli with the captives. He wrote to ʿAmr ibn ʿUthmān, who came to him from the region of Surt. He brought the captives forward, struck off their heads, and crucified them. He appointed ʿAmr ibn Suwayd al-Murādī over Tripoli and ordered him to distribute booty.

The end of Part Five

The Judges of Egypt

On the Disapproval of Holding Judicial Office

ʿAbd al-ʿAzīz ibn ʿAbd Allāh al-Uwaysī related to us; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar al-Zuhrī related to us, from ʿUthmān ibn Muḥammad al-Akhnasī, from Saʿīd al-Maqburī, from Abū Hurayra, who said: The Messenger of God ﷺ said: “Whoever is appointed judge [and judges] among the people has been slaughtered without a knife.”

Yaʿqūb ibn Muḥammad related to us; Ibrāhīm ibn Saʿd related to us; ʿAbd Allāh ibn Jaʿfar related to us, from ʿUthmān ibn Muḥammad, from al-Aʿraj, from Abū Hurayra, from the Messenger of God ﷺ with the like of it.

My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, and ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ related to us. They said: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Ibn al-ʿAjlān, from al-Ghaḍbān ibn Yazīd al-Bajalī: One of their commanders appointed a man from among them to hold judicial office. The man asked to be excused, but the commander refused. After some time, the man managed to gain access to him and stood before him. He said, “This is the station of one seeking refuge from the Fire.” The commander said, “Woe to you! Do I have any power over the Fire?” The man said, “I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: ‘There are three kinds of judges: a man who judged and failed, thereby destroying people’s property and destroying himself—he is in the Fire; a man who judged knowingly, thereby destroying people’s property and destroying himself—he is in the Fire; and a man who judged with knowledge and acted justly, thereby preserving people’s property and preserving himself—he is in Paradise.’”

Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us; al-Ḥammānī related to us; Khalaf ibn Khalīfa related to us, from Abū Hāshim, from Ibn Burayda, from his father, who said: The Messenger of God ﷺ said: “There are three judges: two are in the Fire and one is in Paradise. A man who possesses knowledge and judges according to what he knows is in Paradise; a man who is ignorant and judges out of ignorance is in the Fire; and a man who judges according to what he does not know is in the Fire.”

Asad ibn Mūsā related to us; Shuʿba ibn al-Ḥajjāj related to us, from Qatāda, who said: I heard Abū al-ʿĀliya mention ʿAlī, whom he had met. He said: “There are three kinds of judges: one is in Paradise and two are in the Fire. As for the one who—” is in Paradise: a man who exercised independent judgment and reached the truth is in Paradise; a man who acted unjustly deliberately is in Hell; and a man who exercised his own judgment and erred is in Hell. I said to Abū al-ʿĀliya: “What sin is there for this man, when he exercised his judgment?” He replied: “If he does not know, why did he sit as a judge, passing judgments?”

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: “Qatāda heard only three ḥadīths from Abū al-ʿĀliya; this is one of them.”

He said: “Ḥaywa ibn Shurayḥ narrated from the client of Ḥassān ibn al-Nuʿmān, from Yaḥyā ibn Abī ʿAmr al-Shaybānī, that he heard him say: Abū Hurayra used to say: ‘Whoever is summoned to adjudicate and accepts, while competent, but rules contrary to the truth is in Hell. Whoever is summoned to adjudicate and accepts, while not competent, but rules contrary to the truth is in Hell. And whoever is summoned to adjudicate while competent, accepts, and rules according to the truth has saved himself.’”

Ḥaywa said: “I was also told, from ʿAbd al-Quddūs ibn Ḥabīb, from al-Ḥasan, that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb said: ‘There are three kinds of judges: a judge who accepted a bribe and perished; a judge who exercised independent judgment and erred, and wished his mother had never borne him; and a judge who exercised independent judgment and reached the right decision, and escaped—though he barely escaped.’”

ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ and Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr narrated to us; they said: al-Layth ibn Saʿd narrated to us, from Ibn al-Hād. And Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār narrated to us: Nāfiʿ ibn Yazīd narrated to us, from Ibn al-Hād. And Nuʿaym ibn Ḥammād narrated to us: al-Darāwardī narrated to us, from Ibn al-Hād, from Muḥammad ibn Ibrāhīm ibn al-Ḥārith al-Taymī, from Bishr ibn Saʿīd, from Abū Qays, the client of ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, from ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, who heard the Messenger of God ﷺ say: “When a judge gives judgment and exercises independent judgment, then reaches the right decision, he has two rewards; and when he gives judgment and exercises independent judgment, then errs, he has one reward”. I related this ḥadīth to Abū Bakr ibn Muḥammad ibn ʿAmr ibn Ḥazm, and he said: “Abū Salama related it to me in just this way, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān, from Abū Hurayra.”

ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslama narrated to us: Ibn Lahīʿa narrated to us, from al-Ḥārith ibn Yazīd, from Salama ibn Aksūm, from Ibn Ḥujayra, who asked al-Qāsim ibn al-Barḥī: “How did you hear ʿAbd Allāh…”

Ibn ʿUmar report? He said: I heard him say: “Two litigants brought their dispute before ʿUmar, and he judged between them. The one against whom judgment was given was displeased, so he went to the Messenger of God ﷺ—and told him. The Messenger of God ﷺ—said: ‘When a judge gives judgment and exercises his judgment, then gets it right, he receives ten rewards. If he exercises his judgment and errs, he receives one or two rewards.’”

Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us: Shabābah ibn Sawwār related to us: al-Faraj ibn Faḍālah related to us, from Rabīʿah ibn Yazīd, from ʿUqbah ibn ʿĀmir al-Juhanī, that two litigants brought their dispute before the Messenger of God ﷺ—and he said: “Judge between them.” I said: “Messenger of God, you have the greater right to judge.” He said: “Even so.” I said: “Then on what basis?” He said: “On the basis that if you exercise your judgment and get it right, you receive ten rewards; and if you exercise your judgment and err, you receive one reward.”

Muḥammad ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us: Muḥammad ibn Kathīr related to us: Isrāʾīl related to us: ʿAbd al-Aʿlā related to us, from Bilāl ibn Abī Mūsā, from Anas ibn Mālik. Al-Ḥajjāj had intended to appoint him judge of al-Baṣrah, and Anas said: I heard the Messenger of God ﷺ—say: “Whoever seeks judicial office and seeks help in attaining it is entrusted to it; and whoever does not seek it or seek help in attaining it, God sends down an angel to guide him aright”.

My father, ʿAbd Allāh ibn ʿAbd al-Ḥakam, related to us: Mālik ibn Anas informed us, from Yaḥyā ibn Saʿīd, from Saʿīd ibn al-Musayyib, that a Muslim and a Jew brought their dispute before ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb. He saw that the right belonged to the Jew and ruled in his favor. The Jew said: “By God, you have judged justly.” ʿUmar struck him with the whip, then said: “How do you know?” The Jew said: “We find that no judge judges justly without an angel at his right and an angel at his left, guiding him aright and granting him success in the truth so long as he remains with the truth. When he abandons the truth, they ascend and leave him.”

Abū al-Aswad al-Naḍr ibn ʿAbd al-Jabbār related to us, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Zayd ibn Aslam, who said:

“When the judges among the Children of Israel were such that no blame from a blamer could deter them in God’s cause, He did not give power over…”

Decay had not touched his body, nor had any creature that eats clothes gnawed his garments, which had dried upon him without decaying. One of their worshippers was in that state. In those days, they would place some of their dead on top of others in houses, and some in coffins. A brother of his came to him and said, “Bring him to me so I may pray over him.” He was brought to him, and there was a creature that had torn through the shroud until it came out of his ear. This grieved him. When he slept, the spirit of his companion came to him and said, “My brother, I saw your grief over the creature that came out of my ear. By God’s grace, it was nothing we need abhor. Two men sat with me: one was to my liking, and the other was not. I listened attentively to the one I liked, and did not listen attentively to the other. Yet, by God’s grace, I brought them both to [uncertain wording: mujlūd al-ḥaqq] in judgment.”

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: According to Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr, the first man appointed as a judge in Egypt in Islam was Qays ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ al-Sahmī. He died, and ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, instructing him to appoint Kaʿb ibn Yasār ibn Ḍanna al-ʿAbsī as judge. Ibn Abī Maryam said: He was the son of the daughter of Khālid ibn Sinān al-ʿAbsī, whom the ʿAbs claim became a prophet during the interval between the Messenger of God ﷺ—and Jesus son of Mary ﵉. There is a lengthy report about Khālid ibn Sinān. Kaʿb refused to accept the judgeship, saying, “I judged in the Jāhiliyyah, and I will not return to it in Islam .”

Saʿīd ibn ʿUfayr related to us; Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, saying: Qays ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ was in Egypt, and ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ appointed him as judge. It has also been said that the first man appointed as a judge in Egypt was Kaʿb ibn Ḍanna, by a letter from ʿUmar, but he refused. God knows best.

Al-Muqriʾ ʿAbd Allāh ibn Yazīd related to us; Ḥaywah ibn Shurayḥ related to us; al-Ḍaḥḥāk ibn Shurayḥil al-Ghāfiqī informed us; ʿAmmār ibn Saʿd al-Tujībī informed them that ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ, instructing him to appoint Kaʿb ibn Ḍanna as judge. ʿAmr sent for him and read him the letter: “ Commander of the Faithful.” Kaʿb said, “By God, after God has delivered him from the affair of the Jāhiliyyah and the ruin that was in it, He will never let him return to it.” He refused to accept the judgeship, so ʿAmr left him to it.

Ibn ʿUfayr said: He had been an arbitrator in the Jāhiliyyah. Kaʿb ibn Ḍannah’s quarter in Egypt was at the Sūq Barbir, in the house known as Dār al-Nakhla.

When Kaʿb refused to accept the judgeship, ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ appointed ʿUthmān ibn Qays ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ to the judgeship.

He said: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb had written to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ instructing him to grant him an allowance in the higher rank.

Shuʿayb ibn al-Layth, ʿAbd Allāh ibn Ṣāliḥ, Yaḥyā ibn ʿAbd Allāh ibn Bukayr, and ʿAbd al-Malik ibn Maslamah related to us; they said: al-Layth ibn Saʿd related to us, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, who said: ʿUmar ibn al-Khaṭṭāb wrote to ʿAmr ibn al-ʿĀṣ: “Grant an allowance of two hundred to each of those with you who pledged allegiance beneath the tree, and grant yourself that amount on account of your governorship. Grant Khārijah ibn Ḥudhāfah an allowance in the higher rank for his courage, and grant ʿUthmān ibn Qays ibn Abī al-ʿĀṣ an allowance in the higher rank for his hospitality.”

He said: ʿAmr summoned Khālid ibn Thābit al-Fahmī to put him in charge of the customs tax, but Khālid asked to be excused from it. Thus, Sharḥabīl ibn Ḥasanah was in charge of the customs tax, and Maslamah ibn Mukhallad was in charge of the mills. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān said: the mills of al-Balqas.

Ibn ʿUfayr related to us; Ibn Lahīʿah related to us, from Ibn Hubayrah, that ʿAmr summoned Khālid ibn Thābit al-Fahmī, the grandfather of Ibn Rifāʿah, to put him in charge of the customs tax. Khālid asked to be excused from it, so ʿAmr said to him, “What do you dislike about it?” He replied, “Kaʿb said: ‘Do not approach the customs tax, for its collector is in the Fire.’”

ʿAlī ibn Maʿbad related to us; ʿUbayd Allāh ibn ʿAmr al-Jazarī related to us, from Muḥammad ibn Isḥāq, from Yazīd ibn Abī Ḥabīb, from ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Tujībī, from ʿUqbah ibn ʿĀmir, that the Messenger of God ﷺ—said: “The collector of the customs tax will not enter Paradise”. ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn ʿAbd Allāh said: It is not he.

ʿAbd al-Raḥmān al-Tujībī—rather, he is ʿAbd al-Raḥmān ibn Shamāsa al-Mahrī; however, this is how ʿAlī ibn Maʿbad related it to us.
